<!-- source: notes/a-level/biology/10-mass-transport-in-animals.md -->

## Mass transport in animals: haemoglobin, circulation, heart and tissue fluid

Mass transport moves substances in bulk around large organisms. This file covers haemoglobin and oxygen dissociation, the mammalian circulatory system, the heart and cardiac cycle, blood vessels and tissue fluid.

### Overview

AS content for most boards. How the heartbeat is started and controlled (SAN, AVN, ECGs) is in file 18. Cardiovascular disease is in file 38. Open circulations and other animals' blood pigments are in file 46. Blood cells, blood pressure measurement and first aid are in file 49.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.3.4.1, RP5. Edexcel A 1.1, 1.3, 1.4, 7.8. Edexcel B 4.4 i to iii, v, vii, 4.5, 4.6. OCR A 3.1.2(a) to (f), (i), (j). OCR B 2.2.1(a) to (c), 2.2.2(a) to (d), 4.1.2(g) to (i). Eduqas C3.2(b) to (h).
- OCR A and Eduqas ask for carbon dioxide transport and the chloride shift (OCR A 3.1.2(i); Eduqas C3.2(f)); included below.
- Edexcel B asks for myoglobin (4.5 iii) and fetal haemoglobin; OCR A for fetal haemoglobin. Included below.
- OCR A and Edexcel B ask for the lymphatic system.
- AQA asks about cardiovascular disease risk data here; that is in file 38 with Edexcel A Topic 1.
- AQA alone uses "required practical 5" for heart dissection; OCR A 3.1.2(e) and PAG2 also require it.

### 3.3.4.1 Mass transport in animals

*Refs: AQA 3.3.4.1, RP5 · Edexcel A 1.1, 1.3, 1.4, 7.8 · Edexcel B 4.4 i to iii, v, vii, 4.5 i to iv, 4.6 i, ii · OCR A 3.1.2(a) to (f), (i), (j) · OCR B 2.2.1(a) to (c), 2.2.2(a) to (d), 4.1.2(g) to (i) · Eduqas C3.2(b) to (h). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- The haemoglobins are a group of chemically similar molecules found in many different organisms. Haemoglobin is a protein with a quaternary structure.
- The role of haemoglobin and red blood cells in transporting oxygen; loading, transport and unloading of oxygen; the oxygen dissociation curve; the cooperative nature of oxygen binding; the effects of carbon dioxide concentration (Bohr effect).
- Many animals are adapted to their environment by having haemoglobin with different affinities for oxygen.
- The general pattern of blood circulation in a mammal, limited to: the coronary arteries; the blood vessels entering and leaving the heart, lungs and kidneys.
- The gross structure of the human heart; pressure and volume changes and associated valve movements during the cardiac cycle.
- The structure of arteries, arterioles and veins in relation to their function; the structure of capillaries and the importance of capillary beds as exchange surfaces.
- The formation of tissue fluid and its return to the circulatory system.
- Analyse and interpret data on the cardiac cycle; calculate cardiac output = stroke volume × heart rate.

#### Explained

**Haemoglobin.** Four polypeptide chains (two α and two β), each with a haem group containing Fe²⁺. Each haem binds one O₂ molecule, so one haemoglobin carries up to four O₂. Oxygen plus haemoglobin forms oxyhaemoglobin. Red blood cells have no nucleus and few organelles, leaving more room for haemoglobin, and a biconcave shape for a large surface area.

- *Loading* (association): in the lungs, where the partial pressure of oxygen (pO₂) is high, haemoglobin has a high affinity and becomes nearly saturated.
- *Unloading* (dissociation): in respiring tissues, where pO₂ is low, haemoglobin releases oxygen.

**The oxygen dissociation curve** plots percentage saturation of haemoglobin against pO₂. It is S-shaped (sigmoid):

1. At low pO₂ the first O₂ molecule binds with difficulty, because the haem groups are buried inside the molecule.
2. Binding of the first O₂ changes the shape (quaternary structure) of the haemoglobin, uncovering the other haem groups. The second and third bind much more easily. This is **cooperative binding**, and explains the steep middle part.
3. Near saturation it gets harder for the fourth O₂ to find a site, so the curve flattens.

The steep middle matters: a small fall in pO₂ in tissues releases a lot of oxygen.

**The Bohr effect.** At high CO₂ concentration (respiring tissues), haemoglobin has a lower affinity for oxygen. CO₂ dissolves to form carbonic acid; the H⁺ ions lower pH and change haemoglobin's shape. The curve shifts to the right. So more oxygen is unloaded where it is most needed. In the lungs, CO₂ is low, the curve is to the left, and loading is easy.

**Haemoglobins of different affinities.**

| Animal | Curve | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Lugworm, llama, animals at altitude | Left of human | Live where pO₂ is low; high affinity lets haemoglobin load enough oxygen |
| Small mammals (mouse) | Right of human | High metabolic rate; lower affinity unloads oxygen more readily |
| Fetus (OCR A, Edexcel B) | Left of adult | Fetal haemoglobin must take oxygen from the mother's haemoglobin across the placenta, where pO₂ is low |
| Myoglobin (Edexcel B) | Far left, hyperbolic (one chain) | Stores oxygen in muscle; releases it only when pO₂ is very low, during hard exercise |

**Carbon dioxide transport** (OCR A, Eduqas, Edexcel B 4.5). About 5% dissolves in plasma; about 10% binds to haemoglobin as carbaminohaemoglobin; about 85% travels as hydrogencarbonate ions:

1. CO₂ diffuses into red blood cells.
2. Carbonic anhydrase catalyses CO₂ + H₂O → H₂CO₃ (carbonic acid).
3. H₂CO₃ dissociates into H⁺ and HCO₃⁻.
4. HCO₃⁻ diffuses out into the plasma. Chloride ions (Cl⁻) move in to balance the charge: the **chloride shift**.
5. H⁺ binds to haemoglobin, forming haemoglobinic acid. This buffers the blood pH and makes haemoglobin release its oxygen (the Bohr effect).

In the lungs the process reverses and CO₂ diffuses out.

**The circulation.** Mammals have a closed, double circulation: blood passes through the heart twice per circuit, once to the lungs (pulmonary) and once to the body (systemic). The heart raises blood pressure again after the lungs, so blood reaches the body quickly at high pressure, while the low pressure in the lungs avoids damage to capillaries there.

Vessels to know:

| Organ | In | Out |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Heart muscle | Coronary arteries (from the aorta) | Coronary veins |
| Lungs | Pulmonary artery (deoxygenated, from right ventricle) | Pulmonary vein (oxygenated, to left atrium) |
| Kidneys | Renal artery | Renal vein |
| Rest of body | Aorta (from left ventricle) | Vena cava (to right atrium) |

**The heart.** Four chambers. The right side pumps deoxygenated blood to the lungs; the left side pumps oxygenated blood to the body. The left ventricle wall is much thicker, because it must pump blood all round the body at high pressure. The atria have thin walls; they only pump blood into the ventricles.

- Atrioventricular valves (tricuspid on the right, bicuspid or mitral on the left) between atria and ventricles, held by tendinous cords so they do not turn inside out.
- Semilunar valves at the base of the aorta and pulmonary artery.
- Valves open when pressure behind them is higher than in front, and close when pressure in front is higher; they stop backflow.
- A septum separates the two sides so oxygenated and deoxygenated blood do not mix.

**The cardiac cycle** (about 0.8 s at rest).

| Stage | What happens | Valves |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Atrial systole | Atria contract; pressure in atria rises above ventricles; blood pushed into ventricles | AV open; semilunar closed |
| Ventricular systole | Ventricles contract from the bottom up; ventricular pressure rises above atrial pressure | AV close (first heart sound "lub"); when ventricular pressure exceeds pressure in the arteries, semilunar valves open and blood is ejected |
| Diastole | Ventricles and atria relax; ventricular pressure falls below arterial pressure | Semilunar close (second sound "dub"); when ventricular pressure falls below atrial pressure, AV valves open and blood flows passively into the ventricles |

On a pressure graph: the AV valves close where ventricular pressure crosses above atrial pressure; the semilunar valves open where ventricular pressure crosses above aortic pressure; they close where it falls below; AV valves open where ventricular pressure falls below atrial pressure.

**Cardiac output** = stroke volume × heart rate. Stroke volume is the volume pumped by one ventricle per beat.

**Blood vessels.**

| Vessel | Structure | Function link |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Artery | Thick muscle layer; thick elastic layer; thick wall; small lumen; folded endothelium; no valves except at the base of the aorta and pulmonary artery | Elastic tissue stretches during systole and recoils during diastole, smoothing flow and keeping pressure high; thick wall resists bursting |
| Arteriole | Thicker muscle layer relative to elastic layer | Muscle contracts (vasoconstriction) or relaxes to control blood flow into capillary beds |
| Capillary | Wall one cell thick (endothelium); narrow lumen; many branches; gaps between cells | Short diffusion distance; large surface area; slow flow; tissue fluid forms through the gaps |
| Vein | Thin muscle and elastic layers; large lumen; valves | Low pressure, so little muscle or elastic tissue needed; valves stop backflow; contraction of surrounding skeletal muscle squeezes blood towards the heart |

**Tissue fluid** surrounds cells and is how substances pass between blood and cells.

1. At the arteriole end of a capillary, hydrostatic pressure (from the heart) is higher than the opposing forces, so water and small molecules (glucose, amino acids, ions, oxygen) are forced out through the gaps: ultrafiltration. Large plasma proteins and blood cells stay in.
2. Proteins in the blood give the plasma a lower water potential (an oncotic pressure).
3. At the venule end, hydrostatic pressure has fallen (fluid has left, and friction lowers it), so water moves back into the capillary by osmosis, carrying CO₂ and urea.
4. Not all fluid returns. The excess drains into lymph capillaries and is carried in lymph vessels, which have valves, back to the blood via ducts that join veins near the heart. Lymph is moved by contraction of nearby muscles.

If blood pressure is high, more fluid is forced out and less returns, so fluid builds up in tissues (oedema). A diet very low in protein also causes oedema, because the plasma water potential is higher.

#### Worked example

1. A person's heart rate is 72 beats min⁻¹ and stroke volume is 70 cm³. Calculate cardiac output in dm³ min⁻¹.

72 × 70 = 5040 cm³ min⁻¹ = 5.04 dm³ min⁻¹.

2. On a cardiac cycle graph, one cycle lasts 0.75 s. Calculate the heart rate.

60 ÷ 0.75 = 80 beats min⁻¹.

3. At pO₂ = 12 kPa haemoglobin is 98% saturated; at 3 kPa it is 40% saturated. Each 100 cm³ of fully saturated blood carries 20 cm³ of oxygen. How much oxygen does 100 cm³ of blood unload?

(98 − 40)% of 20 = 0.58 × 20 = 11.6 cm³.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying a shift to the right means "higher affinity". It means lower affinity: at any pO₂, less oxygen is bound.
- Saying the first oxygen binds easily. The first is hardest; then cooperative binding makes the next ones easier.
- Saying the left ventricle is thicker "to pump more blood". Both ventricles pump the same volume; the left pumps at higher pressure.
- Saying arteries "pump" blood. Their elastic recoil maintains pressure; the heart pumps.
- Saying "valves open because the heart contracts". Valves move because of pressure differences.
- Saying tissue fluid returns by "diffusion". Water returns by osmosis.
- Saying pulmonary veins carry deoxygenated blood.

#### Practical

**Heart dissection** (AQA RP5, OCR A 3.1.2(e) and PAG2).

1. Identify the front (ventral) side: the coronary arteries run diagonally across it.
2. Find the aorta, pulmonary artery, vena cava and pulmonary veins at the top. Push a blunt probe or glass rod into vessels to find which chamber each joins.
3. Cut through the wall of the right ventricle from the pulmonary artery downwards; then do the same on the left. Compare wall thicknesses.
4. Find the AV valves and tendinous cords, and the semilunar valves (cup-shaped pockets) at the base of the arteries.
5. Make a labelled drawing.

Safety: scalpel cuts away from the body; gloves; disinfect surfaces; wash hands. Ethics: use hearts from animals killed for food.

### Check questions

1. Explain the S-shape of the oxygen dissociation curve. (3 marks)
2. Explain how the Bohr effect helps supply oxygen to muscles during exercise. (3 marks)
3. The lugworm lives in burrows where water has a low oxygen concentration. Its haemoglobin dissociation curve is to the left of human haemoglobin. Explain the advantage. (2 marks)
4. Describe the events of ventricular systole, including the valves. (3 marks)
5. Explain how the structure of an artery relates to its function. (3 marks)
6. Explain how tissue fluid is formed and how most of it returns to the blood. (5 marks)
7. A person's cardiac output is 6.3 dm³ min⁻¹ and heart rate 90 beats min⁻¹. Calculate stroke volume in cm³. (2 marks)
8. Describe the role of carbonic anhydrase and explain the chloride shift. (3 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. First O₂ binds with difficulty (haem groups not exposed) (1); binding changes quaternary structure / shape, making other sites easier to bind: cooperative binding, giving the steep part (1); last O₂ harder because few sites left, so curve levels off (1).
2. Muscles respire faster, releasing more CO₂ (1); CO₂ / H⁺ lowers haemoglobin's affinity / shape changes / curve shifts right (1); more oxygen unloaded to muscles (1).
3. Haemoglobin has higher affinity for oxygen (1); so can load / become saturated at low pO₂ (1).
4. Ventricles contract and pressure rises (1); AV valves close when ventricular pressure exceeds atrial pressure (1); semilunar valves open when ventricular pressure exceeds arterial pressure; blood leaves (1).
5. Elastic tissue stretches and recoils, smoothing flow / maintaining pressure (1); thick muscle / wall withstands high pressure (1); folded endothelium allows stretching; small lumen maintains pressure (1).
6. High hydrostatic pressure at arteriole end (1); forces water and small molecules out through gaps (1); large proteins / cells remain, so plasma has lower water potential (1); at venule end hydrostatic pressure lower, so water returns by osmosis (1); excess drains into lymph (1).
7. 6300 cm³ ÷ 90 (1) = 70 cm³ (1).
8. Carbonic anhydrase catalyses CO₂ + water → carbonic acid (1); which dissociates to H⁺ and HCO₃⁻; HCO₃⁻ diffuses out of red blood cell (1); Cl⁻ moves in to balance charge (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/biology/03-nucleic-acids-atp-water-and-inorganic-ions.md -->

## Nucleic acids, DNA replication, ATP, water and inorganic ions

DNA and RNA carry genetic information, ATP carries energy, and water and ions make life's chemistry possible. This file covers their structures and properties and how DNA copies itself.

### Overview

AS content. How DNA codes for proteins (transcription and translation) is in file 12.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.1.5 to 3.1.8. Edexcel A 1.2, 2.5, 2.11, 5.6. Edexcel B 1.4 i, ii, 1.6, 1.7, 4.2 vi. OCR A 2.1.2(a), (p), 2.1.3(a) to (e). OCR B 2.1.2(a), 2.1.4(a) to (d). Eduqas CC1(a), (b), CC5(a) to (h), C1.1(a).
- Edexcel A, Edexcel B and Eduqas ask for the Meselson–Stahl experiment by name. AQA asks you to evaluate the work that validated the Watson–Crick model, which is the same experiment.
- OCR A asks for purines and pyrimidines and a practical: purifying DNA by precipitation (2.1.3(d)).
- OCR A lists more ions: Ca²⁺, Na⁺, K⁺, H⁺, NH₄⁺, NO₃⁻, HCO₃⁻, Cl⁻, PO₄³⁻, OH⁻. Eduqas lists Mg²⁺, Fe²⁺, Ca²⁺, PO₄³⁻. Edexcel B asks for plant ions (nitrate, calcium, magnesium, phosphate) and animal ions; plant mineral uses are expanded in file 41.

### 3.1.5.1 Structure of DNA and RNA

*Refs: AQA 3.1.5.1 · Edexcel A 2.5 · Edexcel B 1.4 i · OCR A 2.1.3(a), (b), (d) · OCR B 2.1.4(a), (c) · Eduqas CC5(a), (e) to (g). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- DNA holds genetic information; RNA transfers it from DNA to the ribosomes. Ribosomes are made of RNA and protein.
- DNA and RNA are polymers of nucleotides. A nucleotide = a pentose sugar + a nitrogen-containing base + a phosphate group.
- DNA nucleotide: deoxyribose, phosphate, and adenine, cytosine, guanine or thymine.
- RNA nucleotide: ribose, phosphate, and adenine, cytosine, guanine or uracil.
- Nucleotides join by condensation, forming phosphodiester bonds.
- DNA is a double helix of two polynucleotide strands held by hydrogen bonds between complementary base pairs. RNA is a relatively short single strand.
- DNA's simplicity made many scientists doubt it could carry the genetic code.

#### Explained

**The backbone.** The phosphate of one nucleotide bonds to the sugar of the next, giving a sugar–phosphate backbone with the bases sticking out.

**Pairing.** A pairs with T (two hydrogen bonds); C pairs with G (three hydrogen bonds). Adenine and guanine are purines (two rings); cytosine, thymine and uracil are pyrimidines (one ring). Each pair is one purine plus one pyrimidine, so the rungs are all the same width and the helix is regular.

**Antiparallel strands.** The two strands run in opposite directions (one 5′ to 3′, the other 3′ to 5′).

**Why DNA suits its job.**

- Very long, so it stores a lot of information.
- Two strands, each a template, so it can be copied accurately.
- Bases protected inside the helix by the backbone; many hydrogen bonds make it stable.
- Base sequence can vary without limit, so it can code for any protein.

**DNA compared with RNA** (Eduqas):

| | DNA | RNA |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Sugar | Deoxyribose | Ribose |
| Bases | A, T, C, G | A, U, C, G |
| Strands | Two, double helix | One |
| Length | Very long | Short |

**Why scientists doubted DNA.** It has only four kinds of nucleotide, while proteins have 20 amino acids. Proteins seemed the more likely carrier of complex information until experiments in the 1940s and 1950s showed DNA was the genetic material.

**Purifying DNA** (OCR A practical). Grind tissue (onion or kiwi) with a solution of detergent and salt; detergent breaks membranes, salt helps the DNA clump. Add protease to break down histone proteins. Filter, then pour ice-cold ethanol gently down the side. DNA is insoluble in cold ethanol and precipitates as white strands at the boundary.

#### Worked example

In a DNA sample, 21% of bases are thymine. What percentage are guanine?

1. A pairs with T, so A = 21%. A + T = 42%.
2. C + G = 100 − 42 = 58%.
3. C = G, so G = 29%.

Answer: 29%.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying DNA contains ribose or uracil.
- Saying the two strands are held by "phosphodiester bonds". Strands are held together by hydrogen bonds; phosphodiester bonds are within each strand.
- Using base pairing rules for RNA in a single strand. In single-stranded RNA there is no rule that A = U.

### 3.1.5.2 DNA replication

*Refs: AQA 3.1.5.2 · Edexcel A 2.11 · Edexcel B 1.4 ii · OCR A 2.1.3(e) · OCR B 2.1.4(d) · Eduqas CC5(h). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Semi-conservative replication keeps genetic information the same between generations of cells.
- Steps: DNA helicase unwinds the double helix and breaks the hydrogen bonds between bases; free DNA nucleotides are attracted to exposed complementary bases on both template strands; DNA polymerase joins adjacent nucleotides by condensation, forming phosphodiester bonds.
- Evaluate the work that confirmed the Watson–Crick model.

#### Explained

Semi-conservative means each new molecule has one original strand and one new strand.

DNA polymerase can only add nucleotides in one direction (to the 3′ end of the growing strand). Because the strands are antiparallel, one new strand is made continuously and the other in short fragments that are later joined by ligase. (Only OCR and Edexcel B mention this; it is useful for explaining why the active site of DNA polymerase is specific to one orientation.)

**Meselson and Stahl (1958).** Two models were possible: conservative (the original molecule stays whole; the copy is entirely new) and semi-conservative.

1. Bacteria were grown for many generations on a medium with heavy nitrogen (¹⁵N), so all their DNA was heavy.
2. They were moved to light nitrogen (¹⁴N) and allowed to divide once. DNA was extracted and centrifuged in a solution that forms a density gradient.
3. After one generation, there was one band at intermediate density. This ruled out conservative replication, which would give one heavy and one light band.
4. After two generations, there were two bands, intermediate and light, in equal amounts. This is exactly what semi-conservative replication predicts.

Why it convinced people: a clear prediction from each model, a single variable changed, a result that matched only one model, and others could repeat it.

#### Worked example

Bacteria with all-¹⁵N DNA are moved to ¹⁴N and divide three times. What fraction of DNA molecules are hybrid (¹⁵N/¹⁴N)?

1. One molecule becomes 2, then 4, then 8.
2. The two original heavy strands are always in separate molecules: 2 hybrid molecules.
3. Fraction = 2 ÷ 8 = 1/4. The other 3/4 are light.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying DNA polymerase breaks hydrogen bonds or pairs bases. Helicase separates strands; bases pair by themselves; polymerase forms phosphodiester bonds.
- Saying DNA polymerase forms hydrogen bonds.
- Saying "half the DNA is new" instead of "each molecule has one old and one new strand".

### 3.1.6 ATP

*Refs: AQA 3.1.6 · Edexcel A 5.6 · Edexcel B 4.2 vi, 5.4 iv · OCR A 2.1.3(c) · OCR B 2.1.4(b) · Eduqas CC5(b) to (d), C1.1(a). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- ATP (adenosine triphosphate) is a nucleotide derivative: ribose + adenine + three phosphate groups.
- ATP hydrolase catalyses ATP + water → ADP + Pi (inorganic phosphate), releasing energy.
- Hydrolysis of ATP can be coupled to energy-requiring reactions.
- The Pi released can phosphorylate other compounds, often making them more reactive.
- ATP is remade by condensation of ADP and Pi, catalysed by ATP synthase, during photosynthesis and respiration.

#### Explained

ATP is the cell's immediate energy source, not a long-term store. Why it suits this:

- Energy is released in small, manageable amounts, so little is wasted as heat.
- Hydrolysis is a single, fast reaction.
- ATP is soluble and moves easily within the cell.
- It is quickly remade.
- It can phosphorylate substances, such as glucose at the start of glycolysis, making them more reactive.

Uses: active transport (changing the shape of carrier proteins), muscle contraction, DNA and protein synthesis, nerve impulse recovery, secretion by exocytosis.

ATP cannot pass out of cells, so every cell makes its own. A person turns over roughly their own body mass in ATP each day, which shows it is constantly recycled.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying "breaking the bond releases energy". Bond breaking needs energy; the hydrolysis reaction as a whole releases energy.
- Saying ATP is "a store of energy" like glycogen.
- Calling ATP synthase "ATP hydrolase" or the reverse.

### 3.1.7 Water

*Refs: AQA 3.1.7 · Edexcel A 1.2 · Edexcel B 1.7 i · OCR A 2.1.2(a) · OCR B 2.1.2(a) · Eduqas CC1(b). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

Water is a major component of cells. It:

- is a metabolite in many reactions, including condensation and hydrolysis
- is an important solvent in which metabolic reactions occur
- has a relatively high specific heat capacity, buffering temperature changes
- has a relatively large latent heat of vaporisation, giving cooling with little water lost
- has strong cohesion between molecules, which supports columns of water in xylem and gives surface tension.

#### Explained

All of these come from water being polar. Oxygen pulls electrons more strongly than hydrogen, so O is slightly negative (δ−) and each H slightly positive (δ+). Hydrogen bonds form between the O of one molecule and the H of another.

| Property | Cause | Importance |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Solvent | Polar molecules and ions dissolve as water clusters around them | Reactions happen in solution; transport in blood, xylem and phloem |
| High specific heat capacity | Hydrogen bonds absorb a lot of energy before molecules speed up | Stable temperature in cells and aquatic habitats |
| High latent heat of vaporisation | Many hydrogen bonds must break to evaporate water | Sweating and transpiration cool organisms well |
| Cohesion | Hydrogen bonds between water molecules | Continuous columns in xylem; surface tension lets small insects walk on ponds |
| Ice is less dense than liquid water (OCR, Edexcel B) | Hydrogen bonds hold molecules apart in a lattice | Ice floats and insulates water below |
| Metabolite | | Hydrolysis, photosynthesis (photolysis) |

#### Common mistakes

- Saying water molecules are held by "hydrogen bonds within the molecule". Hydrogen bonds are between molecules.
- Saying high specific heat capacity means water "heats up quickly". It is the opposite.

### 3.1.8 Inorganic ions

*Refs: AQA 3.1.8 · Edexcel B 1.6 i · OCR A 2.1.2(p) · Eduqas CC1(a). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Inorganic ions are in solution in the cytoplasm and body fluids, some in high and some in very low concentrations. Each has a specific role.
- Recognise their roles in: hydrogen ions and pH; iron ions in haemoglobin; sodium ions in co-transport of glucose and amino acids; phosphate ions in DNA and ATP.

#### Explained

| Ion | Role |
| --- | --- |
| H⁺ | Determines pH, which affects enzyme shape; proton gradients drive ATP synthesis; Bohr effect |
| Fe²⁺ | In haem, binds oxygen in haemoglobin |
| Na⁺ | Co-transport of glucose and amino acids into epithelial cells; nerve impulses |
| PO₄³⁻ | Part of nucleotides, DNA, RNA, ATP and phospholipids |
| Ca²⁺ (OCR A, Eduqas, Edexcel B) | Neurotransmitter release; muscle contraction; blood clotting; bone; calcium pectate in plant middle lamella |
| K⁺ (OCR A) | Nerve impulses; stomatal opening |
| Cl⁻ (OCR A) | Chloride shift; cofactor for amylase |
| HCO₃⁻ (OCR A) | Carries CO₂ in blood; buffers pH |
| NO₃⁻, NH₄⁺ (OCR A, Edexcel B) | Nitrogen source for amino acids and nucleotides in plants |
| Mg²⁺ (Eduqas, Edexcel B) | Part of chlorophyll |
| OH⁻ (OCR A) | Affects pH; involved in bonding in proteins |

#### Common mistakes

- Saying iron is in the globin chains. It is in the haem group.
- Writing "sodium" where "sodium ions" is needed; ions carry the charge that makes them work.

### Check questions

1. Give two differences between a DNA nucleotide and an RNA nucleotide. (2 marks)
2. A DNA molecule has 1800 bases in total, and 20% are cytosine. How many adenine bases are there? (2 marks)
3. Describe the roles of DNA helicase and DNA polymerase in replication. (3 marks)
4. Meselson and Stahl moved ¹⁵N bacteria to ¹⁴N medium. Explain why the result after one generation ruled out conservative replication but did not by itself prove semi-conservative replication. (3 marks)
5. Give three properties of ATP that make it suited to being the immediate energy source in cells. (3 marks)
6. Explain how sweating cools the body. (2 marks)
7. Explain how cohesion between water molecules helps transport in plants. (2 marks)
8. State the role of each ion: (a) iron ions in red blood cells; (b) phosphate ions in DNA. (2 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Any two (1 each): deoxyribose vs ribose; thymine vs uracil; (accept: DNA nucleotides form double-stranded polymer).
2. C = G = 20%, so A + T = 60%, A = 30% (1); 0.30 × 1800 = 540 (1).
3. Helicase unwinds the helix and breaks hydrogen bonds between bases (1); free nucleotides pair with complementary bases on each template strand (1); DNA polymerase joins adjacent nucleotides by condensation / phosphodiester bonds (1).
4. Conservative would give one heavy and one light band (1); a single intermediate band was found (1); but dispersive replication (mixed segments) would also give intermediate DNA, so the second generation was needed (1).
5. Any three: releases energy in small amounts; single hydrolysis step, so quick; soluble / easily moved; quickly resynthesised; can phosphorylate other molecules.
6. Water evaporates using latent heat from the skin (1); high latent heat of vaporisation, so a lot of heat is removed for little water lost (1).
7. Hydrogen bonds between water molecules (1); water pulled up as a continuous column when water evaporates from leaves (1).
8. (a) Part of haem / haemoglobin, binds oxygen (1). (b) Part of nucleotides / sugar-phosphate backbone (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/biology/38-cardiovascular-disease-diet-and-risk.md -->

## Cardiovascular disease, diet and health risk

Cardiovascular disease (CVD) is the leading cause of death in the UK. This file covers atherosclerosis, blood clotting, risk factors, diet and energy balance, blood cholesterol, how to judge evidence about health risks, treatments, and Edexcel A core practicals 1 and 2.

### Overview

AS content. The heart and blood vessels are in file 10. Measuring blood pressure is in file 49. AQA asks students to interpret data on CVD risk factors (3.3.4.1); the reasoning skills here apply.

#### Board differences

- Edexcel A 1.5 to 1.11, 1.15 to 1.18, CP1, CP2. Edexcel B 4.4 viii, ix. OCR B 2.2.2(e). Eduqas CC1(g).
- Edexcel A treats this as a whole topic (Lifestyle, Health and Risk), including energy budgets, BMI, perception of risk and evaluating studies.
- Edexcel B asks for clotting and atherosclerosis. OCR B asks how to interpret systolic and diastolic blood pressure. Eduqas asks for the effects of saturated and unsaturated fats on health.
- AQA's equivalent skill (3.3.4.1) is to analyse and interpret data on risk factors and CVD, and evaluate conflicting evidence.

### Atherosclerosis and blood clotting

*Refs: Edexcel A 1.5, 1.6 · Edexcel B 4.4 viii, ix. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- The course of events that leads to atherosclerosis: endothelial damage, inflammatory response, plaque formation, raised blood pressure.
- How atherosclerosis can lead to CVD (heart disease, stroke).
- The blood-clotting process: thromboplastin release, prothrombin to thrombin, fibrinogen to fibrin.

#### Explained

**Atherosclerosis.**

1. The endothelium (inner lining) of an artery is damaged, for example by high blood pressure, chemicals in tobacco smoke or high LDL.
2. An inflammatory response: white blood cells (macrophages) move into the artery wall and take up LDL cholesterol.
3. Cholesterol, calcium salts and fibrous tissue build up into a hard **plaque (atheroma)** under the endothelium.
4. The lumen narrows and the artery wall becomes less elastic, so blood pressure rises; this causes more damage elsewhere: positive feedback.
5. A plaque may rupture, causing a blood clot (thrombosis).

**Consequences.**

- *Coronary heart disease:* narrowed coronary arteries reduce oxygen to heart muscle (angina, chest pain on exercise). If a clot blocks a coronary artery, heart muscle downstream dies: a **myocardial infarction** (heart attack).
- *Stroke:* a clot blocks an artery to the brain, or a weakened artery bursts.
- *Aneurysm:* an artery wall weakened by plaque bulges and may burst.
- Peripheral artery disease (legs).

**Blood clotting.**

1. Damage to a blood vessel exposes collagen; platelets stick to it and release **thromboplastin** (an enzyme); damaged tissue also releases it.
2. Thromboplastin, with calcium ions and vitamin K, catalyses the conversion of **prothrombin** (a soluble plasma protein) to **thrombin** (an enzyme).
3. Thrombin catalyses the conversion of soluble **fibrinogen** to insoluble **fibrin**.
4. Fibrin forms a mesh of threads that traps platelets and red blood cells, forming a clot.

The cascade amplifies a small signal into a fast response. Clotting inside an intact artery, over a ruptured plaque, is dangerous.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying fatty deposits build up "in the lumen" rather than in the artery wall under the endothelium.
- Saying fibrinogen is an enzyme.

### Risk factors, diet and energy balance

*Refs: Edexcel A 1.7, 1.11, 1.15, 1.16 · OCR B 2.2.2(e) · Eduqas CC1(g). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- How factors such as genetics, diet, age, sex, high blood pressure, smoking and inactivity increase the risk of CVD.
- Analyse data on energy budgets and diet; the consequences of energy imbalance; BMI and waist-to-hip ratio as indicators of obesity.
- The possible significance for health of blood cholesterol levels (HDL and LDL); the effects of saturated and unsaturated fats.
- How people use scientific knowledge to reduce their risk.
- Interpret systolic and diastolic blood pressure (OCR B).

#### Explained

**Risk factors.**

| Factor | Why |
| --- | --- |
| Genetics | Inherited alleles affect cholesterol levels, blood pressure and artery structure |
| Age | Arteries become less elastic; plaques build up over time |
| Sex | Men are at higher risk before middle age; oestrogen may protect women until the menopause |
| High blood pressure | Damages endothelium; heart works harder |
| Smoking | Carbon monoxide binds haemoglobin (less oxygen carried); nicotine raises heart rate and blood pressure and makes platelets stickier; chemicals damage the endothelium |
| Diet | High saturated fat raises LDL; high salt raises blood pressure; high energy intake causes obesity |
| Inactivity | Obesity, high blood pressure, raised LDL |
| Obesity and diabetes | Raised blood pressure, cholesterol and glucose damage arteries |
| Alcohol (heavy use) | Raises blood pressure |

Risk factors that cannot be changed: age, sex, genes. Those that can: diet, smoking, exercise, weight, alcohol.

**Energy balance.** Energy budget = energy in (food) − energy out (basal metabolic rate + activity). If intake exceeds use, the extra is stored as fat and mass increases; if use exceeds intake, mass decreases.

**Indicators of obesity.**

- **BMI** = mass (kg) ÷ height² (m²). 18.5 to 24.9 healthy; 25 to 29.9 overweight; 30 or more obese. Does not distinguish fat from muscle.
- **Waist-to-hip ratio** = waist circumference ÷ hip circumference. Above about 0.9 (men) or 0.85 (women) shows fat stored around the abdomen, which carries more risk.

**Cholesterol and lipoproteins.** Cholesterol is carried in the blood in lipoproteins (lipid plus protein).

- **LDL** (low-density lipoprotein): more lipid, less protein. Carries cholesterol to tissues; high LDL is associated with plaque formation ("bad").
- **HDL** (high-density lipoprotein): more protein. Carries cholesterol from tissues back to the liver for removal; high HDL is associated with lower risk ("good").
- Diets high in **saturated fats** raise LDL; **unsaturated fats** (especially polyunsaturated, in vegetable oils and oily fish) lower LDL and may raise HDL. **Trans fats** (partially hydrogenated oils) are particularly harmful. (Eduqas CC1(g).)

**Blood pressure** (OCR B 2.2.2(e)). Measured with a sphygmomanometer (file 49) in mmHg, as systolic (pressure in arteries when the ventricles contract) over diastolic (pressure when the heart relaxes). Around 120/80 is normal; 140/90 or above is high blood pressure (hypertension); below 90/60 is low. High blood pressure damages arteries, kidneys and the heart, often without symptoms.

**Reducing risk** (Edexcel A 1.16). People use knowledge of risk to make choices: stopping smoking, eating less saturated fat and salt, more fruit, vegetables and fibre, exercising, keeping a healthy weight; taking statins if advised. Knowing the risk does not always change behaviour; habits, cost, culture and how people perceive risk all matter.

#### Worked example

1. A person is 1.75 m tall and has a mass of 84 kg. BMI = 84 ÷ 1.75² = 84 ÷ 3.0625 = 27.4 (overweight).

2. A man's daily intake is 11 500 kJ; his basal metabolic rate and activity use 10 300 kJ. Energy surplus = 1200 kJ per day. If 1 kg of fat stores about 37 000 kJ, how long to gain 1 kg? 37 000 ÷ 1200 ≈ 31 days.

### Evaluating evidence about health risks

*Refs: Edexcel A 1.8, 1.9, 1.10. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Analyse quantitative data on illness and mortality rates; distinguish correlation and causation.
- Evaluate the design of studies of health risk factors (sample selection, sample size, controls, validity, reliability).
- Why people's perceptions of risk often differ from the actual risk.

#### Explained

**Risk** is the probability of an event, often expressed as a percentage, or as relative risk (how many times more likely in one group than another). Example: if lung cancer death rates are 1 in 1000 per year for non-smokers and 20 in 1000 for smokers, the relative risk is 20.

**Study designs.**

- *Cohort (prospective) studies:* follow a large group over years, recording lifestyle and who develops disease. Can show the order of events, but slow and costly.
- *Case-control (retrospective) studies:* compare people with the disease with similar people without it, looking back at past exposure. Quicker, but relies on memory.
- *Randomised controlled trials:* assign people randomly to treatment or control; best for showing cause, but not ethical for harmful exposures.

**Judging a study.** Is the sample large and representative (age, sex, ethnicity)? Were other variables controlled or matched? Were measurements reliable and objective? Are confidence intervals or statistical tests given? Has it been peer-reviewed and repeated? Who funded it?

**Correlation and cause.** A correlation between, say, coffee drinking and heart disease may be due to a confounding factor (coffee drinkers smoking more). A causal link needs a plausible mechanism and consistent evidence from different types of study.

**Perception of risk** (Edexcel A 1.10). People overestimate risks that are rare but dramatic, unfamiliar, out of their control or widely reported (plane crashes), and underestimate common, familiar risks they choose (driving, smoking, poor diet).

### Treatments for CVD

*Refs: Edexcel A 1.18. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- The benefits and risks of treatments for CVD: antihypertensives, statins, anticoagulants and platelet inhibitors.

#### Explained

| Treatment | How it works | Benefits | Risks |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Antihypertensives (diuretics, beta-blockers, ACE inhibitors, calcium channel blockers) | Lower blood pressure: remove more water in urine; reduce heart rate and force; widen arteries | Less damage to endothelium; lower risk of heart attack and stroke | Dizziness, tiredness, cough; some interact with other drugs |
| Statins | Inhibit an enzyme in the liver that makes cholesterol; lower LDL | Reduce plaque formation and heart attacks | Muscle pain; rare liver damage; possible slight increase in diabetes risk |
| Anticoagulants (warfarin, heparin) | Reduce clotting (warfarin blocks vitamin K action) | Prevent clots, strokes | Excessive bleeding; need monitoring |
| Platelet inhibitors (aspirin, clopidogrel) | Make platelets less sticky | Prevent clots forming on plaques | Stomach bleeding; allergic reactions |

Other treatments: angioplasty and stents (widening arteries), bypass surgery, lifestyle changes.

### Edexcel A core practicals 1 and 2

*Refs: Edexcel A 1.17, CP1, CP2. Level: AS & A.*

#### Practical

**CP1: the effect of caffeine on the heart rate of *Daphnia*.**

1. Place a water flea (*Daphnia*) in a drop of pond water on a cavity slide, with cotton wool fibres to restrict movement.
2. Let it acclimatise for a few minutes; view under low power; count heartbeats for 15 seconds (using a tally or recording video and slowing it), and multiply by 4.
3. Replace the water with a caffeine solution; wait the same time; count again. Use a range of caffeine concentrations, a fresh *Daphnia* for each, and repeat.

Control variables: temperature (the light of the microscope warms the slide, so limit the time); size and age of *Daphnia*; volume of solution; time in solution. Ethics (Edexcel A 1.17): *Daphnia* are invertebrates with a simple nervous system and are thought to be less aware of pain; they are plentiful; but they should be handled gently, used for as short a time as possible and returned to pond water. Some people object to using any animal.

**CP2: the vitamin C content of food and drink.**

1. DCPIP is a blue dye that vitamin C (ascorbic acid, a reducing agent) turns colourless.
2. Add a fixed volume of DCPIP (1 cm³) to a test tube. Add a known concentration of vitamin C solution drop by drop from a burette or pipette, shaking, until the blue colour just disappears. Record the volume needed.
3. Repeat for a range of known concentrations to make a calibration curve of volume needed against concentration.
4. Repeat with fruit juices; read their concentration from the curve.

Control variables: same volume and concentration of DCPIP; same shaking; judge the end point against a white background. Limitations: coloured juices make the end point hard to see; vitamin C oxidises in air, so use fresh samples.

### Check questions

1. Describe the events that lead to the formation of an atheroma. (4 marks)
2. Explain how atherosclerosis in a coronary artery can cause a heart attack. (3 marks)
3. Describe the role of thrombin in blood clotting. (2 marks)
4. Explain why high LDL and low HDL increase the risk of CVD. (3 marks)
5. Calculate the BMI of a person with mass 68 kg and height 1.62 m, and give one limitation of BMI. (3 marks)
6. Explain why a correlation between a factor and CVD does not show that the factor causes CVD. (2 marks)
7. Give one benefit and one risk of statins and of anticoagulants. (4 marks)
8. In CP1, explain why each *Daphnia* should be left to acclimatise and why the time under the microscope is limited. (2 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Endothelium damaged (by high blood pressure / smoking) (1); inflammatory response / white blood cells enter wall (1); LDL / cholesterol taken up and builds up (1); with calcium salts / fibrous tissue, forming plaque (1).
2. Plaque narrows lumen / ruptures (1); clot forms and blocks coronary artery (1); heart muscle receives no oxygen / cannot respire aerobically, and dies (1).
3. Thrombin is an enzyme formed from prothrombin (1); converts soluble fibrinogen to insoluble fibrin (1).
4. LDL carries cholesterol to tissues / associated with plaque formation (1); HDL carries cholesterol to the liver for removal (1); so high LDL / low HDL means more cholesterol deposited in arteries (1).
5. 68 ÷ 1.62² = 25.9 (2); limitation: does not distinguish fat from muscle / ignores fat distribution (1).
6. May be due to another (confounding) factor (1); a mechanism / controlled experiments are needed to show cause (1).
7. Statins: benefit lower LDL / fewer heart attacks (1); risk muscle pain / liver damage (1). Anticoagulants: benefit fewer clots / strokes (1); risk excessive bleeding (1).
8. Acclimatise so heart rate is not raised by handling / new conditions (1); microscope lamp warms the water, and temperature affects heart rate (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/biology/26-practical-skills-and-statistics.md -->

## Practical skills, data handling and statistical tests

Every A-level Biology course tests practical skills in the written papers and through a practical endorsement. This file covers planning, carrying out, analysing and evaluating experiments, the statistical tests the boards use, and a list of each board's required practicals with the file that covers each one.

### Overview

AS and A-level, all boards. OCR A Module 1 and OCR B Module 1 are written out as spec content; the other boards list the same skills in an appendix. Chi-squared is worked through in file 21 and diversity indices in file 14.

#### Board differences

- OCR A 1.1.1 to 1.1.4 (planning, implementing, analysis, evaluation) and 1.2.1, 1.2.2 (practical endorsement and the practical activity groups, PAG1 to PAG12). OCR B 1.1 and 1.2 (the same structure).
- AQA has 12 required practicals (RP1 to RP12), Edexcel A 18 core practicals (CP1 to CP18), Edexcel B 16 (CP1 to CP16), and Eduqas a list of specified practical work in each topic. All boards share the Common Practical Assessment Criteria (CPAC) for the practical endorsement.
- Statistical tests named by boards: Student's t-test (OCR A 4.2.2(f), Edexcel B 10.1 vi), Spearman's rank correlation (Edexcel B 10.1 vi, OCR A), chi-squared (all boards), standard deviation (all). AQA lists the tests in its mathematical requirements and expects students to choose the right test.
- Edexcel A 1.8 and 1.9 ask how to interpret health data and evaluate study design (correlation and cause); Edexcel A 5.18 and Edexcel B 3.1 vi and 10.4 iv ask how the scientific community validates findings (journals, peer review, conferences).

### Planning, implementing, analysing and evaluating

*Refs: OCR A 1.1.1 to 1.1.4, 1.2.1, 1.2.2 · OCR B 1.1, 1.2 · Edexcel A 1.8, 5.18 · Edexcel B 3.1 vi, 10.4 iv. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Plan an experiment: identify independent, dependent and control variables; choose apparatus and techniques; write a hypothesis; consider risks and ethics.
- Carry out experiments safely using a wide range of apparatus and techniques; record results in suitable tables.
- Process and analyse data: calculate means, rates, percentages, percentage change; plot graphs; find gradients and intercepts; use statistical tests.
- Evaluate: identify anomalies; consider accuracy, precision, repeatability, reproducibility and validity; discuss limitations and improvements; calculate uncertainty.
- Recognise how scientific findings are validated by the scientific community.

#### Explained

**Variables.**

- *Independent:* the one you change.
- *Dependent:* the one you measure.
- *Control variables:* others that could affect the result, kept the same.
- *Control experiment:* a set-up without the independent variable (or with it set to zero), to show that the effect is due to it. For example, boiled enzyme; tube with no organisms.

**Key terms.**

| Term | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| Accurate | Close to the true value |
| Precise | Repeated readings are close together (small spread) |
| Repeatable | The same person, method and equipment give similar results |
| Reproducible | Different people or methods give similar results |
| Valid | The experiment measures what it claims to, with control variables kept constant |
| Anomaly | A result that does not fit the pattern; investigate it and exclude it from the mean if there is a reason |
| Uncertainty | The range within which the true value probably lies; usually ± half the smallest division of the instrument |

**Percentage uncertainty** = (uncertainty ÷ measured value) × 100. A burette read to ±0.05 cm³ for a 10.00 cm³ reading: 0.5%.

**Tables:** independent variable in the first column; headings with units in the heading, not in the cells; consistent decimal places matching the instrument's resolution.

**Graphs:** independent variable on x; dependent on y; label both axes with units; use more than half the grid; plot points accurately with small crosses; draw a line or curve of best fit (or join points with straight lines if intermediate values cannot be assumed). Bar charts for categories; histograms for continuous grouped data; scatter graphs for correlations.

**Rates.** Rate = change ÷ time, or 1 ÷ time. For a curve, draw a tangent and find its gradient.

**Correlation and cause** (Edexcel A 1.8, 1.9). A correlation shows that two variables change together. It does not show that one causes the other: a third factor may cause both. Causation needs a plausible mechanism and controlled experiments. Good studies have large samples, matched groups, controls, and blinding (the people involved do not know who had the treatment); they are repeated by others.

**Validation** (Edexcel A 5.18, Edexcel B 3.1 vi, 10.4 iv). Scientists publish in peer-reviewed journals (other experts check methods, analysis and conclusions before publication), present at conferences, and other scientists try to repeat the work. Conflicting evidence is resolved by further investigation. This process makes accepted knowledge more reliable but takes time.

**Ethics and safety.** Do a risk assessment: hazard, risk, how to reduce it. Treat living organisms with respect: use the minimum number, return animals to their habitat, use material from food animals for dissection. Human volunteers must give informed consent.

#### Common mistakes

- Confusing accurate and precise.
- Calling a set of results "reliable" because the line is straight.
- Writing units in the cells of a table.
- Saying "repeat to make it more accurate". Repeats improve repeatability and let you spot anomalies; accuracy depends on the method and instruments.

### Statistical tests

*Refs: Edexcel B 8.2 vi, 10.1 vi · OCR A 4.2.2(f), 6.1.2(c). Level: AS & A (OCR A 4.2.2(f) AS); A-level (Edexcel B).*

#### What you need to know

- Calculate the mean, range, standard deviation and (for AQA and others) the standard error and 95% confidence interval.
- Choose the right test: chi-squared for comparing observed and expected frequencies in categories; Student's t-test for comparing the means of two sets of continuous data; Spearman's rank correlation coefficient for a correlation between two variables.
- Write a null hypothesis; compare the test statistic with the critical value at p = 0.05; draw a conclusion.

#### Explained

**Standard deviation** measures spread around the mean:

s = √[ Σ(x − x̄)² ÷ (n − 1) ]

About 68% of data lie within ±1 s of the mean, 95% within ±2 s (for normally distributed data).

**Standard error** of the mean: SE = s ÷ √n. The 95% confidence interval is about mean ± 2 × SE. If the 95% confidence intervals of two means do not overlap, the difference is likely to be significant. Error bars on graphs may show SD, SE or 95% confidence intervals; always say which.

**Choosing a test.**

| Data and question | Test |
| --- | --- |
| Counts in categories; do observed numbers fit an expected ratio? | Chi-squared (χ²) |
| Two sets of measurements; is there a significant difference between their means? (Data roughly normal) | Student's t-test |
| Paired measurements of two variables; is there a significant correlation? | Spearman's rank correlation coefficient (rs) |

**Null hypothesis.** States there is no difference (t-test, χ²) or no correlation (Spearman's). The probability level used is p = 0.05: if the test statistic is greater than the critical value at p = 0.05, there is less than a 5% probability that the result is due to chance, so reject the null hypothesis.

**Student's t-test (unpaired).**

t = |x̄₁ − x̄₂| ÷ √(s₁²/n₁ + s₂²/n₂)

Degrees of freedom = n₁ + n₂ − 2.

**Spearman's rank correlation coefficient.**

rs = 1 − (6Σd²) ÷ (n(n² − 1))

1. Rank each variable separately (1 = smallest). Tied values share the mean of their ranks.
2. Find d, the difference in ranks for each pair; square; sum.
3. rs ranges from −1 (perfect negative) to +1 (perfect positive).
4. Compare |rs| with the critical value for n pairs at p = 0.05. You need at least about 7 pairs.

#### Worked example

**Standard deviation and standard error.** Data: 4, 7, 5, 6, 8. Mean = 6. Deviations² = 4, 1, 1, 0, 4; Σ = 10. s = √(10 ÷ 4) = 1.58. SE = 1.58 ÷ √5 = 0.71. 95% CI ≈ 6 ± 1.4.

**t-test.** Leaf lengths (cm) in sun: 12, 14, 15, 13, 16 (mean 14.0, s = 1.58). In shade: 10, 11, 12, 11, 11 (mean 11.0, s = 0.71).

1. Null hypothesis: there is no significant difference between the mean lengths.
2. t = 3.0 ÷ √(1.58²/5 + 0.71²/5) = 3.0 ÷ √(0.5 + 0.1) = 3.0 ÷ 0.775 = 3.87.
3. Degrees of freedom = 8; critical value at p = 0.05 = 2.31.
4. 3.87 > 2.31, so reject the null hypothesis: the difference is significant (p < 0.05).

**Spearman's.** Light intensity ranks (1 to 8) and moss cover ranks: 1, 3, 2, 5, 4, 6, 8, 7.

1. d values: 0, 1, 1, 1, 1, 0, 1, 1; Σd² = 6.
2. rs = 1 − (6 × 6) ÷ (8 × 63) = 1 − 36 ÷ 504 = 0.93.
3. Critical value for n = 8 at p = 0.05 is 0.74 (two-tailed, from tables). 0.93 > 0.74, so there is a significant positive correlation.

#### Common mistakes

- Using a t-test on counts in categories, or chi-squared on means.
- Saying a significant correlation proves cause.
- Forgetting n − 1 in the standard deviation of a sample.
- Saying the null hypothesis is "proved true".
- Using the wrong degrees of freedom.

### Each board's practicals and where they are covered

*Refs: OCR A 1.2.2 · OCR B 1.2. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

The practical endorsement is reported separately as Pass or Not classified. Students keep a record of their practical work. Every board's written papers also include questions on the required practicals.

#### Explained

**AQA 7402 required practicals.**

| RP | Practical | File |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Effect of a variable on the rate of an enzyme-controlled reaction | 02 |
| 2 | Root tip squash; stages of mitosis; mitotic index | 05 |
| 3 | Dilution series; water potential of plant tissue | 06 |
| 4 | Effect of a variable on membrane permeability | 06 |
| 5 | Dissection of a gas exchange or mass transport system | 08, 10 |
| 6 | Aseptic technique; antimicrobial substances | 13 |
| 7 | Chromatography of photosynthetic pigments | 15 |
| 8 | Dehydrogenase activity in chloroplast extracts | 15 |
| 9 | Rate of respiration of single-celled organisms | 16 |
| 10 | Choice chamber or maze; animal behaviour | 18 |
| 11 | Glucose dilution series and calibration curve | 20 |
| 12 | Environmental factor and distribution of a species | 23 |

**Edexcel A (9BN0) core practicals.**

| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Caffeine and heart rate in *Daphnia* | 38 |
| 2 | Vitamin C content of food and drink | 38 |
| 3 | Membrane permeability (alcohol or temperature) | 06 |
| 4 | Enzyme and substrate concentration and initial rate | 02 |
| 5 | Root tip squash; mitosis | 05 |

| 7 | Plant mineral deficiencies | 41 |
| 8 | Tensile strength of plant fibres | 41 |
| 9 | Antimicrobial properties of plants | 41 |

**Edexcel B (9BI0) core practicals.**

| CP | Practical | File |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | A factor affecting initial rate of an enzyme reaction | 02 |
| 2 | Light microscope; micrometers; drawing | 04 |
| 3 | Root tip squash; mitosis | 05 |
| 4 | Sucrose and pollen tube growth | 37 |
| 5 | Temperature and beetroot membrane permeability | 06 |

| 9 | Respirometer; rate of respiration | 16 |
| 10 | Wavelength of light and rate of photosynthesis | 15 |

| 12 | Growth of bacteria in liquid culture | 28 |
| 13 | Streak plating; isolating species | 28 |

| 15 | Sampling methods and population estimates | 23 |
| 16 | Abiotic factor and distribution or morphology | 23 |

**OCR A (H420) and OCR B (H422) practical activity groups.** Both boards use the same 12 PAGs: PAG1 microscopy; PAG2 dissection; PAG3 sampling techniques; PAG4 rates of enzyme-controlled reactions; PAG5 colorimeter or potometer; PAG6 chromatography or electrophoresis; PAG7 microbiological techniques; PAG8 transport in and out of cells; PAG9 qualitative testing; PAG10 investigation using a data logger or computer modelling; PAG11 investigation into the measurement of plant or animal responses; PAG12 research skills. Schools choose the activities. Files: microscopy 04, 05, 30; dissection 08, 10; sampling 14, 23; enzymes 02; colorimeter and potometer 01, 06, 11; chromatography and electrophoresis 02, 15, 25; microbiology 13, 28, 29; transport 06; qualitative tests 01, 02, 03, 30; responses 18, 31.

**Eduqas (A400QS) specified practical work.** Food tests (01); calibration of the light microscope (04); slide of living cells (04); water potential by mass or length change (06); solute potential by incipient plasmolysis (06); beetroot membrane permeability (06); temperature or pH and enzyme activity (02); enzyme or substrate concentration (02); DNA extraction (03); dehydrogenase activity with artificial hydrogen acceptors (15); chromatography of chloroplast pigments (15); rate of photosynthesis (15); nitrogen and magnesium in plant growth (41); respiration in yeast (16); bacteria in fresh and stale milk (28); abundance and distribution of organisms (23); biodiversity in a habitat (14); drawing root tip mitosis (05); drawing developing anthers to show meiosis (37); digestion of starch agar by germinating seeds (37); dissection of wind- and insect-pollinated flowers (37); drawing an anther (37); gene segregation with chi-squared (21); continuous variation with a t-test (26); stomatal numbers in leaves (08); dissection of a fish head (08); drawing a dicot stem or root section (11); potometer (11); drawing an artery and vein (10); heart dissection (10); kidney dissection (30).

**Incipient plasmolysis** (Eduqas). Put strips of red onion epidermis in a range of sucrose concentrations. After 20 minutes, count the proportion of cells plasmolysed under the microscope. The concentration at which 50% are plasmolysed (incipient plasmolysis) has a solute potential equal to that of the cell sap, because at that point the pressure potential is zero.

### Check questions

1. Explain the difference between accuracy and precision. (2 marks)
2. A student measured the time for a colour change with a stopwatch reading to 0.01 s. Suggest why the uncertainty is larger than 0.01 s. (2 marks)
3. State which statistical test you would use for each: (a) whether woodlice prefer damp or dry conditions in a choice chamber; (b) whether the mean mass of limpets differs between two shores; (c) whether there is a correlation between soil pH and the number of earthworms. (3 marks)
4. Calculate the standard deviation of 3, 5, 7, 5. (2 marks)
5. Two means have 95% confidence intervals of 12.0 ± 1.5 and 16.0 ± 1.8. What can you conclude? (2 marks)
6. A t-test gave t = 1.9 with 10 degrees of freedom; the critical value at p = 0.05 is 2.23. State the conclusion. (2 marks)
7. Explain why peer review is important before a finding is published. (2 marks)
8. Calculate Spearman's rs for 7 pairs with Σd² = 10, and state whether there is a significant correlation (critical value 0.786 at p = 0.05). (3 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Accurate: close to the true value (1); precise: repeated values close together / small spread (1).
2. Human reaction time when starting / stopping (1); judging the end point of the colour change (1).
3. (a) chi-squared (1); (b) t-test (1); (c) Spearman's rank (1).
4. Mean 5; Σ(x − x̄)² = 4 + 0 + 4 + 0 = 8 (1); s = √(8/3) = 1.63 (1).
5. Intervals do not overlap (12.0 to 13.5 and 14.2 to 17.8) (1); difference is likely to be significant / not due to chance (1).
6. 1.9 < 2.23, so accept null hypothesis (1); no significant difference between the means; difference due to chance (1).
7. Other experts check methods, data and conclusions (1); reduces errors / fraud / unsupported claims; findings more reliable (1).
8. rs = 1 − (6 × 10) ÷ (7 × 48) (1) = 1 − 60/336 = 0.82 (1); 0.82 > 0.786, so reject null hypothesis: significant positive correlation (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/biology/01-monomers-carbohydrates-and-lipids.md -->

## Biological molecules: monomers, carbohydrates and lipids

All living things are built from the same few kinds of carbon-based molecule. This file covers how small units join into large molecules, the structure and jobs of carbohydrates and lipids, and the food tests used to find them.

### Overview

AS content for every board. Proteins are in file 02; nucleic acids, ATP, water and ions in file 03.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.1.1 to 3.1.3. Edexcel A 1.12 to 1.14 and 4.9 (starch and cellulose sit in the plants topic). Edexcel B 1.1 and 1.2. OCR A 2.1.2(b) to (j), (q), (r). OCR B 2.1.2(b) to (f). Eduqas CC1(c) to (g).
- OCR A also asks for the elements in each molecule group (2.1.2(c)), cholesterol (2.1.2(j)), and quantitative tests: colorimetry and biosensors (2.1.2(r)).
- OCR B and OCR A name cholesterol; Edexcel B names waterproofing and insulation as lipid roles.
- Eduqas CC1(g) and Edexcel A 1.15 link saturated fat to heart disease: see file 38.
- Edexcel A puts glycogen with starch in Topic 1 (diet) and cellulose in Topic 4 (plant fibres, file 41).

### 3.1.1 Monomers and polymers

*Refs: AQA 3.1.1 · Edexcel B 1.1 iii · OCR A 2.1.2(b), (c) · OCR B 2.1.2(d) · Eduqas CC1(c). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- The chemistry of life is the same in all organisms, which is indirect evidence for a common ancestor.
- A monomer is a small unit that can join with others to make a larger molecule. Monosaccharides, amino acids and nucleotides are monomers.
- A polymer is a large molecule made of many monomers joined together.
- A condensation reaction joins two molecules with a new chemical bond and releases a molecule of water.
- A hydrolysis reaction breaks a chemical bond between two molecules using a molecule of water.
- (OCR A) Carbohydrates contain C, H and O; lipids contain C, H and O; proteins contain C, H, O, N and S; nucleic acids contain C, H, O, N and P.

#### Explained

Think of condensation as "making, with water out" and hydrolysis as "splitting, with water in" (hydro = water, lysis = splitting). Digestion is hydrolysis. Building starch in a leaf or glycogen in the liver is condensation. Each needs its own enzyme.

Not every large molecule is a polymer. A triglyceride is large but it is made of one glycerol and three fatty acids, which are not repeating identical units, so it is not a polymer.

#### Common mistakes

- Calling lipids polymers.
- Saying hydrolysis "releases" water. It uses water.
- Saying condensation "removes water from the molecule" without saying a bond forms.

### 3.1.2 Carbohydrates

*Refs: AQA 3.1.2 · Edexcel A 1.12, 1.13, 4.9 · Edexcel B 1.1 i to iv · OCR A 2.1.2(d) to (g), (q), (r) · OCR B 2.1.2(b), (c), (e) · Eduqas CC1(c) to (e). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Monosaccharides are the monomers of carbohydrates. Glucose, galactose and fructose are common ones (all hexoses, C₆H₁₂O₆). Ribose and deoxyribose are pentoses.
- Two monosaccharides join by condensation to form a glycosidic bond, making a disaccharide:
  - maltose = glucose + glucose
  - sucrose = glucose + fructose
  - lactose = glucose + galactose
- Glucose has two isomers. In α-glucose the OH on carbon 1 is below the ring; in β-glucose it is above the ring.
- Polysaccharides are made by condensation of many glucose units:
  - starch and glycogen are made from α-glucose
  - cellulose is made from β-glucose.
- Tests: Benedict's test for reducing sugars; a non-reducing sugar test (hydrolyse with acid first); iodine in potassium iodide for starch.

#### Explained

**Glucose ring.** Six carbons; carbons 1 to 5 and an oxygen form the ring, carbon 6 sticks out. The only difference between α and β is which way the OH on carbon 1 points. This small difference changes everything about the polymers they make.

**Starch** is the energy store of plants. It is a mix of two polymers of α-glucose:

| | Amylose | Amylopectin |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Bonds | 1,4-glycosidic only | 1,4 with 1,6 branches |
| Shape | Unbranched chain that coils into a helix | Branched |
| Why it helps | Compact: lots of glucose in a small space | Many ends where enzymes can hydrolyse glucose off quickly |

Starch is insoluble, so it does not lower the water potential of the cell and water does not move in by osmosis. It is large, so it cannot leave the cell.

**Glycogen** is the energy store of animals and fungi. It is like amylopectin but more branched, with shorter chains. More branches means more ends, so glucose can be released fast, which suits animals that move and have a high metabolic rate. It is also insoluble and compact.

**Cellulose** is the main part of plant cell walls. In a chain of β-glucose every other glucose is turned upside down. This gives straight, unbranched chains. Many parallel chains are held together by hydrogen bonds to form microfibrils, and microfibrils group into fibres. Each hydrogen bond is weak, but there are so many that cellulose is very strong. The strength lets the wall resist the pressure of water entering by osmosis, keeping the cell turgid.

**Benedict's test.** All monosaccharides and some disaccharides (maltose, lactose) are reducing sugars. Heat the sample with Benedict's reagent in a water bath. A reducing sugar gives a colour change from blue through green, yellow and orange to brick red, depending on how much sugar there is.

**Non-reducing sugar test.** Sucrose gives no change with Benedict's. If the first test is negative: boil a fresh sample with dilute hydrochloric acid (hydrolyses the glycosidic bond), cool, neutralise with sodium hydrogencarbonate (Benedict's does not work in acid), then do the Benedict's test again. A red-brown precipitate now shows a non-reducing sugar was present.

**Starch test.** Add iodine in potassium iodide solution. Orange-brown turns blue-black.

**Making the tests quantitative.** The colour of the Benedict's result is only a rough guide. Better methods:

- Filter and dry the precipitate, then weigh it.
- Use a colorimeter. Make a dilution series of glucose solutions of known concentration, test each, measure absorbance (or transmission) and plot a calibration curve. Read the unknown's concentration off the curve. (Required practical 11 in AQA uses this for urine-style glucose samples: file 20.)
- (OCR A) A biosensor uses an immobilised enzyme, such as glucose oxidase, and a transducer that turns the reaction into an electrical signal read as a concentration.

#### Worked example

A student makes glucose solutions of 0, 2, 4, 6, 8 and 10 mmol dm⁻³ from a 10 mmol dm⁻³ stock, each with a final volume of 10 cm³. What volumes of stock and water make the 4 mmol dm⁻³ solution?

1. Fraction needed = 4 ÷ 10 = 0.4.
2. Stock volume = 0.4 × 10 = 4 cm³.
3. Water = 10 − 4 = 6 cm³.

Answer: 4 cm³ of stock and 6 cm³ of distilled water.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying starch is made of β-glucose or cellulose of α-glucose.
- Saying cellulose is branched. It is unbranched and straight.
- Saying "cellulose forms hydrogen bonds with other glucose" instead of "between parallel chains".
- Forgetting to neutralise after acid hydrolysis in the non-reducing sugar test.
- Saying "Benedict's turns red" for a reducing sugar without mentioning heating.
- Saying glycogen is found in plants.

### 3.1.3 Lipids

*Refs: AQA 3.1.3 · Edexcel A 1.14 · Edexcel B 1.2 i to iv · OCR A 2.1.2(h) to (j), (q) · OCR B 2.1.2(f) · Eduqas CC1(f), (g). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Triglycerides and phospholipids are two groups of lipid.
- A triglyceride is made by condensation of one glycerol and three fatty acids. Each condensation forms an ester bond and releases a water molecule (three in total).
- A fatty acid is RCOOH. The R group (hydrocarbon chain) may be saturated (no C=C double bonds) or unsaturated (one or more C=C double bonds).
- In a phospholipid, one fatty acid is replaced by a phosphate-containing group.
- You should explain the different properties of triglycerides and phospholipids from their structures, and recognise saturated and unsaturated fatty acids in diagrams.
- The emulsion test for lipids.

#### Explained

**Triglycerides** are the main energy store in animals and many seeds.

| Property | Why | Use |
| --- | --- | --- |
| High ratio of C–H bonds to C atoms | Many bonds to break in respiration | Release about twice as much energy per gram as carbohydrate |
| Low mass for the energy stored | As above | Good store for animals that move |
| Insoluble in water | Fatty acid tails are non-polar | Do not affect water potential |
| High ratio of hydrogen to oxygen | Oxidation releases water | Metabolic water source (desert animals) |
| Poor conductor of heat (OCR, Edexcel B) | | Insulation under the skin; waterproofing in waxy cuticles |

**Saturated and unsaturated.** A double bond puts a kink in the chain, so unsaturated chains cannot pack tightly. That is why unsaturated fats (oils) are liquid at room temperature and saturated fats are more often solid.

**Phospholipids** have a hydrophilic "head" (the phosphate group, which is charged and attracts water) and two hydrophobic "tails" (the fatty acids, which repel water). In water they form a bilayer with heads facing the water on both sides and tails pointing inwards. This is the basis of every cell membrane (file 06). The hydrophobic core stops water-soluble substances passing freely.

**Cholesterol** (OCR A, OCR B) is a lipid with a four-ring structure. It sits between phospholipids in animal membranes and regulates fluidity. It is also used to make steroid hormones and vitamin D.

**Emulsion test.** Shake the sample with ethanol to dissolve any lipid, then pour into water and shake. A milky-white emulsion shows lipid: the lipid comes out of solution as tiny droplets that scatter light.

#### Worked example

How many water molecules are released when one triglyceride is made, and how many are needed to hydrolyse two triglycerides fully?

1. Three ester bonds form, each releasing one water: 3 water molecules.
2. Hydrolysing one triglyceride uses 3 water; two use 6.

Answer: 3 released; 6 needed.

#### Common mistakes

- Calling the bond in a triglyceride a glycosidic or peptide bond. It is an ester bond.
- Saying phospholipids have "a hydrophobic head".
- Saying unsaturated means "no double bonds". It is the other way round.
- Saying lipids store "more energy" without "per gram".
- In the emulsion test, adding water first. Dissolve in ethanol first.

### Check questions

1. Name the type of reaction that joins two α-glucose molecules and name the bond formed. (2 marks)
2. Explain how the structure of glycogen makes it suited to its role in animals. (3 marks)
3. Explain how cellulose molecules give strength to a plant cell wall. (3 marks)
4. A student suspects a drink contains sucrose but no reducing sugar. Describe how they would show this. (4 marks)
5. Compare the structure of a triglyceride with a phospholipid. (2 marks)
6. Explain why triglycerides are a better long-term energy store for a migrating bird than glycogen. (2 marks)
7. A student made a calibration curve for glucose with a colorimeter. Explain why the unknown samples must be tested with the same volume of Benedict's reagent and for the same time as the standards. (2 marks)
8. Explain why phospholipids form a bilayer in water. (2 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Condensation (1); (1,4) glycosidic bond (1).
2. Any three: highly branched, so many ends for enzymes to hydrolyse, so glucose released quickly (1); insoluble, so no effect on water potential / no osmosis into cell (1); compact, so lots stored in small space (1); large, so cannot leave cell (1).
3. β-glucose gives straight, unbranched chains (1); chains lie parallel and are joined by many hydrogen bonds (1); forming microfibrils / fibres, giving strength (1).
4. Benedict's test on sample, heated, stays blue, so no reducing sugar (1); boil fresh sample with dilute hydrochloric acid (1); neutralise with sodium hydrogencarbonate (1); heat with Benedict's; red/orange/green precipitate shows non-reducing sugar (1).
5. Triglyceride has three fatty acids; phospholipid has two fatty acids and a phosphate-containing group (1); both have glycerol and ester bonds (1).
6. More energy released per gram (1); so lower mass to carry during flight (1). Accept: also releases more metabolic water.
7. Colour depends on volume of reagent and time / temperature as well as glucose concentration (1); so only glucose concentration varies, and the curve applies to the unknowns (1).
8. Heads are hydrophilic and face the water (1); tails are hydrophobic and point away from water, towards each other (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/biology/08-surface-area-and-gas-exchange.md -->

## Surface area to volume ratio and gas exchange

Large organisms cannot rely on diffusion across their body surface. This file covers surface area to volume ratio and how insects, fish, plant leaves and mammals exchange gases, with AQA required practical 5.

### Overview

AS content. Lung diseases (fibrosis, asthma, emphysema) appear here as AQA asks for them; their causes and treatment for OCR B are in file 44. Gas exchange in amoeba, flatworms and earthworms (Eduqas) is in file 46.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.3.1, 3.3.2, RP5. Edexcel A 2.1. Edexcel B 4.1, 4.3, CP7. OCR A 3.1.1(a) to (h). OCR B 2.2.3(a) to (c), (e), (f). Eduqas C3.1(a), (d) to (l).
- OCR A asks for tidal volume, vital capacity and spirometer traces (3.1.1(e)); OCR B for pulmonary ventilation (2.2.3(c)). Included below.
- OCR A, OCR A PAG2 and Edexcel B CP7 ask for dissection of an insect or fish gas exchange system; AQA RP5 allows animal or plant gas exchange or mass transport systems.
- Edexcel A asks for Fick's law and limits gas exchange to the lungs.
- Eduqas C3.1(j) to (l) asks for leaf structure and its role in photosynthesis.

### 3.3.1 Surface area to volume ratio

*Refs: AQA 3.3.1 · Edexcel B 4.1 i, ii · OCR A 3.1.1(a), (b) · Eduqas C3.1(a). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- The relationship between the size of an organism or structure and its surface area to volume ratio.
- Changes to body shape and the development of systems in larger organisms as adaptations that make exchange easier as the ratio falls.
- The relationship between surface area to volume ratio and metabolic rate.
- Calculate surface area to volume ratios.

#### Explained

As an object gets bigger, its volume grows faster than its surface area. A 1 cm cube has SA 6 cm² and volume 1 cm³ (ratio 6:1); a 3 cm cube has SA 54 cm² and volume 27 cm³ (ratio 2:1).

Small organisms (single cells, flatworms) have a large ratio, so diffusion across the body surface supplies enough oxygen and removes enough waste. In large organisms:

- the surface is too small for the volume of cells needing supplies
- the distance from surface to the middle is too long for diffusion.

So large organisms have specialised exchange surfaces (lungs, gills, villi, leaves) and mass transport systems. Flattened or long thin shapes also increase the ratio (flatworms, leaves).

**Metabolic rate.** Small mammals lose heat fast because of their large surface area relative to volume. They have a high metabolic rate (per gram) to make heat, so need more oxygen per gram. Adaptations of small animals in cold places: compact shape, thick fur, hibernation. Large animals in hot places may have large ears (elephants) to lose heat.

**Features of efficient exchange surfaces** (OCR A, Edexcel A): large surface area; thin (short diffusion distance); steep concentration gradient kept up by ventilation and a good blood supply. **Fick's law** sums this up: rate of diffusion ∝ (surface area × difference in concentration) ÷ thickness of the surface.

#### Worked example

A cylindrical cell is 20 µm long and 4 µm in diameter. Calculate its surface area to volume ratio.

1. r = 2 µm.
2. SA = 2πr² + 2πrh = 2π(4) + 2π(2)(20) = 8π + 80π = 88π ≈ 276.5 µm².
3. V = πr²h = π(4)(20) = 80π ≈ 251.3 µm³.
4. Ratio = 88π ÷ 80π = 1.1 : 1.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying "large organisms have a small surface area". They have a large surface area but a small surface area to volume ratio.
- Leaving out units or writing the ratio upside down.

### 3.3.2 Gas exchange

*Refs: AQA 3.3.2, RP5 · Edexcel A 2.1 · Edexcel B 4.3 i, ii, CP7 · OCR A 3.1.1(b) to (h) · OCR B 2.2.3(a) to (c), (e), (f) · Eduqas C3.1(d) to (l). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Adaptations of gas exchange surfaces: across the body surface of a single-celled organism; in the tracheal system of an insect (tracheae, tracheoles and spiracles); across the gills of fish (gill lamellae and filaments, including countercurrent flow); by the leaves of dicotyledonous plants (mesophyll and stomata).
- Structural and functional compromises between efficient gas exchange and limiting water loss, in terrestrial insects and xerophytic plants.
- The gross structure of the human gas exchange system: alveoli, bronchioles, bronchi, trachea and lungs.
- The essential features of the alveolar epithelium as a surface over which gas exchange takes place.
- Ventilation and the exchange of gases in the lungs: the mechanism of breathing, including the roles of the diaphragm and the antagonistic interaction between external and internal intercostal muscles in bringing about pressure changes in the thoracic cavity.
- Interpret information on how lung disease affects lung function; interpret data on risk factors and lung disease; evaluate how experimental data led to statutory restrictions on sources of risk factors; analyse and interpret data on pulmonary ventilation rate.

#### Explained

**Single-celled organisms.** Gases diffuse straight across the cell-surface membrane. The large surface area to volume ratio and short distance make this enough.

**Insects.**

- Air enters through spiracles (openings on the sides of the body that a valve can close).
- Tracheae (tubes stiffened by rings of chitin) branch into tracheoles, which reach right into the tissues; oxygen diffuses straight to the cells, with no blood involved.
- Movement of gases: diffusion down a concentration gradient (cells use oxygen); mass flow from ventilation movements of the abdomen; at the ends of the tracheoles, water is drawn into muscle cells during activity (by lowering the water potential with lactate), so air moves further down the tracheoles and gases diffuse faster.
- Limiting water loss: spiracles close when possible; a waxy, waterproof exoskeleton; hairs around spiracles trap moist air. The compromise: open spiracles let oxygen in but water out.

**Fish.**

- Water enters the mouth and passes over the gills, leaving under the operculum.
- Each gill has many gill filaments; each filament is covered in gill lamellae, giving a large surface area; lamellae have a thin epithelium and many capillaries.
- **Countercurrent flow:** blood flows through the lamellae in the opposite direction to the water. Blood always meets water with a higher oxygen concentration, so a diffusion gradient is maintained along the whole length of the lamella. About 80% of the oxygen in the water can be absorbed. In parallel flow (same direction) the concentrations would even out halfway, and only about 50% would be absorbed.

**Leaves of dicotyledonous plants.**

- Many stomata, mostly on the lower surface, so no cell is far from one.
- Air spaces in the spongy mesophyll, so gases diffuse quickly to and from cells; large internal surface area of mesophyll cells.
- Guard cells open the stomata in light (for CO₂ uptake) and close them when water is short. The compromise: open stomata let CO₂ in but water vapour out.
- (Eduqas) Leaf structure for photosynthesis: thin and broad (large surface for light, short CO₂ path); transparent cuticle and epidermis; palisade mesophyll packed with chloroplasts near the upper surface; veins of xylem bring water and phloem remove sugars. Stomata open when guard cells take up K⁺ ions actively, lowering their water potential; water enters by osmosis, the cells become turgid and bow apart because their inner walls are thicker.

**Xerophytes** (plants adapted to dry places, such as marram grass and cacti):

- thick waxy cuticle
- rolled leaves, sunken stomata and hairs that trap moist air, reducing the water potential gradient
- fewer stomata; small leaves or spines (smaller surface area)
- (more detail in file 11).

**The human gas exchange system.** Air passes down the trachea, which divides into two bronchi (one per lung), which branch into bronchioles, ending in alveoli. The trachea and bronchi have C-shaped cartilage rings to keep them open, ciliated epithelium and goblet cells that make mucus to trap particles; bronchioles have smooth muscle that can narrow them.

**The alveolar epithelium.**

- About 300 million alveoli: very large surface area.
