- The alveolar wall is one layer of thin, flattened (squamous) cells, and the capillary wall is also one cell thick: diffusion path about 1 µm or less.
- A dense network of capillaries; blood flow carries oxygen away and brings carbon dioxide, maintaining the gradients.
- Ventilation replaces alveolar air, maintaining the gradients.
- Red blood cells are pressed flat against capillary walls, slowing flow and shortening the distance.
- Elastic fibres let alveoli stretch and recoil; surfactant stops them collapsing.

**Ventilation.**

| | Inspiration (inhalation) | Expiration (exhalation, at rest) |
| --- | --- | --- |
| External intercostal muscles | Contract: ribcage moves up and out | Relax |
| Internal intercostal muscles | Relax | Relax at rest; contract in forced expiration, pulling ribs down and in |
| Diaphragm | Contracts and flattens | Relaxes and domes up |
| Thoracic volume | Increases | Decreases |
| Pressure in lungs | Falls below atmospheric | Rises above atmospheric |
| Air | Moves in | Moves out |

Inspiration is active (needs muscle contraction). Normal expiration is mostly passive (elastic recoil of the lungs). The external and internal intercostals are antagonistic.

**Lung volumes** (OCR A, OCR B). A spirometer records a trace of volume against time.

- Tidal volume: volume of air breathed in or out in one normal breath (about 0.5 dm³).
- Vital capacity: the largest volume that can be breathed out after the deepest breath in.
- Residual volume: air left in the lungs after the most forceful exhalation; cannot be measured with a spirometer.
- Pulmonary ventilation rate (dm³ min⁻¹) = tidal volume (dm³) × breathing rate (breaths min⁻¹).
- Oxygen uptake: in a spirometer with soda lime absorbing CO₂, the trace falls over time; the gradient gives the rate of oxygen use.

**Lung disease** (data-handling skills). Diseases reduce gas exchange or ventilation:

- Pulmonary fibrosis: scar tissue thickens the alveolar walls (longer diffusion distance) and makes lungs less elastic (lower tidal volume and vital capacity).
- Asthma: airways inflamed; smooth muscle contracts and extra mucus is made, so airways narrow and less air flows (lower FEV₁, the volume breathed out forcefully in the first second).
- Emphysema: elastic fibres destroyed and alveolar walls break down; fewer, larger air spaces mean a much smaller surface area; lungs do not recoil, so air is trapped.

**Risk factors and evidence.** When interpreting data, separate correlation from cause. Large studies of smokers and non-smokers showed a strong correlation between smoking and lung cancer; animal experiments and identification of carcinogens in tar gave a mechanism. Together these led to laws restricting advertising and smoking in public places. Air pollution and occupational dusts (asbestos) led to similar laws.

#### Worked example

1. A person's tidal volume is 0.45 dm³ and their breathing rate is 16 breaths per minute. Calculate their pulmonary ventilation.

0.45 × 16 = 7.2 dm³ min⁻¹.

2. After exercise their pulmonary ventilation is 36 dm³ min⁻¹ and their breathing rate is 24 min⁻¹. Calculate the new tidal volume.

36 ÷ 24 = 1.5 dm³.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying insects carry oxygen in their blood.
- Saying countercurrent flow means "water and blood flow in opposite directions" without explaining the gradient along the whole lamella.
- Saying the lungs inflate "because air rushes in". Volume increases first, so pressure falls, and then air moves in.
- Saying the internal intercostals contract during normal breathing out.
- Saying the alveolar wall is "one cell thick so it's thin" without linking it to a short diffusion distance.
- Saying stomata are open "to let oxygen in" during photosynthesis.

#### Practical

**AQA required practical 5** (also Edexcel B CP7, OCR A 3.1.1(g) and PAG2): dissection of animal or plant gas exchange or mass transport system or of an organ within such a system. (Heart dissection is described in file 10.)

*Fish gills.* Cut away the operculum with scissors to expose the gills; cut out a gill arch; put it in water and look with a hand lens or under a microscope at the filaments and lamellae, which float apart in water and stick together in air.

*Insect tracheal system* (locust or cockroach). Cut along the dorsal surface and pin the body open under water or saline in a dissecting dish; tracheae appear silvery because of the air inside. A section mounted on a slide shows the chitin rings.

*Lungs.* Identify trachea, bronchi and cartilage; inflate through the trachea using a tube connected to a pump (never by mouth).

Safety and ethics: sharp tools cut away from the body; wear gloves; wash hands; dispose of tissue properly; use material from animals killed for food where possible. Record findings as labelled drawings.

### Check questions

1. Explain why a large multicellular organism needs a specialised gas exchange surface. (2 marks)
2. Explain how countercurrent flow makes gas exchange in fish gills efficient. (3 marks)
3. Describe how air moves into the lungs. (4 marks)
4. Explain two ways the alveolar epithelium is adapted for rapid gas exchange. (4 marks)
5. Explain the compromise between gas exchange and water loss in a terrestrial insect. (3 marks)
6. A person has pulmonary fibrosis. Explain why they feel breathless during exercise. (3 marks)
7. A tidal volume of 0.5 dm³ and a breathing rate of 15 min⁻¹ became 1.2 dm³ and 30 min⁻¹ during exercise. Calculate the percentage increase in pulmonary ventilation. (2 marks)
8. A cube-shaped cell has sides of 2 µm. Calculate its surface area to volume ratio. (2 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Small surface area to volume ratio (1); diffusion distance too long / body surface too small to supply all cells (1).
2. Blood and water flow in opposite directions (1); blood always meets water with higher oxygen concentration (1); so a diffusion gradient is maintained along the whole lamella / more oxygen absorbed (1).
3. External intercostal muscles contract, ribs move up and out (1); diaphragm contracts and flattens (1); thoracic volume increases (1); pressure falls below atmospheric so air moves in (1).
4. Any two features with explanation (2 each): thin / one cell thick, short diffusion distance; many alveoli, large surface area; capillary network / blood flow maintains gradient; ventilation maintains gradient.
5. Spiracles open for oxygen to diffuse in (1); water vapour diffuses out at the same time (1); spiracles close / waxy cuticle / hairs reduce water loss but also limit oxygen supply (1).
6. Thicker alveolar walls / scar tissue, so longer diffusion distance (1); less elastic, so smaller tidal volume / less air ventilated (1); less oxygen diffuses into blood for respiration in muscles (1).
7. Before 7.5 dm³ min⁻¹; after 36 dm³ min⁻¹ (1); increase = 28.5 ÷ 7.5 × 100 = 380% (1).
8. SA = 6 × 4 = 24 µm²; V = 8 µm³ (1); ratio 3 : 1 (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/biology/06-membranes-and-transport-across-them.md -->

## Cell membranes and transport across them

Every cell is wrapped in a membrane that controls what enters and leaves. This file covers the fluid mosaic model and the five ways substances cross membranes, with AQA required practicals 3 and 4.

### Overview

AS content. Co-transport in the ileum is applied in file 09; active transport in root hair cells in file 11; ion movement in neurones in file 19.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.2.3, RP3, RP4. Edexcel A 2.2 to 2.4, CP3. Edexcel B 4.2 i to v, CP5, CP6. OCR A 2.1.5(a) to (e). OCR B 2.1.1(j) to (n), 2.1.2(g), (h). Eduqas CC3(a) to (c).
- OCR A and OCR B ask for the roles of membranes inside cells (compartments, sites of reactions) and cell signalling with membrane receptors (OCR A 2.1.5(a), (b); cell signalling is expanded in file 32).
- Edexcel A asks how the fluid mosaic model was developed from earlier models (2.2 ii); included below.
- Edexcel B asks how a molecule's size, charge and solubility decide how it crosses (4.2 iii).
- OCR A asks for the effect of solvents and temperature on permeability (PAG8); Edexcel A CP3 uses alcohol or temperature; Edexcel B CP5 uses temperature.

### 3.2.3 Transport across cell membranes

*Refs: AQA 3.2.3, RP3, RP4 · Edexcel A 2.2, 2.3, 2.4, CP3 · Edexcel B 4.2 i to v, CP5, CP6 · OCR A 2.1.5(a) to (e) · OCR B 2.1.1(j) to (n), 2.1.2(g), (h) · Eduqas CC3(a) to (c). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- The basic structure of all cell membranes, including cell-surface membranes and the membranes around organelles, is the same.
- The arrangement of phospholipids, proteins, glycoproteins and glycolipids in the fluid mosaic model. Cholesterol may also be present.
- Movement across membranes by simple diffusion (the limits set by the phospholipid bilayer), facilitated diffusion (channel and carrier proteins), osmosis (water potential), active transport (carrier proteins and ATP) and co-transport (sodium ions and glucose).
- How surface area, number of channel or carrier proteins and differences in gradients of concentration or water potential affect the rate of movement.
- How cells may be adapted for rapid transport across their internal or external membranes by more surface area or more channel and carrier proteins.
- Explain the adaptations of specialised cells for rapid transport; use the fluid mosaic model to explain the properties of membranes.

#### Explained

**The fluid mosaic model.**

- A phospholipid bilayer about 7 nm thick: hydrophilic heads outward, hydrophobic tails inward.
- Fluid: phospholipids and many proteins move about within their layer.
- Mosaic: proteins scattered through it like tiles. Intrinsic proteins span the bilayer (channels, carriers); extrinsic proteins sit on one surface (receptors, enzymes).
- Glycoproteins and glycolipids (with carbohydrate chains on the outside) act as receptors and antigens for cell recognition, and help cells stick together.
- Cholesterol sits between phospholipids. It binds to the tails, reducing their movement, which makes the membrane less fluid at high temperatures and stops it becoming too rigid at low temperatures. It also reduces leakage of water and ions.

**How the model was developed** (Edexcel A). In the 1930s the Davson–Danielli model proposed a lipid bilayer sandwiched between two continuous layers of protein. Electron micrographs showed a "railway track" appearance that seemed to fit. Later evidence did not: membranes from different cells had different protein amounts; freeze-fracture images showed proteins embedded within the bilayer; and labelled proteins on fused cells spread across the whole new membrane in minutes, showing they move. Singer and Nicolson proposed the fluid mosaic model in 1972 to fit this evidence. This is an example of a model being changed as new evidence appears.

**Why the bilayer is a barrier.** Small, non-polar molecules (oxygen, carbon dioxide, steroid hormones) dissolve in the hydrophobic core and diffuse straight through. Ions and large polar molecules (glucose, amino acids) cannot, because the hydrophobic tails repel them. They need proteins.

**The five mechanisms.**

| Mechanism | Needs ATP? | Proteins? | Direction |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Simple diffusion | No | No | Down the concentration gradient |
| Facilitated diffusion | No | Channel or carrier | Down the gradient |
| Osmosis | No | Often aquaporins | Water from higher to lower water potential |
| Active transport | Yes | Carrier proteins | Against the gradient |
| Co-transport | Indirectly | Co-transporter | One substance down its gradient drives another against its gradient |

*Channel proteins* form water-filled pores for specific ions; some are gated (open only in response to voltage or a chemical). *Carrier proteins* bind a specific molecule and change shape to carry it across.

**Water potential (ψ)** is the tendency of water to move out of a solution. Pure water has ψ = 0 kPa, the highest possible. Adding solutes lowers ψ, so solutions have negative values. Water moves by osmosis from a higher (less negative) to a lower (more negative) water potential across a partially permeable membrane.

- Animal cells in a solution of higher ψ gain water and may burst (haemolysis in red blood cells). In lower ψ they lose water and shrink (crenate).
- Plant cells in higher ψ gain water and become turgid, but the wall stops them bursting. In lower ψ they lose water; the protoplast pulls away from the wall (plasmolysis).

**Active transport.** A molecule binds to the carrier on one side; ATP is hydrolysed and the phosphate attaches to the carrier, changing its shape; the molecule is released on the other side. Example: the sodium–potassium pump moves 3 Na⁺ out and 2 K⁺ in per ATP.

**Co-transport in the ileum.** Sodium ions are actively pumped out of the epithelial cell into the blood, keeping the Na⁺ concentration inside the cell low. Na⁺ then diffuses into the cell from the gut lumen through a co-transporter protein, carrying glucose (or an amino acid) with it, even against glucose's own gradient. Glucose then leaves the other side of the cell by facilitated diffusion into the blood.

**Bulk transport** (OCR, Edexcel, Eduqas). Endocytosis takes in large particles or fluids by folding the membrane around them into a vesicle (phagocytosis for solids; pinocytosis for liquids). Exocytosis releases substances when a vesicle fuses with the membrane. Both need ATP.

**Factors affecting rate.**

- Simple diffusion: steeper gradient, larger surface area, thinner membrane, higher temperature → faster (Fick's law: rate ∝ surface area × difference in concentration ÷ thickness).
- Facilitated diffusion and active transport: also limited by the number of channel or carrier proteins. When all carriers are in use, raising the concentration has no further effect.
- Adaptations: microvilli on epithelial cells of the ileum and proximal tubule give a large surface area; these cells also have many carrier proteins and many mitochondria for active transport.

**Effect of temperature and solvents on permeability.** Above about 40 °C the phospholipids move more and membrane proteins denature, leaving gaps; the membrane becomes much more permeable. Ethanol and other organic solvents dissolve the lipids, also increasing permeability.

#### Worked example

1. Cell X has ψ = −400 kPa and cell Y has ψ = −650 kPa. Which way does water move?

From X to Y, because X has the higher (less negative) water potential.

2. Potato cylinders were put in sucrose solutions. Percentage change in mass: 0.0 mol dm⁻³: +14%; 0.2: +6%; 0.4: −3%; 0.6: −11%. Estimate the concentration with no change in mass and explain what it shows.

Plot % change against concentration and read where the line crosses zero: between 0.2 and 0.4, roughly 0.33 mol dm⁻³ (by linear interpolation: 0.2 + 0.2 × 6/9 = 0.33). At this concentration the solution has the same water potential as the potato cells. Its value can then be read from a table that converts sucrose concentration to water potential.

3. Percentage change: start mass 2.50 g, end mass 2.85 g. Change = 0.35 g; 0.35 ÷ 2.50 × 100 = +14%. Use percentage change because cylinders start at slightly different masses.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying water moves "from a high to a low concentration". Use water potential.
- Saying water potential "increases" when more solute is added. It decreases (becomes more negative).
- Saying plant cells burst in pure water.
- Saying active transport uses "energy from ATP" with no mention of carrier proteins changing shape.
- Saying facilitated diffusion needs ATP.
- Saying co-transport moves sodium against its gradient. Sodium moves down its gradient; glucose may move against its gradient.

#### Practical

**AQA required practical 3** (also Edexcel B CP6, OCR A PAG8 / 2.1.5(e), OCR B 2.1.2(h)): produce a dilution series of a solute to find the water potential of a plant tissue.

1. Make a dilution series of sucrose (or salt) from a 1.0 mol dm⁻³ stock: for 10 cm³ of 0.4 mol dm⁻³ use 4 cm³ stock + 6 cm³ distilled water.
2. Cut equal cylinders of potato with a cork borer, blot dry, weigh each.
3. Put one cylinder in each concentration for the same time (for example 30 minutes or overnight) at the same temperature.
4. Remove, blot gently the same way, reweigh. Calculate percentage change in mass.
5. Plot percentage change against concentration; the x-intercept is the concentration with the same ψ as the cells.

Control variables: size and surface area of the cylinders, same potato (same variety), time, temperature, volume of solution, blotting method. Repeat at each concentration.

**AQA required practical 4** (also Edexcel A CP3, Edexcel B CP5, OCR A PAG8 / 2.1.5(c), OCR B 2.1.1(m)): investigate the effect of a named variable on the permeability of cell-surface membranes.

1. Cut equal pieces of beetroot and rinse them to remove pigment from damaged cells.
2. Put each piece in the same volume of water at a different temperature (or in different ethanol concentrations) for the same time.
3. Remove the beetroot. Measure the absorbance of the coloured liquid with a colorimeter (blue-green filter, zeroed with distilled water).
4. Higher absorbance means more betalain pigment has leaked out of the vacuoles, so the tonoplast and cell-surface membrane are more permeable.

Explanation of results: above a threshold temperature proteins denature and phospholipids move more, so pigment leaks out; ethanol dissolves lipids. Risks: ethanol is flammable; hot water; sharp knives.

### Check questions

1. Explain why the cell-surface membrane is described as "fluid mosaic". (2 marks)
2. Explain how cholesterol affects the properties of a membrane. (2 marks)
3. Glucose enters epithelial cells of the ileum against its concentration gradient. Describe how. (4 marks)
4. Explain why oxygen can diffuse across a membrane but sodium ions cannot without a protein. (2 marks)
5. Red blood cells were put in a solution with a water potential of −2000 kPa. Their own water potential is −700 kPa. Describe and explain what happens. (3 marks)
6. Explain why the rate of facilitated diffusion stops increasing at high concentrations. (2 marks)
7. In required practical 4, explain why the beetroot pieces are rinsed before the experiment. (2 marks)
8. Describe one piece of evidence that led scientists to reject the Davson–Danielli model. (2 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Fluid: phospholipids / proteins can move (1); mosaic: proteins scattered through the bilayer (1).
2. Binds to phospholipid tails / restricts movement, reducing fluidity at high temperatures (1); reduces leakage of water / ions (1). Accept: keeps membrane fluid at low temperatures.
3. Sodium ions actively transported out of cell into blood (1); Na⁺ concentration in cell lower than in lumen (1); Na⁺ diffuses into cell through a co-transporter protein (1); carrying glucose with it (1).
4. Oxygen is small and non-polar, so dissolves in / passes through the hydrophobic core (1); sodium ions are charged so are repelled by hydrophobic tails (1).
5. Solution has lower water potential than cells (1); water leaves cells by osmosis (1); cells shrink / crenate (1).
6. All channel / carrier proteins in use / saturated (1); number of proteins becomes the limiting factor (1).
7. To remove pigment released from cells cut / damaged during preparation (1); so only pigment leaking due to the variable is measured (1).
8. Any one, with explanation (2): freeze-fracture showed proteins within the bilayer, not only in outer layers; fused-cell experiments showed proteins move, so not a fixed layer; membranes have different amounts of protein.

<!-- source: notes/a-level/biology/12-dna-genes-and-protein-synthesis.md -->

## DNA, genes, chromosomes and protein synthesis

A gene is a length of DNA that codes for a polypeptide. This file covers how DNA is packaged in different cells, the genetic code, and how proteins are made by transcription and translation.

### Overview

AS content. Mutations are in files 13 (introduction) and 24 (A-level detail). Control of gene expression is in file 24.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.4.1, 3.4.2. Edexcel A 2.6 to 2.8, 2.12 (part). Edexcel B 1.4 iii to vii. OCR A 2.1.3(f), (g). OCR B 2.1.4(e) to (g). Eduqas CC5(i) to (n).
- Edexcel B and OCR B ask for the structure of mRNA and tRNA in detail (Edexcel B 1.4 iv, v; OCR B 2.1.4(f)).
- AQA asks how DNA differs in prokaryotes, mitochondria and chloroplasts, and for the terms genome and proteome; other boards cover some of this in cell structure.
- Eduqas names the "one gene – one polypeptide" hypothesis (CC5(n)).
- Splicing: AQA here; Edexcel A 6.10, Edexcel B 7.2 iii and OCR B 5.1.3(a) treat it as gene expression (file 24).

### 3.4.1 DNA, genes and chromosomes

*Refs: AQA 3.4.1 · Edexcel A 2.7, 2.8 · Edexcel B 1.4 iii, vii · OCR A 2.1.3(f) · OCR B 2.1.4(e) · Eduqas CC5(i), (j), (k), (n). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- In prokaryotic cells, DNA molecules are short, circular and not associated with proteins.
- In the nucleus of eukaryotic cells, DNA molecules are very long, linear and associated with proteins called histones. Together a DNA molecule and its associated proteins form a chromosome.
- The mitochondria and chloroplasts of eukaryotic cells also contain DNA which, like prokaryotic DNA, is short, circular and not associated with protein.
- A gene is a base sequence of DNA that codes for the amino acid sequence of a polypeptide, or a functional RNA (including ribosomal RNA and tRNAs).
- A gene occupies a fixed position, called a locus, on a particular DNA molecule.
- A sequence of three DNA bases, called a triplet, codes for a specific amino acid. The genetic code is universal, non-overlapping and degenerate.
- In eukaryotes, much of the nuclear DNA does not code for polypeptides. There are, for example, non-coding multiple repeats of base sequences between genes. Even within a gene only some sequences, called exons, code for amino acid sequences. Within the gene, these exons are separated by one or more non-coding sequences, called introns.

#### Explained

**Packaging.** Each human nucleus holds about 2 m of DNA. It is wound around histone proteins (like thread round a bobbin) and coiled again and again into a compact chromosome. In prokaryotes there are no histones; the circular DNA is just looped and folded.

**The genetic code.**

- *Triplet:* three bases code for one amino acid. Four bases taken three at a time give 4³ = 64 combinations, more than enough for 20 amino acids.
- *Degenerate:* most amino acids have more than one triplet. This means some base substitutions do not change the amino acid.
- *Non-overlapping:* each base is read once, as part of only one triplet.
- *Universal:* the same triplet codes for the same amino acid in (nearly) all organisms. This is evidence of common ancestry and is why a human gene can be expressed in a bacterium.
- *Start and stop:* a start codon (AUG on mRNA, coding for methionine) begins translation; stop codons (UAA, UAG, UGA) code for no amino acid and end translation.

**Non-coding DNA.** Only about 1.5% of human DNA codes for proteins. The rest includes introns, regulatory sequences, genes for functional RNAs, and repeated sequences between genes (useful in genetic fingerprinting, file 25).

#### Common mistakes

- Saying a gene codes for "a protein" or "a characteristic". It codes for a polypeptide (or a functional RNA).
- Saying the genetic code is "universal" means all organisms have the same DNA. It means the same triplets code for the same amino acids.
- Saying introns are removed from DNA. They are removed from pre-mRNA.

### 3.4.2 DNA and protein synthesis

*Refs: AQA 3.4.2 · Edexcel A 2.6, 2.7 · Edexcel B 1.4 iv to vi · OCR A 2.1.3(g) · OCR B 2.1.4(f), (g) · Eduqas CC5(k) to (n). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- The concept of the genome as the complete set of genes in a cell and of the proteome as the full range of proteins that a cell is able to produce.
- The structure of molecules of messenger RNA (mRNA) and of transfer RNA (tRNA).
- Transcription as the production of mRNA from DNA. The role of RNA polymerase in joining mRNA nucleotides. In prokaryotes, transcription results directly in the production of mRNA from DNA. In eukaryotes, transcription results in the production of pre-mRNA; this is then spliced to form mRNA.
- Translation as the production of polypeptides from the sequence of codons carried by mRNA. The role of ribosomes and tRNA, and the role of ATP in translation.
- Relate the base sequence of nucleic acids to the amino acid sequence of polypeptides, when provided with suitable data about the genetic code.
- Interpret data from experimental work investigating the role of nucleic acids.

#### Explained

**Genome and proteome.** Every body cell has the same genome, but each cell uses only some of its genes, so different cells have different proteomes. One gene can give several proteins by alternative splicing (file 24), so the proteome can be larger than the number of genes.

**mRNA and tRNA.**

| | mRNA | tRNA |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Shape | Single, linear strand | Single strand folded into a clover-leaf shape |
| Hydrogen bonds | None | Yes, between paired bases within the molecule |
| Key part | Codons (triplets of bases complementary to the DNA template) | Anticodon (three bases complementary to an mRNA codon); amino acid binding site at one end |
| Length | Varies with gene | Short, about 80 nucleotides |

**Transcription** (in the nucleus).

1. DNA helicase unwinds the gene and breaks the hydrogen bonds between bases, exposing the template strand.
2. Free RNA nucleotides pair with complementary bases on the template strand (A with U, T with A, C with G, G with C).
3. RNA polymerase moves along the template, joining RNA nucleotides with phosphodiester bonds (condensation).
4. At a stop sequence, RNA polymerase detaches; pre-mRNA is released; the DNA rewinds.
5. In eukaryotes, the pre-mRNA is **spliced**: introns are removed and exons are joined to make mature mRNA.
6. mRNA leaves the nucleus through a nuclear pore.

In prokaryotes there are no introns, so transcription makes mRNA directly, and translation can start before transcription finishes.

**Translation** (in the cytoplasm, at ribosomes).

1. A ribosome binds to the mRNA at the start codon.
2. Each tRNA carries a specific amino acid; attaching the amino acid to its tRNA uses ATP.
3. A tRNA with an anticodon complementary to the first codon pairs with it (hydrogen bonds). A second tRNA pairs with the next codon. The ribosome holds two tRNAs at once.
4. A peptide bond forms between the two amino acids (condensation, catalysed by an enzyme in the ribosome, using ATP).
5. The ribosome moves along one codon; the first tRNA is released and goes off to collect another amino acid.
6. This continues until a stop codon is reached; the polypeptide is released.
7. Several ribosomes can translate one mRNA at the same time (a polysome), making many copies quickly.

The polypeptide then folds into its secondary and tertiary structure and may be modified in the Golgi apparatus (file 04).

**Evidence for the role of nucleic acids.** Typical data: when mRNA and ribosomes are added to a cell-free mixture of amino acids, tRNA and ATP, polypeptides are made; if RNase (which hydrolyses RNA) is added, no polypeptide is made. Synthetic mRNA made only of U nucleotides (UUU...) produced a polypeptide of only phenylalanine, showing UUU codes for phenylalanine. This was how the code was cracked.

#### Worked example

A section of DNA template strand reads: TAC GGA TTC CGT.

1. mRNA (complementary, U for A): AUG CCU AAG GCA.
2. tRNA anticodons (complementary to mRNA): UAC GGA UUC CGU.
3. Using a code table: AUG = Met, CCU = Pro, AAG = Lys, GCA = Ala.

The polypeptide begins Met–Pro–Lys–Ala.

Note that the anticodons have the same sequence as the DNA template, with U in place of T.

If a gene has 1200 bases in its exons and no stop codon is counted, how many amino acids does its polypeptide have? 1200 ÷ 3 = 400 (minus one if the last triplet is a stop codon).

#### Common mistakes

- Using T in mRNA or tRNA.
- Saying RNA polymerase forms hydrogen bonds between the mRNA and DNA.
- Saying "the tRNA reads the codon" without mentioning complementary anticodon.
- Saying the ribosome makes the amino acids.
- Saying introns are translated.
- Writing "translation happens in the nucleus".

### Check questions

1. Give two differences between DNA in a eukaryotic nucleus and DNA in a prokaryotic cell. (2 marks)
2. Explain what is meant by the genetic code being degenerate, and why this means some mutations have no effect. (3 marks)
3. Describe the role of RNA polymerase in transcription. (2 marks)
4. Explain why the mRNA of a eukaryotic gene is shorter than the gene itself. (2 marks)
5. Describe the role of tRNA in translation. (3 marks)
6. The mRNA codons for an amino acid sequence are GCU UGG AAA. Give the DNA template strand sequence. (1 mark)
7. Explain why two cells from the same person can have the same genome but different proteomes. (2 marks)
8. Give two differences between the structure of tRNA and mRNA. (2 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Any two: eukaryotic DNA long, linear, associated with histones / in chromosomes; prokaryotic DNA short, circular, not associated with proteins.
2. More than one triplet / codon codes for the same amino acid (1); a substitution may change the triplet into another for the same amino acid (1); so primary structure unchanged (1).
3. Joins RNA nucleotides together (1); by phosphodiester bonds / condensation, forming pre-mRNA (1).
4. Pre-mRNA contains introns (1); introns are removed by splicing (1).
5. Carries a specific amino acid (1); anticodon complementary to mRNA codon (1); brings amino acid to ribosome so that amino acids are joined in the right order (1).
6. CGA ACC TTT (1).
7. Different genes are expressed / switched on (1); so different proteins made (1).
8. Any two: tRNA clover-leaf shape vs mRNA linear; tRNA has hydrogen bonds between bases; tRNA has an anticodon / amino acid binding site; mRNA longer and has codons.

<!-- source: notes/a-level/biology/02-proteins-and-enzymes.md -->

## Proteins and enzymes

Proteins do most of the work in cells. This file covers amino acids, how they join, the four levels of protein structure, and how enzymes work and what changes their rate. It includes AQA required practical 1.

### Overview

AS content. Enzyme inhibitors appear again in later topics (drugs at synapses in file 34; respiratory poisons in file 16).

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.1.4.1, 3.1.4.2, RP1. Edexcel A 2.9, 2.10, CP4, and 5.16 / CP12 (temperature and Q10, A-level). Edexcel B 1.3, 1.5, CP1. OCR A 2.1.2(k) to (o), (s), 2.1.4(a) to (f). OCR B 2.1.3(a) to (f). Eduqas CC1(h) to (j), CC4(a) to (h).
- OCR A names haemoglobin, insulin and pepsin (globular) and keratin, elastin and collagen (fibrous), and asks for cofactors, coenzymes, prosthetic groups and Q10. OCR B asks for enzymes and inhibitors used in medicine (2.1.3(f)).
- Edexcel B names collagen and haemoglobin, and asks for end-product inhibition.
- Eduqas names lysozyme as its induced-fit example.
- OCR A asks for thin layer chromatography of amino acids or sugars with Rf values (2.1.2(s)); AQA lists it only as a skills opportunity.

### 3.1.4.1 General properties of proteins

*Refs: AQA 3.1.4.1 · Edexcel A 2.9 · Edexcel B 1.3 i to v · OCR A 2.1.2(k) to (o), (q), (s) · OCR B 2.1.3(a), (b) · Eduqas CC1(h) to (j). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Amino acids are the monomers of proteins. General structure: a central carbon joined to an amine group (–NH₂), a carboxyl group (–COOH), a hydrogen atom and an R group (side chain).
- The 20 amino acids common to all organisms differ only in their R groups.
- Two amino acids join by condensation to form a peptide bond, making a dipeptide. Many make a polypeptide. A functional protein may contain one or more polypeptides.
- Hydrogen bonds, ionic bonds and disulfide bridges hold proteins in shape.
- Primary, secondary, tertiary and quaternary structure, and how structure relates to function.
- The biuret test for proteins.

#### Explained

**Peptide bond.** The OH from the carboxyl group of one amino acid and an H from the amine group of the next form water. The C of one joins to the N of the other: –CO–NH–.

**Levels of structure.**

| Level | What it is | Bonds |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Primary | The sequence of amino acids in the polypeptide | Peptide bonds |
| Secondary | Local folding into α-helix or β-pleated sheet | Hydrogen bonds between the –NH and –C=O of the backbone |
| Tertiary | The polypeptide folds further into a precise 3D shape | Hydrogen bonds, ionic bonds, disulfide bridges between R groups (and hydrophobic interactions) |
| Quaternary | More than one polypeptide (sometimes with a prosthetic group) joined together | The same bonds as tertiary |

The primary structure decides everything else. Change one amino acid and the R groups that meet may differ, bonds form in different places, and the 3D shape changes. That is why a mutation can stop a protein working.

Disulfide bridges form between two cysteine R groups and are strong covalent bonds. Ionic and hydrogen bonds are weaker and broken by high temperature and extreme pH.

**Globular and fibrous proteins** (OCR A, OCR B, Edexcel B, Eduqas):

| | Globular | Fibrous |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Shape | Compact, roughly spherical | Long strands |
| Solubility | Soluble: hydrophilic R groups face outwards | Insoluble |
| Role | Metabolic: enzymes, hormones, transport | Structural |
| Examples | Haemoglobin (four polypeptides, each with an iron-containing haem prosthetic group, carries oxygen); insulin (hormone); enzymes | Collagen (three chains wound into a triple helix, cross-linked, high tensile strength in tendons, skin, bone); keratin (many disulfide bridges, in hair and nails); elastin (stretches and recoils in artery walls and alveoli) |

**Biuret test.** Add biuret reagent (sodium hydroxide then dilute copper(II) sulfate). Blue turns lilac/purple if peptide bonds are present.

**Chromatography** (OCR A). Spot the mixture on a thin layer plate, stand in solvent, let the solvent run, then locate spots (ninhydrin for amino acids). Rf = distance moved by spot ÷ distance moved by solvent. Compare Rf values with known standards run in the same conditions.

#### Worked example

An amino acid spot moves 3.2 cm; the solvent front moves 8.0 cm. Find Rf.

Rf = 3.2 ÷ 8.0 = 0.40. (Rf has no units and is always less than 1.)

#### Common mistakes

- Saying the primary structure is "the shape". It is the sequence.
- Saying secondary structure is held by bonds between R groups. It is hydrogen bonds in the backbone.
- Saying heat breaks peptide bonds. Denaturing breaks hydrogen and ionic bonds; the primary structure stays.
- Saying biuret detects amino acids. It detects peptide bonds, so free amino acids give no colour.

### 3.1.4.2 Many proteins are enzymes

*Refs: AQA 3.1.4.2, RP1 · Edexcel A 2.10, 5.16, CP4, CP12 · Edexcel B 1.5 i to vii, CP1 · OCR A 2.1.4(a) to (f) · OCR B 2.1.3(c) to (f) · Eduqas CC4(a) to (h). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Each enzyme lowers the activation energy of the reaction it catalyses.
- The induced-fit model of enzyme action. Models have changed over time (lock and key came first).
- An enzyme's properties depend on the tertiary structure of its active site, which is complementary to its substrate(s), so it forms enzyme–substrate complexes. This explains specificity.
- The effects on rate of: enzyme concentration, substrate concentration, competitive and non-competitive inhibitors, pH and temperature.
- Enzymes catalyse intracellular and extracellular reactions that build structures and control functions from cells to whole organisms.
- pH = −log₁₀[H⁺].

#### Explained

**Activation energy** is the energy needed to start a reaction. When an enzyme–substrate complex forms, bonds in the substrate are strained (or, for joining reactions, substrates are held close together in the right orientation), so less energy is needed. More molecules have enough energy at body temperature, so the reaction is faster.

**Lock and key** saw the active site as a rigid shape that exactly fits the substrate. **Induced fit** says the active site is not exactly complementary at first. When the substrate binds, the active site changes shape slightly to mould around it. This puts strain on the substrate's bonds. Induced fit explains things lock and key cannot: why some enzymes bind more than one similar substrate, and how binding something elsewhere (an allosteric site) can change the active site. Eduqas uses lysozyme, which bends the bacterial cell-wall polysaccharide as it binds and so breaks a glycosidic bond.

**Factors affecting rate.**

| Factor | Effect | Reason |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Temperature (rising, below optimum) | Rate increases | Molecules have more kinetic energy, so more successful collisions and more E–S complexes form |
| Temperature (above optimum) | Rate falls fast | Vibrations break hydrogen and ionic bonds; tertiary structure and active site change shape; substrate no longer fits; enzyme is denatured (permanent) |
| pH away from optimum | Rate falls | H⁺ or OH⁻ ions affect the charges on R groups; ionic and hydrogen bonds break; active site changes shape |
| Substrate concentration | Rate rises then levels off | More collisions until all active sites are occupied at any moment; enzyme concentration then limits |
| Enzyme concentration | Rate rises, levels off if substrate becomes limiting | More active sites available |
| Competitive inhibitor | Rate lower, but the same maximum can be reached by adding more substrate | Similar shape to the substrate; binds the active site; substrate and inhibitor compete |
| Non-competitive inhibitor | Rate lower, maximum lower, adding substrate does not help | Binds elsewhere (allosteric site); changes the shape of the active site |

**Q10** (OCR A, Edexcel A). The temperature coefficient: Q10 = rate at (T + 10 °C) ÷ rate at T. For most enzymes below their optimum it is about 2: the rate doubles for every 10 °C rise.

**Cofactors** (OCR A). Some enzymes need a non-protein helper. Inorganic ion cofactors: Cl⁻ for amylase. Coenzymes are organic and often made from vitamins: NAD, FAD, coenzyme A. A prosthetic group is a cofactor permanently bound: Zn²⁺ in carbonic anhydrase.

**Inhibition in metabolism** (OCR A, Edexcel B). In end-product inhibition, the last product of a pathway inhibits the first enzyme non-competitively, so production slows when the product builds up. This is negative feedback. Some inhibitors are reversible (bind weakly); others bind permanently and are poisons, such as cyanide (inhibits cytochrome oxidase in respiration). Many medicines are inhibitors: some antivirals inhibit viral enzymes such as reverse transcriptase; ACE inhibitors lower blood pressure.

**Intracellular and extracellular.** Catalase works inside cells, breaking down hydrogen peroxide. Amylase and trypsin are made in cells and secreted to work outside them in the gut.

#### Worked example

1. A solution has [H⁺] = 3.2 × 10⁻⁵ mol dm⁻³. Find the pH.

pH = −log₁₀(3.2 × 10⁻⁵) = 4.49 (to 3 s.f., 4.49).

2. An enzyme releases 12 cm³ of oxygen per minute at 25 °C and 21 cm³ per minute at 35 °C. Calculate Q10.

Q10 = 21 ÷ 12 = 1.75.

3. Finding an initial rate from a curved graph: draw a tangent to the curve at time 0 and find its gradient (change in y ÷ change in x). If the tangent passes through (0, 0) and (40 s, 30 cm³), the initial rate = 30 ÷ 40 = 0.75 cm³ s⁻¹.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying enzymes are "killed" by heat. They are proteins, not alive: they are denatured.
- Saying the active site and substrate are "the same shape". They are complementary.
- Saying low temperature denatures enzymes. Low temperature just lowers kinetic energy.
- Saying a non-competitive inhibitor "blocks" the active site.
- Measuring the rate at the end of a reaction instead of the initial rate. As substrate is used up the rate falls, so compare initial rates.

#### Practical

**AQA required practical 1** (also Edexcel A CP4 and CP12, Edexcel B CP1, OCR A PAG4): investigate the effect of a named variable on the rate of an enzyme-controlled reaction.

A typical method: catalase (from potato or yeast) breaking down hydrogen peroxide, collecting oxygen in an inverted measuring cylinder or gas syringe; or amylase digesting starch, sampling every 30 s into iodine on a spotting tile and timing how long until no blue-black colour.

- Independent variable: for example temperature (water baths at 20, 30, 40, 50, 60 °C) or pH (buffer solutions).
- Control variables: enzyme concentration and volume, substrate concentration and volume, pH (use a buffer) or temperature (water bath), time allowed to equilibrate before mixing.
- Control: boiled (denatured) enzyme to show any change is due to the enzyme.
- Repeat each condition at least three times and calculate a mean; identify anomalies.
- Rate = 1 ÷ time, or volume ÷ time; use a tangent at time 0 for the initial rate.
- Uncertainty: half the smallest division on the measuring instrument; percentage uncertainty = uncertainty ÷ reading × 100.
- Risks: hydrogen peroxide is an irritant (wear eye protection); hot water.

### Check questions

1. Draw the general structure of an amino acid and label the amine group, carboxyl group and R group. (2 marks)
2. Explain how a change in a single amino acid can stop an enzyme working. (4 marks)
3. Describe the induced-fit model of enzyme action. (3 marks)
4. Explain why the rate of an enzyme reaction stops increasing at high substrate concentrations. (2 marks)
5. A drug binds to the active site of an enzyme. Explain how increasing the substrate concentration would affect the inhibition. (3 marks)
6. Explain why collagen is suited to its role in tendons. (3 marks)
7. A student used amylase and starch. At 30 °C the starch was gone in 160 s; at 40 °C in 70 s. Calculate Q10, using rate = 1/time. (2 marks)
8. Give two variables a student should control when investigating the effect of pH on catalase, and say how. (2 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Central carbon with H; NH₂ on one side and COOH on the other (1); R group labelled (1).
2. Change in primary structure (1); R groups differ, so bonds (hydrogen / ionic / disulfide) form in different places (1); tertiary structure / active site shape changes (1); substrate no longer complementary, so fewer / no E–S complexes (1).
3. Active site not exactly complementary at first (1); substrate binding causes active site to change shape (1); to mould around substrate, straining its bonds / lowering activation energy (1).
4. All active sites occupied / saturated (1); enzyme concentration becomes limiting (1).
5. Competitive inhibitor (1); more substrate means substrate more likely to collide with active site than inhibitor (1); so inhibition decreases / rate approaches maximum (1).
6. Three polypeptide chains wound into a triple helix (1); hydrogen bonds / cross-links between chains (1); high tensile strength / does not stretch (1). Accept: insoluble.
7. Rate at 40 °C = 1/70 = 0.0143; rate at 30 °C = 1/160 = 0.00625 (1); Q10 = 2.3 (1).
8. Any two with method (1 each): temperature, water bath; hydrogen peroxide concentration / volume, measure with syringe from same stock; catalase concentration / mass of potato, same mass / same size discs; time.

<!-- source: notes/a-level/biology/24-mutation-stem-cells-and-gene-expression.md -->

## Gene mutations, stem cells and the control of gene expression

Every cell has the same genes, but each uses only some of them. This file covers the types and effects of gene mutation, stem cells, how transcription and translation are controlled (transcription factors, epigenetics, RNA interference, the lac operon, homeobox genes), and how faulty control leads to cancer.

### Overview

A-level only for AQA. Basic mutations are introduced in file 13; chromosome disorders in file 39; cancer screening and treatment in file 44.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.8.1, 3.8.2.1 to 3.8.2.3. Edexcel A 2.12, 3.11, 3.12, 3.14, 6.10, 7.16. Edexcel B 1.4 viii, ix, 7.2, 7.3, 8.1 i. OCR A 2.1.6(j) to (m), 6.1.1(a) to (d). OCR B 3.1.1(c) to (e), 3.3.1(b) to (d), 5.1.1(b), 5.1.2(f), 5.1.3(a), (h). Eduqas C2.5(f) to (h), C2.7(i).
- OCR A alone asks for the lac operon, homeobox genes, and the formation of erythrocytes, neutrophils, xylem and phloem from stem cells (6.1.1(b), (c), 2.1.6(k), (l)). Included below.
- OCR A and OCR B ask for apoptosis.
- OCR B asks for RNA interference (5.1.3(h)); AQA includes RNAi under translation.
- Edexcel A, Edexcel B and Eduqas use sickle cell anaemia as the example of a point mutation.
- Edexcel B and Edexcel A ask for post-transcriptional modification (alternative splicing) giving more than one protein from one gene.

### 3.8.1 Alteration of the sequence of bases in DNA can alter the structure of proteins

*Refs: AQA 3.8.1 · Edexcel A 2.12 · Edexcel B 1.4 viii, ix, 8.1 i · OCR A 6.1.1(a) · OCR B 5.1.1(b) · Eduqas C2.5(f), (g). Level: A-level only (AS for Edexcel A and B).*

#### What you need to know

- Gene mutations might arise during DNA replication. They include addition, deletion, substitution, inversion, duplication and translocation of bases.
- Gene mutations occur spontaneously. The mutation rate is increased by mutagenic agents. Some mutations result in a different amino acid sequence in the encoded polypeptide. Due to the degenerate nature of the genetic code, not all base substitutions cause a change in the sequence of encoded amino acids. Some gene mutations change only one triplet code; others result in a frame shift.
- Relate the nature of a gene mutation to its effect on the encoded polypeptide.

#### Explained

| Mutation | What happens | Effect |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Substitution | One base replaced by another | One triplet changes; may be silent (same amino acid), missense (one different amino acid) or nonsense (stop codon, short polypeptide) |
| Addition (insertion) | Extra base added | Frame shift: every triplet after it changes |
| Deletion | Base lost | Frame shift |
| Duplication | One or more bases repeated | Frame shift (unless a whole number of triplets) |
| Inversion | A group of bases reversed | Changes the triplets within that section |
| Translocation of bases | A group of bases moves from one location to another (same or different chromosome) | Can disrupt genes at both locations |

Adding or deleting three bases removes or adds one amino acid without a frame shift.

**Effect on the protein.** A different primary structure may change the bonds that form between R groups, so the tertiary structure changes. An enzyme's active site may no longer be complementary to its substrate. Some mutations are harmful, most are neutral, and a few are beneficial.

**Sickle cell anaemia** (Edexcel A, Edexcel B, Eduqas). A substitution in the gene for the β chain of haemoglobin changes one base on the coding strand from A to T, so the triplet GAG (glutamic acid) becomes GTG (valine); on the mRNA, GAG becomes GUG. Valine is non-polar, so haemoglobin molecules stick together at low oxygen, forming long fibres. Red blood cells become sickle-shaped, carry less oxygen and block capillaries. Heterozygotes are protected against malaria, so the allele is common where malaria is.

**Mutagens.** Ionising radiation (breaks DNA strands); UV light (makes adjacent thymines bond together); chemicals such as benzene, mustard gas, nitrogen oxides and tar in tobacco smoke (alter bases or insert between them); some viruses insert DNA into the genome. A carcinogen is a mutagen that causes cancer (Eduqas C2.5(g)).

#### Common mistakes

- Saying all mutations are harmful.
- Saying a substitution "always changes the protein".
- Saying a frame shift changes "one amino acid".

### 3.8.2.1 Most of a cell's DNA is not translated (stem cells)

*Refs: AQA 3.8.2.1 · Edexcel A 3.11, 3.12 · Edexcel B 7.3 i to v · OCR A 2.1.6(j) to (m) · OCR B 3.1.1(e) · Eduqas C2.7(i). Level: A-level only (AS for Edexcel A, OCR A, OCR B).*

#### What you need to know

- Totipotent cells are cells that can mature into any body cell. During development, totipotent cells translate only part of their DNA, resulting in cell specialisation.
- In mature mammals, only a few totipotent cells, called stem cells, remain. Stem cells are undifferentiated cells that can divide indefinitely and can become specialised.
- Totipotent cells occur only for a limited time in early mammalian embryos. Pluripotent cells are found in embryos; multipotent and unipotent cells are found in mature mammals.
- Unipotent cells, exemplified by the formation of cardiomyocytes.
- Induced pluripotent stem cells (iPS cells) can be produced from adult somatic cells using appropriate protein transcription factors.
- Stem cells can be used in the treatment of human disorders.
- Evaluate the use of stem cells in treating human disorders.

#### Explained

| Type | Can become | Found in |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Totipotent | Any cell, including placenta | Zygote and the first few divisions (morula) |
| Pluripotent | Almost any cell type, but not placenta | Inner cell mass of the blastocyst (embryonic stem cells) |
| Multipotent | A few related types | Adult tissues: bone marrow (all blood cells) |
| Unipotent | One type | Adult tissues: cells in the heart that form cardiomyocytes (heart muscle cells) |

**Specialisation.** All cells have the same genes. As cells differentiate, some genes are switched on and others off, so only some proteins are made (differential gene expression, Edexcel A 3.12). Once specialised, most mammalian cells cannot go back.

**Examples of differentiation** (OCR A). Multipotent stem cells in bone marrow produce erythrocytes (lose the nucleus and most organelles, fill with haemoglobin; live about 120 days) and neutrophils (lobed nucleus, many lysosomes; live about 6 hours). In plants, meristem cells in the vascular cambium produce xylem vessels (lignin laid down; cells die; end walls break down) and phloem sieve tubes (lose nucleus and most organelles; sieve plates form; companion cells keep them alive).

**Induced pluripotent stem cells.** Adult cells (such as skin fibroblasts) are made pluripotent by switching on key genes using specific transcription factors (introduced with viral vectors). iPS cells behave like embryonic stem cells.

**Uses.** Bone marrow transplants treat leukaemia and sickle cell disease. Research uses: growing tissues to test drugs; studying development. Possible future uses: type 1 diabetes (new β cells), spinal cord injury, Parkinson's disease, heart damage, macular degeneration. Plant stem cells (meristems) are used in tissue culture (file 29).

**Evaluating.**

| | Benefits | Problems |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Embryonic stem cells | Pluripotent; divide indefinitely | Embryo destroyed (ethical objection: the embryo may be considered a potential person); risk of rejection; risk of tumours |
| Adult stem cells | No embryo destroyed; patient's own cells, so no rejection | Multipotent only; few in number; harder to extract |
| iPS cells | Patient's own cells (no rejection); no embryo destroyed; pluripotent | Viral vectors could cause mutations or cancer; not yet proven safe; may keep some "memory" of their original cell type |

Regulation in the UK: research on embryos is licensed by the HFEA and allowed only up to 14 days.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying stem cells are "only in embryos".
- Saying differentiated cells lose genes. They keep all genes but express only some.

### 3.8.2.2 Regulation of transcription and translation

*Refs: AQA 3.8.2.2 · Edexcel A 3.14, 6.10, 7.16 · Edexcel B 7.2 i to v, 7.3 iii · OCR A 6.1.1(b) to (d) · OCR B 3.1.1(c), (d), 5.1.2(f), 5.1.3(a), (h) · Eduqas C2.5(h). Level: A-level only (AS for Edexcel A 3.14, OCR B 3.1.1).*

#### What you need to know

- In eukaryotes, transcription of target genes can be stimulated or inhibited when specific transcriptional factors move from the cytoplasm into the nucleus.
- The role of the steroid hormone, oestrogen, in initiating transcription.
- Epigenetic control of gene expression in eukaryotes: environmental factors can alter the structure of DNA and associated proteins without changing the base sequence; increased methylation of the DNA or decreased acetylation of associated histones inhibits transcription.
- The relevance of epigenetics on the development and treatment of disease, especially cancer.
- In eukaryotes and some prokaryotes, translation of the mRNA produced from target genes can be inhibited by RNA interference (RNAi).
- Interpret data from investigations into gene expression; evaluate appropriate data for the relative influences of genetic and environmental factors on phenotype.

#### Explained

**Transcription factors** are proteins that bind to the promoter region of DNA (near the start of a gene) and help or stop RNA polymerase binding.

- *Activators* help RNA polymerase bind, so transcription starts.
- *Repressors* block RNA polymerase, so the gene is not transcribed.

**Oestrogen.**

1. Oestrogen is a lipid-soluble steroid, so it diffuses through the cell-surface membrane.
2. In the cytoplasm it binds to a receptor site on a transcription factor (the oestrogen receptor), changing its shape.
3. The shape change releases the transcription factor from an inhibitor molecule, so it can enter the nucleus and bind to DNA.
4. It stimulates transcription of target genes.

(Edexcel A 7.16) Other hormones work the same way, and transcription factors respond to many signals.

**Epigenetics.** Heritable changes in gene expression without any change in the DNA base sequence. The "epigenome" is the set of chemical tags on DNA and histones.

- *DNA methylation:* methyl groups attach to cytosine bases (at CpG sites) in or near the promoter. Methylation stops transcription factors binding, and attracts proteins that condense the chromatin. Gene switched off.
- *Histone acetylation:* adding acetyl groups to histones makes them bind DNA less tightly, so the chromatin is loose and open, and transcription factors can reach the DNA. Decreased acetylation (deacetylation) makes chromatin condense; genes are switched off.

Environmental factors (diet, stress, smoking, toxins, maternal diet during pregnancy) can change these tags. Some changes pass to offspring. Epigenetic changes help keep differentiated cells specialised (Edexcel B 7.2 v), and explain why identical twins become more different as they age.

**Epigenetics and disease.** Cancers often show hypermethylation of tumour suppressor genes (switching them off) and hypomethylation of oncogenes. Treatments include drugs that inhibit DNA methyltransferase (removing methylation) or histone deacetylase inhibitors (keeping chromatin open), reactivating tumour suppressor genes. These drugs must be targeted, because they affect all cells.

**RNA interference (RNAi).** Small double-stranded RNA molecules (siRNA, or microRNA in some cases) are made or introduced. One strand joins a protein complex (RISC). The complex binds to mRNA with a complementary base sequence and the mRNA is cut up, so it is not translated. Uses: research (switching off genes to find out what they do), possible treatments for viral infection and genetic disease.

**Post-transcriptional modification** (Edexcel A 6.10, Edexcel B 7.2 iii, OCR B 5.1.3(a)). Pre-mRNA is spliced, removing introns. In *alternative splicing*, different combinations of exons are kept, so one gene gives several different mRNAs and proteins. OCR B also includes RNA editing (changing particular bases in mRNA).

**The lac operon** (OCR A 6.1.1(b)). In *E. coli*, three structural genes for lactose use (*lacZ* for β-galactosidase, *lacY* for permease, *lacA*) share one promoter and an operator, controlled by a regulatory gene that codes for a repressor protein.

- *No lactose:* the repressor binds to the operator, blocking RNA polymerase. No enzymes are made (saves energy).
- *Lactose present:* lactose binds to the repressor, changing its shape so it falls off the operator. RNA polymerase binds to the promoter and transcribes the genes; the enzymes are made.
- Glucose is the preferred respiratory substrate: when glucose is present, levels of cAMP are low, and transcription stays low even with lactose (cAMP–CRP needed for full transcription).

**Other control** (OCR A). After translation, proteins can be activated by cAMP (as in the second messenger model, file 20), by adding a phosphate group, or by cutting an inactive precursor.

**Homeobox genes** (OCR A 6.1.1(c)). Genes that control the development of the body plan. Each contains a 180-base-pair sequence (the homeobox) that codes for a homeodomain, a part of a transcription factor that binds DNA. Hox genes (a group of homeobox genes) are arranged on the chromosome in the same order as the body regions they control, and are very similar in animals as different as fruit flies and humans, showing they are highly conserved.

**Apoptosis** (OCR A 6.1.1(d), OCR B 3.1.1(c), (d)). Programmed cell death:

1. Enzymes break down the cytoskeleton.
2. The cytoplasm becomes dense; the cell shrinks; the membrane forms bulges (blebs).
3. Chromatin condenses; DNA breaks into fragments; the nucleus breaks up.
4. The cell breaks into membrane-bound vesicles, which phagocytes engulf. No inflammation, because no hydrolytic enzymes leak out.

Apoptosis removes cells that are damaged, unnecessary or harmful. In development it shapes body parts (separating fingers and toes) and removes excess neurones. Too little apoptosis can lead to cancer; too much to degenerative disease. Mitosis and apoptosis together build and shape the body.

**Genetic vs environmental influences.** Twin studies compare identical (monozygotic) and non-identical twins. If identical twins are much more alike for a trait than non-identical twins, genes have a strong influence. Twins raised apart show environmental effects (file 34 for Edexcel A 8.19).

#### Common mistakes

- Saying oestrogen binds to a receptor on the cell-surface membrane.
- Saying methylation changes the base sequence.
- Saying acetylation switches genes off. Acetylation opens chromatin; decreased acetylation switches genes off.
- Saying siRNA "destroys DNA".
- In the lac operon, saying lactose binds to the operator.

### 3.8.2.3 Gene expression and cancer

*Refs: AQA 3.8.2.3 · OCR B 3.3.1(b) to (d) · Eduqas C2.5(g). Level: A-level only (AS for OCR B).*

#### What you need to know

- The main characteristics of benign and malignant tumours.
- The role of the following in the development of tumours: tumour suppressor genes and oncogenes; abnormal methylation of tumour suppressor genes and oncogenes; increased oestrogen concentrations in the development of some breast cancers.
- Evaluate evidence showing correlations between genetic and environmental factors and various forms of cancer. Interpret information relating to the way in which an understanding of the roles of oncogenes and tumour suppressor genes could be used in the prevention, treatment and cure of cancer.

#### Explained

**Tumours.**

| Benign | Malignant |
| --- | --- |
| Grow slowly | Grow rapidly |
| Cells are well differentiated, with a normal nucleus | Cells become unspecialised; large, darker nucleus |
| Enclosed in a capsule; stay in one place | No capsule; invade nearby tissues |
| Do not spread | Spread through blood and lymph to form secondary tumours (metastasis) |
| Usually easier to remove; rarely recur | Need surgery, chemotherapy and radiotherapy; often recur |

**Proto-oncogenes and oncogenes.** Proto-oncogenes code for proteins that stimulate cell division (growth factor receptors, for example). A mutation can make a proto-oncogene into an oncogene that is permanently active: the receptor is switched on without a growth factor, or too much growth factor is made. The cell divides without control. One mutated copy is enough (dominant effect).

**Tumour suppressor genes** code for proteins that slow cell division, repair DNA, or trigger apoptosis (such as the p53 protein, and the BRCA1 gene product). A mutation that inactivates them removes the brake. Usually both copies must be inactivated.

**Abnormal methylation.** Hypermethylation of a tumour suppressor gene switches it off; hypomethylation of a proto-oncogene switches it on. Both lead to uncontrolled division.

**Oestrogen and breast cancer.** After the menopause, fat tissue in the breast can make oestrogen. Raised oestrogen concentrations activate transcription factors that switch on genes for cell division. If a tumour has formed, oestrogen helps it grow. Drugs such as tamoxifen bind to oestrogen receptors in breast cells and block them.

**Using this knowledge.**

- Screening: testing for mutations in BRCA1 and BRCA2 identifies people at high risk, who may choose more frequent screening or preventive surgery.
- Treatment: drugs targeted at an oncogene's protein product (monoclonal antibodies blocking an overactive receptor, such as HER2 in some breast cancers); demethylating drugs (above).
- Prevention: avoiding mutagens (smoking, UV).
- Evaluating data: correlations between, say, diet and a cancer may be due to other factors; large, controlled studies are needed.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying oncogenes are always present and "cause cancer". Proto-oncogenes are normal genes.
- Saying a tumour suppressor gene mutation "speeds up division". It removes the control that slows division.
- Saying benign tumours are always harmless (they can press on organs).

### Check questions

1. Explain why a deletion of one base usually has a bigger effect than a substitution. (3 marks)
2. Define the terms totipotent and multipotent, giving where each is found. (2 marks)
3. Evaluate the use of induced pluripotent stem cells compared with embryonic stem cells. (4 marks)
4. Describe how oestrogen stimulates transcription of a target gene. (4 marks)
5. Explain how increased DNA methylation and decreased histone acetylation affect gene expression. (4 marks)
6. Describe how RNA interference prevents translation of a gene. (3 marks)
7. Explain how a mutation in a tumour suppressor gene can lead to a tumour. (3 marks)
8. Describe how the lac operon is switched on in the presence of lactose. (3 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Deletion causes frame shift (1); all triplets after it change (1); many amino acids change, so protein very different / non-functional; a substitution changes one triplet only, possibly no amino acid change (1).
2. Totipotent: can become any cell (including placental); early embryo (1). Multipotent: can become a few related cell types; adult tissues such as bone marrow (1).
3. Any four: iPS no embryo destroyed (ethical advantage); from patient's own cells, so no rejection; pluripotent like ESCs; but viral vectors / risk of mutation or cancer; not yet proven / less well understood; ESCs ethical objections / risk of rejection.
4. Oestrogen is lipid-soluble, diffuses through membrane (1); binds to receptor on transcription factor in cytoplasm, changing shape (1); transcription factor released from inhibitor and enters nucleus (1); binds to promoter, helping RNA polymerase / stimulating transcription (1).
5. Methyl groups added to cytosine / promoter (1); prevents transcription factors binding (1); decreased acetylation makes histones bind DNA more tightly / chromatin condensed (1); transcription inhibited / gene switched off (1).
6. siRNA (single strand) associates with protein complex (1); binds to complementary mRNA (1); mRNA cut / broken down so not translated (1).
7. Tumour suppressor genes normally slow cell division / cause apoptosis / repair DNA (1); mutation inactivates the protein (1); cell divides uncontrollably / damaged cells not removed (1).
8. Lactose binds to repressor (1); repressor changes shape and leaves operator (1); RNA polymerase binds to promoter and transcribes structural genes / enzymes made (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/biology/21-inheritance.md -->

## Inheritance

Genetic crosses predict the offspring of parents with known genotypes. This file covers monohybrid and dihybrid crosses, codominance, multiple alleles, sex linkage, autosomal linkage, epistasis, polygenic inheritance and the chi-squared test.

### Overview

A-level only for AQA. Meiosis is in file 13; Hardy–Weinberg in file 22; genetic disorders, screening and counselling in file 39.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.7.1. Edexcel A 2.13, 3.8, 3.15. Edexcel B 8.2 i to vi. OCR A 6.1.2(a) to (d). OCR B 5.1.1(a), (c) to (e). Eduqas C2.5(a) to (e).
- Edexcel A asks for pedigree diagrams and cystic fibrosis as its monohybrid example (CF itself in file 39), and polygenic inheritance (3.15).
- OCR A asks for continuous and discontinuous variation linked to polygenes (6.1.2(d)), and for environmental effects such as chlorosis and etiolation in plants (6.1.2(a)).
- OCR B asks for model organisms used in genetics (5.1.1(e)).
- Eduqas names haemophilia and Duchenne muscular dystrophy as sex-linked examples (C2.5(e)); AQA uses no fixed examples.

### 3.7.1 Inheritance

*Refs: AQA 3.7.1 · Edexcel A 2.13, 3.8, 3.15 · Edexcel B 8.2 i to vi · OCR A 6.1.2(a) to (d) · OCR B 5.1.1(a), (c) to (e) · Eduqas C2.5(a) to (e). Level: A-level only (AS for Edexcel A).*

#### What you need to know

- The genotype is the genetic constitution of an organism. The phenotype is the expression of this genetic constitution and its interaction with the environment.
- There may be many alleles of a single gene. Alleles may be dominant, recessive or codominant.
- In a diploid organism, the alleles at a specific locus may be either homozygous or heterozygous.
- The use of fully labelled genetic diagrams to interpret, or predict, the results of: monohybrid and dihybrid crosses involving dominant, recessive and codominant alleles; crosses involving sex-linkage, autosomal linkage, multiple alleles and epistasis.
- The use of the chi-squared (χ²) test to compare the goodness of fit of observed phenotypic ratios with expected ratios.

#### Explained

**Terms.**

- *Gene:* a length of DNA at a locus that codes for a polypeptide.
- *Allele:* a different form of the same gene.
- *Dominant:* always expressed in the phenotype when present (capital letter).
- *Recessive:* expressed only when two copies are present (lower case).
- *Codominant:* both alleles are expressed in the heterozygote.
- *Homozygous:* two identical alleles (AA or aa). *Heterozygous:* two different alleles (Aa).

**Monohybrid cross.** One gene. Two heterozygotes (Aa × Aa) give offspring genotypes 1 AA : 2 Aa : 1 aa, and phenotypes 3 dominant : 1 recessive.

A full genetic diagram has: parental phenotypes; parental genotypes; gametes (circled); a Punnett square; offspring genotypes; offspring phenotypes; ratio.

*Test cross:* cross an organism with the dominant phenotype with a homozygous recessive. If any offspring show the recessive phenotype, the parent was heterozygous.

**Codominance.** In snapdragons, C^R C^R gives red, C^W C^W white, C^R C^W pink. A cross of two pink gives 1 red : 2 pink : 1 white. Human example: the sickle cell alleles (Hb^A Hb^S gives sickle cell trait).

**Multiple alleles.** Human ABO blood groups: one gene with three alleles, I^A, I^B and I^O. I^A and I^B are codominant; both are dominant to I^O.

| Genotype | Blood group |
| --- | --- |
| I^A I^A, I^A I^O | A |
| I^B I^B, I^B I^O | B |
| I^A I^B | AB |
| I^O I^O | O |

A parent with group A (I^A I^O) and a parent with group B (I^B I^O) can have children of all four groups.

**Dihybrid cross.** Two genes on different chromosomes. AaBb × AaBb: each parent makes four kinds of gamete (AB, Ab, aB, ab) in equal numbers because of independent segregation. Offspring phenotypes: 9 : 3 : 3 : 1.

**Sex linkage.** Genes on the X chromosome. Females are XX; males XY. The Y chromosome is small and carries few of the genes on the X, so males have only one allele of X-linked genes and express it, even if recessive. Example: haemophilia (a recessive allele, X^h, for a missing clotting factor).

- Carrier mother (X^H X^h) × normal father (X^H Y): daughters X^H X^H or X^H X^h (none affected, half carriers); sons X^H Y or X^h Y (half affected).
- Males inherit their X from their mother, so a father cannot pass an X-linked allele to his sons.
- Duchenne muscular dystrophy (Eduqas) and red-green colour blindness are other X-linked recessive conditions.

**Autosomal linkage.** Genes on the same autosome are linked: they tend to be inherited together, because they move together into gametes. A dihybrid cross then does not give 9:3:3:1. Parental combinations appear more often than expected. New (recombinant) combinations appear only when crossing over separates the alleles; the further apart the genes are on the chromosome, the more often crossing over separates them.

*Recombination frequency* = number of recombinant offspring ÷ total offspring.

**Epistasis.** The allele of one gene affects the expression of another gene. Often one gene controls an enzyme early in a pathway:

- *Recessive epistasis:* homozygous recessive at gene A (aa) masks gene B. In some mammals, aa means no pigment at all (white), whatever the colour alleles at B. AaBb × AaBb gives 9 : 3 : 4.
- *Dominant epistasis:* a dominant allele at gene A masks gene B. AaBb × AaBb gives 12 : 3 : 1 (or 13 : 3).

**Polygenic inheritance** (Edexcel A, OCR A). Many genes, each with a small additive effect, affect one characteristic (height, skin colour). With the environment, this gives continuous variation and a normal distribution. Discontinuous variation is usually one gene with distinct phenotypes.

**Environment** (OCR A). Phenotype = genotype + environment. Plants grown in the dark are tall, thin and yellow (etiolation); plants short of magnesium are yellow (chlorosis), even if their genes for chlorophyll are normal.

**Pedigree diagrams** (Edexcel A, Edexcel B). Squares are males, circles females; shaded symbols are affected. If two unaffected parents have an affected child, the condition is recessive and both parents are carriers. If an affected child always has an affected parent, it is likely dominant.

**Model organisms** (OCR B). Fruit flies (*Drosophila*), mice, peas and *Arabidopsis* are used to study inheritance because they breed quickly, produce many offspring, are cheap and easy to keep, have visible variation, and their genomes are known. Results often apply to humans because genes and processes are shared.

**The chi-squared test.** Tests whether the difference between observed (O) and expected (E) results is due to chance.

χ² = Σ (O − E)² ÷ E

1. Null hypothesis: there is no significant difference between observed and expected results.
2. Calculate expected numbers from the ratio and the total.
3. Calculate χ².
4. Degrees of freedom = number of categories − 1.
5. Compare with the critical value at p = 0.05.
6. If χ² is less than the critical value, accept the null hypothesis: any difference is due to chance. If greater, reject it: the difference is significant (perhaps the genes are linked, or there is epistasis).

#### Worked example

A dihybrid cross in peas gave: round yellow 296, round green 104, wrinkled yellow 92, wrinkled green 28. Total = 520. Expected ratio 9:3:3:1.

1. Expected: 520 × 9/16 = 292.5; 520 × 3/16 = 97.5; 97.5; 520 × 1/16 = 32.5.
2. (O − E)²/E:
   - (296 − 292.5)² ÷ 292.5 = 12.25 ÷ 292.5 = 0.042
   - (104 − 97.5)² ÷ 97.5 = 42.25 ÷ 97.5 = 0.433
   - (92 − 97.5)² ÷ 97.5 = 30.25 ÷ 97.5 = 0.310
   - (28 − 32.5)² ÷ 32.5 = 20.25 ÷ 32.5 = 0.623
3. χ² = 1.41.
4. Degrees of freedom = 3. Critical value at p = 0.05 is 7.82.
5. 1.41 < 7.82, so accept the null hypothesis. The results fit a 9:3:3:1 ratio; the difference is due to chance.

Sex linkage example: a colour-blind man (X^b Y) and a woman with normal vision whose father was colour-blind (X^B X^b). Probability that their next child is a colour-blind son: gametes X^B, X^b from mother; X^b, Y from father. Children: X^B X^b, X^B Y, X^b X^b, X^b Y. Colour-blind son = X^b Y = 1/4.

#### Common mistakes

- Writing gametes with two alleles of the same gene (Aa) or omitting the circle convention.
- Writing sex-linked alleles without the X (just "h") or putting alleles on the Y.
- Saying a 3:1 ratio means "exactly 3 out of every 4". It is a probability.
- Concluding the null hypothesis is "proved". It is accepted or rejected.
- Using the wrong degrees of freedom (number of categories − 1, not number of individuals).
- Saying linked genes "are on different chromosomes".

### Check questions

1. Define the terms genotype and codominant. (2 marks)
2. In cats, the allele for orange fur (X^O) and the allele for black fur (X^o) are codominant and X-linked; heterozygous females are tortoiseshell. Explain why male tortoiseshell cats are very rare. (2 marks)
3. Two parents with blood groups A and B have a child with group O. Give the parents' genotypes and the probability that their next child is group AB. (3 marks)
4. A dihybrid cross between two heterozygotes gave far more offspring with the parental combinations than expected. Suggest and explain why. (3 marks)
5. In a species, gene C controls pigment production (cc = no pigment, white) and gene B gives black (B) or brown (bb). Predict the phenotype ratio from CcBb × CcBb. (3 marks)
6. A monohybrid cross gave 72 dominant and 32 recessive. Use chi-squared to test whether this fits 3:1 (critical value 3.84 at 1 degree of freedom, p = 0.05). (4 marks)
7. Explain why a father cannot pass haemophilia to his son. (2 marks)
8. Give three features that make *Drosophila* a useful model organism in genetics. (3 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Genotype: alleles an organism has / genetic constitution (1); codominant: both alleles expressed in the heterozygote (1).
2. Tortoiseshell needs both X^O and X^o / two X chromosomes (1); males have only one X / XY (1).
3. I^A I^O and I^B I^O (1); genetic diagram / four outcomes (1); probability of AB = 1/4 (1).
4. Genes linked / on the same chromosome (1); alleles inherited together (1); recombinants only from crossing over, which is infrequent (1).
5. Diagram or reasoning (1); 9 black (C_B_) : 3 brown (C_bb) (1) : 4 white (cc__) (1).
6. Total 104; expected 78 and 26 (1); χ² = (72 − 78)²/78 + (32 − 26)²/26 = 0.46 + 1.38 = 1.85 (1); less than 3.84 (1); accept null hypothesis: difference due to chance, fits 3:1 (1).
7. Haemophilia allele on X chromosome (1); father gives son a Y chromosome (1).
8. Any three: short life cycle / breed quickly; many offspring; cheap / easy to keep; visible mutations / variation; small genome / sequenced; few chromosomes.

<!-- source: notes/a-level/biology/39-genetic-disorders-screening-and-antenatal-care.md -->

## Genetic disorders, chromosome mutations, screening and antenatal care

This file covers cystic fibrosis as a model genetic disorder, chromosome mutations (non-disjunction and translocation) and karyotypes, care and screening during pregnancy (diet, alcohol, smoking, ultrasound, amniocentesis, chorionic villus sampling), genetic screening and counselling, and the ethical issues they raise.

### Overview

Inheritance and pedigrees are in file 21; gene mutations and sickle cell anaemia in file 24; DNA probes and screening technology in file 25; human reproduction in file 36. AQA covers screening with DNA probes (file 25) but not this content.

#### Board differences

- Edexcel A 2.14 to 2.16. Edexcel B 1.4 ix, 2.3 vi, vii. OCR B 3.1.2(d) to (i), 5.1.1(f), (g), 5.1.2(b). Eduqas C2.5(f).
- Edexcel A uses cystic fibrosis to show how a gene mutation affects the body, and asks for genetic screening (carrier testing, pre-implantation genetic diagnosis, prenatal testing) and its ethics.
- Edexcel B asks for translocations and non-disjunction (including Down's syndrome).
- OCR B asks for the UK antenatal care programme, diet, alcohol and smoking in pregnancy, fetal monitoring, amniocentesis and CVS, karyotypes, and the role of the genetic counsellor.
- Eduqas uses sickle cell anaemia (gene mutation) and Down's syndrome (chromosome mutation).

### Cystic fibrosis

*Refs: Edexcel A 2.14. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- How the expression of a gene mutation in people with cystic fibrosis impairs the functioning of the gaseous exchange, digestive and reproductive systems.

#### Explained

Cystic fibrosis (CF) is caused by recessive alleles of the **CFTR** gene, which codes for a chloride ion channel protein in the cell-surface membranes of epithelial cells. The most common mutation deletes three bases, removing one amino acid (phenylalanine); the protein misfolds and is broken down, so few channels reach the membrane. Inheritance is autosomal recessive: affected people are homozygous (ff); carriers (Ff) are healthy.

**Normal CFTR:** in airway epithelium, CFTR lets chloride ions out of cells onto the surface; sodium follows; this lowers the water potential of the surface fluid, so water moves out of the cells by osmosis. The mucus stays thin and runny and cilia move it away.

**In CF:** chloride ions are not secreted and sodium is absorbed more, so water moves into the cells by osmosis. Mucus becomes thick and sticky.

| System | Effect |
| --- | --- |
| Gas exchange | Cilia cannot move the thick mucus; it blocks bronchioles; less air reaches alveoli; bacteria trapped in mucus cause repeated infections, which damage lung tissue; less surface area and longer diffusion distance |
| Digestive | Mucus blocks the pancreatic duct, so less pancreatic enzyme reaches the duodenum; food is poorly digested; mucus on the gut lining reduces absorption; poor growth. The pancreas may become damaged (CF-related diabetes) |
| Reproductive | Men: sperm ducts blocked or absent, so most are infertile. Women: thick cervical mucus reduces fertility |

**Treatment:** physiotherapy to loosen mucus; antibiotics; mucus-thinning drugs; enzyme capsules with meals; modulator drugs that help faulty CFTR reach the membrane and open; lung transplants; gene therapy is under research.

### Chromosome mutations and karyotypes

*Refs: Edexcel B 2.3 vi, vii · OCR B 3.1.2(i), 5.1.1(f) · Eduqas C2.5(f). Level: AS & A (Edexcel B, OCR B); A-level only (Eduqas).*

#### What you need to know

- Chromosome mutations, as illustrated by translocations.
- How non-disjunction leads to polysomy (an extra chromosome), including Down's syndrome.
- The production and use of karyotypes.

#### Explained

**Chromosome mutations** change the structure or number of chromosomes.

- **Translocation:** part of one chromosome breaks off and attaches to another (often a non-homologous chromosome). In a *balanced* translocation all the genetic material is present and the person is usually healthy, but their gametes may have too much or too little of a chromosome, leading to miscarriage or disorders in children. Some cancers (such as a form of leukaemia, where parts of chromosomes 9 and 22 swap, making a fused oncogene) are caused by translocations in body cells.
- **Non-disjunction:** homologous chromosomes (meiosis I) or sister chromatids (meiosis II) fail to separate, so a gamete has one extra or one missing chromosome (file 13).
  - **Down's syndrome:** trisomy 21 (three copies of chromosome 21, 47 chromosomes). Effects include learning disability, characteristic facial features, heart defects. The risk rises with the mother's age, because oocytes have been paused in meiosis I for decades.
  - **Turner's syndrome:** 45, X (one X chromosome, no second sex chromosome) in females: short stature, infertile.
  - **Klinefelter's syndrome:** 47, XXY in males.
