  - **Polysomy** means extra copies of a chromosome; **polyploidy** (whole extra sets) is common in plants.

**Karyotypes.** A karyotype is an image of a person's chromosomes arranged in pairs by size, banding pattern and centromere position.

1. Obtain cells that are dividing (white blood cells, or fetal cells from amniocentesis or CVS; cultured to increase numbers).
2. Add colchicine, which stops spindle formation, so cells stop at metaphase, when chromosomes are most condensed.
3. Put cells in a hypotonic (dilute) solution so they swell and chromosomes spread out; fix and stain (giving bands); photograph.
4. Arrange chromosomes into 22 pairs of autosomes plus the sex chromosomes.

Karyotypes show extra or missing chromosomes, large deletions and translocations, and the sex of the fetus. They cannot show single gene mutations.

### Antenatal care and screening in pregnancy

*Refs: OCR B 3.1.2(d) to (h) · Edexcel A 2.15. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- The programme of antenatal care in the United Kingdom.
- Dietary changes recommended during pregnancy.
- The effects of alcohol consumption and smoking during pregnancy.
- Monitoring fetal growth; screening tests.
- Amniocentesis and chorionic villus sampling: methods, advantages and disadvantages.

#### Explained

**Antenatal care in the UK** (OCR B). Regular appointments with midwives and doctors: checks of the mother's blood pressure (high blood pressure can signal pre-eclampsia), urine (protein, glucose for gestational diabetes), weight; blood tests (blood group and rhesus factor, anaemia, infections such as HIV, hepatitis B and syphilis); ultrasound scans (dating at about 12 weeks; anomaly scan at about 20 weeks); screening for Down's syndrome and other conditions; advice on diet, smoking, alcohol and exercise; offered vaccines (whooping cough, flu).

**Diet in pregnancy.**

- **Folic acid** supplements before conception and for the first 12 weeks: lowers the risk of neural tube defects such as spina bifida.
- **Vitamin D** supplements (bones; calcium absorption).
- Enough iron (more blood is made; anaemia is common) and calcium (fetal skeleton).
- Avoid: too much vitamin A (liver; can harm the fetus); unpasteurised dairy and undercooked meat (*Listeria*, *Toxoplasma*); certain fish high in mercury; limit caffeine.
- Only a small increase in energy intake is needed, mostly in the last 3 months.

**Alcohol.** Alcohol crosses the placenta, and the fetal liver cannot break it down well. It can cause miscarriage, low birth weight and **fetal alcohol spectrum disorders** (FASD): learning difficulties, behavioural problems, distinctive facial features, poor growth. UK advice: no alcohol in pregnancy.

**Smoking.** Carbon monoxide binds to haemoglobin, reducing oxygen supply to the fetus; nicotine narrows blood vessels, reducing blood flow through the placenta. Effects: low birth weight, premature birth, miscarriage, stillbirth, higher risk of sudden infant death. Stopping smoking at any stage helps.

**Monitoring fetal growth.** Ultrasound measurements (head circumference, abdominal circumference, femur length) compared with growth charts; measuring the height of the uterus (fundal height) at appointments. Poor growth may show placenta problems.

**Screening and diagnostic tests.** *Screening* tests give a risk; *diagnostic* tests give a definite answer.

- **Combined test** (screening, 10 to 14 weeks): ultrasound measurement of fluid at the back of the fetal neck (nuchal translucency) plus blood tests for hormones (hCG and a placental protein), combined with the mother's age to give a risk of Down's syndrome and some other trisomies.
- **Non-invasive prenatal testing (NIPT):** analyses fragments of fetal DNA in the mother's blood; more accurate screening, no risk to the fetus.
- If the risk is high, a diagnostic test is offered:

| | Amniocentesis | Chorionic villus sampling (CVS) |
| --- | --- | --- |
| When | 15 to 20 weeks | 11 to 14 weeks |
| Method | Needle through the abdomen, guided by ultrasound, into the amniotic sac; a sample of amniotic fluid containing fetal cells is withdrawn | Small sample of placental tissue (chorionic villi) taken through the abdomen or the cervix |
| Advantages | Slightly lower miscarriage risk (about 0.5%) | Earlier, so decisions can be made sooner |
| Disadvantages | Later in pregnancy; results take up to 2 to 3 weeks if cells are cultured | Slightly higher miscarriage risk (about 1%); placental cells occasionally differ from fetal cells |

Both give cells for karyotyping or DNA analysis (CF, sickle cell, and others). Both carry a risk of miscarriage and infection.

**Pre-implantation genetic diagnosis (PGD)** (Edexcel A 2.15). During IVF, one cell is removed from an early embryo and tested; only embryos without the disorder are implanted. Avoids later termination, but requires IVF, is expensive, and has ethical questions about selecting embryos.

**Carrier testing.** Testing relatives of affected people (or whole groups with a high frequency of an allele) to see if they are heterozygous carriers, so couples know the chance of an affected child.

### Genetic screening ethics and counselling

*Refs: Edexcel A 2.15, 2.16 · OCR B 5.1.1(g). Level: AS & A (Edexcel A); A-level only (OCR B).*

#### What you need to know

- The social and ethical issues of genetic screening, from different viewpoints.
- The role of the genetic counsellor and the ethical considerations of genetic testing.

#### Explained

**Ethical frameworks.** Decisions can be judged by:

- *Rights and duties:* people have a right to know (or not to know) their genetic information; there may be a duty to inform relatives.
- *Utilitarianism:* the right choice gives the greatest good for the greatest number.
- *Autonomy:* respecting people's informed choices.
- *Virtue ethics:* what a person of good character would do.

**Issues.**

- Results may cause anxiety; false positives may lead to unnecessary decisions; false negatives give false reassurance.
- Prenatal diagnosis may lead to a decision about whether to continue a pregnancy; some people and religious views object to terminations, especially for conditions that can be managed; others value the information to prepare.
- Who should be tested, and who decides; consent for children.
- Confidentiality: could insurers or employers use results? Should relatives be told?
- Concerns about "designer babies" and attitudes to disability.
- Cost and access across the population.

**The genetic counsellor** (OCR B). A trained professional who: explains how a condition is inherited and the chance of passing it on; explains what tests are available, how accurate they are and their risks; helps people understand results; discusses options (prenatal testing, PGD, adoption, using donor gametes, or accepting the risk) without directing a choice (non-directive counselling); offers emotional support and links to support groups; considers implications for other family members.

#### Worked example

Two carriers of cystic fibrosis (Ff × Ff) plan a family. What is the probability that their first child has CF? That a child with no symptoms is a carrier?

1. Offspring: FF : Ff : ff = 1 : 2 : 1. P(CF) = 1/4.
2. Among unaffected children (FF, Ff, Ff), 2 of 3 are carriers: probability 2/3.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying CF is caused by too much chloride in mucus.
- Saying a karyotype can detect CF (it cannot; CF is a gene mutation).
- Saying screening tests give a diagnosis.
- Saying Down's syndrome is inherited from a parent with Down's syndrome (it usually arises by non-disjunction during gamete formation).

### Check questions

1. Explain why the mucus in the lungs of a person with cystic fibrosis is thick and sticky. (4 marks)
2. Explain how cystic fibrosis affects digestion. (3 marks)
3. Describe how non-disjunction can lead to Down's syndrome. (3 marks)
4. Describe how a karyotype is prepared. (4 marks)
5. Explain why women are advised to take folic acid and avoid alcohol in pregnancy. (3 marks)
6. Compare amniocentesis and CVS. (4 marks)
7. Give two arguments for and two against offering genetic screening for a condition that has no treatment. (4 marks)
8. Describe the role of a genetic counsellor. (3 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Faulty CFTR protein / chloride channel (1); chloride ions not secreted onto surface of epithelium (1); sodium absorbed into cells, so water potential of surface fluid not lowered (1); water moves into cells by osmosis / less water in mucus (1).
2. Thick mucus blocks pancreatic duct (1); fewer enzymes reach duodenum / food not fully digested (1); mucus on gut lining reduces absorption (1).
3. Chromosome 21 pair / chromatids fail to separate in meiosis (1); gamete with two copies of chromosome 21 (1); fertilised by normal gamete, so zygote has three copies / 47 chromosomes (1).
4. Dividing cells obtained / cultured (1); colchicine stops cells at metaphase (1); hypotonic solution spreads chromosomes, then stained and photographed (1); arranged in pairs by size / banding / centromere (1).
5. Folic acid reduces risk of neural tube defects / spina bifida (1); alcohol crosses placenta (1); causes FASD / low birth weight / miscarriage (1).
6. Any four: amnio later (15 to 20 weeks) vs CVS earlier (11 to 14 weeks); amnio samples amniotic fluid vs CVS samples placenta; CVS slightly higher miscarriage risk; CVS results earlier; both diagnostic / provide fetal cells for karyotype or DNA testing.
7. For (2): allows preparation / informed decisions about having children / reproductive choices / research. Against (2): anxiety with no treatment; possible discrimination; false results; pressure on termination decisions.
8. Any three: explains inheritance and risk; explains tests, accuracy and risks; helps interpret results; discusses options non-directively; supports emotionally / implications for family.

<!-- source: notes/a-level/biology/04-cell-structure-and-microscopy.md -->

## Cell structure, viruses and methods of studying cells

Cells are the basic units of life. This file covers the organelles of eukaryotic cells, prokaryotic cells and viruses, microscopes and magnification, and cell fractionation.

### Overview

AS content. Gram staining and culturing bacteria are in file 28; how viruses cause disease is in file 27; cell membranes in file 06.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.2.1.1 to 3.2.1.3. Edexcel A 3.1 to 3.5, 3.13, 4.7, 4.8, 6.5. Edexcel B 2.1 i to iii, v to vii, 2.2 i, CP2. OCR A 2.1.1(a) to (k), 2.1.6(h), (i). OCR B 2.1.1(a) to (d), (g) to (i), (o). Eduqas CC2(a) to (d), CC5(o).
- OCR A asks for the cytoskeleton (2.1.1(j)) and preparing and drawing slides (2.1.1(b) to (d)). Edexcel B CP2 and OCR PAG1 use stage and eyepiece micrometers.
- Edexcel A names extra plant organelles: amyloplasts, plasmodesmata, pits, middle lamella, tonoplast (4.7). These are included below.
- OCR B 2.1.1(a), (b), (e), (f) use blood smears, differential staining, haemocytometers and flow cytometry: file 49.
- Edexcel B 2.2 i classifies viruses by nucleic acid; this is included here, with the lytic cycle in file 27.

### 3.2.1.1 Structure of eukaryotic cells

*Refs: AQA 3.2.1.1 · Edexcel A 3.2, 3.3, 3.5, 3.13, 4.7, 4.8 · Edexcel B 2.1 i, ii, v · OCR A 2.1.1(g) to (j), 2.1.6(h), (i) · OCR B 2.1.1(g), (o) · Eduqas CC2(a), (c), (d), CC5(o). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

The structure of eukaryotic cells, limited to the standard cells of plants, animals, fungi and protoctists, and the function of:

- cell-surface membrane
- nucleus (with chromosomes, nuclear envelope with pores, and nucleolus)
- mitochondria
- chloroplasts (in plants and algae)
- Golgi apparatus and Golgi vesicles
- lysosomes (a type of Golgi vesicle that releases lysozymes)
- ribosomes
- rough and smooth endoplasmic reticulum
- cell wall (cellulose in plants and algae; chitin in fungi)
- cell vacuole (in plants).

Also: in multicellular organisms, cells are specialised and organised into tissues, organs and organ systems. Recognise organelles in electron micrographs.

#### Explained

| Organelle | Structure | Function |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Nucleus | Double membrane (nuclear envelope) with pores; chromatin (DNA with histones); one or more nucleoli | Holds the genetic information; controls the cell; nucleolus makes ribosomal RNA and assembles ribosomes; pores let mRNA out |
| Mitochondrion | Double membrane; inner membrane folded into cristae; matrix with enzymes, ribosomes and DNA | Aerobic respiration; makes ATP |
| Chloroplast | Double membrane; thylakoids stacked into grana; fluid stroma with enzymes, starch grains, ribosomes and DNA | Photosynthesis |
| Rough ER | Flattened membrane sacs (cisternae) covered with ribosomes | Makes and transports proteins |
| Smooth ER | Membrane sacs without ribosomes | Makes and processes lipids, including steroids |
| Golgi apparatus | Stack of flattened membrane sacs, with vesicles | Modifies proteins (adds carbohydrate to make glycoproteins), packages them into vesicles; makes lysosomes |
| Golgi vesicles | Small membrane sacs | Carry proteins to the cell-surface membrane for secretion |
| Lysosome | Vesicle containing lysozymes (hydrolytic enzymes) | Hydrolyse pathogens taken in by phagocytes, worn-out organelles, and dead cells |
| Ribosome | Two subunits of RNA and protein, no membrane; 80S in eukaryotic cytoplasm, 70S in prokaryotes, mitochondria and chloroplasts | Site of protein synthesis |
| Cell wall | Cellulose microfibrils (plants, algae); chitin (fungi) | Strength; stops the cell bursting when turgid |
| Vacuole | Large, surrounded by the tonoplast, filled with cell sap | Keeps cell turgid; stores pigments and some solutes |
| Centrioles (OCR A, Edexcel) | Two bundles of microtubules at right angles; not in most plant cells | Organise the spindle in cell division |
| Cytoskeleton (OCR A) | Network of microfilaments, microtubules and intermediate filaments | Support; moves organelles and vesicles; cell movement; forms cilia, flagella and the spindle |
| Cilia and flagella (OCR A) | Microtubules in a 9 + 2 arrangement | Move fluid past cells (cilia) or move the cell (flagella) |

Extra plant structures (Edexcel A):

- Amyloplasts store starch (abundant in potato tubers).
- Plasmodesmata are channels through cell walls that join the cytoplasm of neighbouring cells.
- Pits are thin regions of the cell wall where water can pass between cells.
- The middle lamella, made mostly of calcium pectate, sticks neighbouring plant cells together.

**Making and secreting a protein** (AQA, OCR A, Edexcel A). The gene is transcribed in the nucleus; mRNA leaves through a nuclear pore; ribosomes on the rough ER translate it; the polypeptide enters the ER and folds; it is carried in a vesicle to the Golgi apparatus; the Golgi modifies it (for example adds carbohydrate) and packages it; a vesicle carries it to the cell-surface membrane; the vesicle fuses with the membrane and releases the protein by exocytosis. ATP from mitochondria powers the transport. (Eduqas CC5(o): some polypeptides are joined or modified after translation, as in haemoglobin or insulin.)

**Specialised cells** (all boards). The organelles a cell has match its job. Cells in a pancreas that secrete enzymes have lots of rough ER and Golgi; sperm and muscle cells have many mitochondria; root hair cells have no chloroplasts. Examples OCR A names: erythrocytes (no nucleus, biconcave, full of haemoglobin), neutrophils (lobed nucleus, many lysosomes), squamous and ciliated epithelia, palisade cells, guard cells, root hair cells.

**Levels of organisation.** Cells → tissues (similar cells working together, such as squamous epithelium, xylem) → organs (several tissues for one or more functions, such as stomach, leaf) → organ systems (organs working together, such as the digestive system).

#### Common mistakes

- Saying mitochondria "make energy". They release energy from respiratory substrates and make ATP.
- Saying ribosomes are organelles with a membrane.
- Saying plant cells do not have mitochondria.
- Mixing up lysosomes (vesicles) and lysozymes (the enzymes inside).
- Describing protein secretion with the Golgi before the rough ER.

### 3.2.1.2 Structure of prokaryotic cells and of viruses

*Refs: AQA 3.2.1.2 · Edexcel A 3.1, 3.4, 6.5 · Edexcel B 2.1 iii, 2.2 i · OCR A 2.1.1(k) · OCR B 2.1.1(h) · Eduqas CC2(b), (c). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Prokaryotic cells are much smaller than eukaryotic cells. They have cytoplasm without membrane-bound organelles; smaller ribosomes (70S); no nucleus, with a single circular DNA molecule not associated with proteins; and a cell wall containing murein (a glycoprotein, also called peptidoglycan).
- Many have one or more plasmids, a capsule around the cell and one or more flagella.
- Viruses are acellular and non-living. Viral particles have genetic material, a capsid and attachment proteins. (The material asked for at AQA AS is limited to these.)

#### Explained

| Feature | Prokaryote | Eukaryote |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Size | About 0.5 to 5 µm | About 10 to 100 µm |
| Nucleus | None; DNA free in cytoplasm | Present |
| DNA | Circular, not bound to histones; plasmids | Linear, in chromosomes bound to histones |
| Ribosomes | 70S | 80S in cytoplasm |
| Organelles with membranes | None | Many |
| Cell wall | Murein | Cellulose (plants), chitin (fungi), none (animals) |

Plasmids often carry genes for antibiotic resistance and can be passed between bacteria. The capsule (slime layer) protects against drying and helps bacteria avoid phagocytes.

**Viruses.** A virus particle has a nucleic acid core (DNA or RNA, never both) inside a protein coat called a capsid. Some have a lipid envelope taken from the host's membrane. Attachment proteins on the surface bind to receptors on host cells, which is why each virus infects only certain cell types. Viruses cannot reproduce outside a host cell. Edexcel B classifies viruses by nucleic acid and structure: DNA viruses (such as λ phage, a bacteriophage), RNA viruses (such as tobacco mosaic virus, Ebola) and retroviruses (such as HIV, which carry reverse transcriptase).

#### Common mistakes

- Saying bacteria have "no DNA" or "no ribosomes".
- Saying viruses are prokaryotes. They are not cells at all.
- Saying the bacterial cell wall is made of cellulose.

### 3.2.1.3 Methods of studying cells

*Refs: AQA 3.2.1.3 · Edexcel B 2.1 vi, vii, CP2 · OCR A 2.1.1(a) to (f) · OCR B 2.1.1(c), (d), (i) · Eduqas CC2(a). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- The principles and limitations of optical microscopes, transmission electron microscopes (TEM) and scanning electron microscopes (SEM).
- Measuring the size of an object with an optical microscope using an eyepiece graticule calibrated with a stage micrometer.
- The difference between magnification and resolution.
- magnification = size of image ÷ size of real object.
- The principles of cell fractionation and ultracentrifugation to separate organelles.
- Appreciate that there was a considerable period when artefacts in electron micrographs were mistaken for cell organelles.

#### Explained

**Magnification** is how many times larger the image is than the object. **Resolution** is the minimum distance apart two objects can be and still be seen as separate. Magnifying beyond the resolution limit just makes a bigger blur.

| | Light (optical) | TEM | SEM |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Beam | Light | Electrons pass through | Electrons bounce off the surface |
| Resolution | About 200 nm | About 0.1 nm in practice | About 10 nm (lower than TEM) |
| Image | 2D, colour | 2D, black and white, internal detail | 3D surface, black and white |
| Specimen | Living or dead | Dead; vacuum; very thin sections; stained with heavy metals | Dead; vacuum; coated with metal |
| Limits | Cannot see small organelles | Complex preparation may create artefacts; cannot view living cells | Lower resolution than TEM; no internal detail |

Electrons have a much shorter wavelength than light, which is why electron microscopes resolve finer detail.

**Artefacts** are things that appear in a preparation but are not in the living cell, caused by fixing, staining or cutting. Scientists checked whether a structure was real by preparing specimens in different ways and seeing whether it always appeared.

**Graticule calibration.**

1. Line up the eyepiece graticule (a scale with arbitrary units) with the stage micrometer (a slide with a scale of known size, often 1 division = 10 µm).
2. Count how many graticule divisions match a known length on the micrometer.
3. One graticule division = that length ÷ number of divisions.
4. Recalibrate at each objective lens, because the graticule stays the same size but the specimen is magnified more.

**Slides and staining** (OCR A, Edexcel B, OCR B). Dry mounts (pollen, hair), wet mounts (onion epidermis in water, with a coverslip lowered at an angle to avoid bubbles), squash preparations (root tip) and sections. Stains increase contrast because most cell structures are transparent: iodine stains starch, methylene blue stains DNA, eosin stains cytoplasm. Differential staining shows different structures in different colours (as in Gram staining). Biological drawings: sharp pencil, clear continuous lines, no shading, label lines ruled and not crossing, magnification or scale stated.

**Cell fractionation.**

1. Homogenise the tissue (blender) to break cells open.
2. Keep the solution ice-cold (reduces enzyme activity that would break down organelles), isotonic (same water potential, so organelles do not gain or lose water and burst or shrivel) and buffered (keeps pH constant so proteins are not denatured).
3. Filter to remove large debris.
4. Ultracentrifugation: spin at a low speed; the heaviest organelles (nuclei) form a pellet. Remove the supernatant and spin it faster; the next pellet is mitochondria (and chloroplasts in plants); faster again gives ER, Golgi and lysosomes; fastest gives ribosomes.

#### Worked example

1. An image of a mitochondrion is 24 mm long. The magnification is ×8000. How long is the real mitochondrion in µm?

Real size = image ÷ magnification = 24 mm ÷ 8000 = 0.003 mm = 3 µm.

2. At ×400, 25 eyepiece graticule divisions line up with 0.1 mm on the stage micrometer. A cell is 18 graticule divisions long. How long is it?

One division = 0.1 mm ÷ 25 = 0.004 mm = 4 µm. Cell = 18 × 4 = 72 µm.

#### Common mistakes

- Defining resolution as "how clear the image is".
- Forgetting to convert units: 1 mm = 1000 µm; 1 µm = 1000 nm.
- Saying the solution must be cold "to stop the organelles moving".
- Saying the TEM gives a 3D image.

### Check questions

1. Describe how a protein made on the rough ER is secreted from the cell. (4 marks)
2. Give three structures found in a bacterial cell but not in an animal cell. (3 marks)
3. Explain why a TEM gives higher resolution than an optical microscope. (1 mark)
4. Give two limitations of a TEM. (2 marks)
5. Explain why tissue is homogenised in a cold, isotonic, buffered solution before cell fractionation. (3 marks)
6. A ribosome has a diameter of 25 nm. In a micrograph it measures 2 mm. Calculate the magnification. (2 marks)
7. Explain why cells in the pancreas that secrete enzymes contain many mitochondria and a large Golgi apparatus. (2 marks)
8. Describe the structure of a virus particle. (3 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Polypeptide enters rough ER / folds (1); transported in vesicle to Golgi (1); Golgi modifies (e.g. adds carbohydrate) and packages into vesicles (1); vesicle fuses with cell-surface membrane; exocytosis (1).
2. Any three: cell wall (of murein); plasmid; capsule; flagellum; circular DNA free in cytoplasm; 70S ribosomes (accept "smaller ribosomes").
3. Electrons have a shorter wavelength than light (1).
4. Any two: cannot view living cells / vacuum; very thin section needed; artefacts from preparation; black and white image; expensive / large.
5. Cold: reduces enzyme activity that would digest organelles (1); isotonic: no osmosis, so organelles do not burst or shrivel (1); buffer: keeps pH constant so enzymes / proteins not denatured (1).
6. 2 mm = 2 000 000 nm (1); 2 000 000 ÷ 25 = ×80 000 (1).
7. Mitochondria release ATP for vesicle transport / exocytosis / protein synthesis (1); Golgi modifies and packages many enzymes into vesicles (1).
8. Nucleic acid (DNA or RNA) (1); capsid / protein coat (1); attachment proteins (1). Accept: lipid envelope in some.

<!-- source: notes/a-level/biology/36-human-reproduction-and-fertility.md -->

## Human reproduction, fertility and ageing of the reproductive system

This file covers the human reproductive systems, how sperm and eggs are made and how they are adapted, hormonal control of gametogenesis and the menstrual cycle, fertilisation, early development and the placenta, infertility and assisted reproduction, and the effects of ageing.

### Overview

Meiosis is in file 13. Pregnancy tests with monoclonal antibodies are in files 07 and 30. Plant reproduction is in file 37. Antenatal care and screening are in file 39. AQA does not have this content.

#### Board differences

- Edexcel A 3.6, 3.7 (mammals). Edexcel B 2.4 i to iii. OCR B 4.2.1(a) to (j), 4.2.2(a) to (c). Eduqas C2.3(a) to (d).
- Edexcel A asks for the specialisation of mammalian gametes and fertilisation (acrosome and cortical reactions). Edexcel B adds oogenesis, spermatogenesis and development to the blastocyst.
- OCR B asks for the urinogenital systems, gonad histology, hormonal control, pregnancy testing, the biological and ethical issues of infertility, assisted reproduction and the effects of ageing.
- Eduqas asks for the reproductive systems, gametogenesis, the menstrual cycle and the role of the placenta.

### The reproductive systems and gametogenesis

*Refs: OCR B 4.2.1(a) to (e) · Edexcel A 3.6 · Edexcel B 2.4 i · Eduqas C2.3(a), (b). Level: AS & A (Edexcel); A-level only (OCR B, Eduqas).*

#### What you need to know

- The structure and functions of the male and female reproductive systems.
- The histology of the testis and ovary.
- Spermatogenesis and oogenesis.
- The structure of the sperm and the secondary oocyte and how they are adapted.
- The role of hormones in gametogenesis.

#### Explained

**Male system.** Testes (in the scrotum, kept about 2 to 3 °C below body temperature, which suits sperm production) make sperm and testosterone. Epididymis (sperm mature and are stored); sperm duct (vas deferens); seminal vesicles and prostate gland (add fluid with fructose for respiration and alkaline substances); urethra (shared with the urinary system, hence "urinogenital").

**Female system.** Ovaries (make eggs, oestrogen and progesterone); oviducts (Fallopian tubes: fertilisation happens here; cilia move the egg); uterus (thick muscular wall; lining, the endometrium, receives the embryo); cervix; vagina.

**Testis histology.** The testis is packed with **seminiferous tubules**. Around the outside of each tubule are spermatogonia; cells divide and move inwards as they develop, so the most mature sperm are near the lumen. **Sertoli cells** in the tubule wall nourish developing sperm. Between the tubules are **interstitial (Leydig) cells**, which make testosterone.

**Ovary histology.** Follicles at different stages: primary follicles (an oocyte with one layer of cells) near the edge; developing follicles with a fluid-filled space; a mature (Graafian) follicle that bulges at the surface; after ovulation, the empty follicle becomes the **corpus luteum**.

**Spermatogenesis** (in seminiferous tubules, continuous from puberty).

1. Spermatogonia (diploid) divide by mitosis to make more cells.
2. Some grow into primary spermatocytes (diploid).
3. Meiosis I gives two secondary spermatocytes (haploid).
4. Meiosis II gives four spermatids (haploid).
5. Spermatids differentiate into spermatozoa (developing a tail, mitochondria, acrosome).

**Oogenesis.**

1. Before birth, oogonia divide by mitosis and grow into primary oocytes, which begin meiosis I and stop in prophase I. A girl is born with all her primary oocytes.
2. From puberty, each month a few follicles develop; one primary oocyte completes meiosis I, producing a large secondary oocyte and a small polar body (most cytoplasm goes to the oocyte).
3. The secondary oocyte begins meiosis II and stops at metaphase II. It is released at ovulation.
4. Meiosis II completes only if a sperm enters, producing the ovum and a second polar body.

| | Spermatogenesis | Oogenesis |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Number from one cell | Four sperm | One egg (plus polar bodies) |
| Timing | Continuous from puberty | Starts before birth; pauses; completes one at a time from puberty to menopause |
| Size | Small, little cytoplasm | Large, much cytoplasm |

**Gamete adaptations** (Edexcel A 3.6).

- *Sperm:* head with a haploid nucleus and an acrosome (a modified lysosome with enzymes to digest the egg's coverings); midpiece packed with mitochondria (ATP for the tail); tail (flagellum) for swimming.
- *Secondary oocyte:* large, with much cytoplasm containing nutrients for the early embryo; haploid nucleus; surrounded by the **zona pellucida** (a glycoprotein layer) and outer follicle cells (corona radiata); cortical granules under its membrane.

**Hormones in gametogenesis.** The hypothalamus releases GnRH, which makes the anterior pituitary release **FSH** and **LH**. In males, FSH stimulates Sertoli cells to support spermatogenesis; LH stimulates interstitial cells to make testosterone, which is needed for spermatogenesis and male secondary sexual characteristics. Testosterone inhibits GnRH and LH (negative feedback).

### The menstrual cycle, fertilisation and the placenta

*Refs: OCR B 4.2.1(f), (g) · Edexcel A 3.7 · Edexcel B 2.4 ii, iii · Eduqas C2.3(c), (d). Level: as above.*

#### What you need to know

- The hormonal control of the menstrual cycle.
- The events of fertilisation: from first contact to the fusion of nuclei, including the acrosome reaction and the cortical reaction.
- Early development to the blastocyst; implantation.
- The role of the placenta, including hormones.

#### Explained

**The menstrual cycle** (about 28 days).

| Days | Ovary | Uterus | Hormones |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 to 5 | Follicles start to develop | Lining shed (menstruation) | Low progesterone; FSH rising |
| 6 to 13 | A follicle matures (follicular phase) | Lining rebuilds and thickens | FSH stimulates follicle growth; follicle makes oestrogen; oestrogen repairs lining and at first inhibits FSH and LH |
| About 14 | Ovulation | | High oestrogen now stimulates (positive feedback) a surge of LH (and FSH); the LH surge causes ovulation |
| 15 to 28 | Corpus luteum forms (luteal phase) | Lining maintained, glands and blood vessels develop | LH stimulates the corpus luteum to make progesterone (and oestrogen); progesterone inhibits FSH and LH |
| End | If no pregnancy, corpus luteum breaks down | Lining is shed | Progesterone falls; FSH and LH rise again; new cycle |

**Fertilisation.**

1. Sperm swim through the cervix and uterus to the oviduct. **Capacitation:** in the female tract, changes to the sperm's membrane make it able to fertilise.
2. The sperm passes between the follicle cells and binds to receptors on the zona pellucida.
3. **Acrosome reaction:** the acrosome releases enzymes (proteases) that digest a path through the zona pellucida.
4. The sperm's membrane fuses with the oocyte's membrane; the sperm nucleus enters.
5. **Cortical reaction:** cortical granules release their contents by exocytosis into the zona pellucida, which hardens it and changes its receptors, so no more sperm can enter (preventing polyspermy).
6. The secondary oocyte completes meiosis II.
7. The sperm and egg nuclei fuse, forming a diploid zygote.

**Early development** (Edexcel B 2.4 iii). The zygote divides by mitosis as it moves down the oviduct (cleavage): 2, 4, 8 cells, then a solid ball (morula). After about 5 days it is a **blastocyst**: a hollow ball with an outer layer (trophoblast, which forms the placenta) and an inner cell mass (pluripotent cells that form the embryo). The blastocyst implants in the endometrium.

**The placenta** (Eduqas C2.3(d)). Formed from embryonic (chorion) and maternal tissue; villi give a large surface area; fetal blood in capillaries in the villi is very close to maternal blood in spaces around them, but the two do not mix.

- Exchange: oxygen, glucose, amino acids, antibodies (passive immunity) pass to the fetus; CO₂ and urea pass to the mother. Fetal haemoglobin's higher affinity helps (file 10).
- Barrier: blocks many bacteria and large molecules, though some viruses, drugs and alcohol cross.
- Hormones: the embryo first makes **hCG**, which keeps the corpus luteum making progesterone, so the lining is not shed. Later the placenta itself makes progesterone and oestrogen (maintaining the endometrium and inhibiting FSH and LH, so no more ovulation) and human placental lactogen.
- Oxytocin (from the posterior pituitary) causes contractions at birth; prolactin causes milk production.

### Infertility, assisted reproduction and ageing

*Refs: OCR B 4.2.1(h) to (j), 4.2.2(a) to (c). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- The biological and ethical issues surrounding infertility.
- Assisted reproduction, including IVF.
- The effects of ageing on the female and male reproductive systems and how the effects are managed.

#### Explained

**Causes of infertility.** In women: no ovulation (hormone imbalance), blocked oviducts (after infection), endometriosis. In men: low sperm count or poor motility. Age reduces fertility.

**Assisted reproduction.**

- *Fertility drugs:* FSH and LH (or drugs that increase them, such as clomifene) stimulate follicle development and ovulation.
- *In vitro fertilisation (IVF):* hormones stimulate many follicles; eggs are collected with a needle; eggs and sperm are mixed in a dish (or a single sperm is injected into an egg, ICSI, when sperm are few or weak); embryos grow for 2 to 5 days; one or two are placed in the uterus. Success rates fall with the woman's age. Spare embryos can be frozen. Pre-implantation genetic diagnosis can test embryos for serious inherited disorders.
- Issues: cost and who should receive NHS-funded treatment; multiple pregnancies (higher risk); what happens to unused embryos; the use of donated eggs or sperm and the child's right to know their genetic parent; age limits; the emotional effect of failed cycles; HFEA regulation in the UK. Religious and personal views differ on which treatments are acceptable.

**Ageing: women.** The **menopause** (usually between 45 and 55): the supply of follicles runs out; the ovaries stop responding; oestrogen and progesterone fall; menstruation and ovulation stop. FSH and LH rise (less negative feedback). Effects: hot flushes, mood changes, thinning of the vaginal lining; loss of bone density (osteoporosis risk, file 47); higher risk of heart disease. **Hormone replacement therapy (HRT)** gives oestrogen (with progesterone if the woman has a uterus) to relieve symptoms and protect bone; risks for some women include blood clots and a small increase in some cancers, so benefits and risks are weighed individually.

**Ageing: men.** Testosterone falls gradually; sperm production continues but declines; the **prostate gland enlarges** (benign prostatic hyperplasia), pressing on the urethra and causing difficulty urinating; prostate cancer becomes more common (detected with PSA blood tests and examination). Erectile problems are more common; treatment with drugs that increase blood flow.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying ovulation releases a mature ovum (it releases a secondary oocyte).
- Saying LH makes the follicle grow (FSH does; LH triggers ovulation).
- Saying the placenta lets maternal and fetal blood mix.
- Saying the cortical reaction digests the zona pellucida (the acrosome reaction does).

### Check questions

1. Give three differences between spermatogenesis and oogenesis. (3 marks)
2. Describe how a sperm is adapted for its function. (3 marks)
3. Explain the roles of FSH and LH in the menstrual cycle. (4 marks)
4. Describe the acrosome reaction and the cortical reaction, and explain the importance of each. (4 marks)
5. Explain why the endometrium is not shed at the end of the cycle if an embryo implants. (3 marks)
6. Give three biological causes of infertility and one ethical issue raised by IVF. (4 marks)
7. Describe the stages of IVF. (4 marks)
8. Explain why women are more at risk of osteoporosis after the menopause. (2 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Any three: four sperm vs one egg (plus polar bodies); continuous from puberty vs starts before birth and pauses; equal vs unequal division of cytoplasm; millions vs one per month.
2. Acrosome with enzymes to digest zona pellucida (1); many mitochondria to release energy / ATP for tail (1); flagellum / tail to swim; haploid nucleus (1).
3. FSH stimulates follicle development (1); follicle makes oestrogen (1); LH surge causes ovulation (1); LH stimulates corpus luteum to make progesterone (1).
4. Acrosome releases enzymes / proteases to digest zona pellucida (1), allowing sperm to reach oocyte membrane (1); cortical granules release contents, hardening zona pellucida (1), preventing polyspermy (1).
5. Embryo / trophoblast makes hCG (1); maintains corpus luteum (1); which keeps making progesterone, maintaining the lining (1).
6. Any three (1 each): no ovulation / hormone imbalance; blocked oviducts; endometriosis; low sperm count / poor motility; age. Ethical issue (1): unused embryos; multiple births; cost / access; donor anonymity.
7. Hormones to stimulate many follicles (1); eggs collected (1); fertilised with sperm in a dish (1); embryos grown and one or two transferred to uterus (1).
8. Oestrogen falls (1); oestrogen helps maintain bone density, so bone is lost faster (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/biology/37-plant-reproduction.md -->

## Sexual reproduction in flowering plants

Flowering plants reproduce sexually by pollination and fertilisation, forming seeds inside fruits. This file covers flower structure, wind and insect pollination, how pollen and embryo sacs form, pollen tube growth, double fertilisation, seeds and fruits, germination and the importance of cereals.

### Overview

Meiosis is in file 13. Control of flowering by phytochrome and gibberellin in germination are in file 31. AQA does not have this content.

#### Board differences

- Edexcel A 3.7 (plants). Edexcel B 2.5 i to iii, CP4. OCR B 4.4.1(a) to (e). Eduqas C2.4(a) to (g).
- Edexcel B asks for pollen and embryo sac formation, pollen tube growth and double fertilisation; CP4 investigates sucrose concentration and pollen tube growth.
- Eduqas asks for flower structure, pollen and ovule development (with slides of anthers), cross- and self-pollination, double fertilisation, seed and fruit structure in broad bean and maize, and germination of broad bean.
- OCR B asks for flowering control, pollination, fertilisation and seed formation, germination, and cereals as staple foods.

### Flowers and pollination

*Refs: OCR B 4.4.1(b) · Eduqas C2.4(a), (c). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- The structure of flowers; comparing wind-pollinated and insect-pollinated flowers.
- Cross-pollination and self-pollination.

#### Explained

**Flower parts.**

- **Sepals:** protect the bud.
- **Petals:** attract insects (in insect-pollinated flowers).
- **Stamens** (male): **anther** (makes pollen) on a **filament**.
- **Carpels** (female): **stigma** (receives pollen), **style**, **ovary** containing one or more **ovules**.
- **Nectaries** (in insect-pollinated flowers): make sugary nectar.

**Pollination** is the transfer of pollen from an anther to a stigma.

| Feature | Insect-pollinated (e.g. legumes, such as peas and beans) | Wind-pollinated (e.g. grasses and cereals) |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Petals | Large, brightly coloured, often with guide lines | Small, green or absent |
| Scent and nectar | Usually present | Absent |
| Anthers | Inside the flower, so insects brush against them | Hang outside the flower on long filaments |
| Stigmas | Inside, sticky | Large, feathery, hang outside to catch pollen |
| Pollen | Larger, sticky or spiky, smaller amounts | Small, light, smooth, very large amounts |

**Self-pollination:** pollen reaches the stigma of the same flower or another flower on the same plant. Reliable, but offspring have less genetic variation (inbreeding).

**Cross-pollination:** pollen from another plant of the same species. More genetic variation, so populations adapt better; but relies on wind or animals and wastes pollen. Mechanisms that favour cross-pollination: anthers and stigmas mature at different times; separate male and female flowers or plants; self-incompatibility (pollen tubes from the same plant cannot grow).

### Gamete formation, pollen tubes and double fertilisation

*Refs: Edexcel A 3.7 · Edexcel B 2.5 i to iii, CP4 · OCR B 4.4.1(c) · Eduqas C2.4(b), (d). Level: AS & A (Edexcel); A-level only (OCR B, Eduqas).*

#### What you need to know

- How a pollen grain forms in the anther and the embryo sac forms in the ovule.
- How the pollen tube grows and delivers male nuclei to the embryo sac.
- Double fertilisation to form a diploid zygote and a triploid endosperm.

#### Explained

**Pollen formation (in the anther).** The anther has four pollen sacs. Inside, diploid pollen mother cells divide by meiosis, each giving four haploid cells (a tetrad), which separate and develop into pollen grains. Each pollen grain's nucleus divides by mitosis into a **tube nucleus** and a **generative nucleus**. The grain has a tough outer wall (exine) that protects it. The tapetum (a nutritive layer lining the pollen sac) feeds developing pollen. When mature, the anther wall dries and splits (dehisces), releasing pollen.

**Embryo sac formation (in the ovule).** Inside the ovule, a diploid megaspore mother cell divides by meiosis to give four haploid cells; three degenerate. The surviving megaspore divides by mitosis three times, giving eight haploid nuclei in the **embryo sac**. These include the **egg cell** (female gamete), two **polar nuclei** in the centre, and other cells (synergids and antipodal cells). The ovule is covered by integuments, with a small gap called the micropyle.

**Pollen tube growth.**

1. A compatible pollen grain lands on the stigma and absorbs water and sugar.
2. A **pollen tube** grows out through a pore, down through the style towards the ovary, digesting a path with enzymes. The tube nucleus is at the tip and directs growth (it grows towards chemicals from the ovule).
3. The generative nucleus divides by mitosis to form **two male nuclei** (male gametes), which travel down the tube.
4. The tube enters the ovule through the micropyle and the tip bursts, releasing the male nuclei into the embryo sac.

**Double fertilisation.**

- One male nucleus fuses with the egg cell nucleus, forming a **diploid zygote**, which develops into the embryo.
- The other male nucleus fuses with the two polar nuclei, forming a **triploid (3n) endosperm nucleus**, which develops into the endosperm, a food store for the embryo.

This is unique to flowering plants.

#### Practical

**Pollen tube growth** (Edexcel B CP4).

1. Make a series of sucrose solutions (for example 0, 5, 10, 15, 20%) on cavity slides or in dishes.
2. Dust pollen (from a freshly opened flower) onto each.
3. Incubate at a constant temperature; after a set time (30 to 60 minutes), view under the microscope.
4. Count the percentage of grains that have germinated, or measure tube length with an eyepiece graticule.

Sucrose supplies a respiratory substrate and affects water potential: too dilute and the pollen may burst; too concentrated and water is drawn out. Control variables: same species, temperature, time, volume. Draw anther sections (Eduqas): a low power plan of a transverse section, showing pollen sacs, tapetum and pollen grains.

### Seeds, fruits and germination

*Refs: OCR B 4.4.1(c) to (e) · Eduqas C2.4(e) to (g). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- How seeds and fruits form; the structure of seeds and fruits of broad bean (a dicotyledon) and maize (a monocotyledon).
- The germination of broad bean.
- The importance of cereals as staple foods (OCR B).

#### Explained

**After fertilisation.** The zygote divides by mitosis to form the **embryo** (radicle, which becomes the root; plumule, which becomes the shoot; and one or two cotyledons). The endosperm grows. The integuments harden into the **testa** (seed coat). The ovule becomes the **seed**. The ovary wall develops into the **fruit** (pericarp), which protects seeds and helps disperse them. Water is removed from the seed so it becomes dormant.

| | Broad bean (*Vicia faba*, dicot) | Maize (*Zea mays*, monocot) |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Cotyledons | Two, large, store the food (endosperm absorbed during development) | One, thin (scutellum), absorbs food from the endosperm |
| Endosperm | Little or none in the mature seed | Large; main food store (starch) |
| Fruit | Pod (the ovary wall) containing several seeds | Each grain is a single-seeded fruit; fruit wall fused with seed coat |
| Other | Testa, micropyle, hilum (scar where seed was attached) | Aleurone layer (protein-rich, makes amylase) |

**Germination** needs water, oxygen and a suitable temperature (and sometimes light, file 31).

1. The seed absorbs water through the micropyle (imbibition); the seed swells and the testa splits.
2. Enzymes are activated (gibberellin switches on amylase genes in cereals, file 31): starch → maltose → glucose; proteins → amino acids; lipids → fatty acids and glycerol.
3. These are transported to the embryo, respired for energy, and used to build new cells.
4. The radicle grows out first and down; then the plumule grows up. In broad bean, the cotyledons stay underground (hypogeal germination) and the plumule is hooked to protect its tip as it pushes through soil.

**Cereals as staple foods** (OCR B 4.4.1(e)). Wheat, rice and maize provide most of the energy in the human diet worldwide. Their grains store much starch in the endosperm, plus protein and some oils; they store well when dry; plants are wind-pollinated, grow fast and give high yields per hectare. Selective breeding (file 22) has produced dwarf, high-yield varieties. Concerns: relying on a few varieties makes crops vulnerable to disease; climate change threatens yields (file 42).

#### Common mistakes

- Confusing pollination (transfer of pollen) with fertilisation (fusion of nuclei).
- Saying the pollen grain itself travels down the style.
- Saying the endosperm is diploid.
- Saying the fruit develops from the ovule (it develops from the ovary; the ovule becomes the seed).

### Check questions

1. Give four differences between wind-pollinated and insect-pollinated flowers. (4 marks)
2. Explain the advantage of cross-pollination. (2 marks)
3. Describe how pollen grains form in an anther. (3 marks)
4. Describe the growth of a pollen tube and the events of double fertilisation. (5 marks)
5. State the ploidy of: the zygote, the endosperm, a pollen grain nucleus. (3 marks)
6. In CP4, explain why pollen in distilled water may burst. (2 marks)
7. Describe how a broad bean seed germinates. (4 marks)
8. Give three reasons cereals are important staple foods. (3 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Any four: petals small vs large and coloured; no scent / nectar vs present; anthers outside vs inside; feathery vs sticky stigmas; light, smooth, abundant pollen vs sticky / spiky, less pollen.
2. Greater genetic variation (1); population more able to adapt / survive changes (1).
3. Pollen mother cells divide by meiosis (1); four haploid cells / tetrad (1); develop into pollen grains with tube and generative nuclei / tough wall (1).
4. Pollen tube grows from grain down style to ovary (1); tube nucleus at tip / grows towards ovule (1); generative nucleus divides by mitosis to form two male nuclei (1); one fuses with egg to form diploid zygote (1); other fuses with two polar nuclei to form triploid endosperm (1).
5. Zygote diploid (1); endosperm triploid (1); pollen nucleus haploid (1).
6. Water potential of water higher than inside pollen (1); water enters by osmosis and grain bursts (1).
7. Water absorbed through micropyle; testa splits (1); enzymes activated / hydrolyse stored food in cotyledons (1); products used in respiration and growth of embryo (1); radicle emerges first, then plumule (1).
8. Any three: high starch / energy; also protein; store well / dry; high yields; grow in many climates; fast growing.

<!-- source: notes/a-level/biology/13-mutation-meiosis-and-genetic-diversity.md -->

## Mutation, meiosis, genetic diversity and natural selection

Genetic diversity comes from mutation, meiosis and random fertilisation. Natural selection acts on that diversity. This file covers each, with AQA required practical 6 (aseptic technique and antimicrobial substances).

### Overview

AQA AS content. Gene mutations at A-level depth (frameshifts, mutagens, effects on proteins) are in file 24. Chromosome disorders in medicine (Down's syndrome, karyotypes) are in file 39. Evolution, Hardy–Weinberg and speciation are in file 22.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.4.3, 3.4.4, RP6. Edexcel A 3.9, 4.4. Edexcel B 2.3 iv, v, vii, 3.2 i, 8.1. OCR A 2.1.6(f), (g), 4.2.2(f) to (i), 6.1.2(a). OCR B 3.1.2(a) to (c), 3.1.3(e), (g). Eduqas C2.1(m), (n), C2.2(d), (e), C2.6(a), (b).
- OCR A asks for types of variation (continuous, discontinuous; genetic, environmental) with standard deviation and Student's t-test (4.2.2(f)); statistics are in file 26.
- OCR A and Eduqas ask for adaptations classed as anatomical, physiological and behavioural, and for convergent evolution (OCR A 4.2.2(g)).
- OCR A 4.2.2(i) asks for evolution of resistance in bacteria and pests; antibiotic resistance is also in file 27.
- AQA RP6 (aseptic technique) is grouped here because AQA places it under 3.4.4 (antibiotic resistance). Culturing for other boards is in file 28.

### 3.4.3 Genetic diversity can arise as a result of mutation or during meiosis

*Refs: AQA 3.4.3 · Edexcel A 3.9 · Edexcel B 2.3 iv, v, vii, 8.1 i, ii · OCR A 2.1.6(f), (g), 6.1.2(a) · OCR B 3.1.2(a) to (c) · Eduqas C2.2(d), (e). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Gene mutations involve a change in the base sequence of chromosomes. They can arise spontaneously during DNA replication and include base deletion and base substitution. Due to the degenerate nature of the genetic code, not all base substitutions cause a change in the sequence of encoded amino acids. Mutagenic agents can increase the rate of gene mutation.
- Mutations in the number of chromosomes can arise spontaneously by chromosome non-disjunction during meiosis.
- Meiosis produces daughter cells that are genetically different from each other. The process of meiosis only in sufficient detail to show how: two nuclear divisions result in the formation of haploid daughter cells from a single diploid parent cell; genetically different daughter cells result from the independent segregation of homologous chromosomes; crossing over between homologous chromosomes results in further genetic variation among daughter cells.
- Appreciate that random fertilisation of haploid gametes further increases genetic variation within a species.
- Complete diagrams showing the chromosome content of cells after the first and second meiotic division, when given the chromosome content of the parent cell. Explain the different outcomes of mitosis and meiosis. Recognise where meiosis occurs when given information about an unfamiliar life cycle. Explain how random fertilisation produces genetic variation. Calculate the number of possible combinations of chromosomes, using 2ⁿ.

#### Explained

**Gene mutations.**

- *Substitution:* one base replaced by another. Only one triplet changes. It may give the same amino acid (silent, because the code is degenerate), a different amino acid (missense), or a stop codon (nonsense, giving a short polypeptide).
- *Deletion:* one base lost. Every triplet after it changes: a frameshift. Usually the polypeptide is very different and does not work.

Mutagenic agents (mutagens) include ionising radiation (X-rays, gamma rays), ultraviolet light, and chemicals such as benzene and some in tobacco smoke.

**Homologous chromosomes** are a matching pair, one from each parent, with the same genes at the same loci but possibly different alleles. A diploid cell (2n) has two sets; a haploid cell (n) has one set. Human body cells: 2n = 46; gametes: n = 23.

**Meiosis.**

| Division | Main events |
| --- | --- |
| Meiosis I | Homologous chromosomes pair up (forming bivalents); crossing over happens; pairs line up on the equator; homologous chromosomes (each still two chromatids) are separated to opposite poles. Result: two haploid cells. |
| Meiosis II | Like mitosis: chromosomes line up singly; sister chromatids separate. Result: four haploid cells. |

Named stages for OCR A, OCR B, Edexcel B and Eduqas: prophase I, metaphase I, anaphase I, telophase I, then prophase II, metaphase II, anaphase II, telophase II.

**How meiosis makes variety.**

1. *Independent segregation (independent assortment):* at metaphase I, each homologous pair lines up independently of the others. Which parent's chromosome goes to which pole is random. With n pairs, the number of possible combinations in the gametes is 2ⁿ.
2. *Crossing over:* in prophase I, chromatids of homologous chromosomes wrap round each other; they break and swap equivalent sections at points called chiasmata. New combinations of alleles appear on each chromatid.
3. *Random fertilisation:* any male gamete can fuse with any female gamete. The number of combinations of chromosomes in a zygote from two parents is (2ⁿ)², even ignoring crossing over.

**Non-disjunction.** If homologous chromosomes (in meiosis I) or sister chromatids (in meiosis II) fail to separate, one gamete gets an extra chromosome and another gets one fewer. Fertilisation then produces a zygote with 2n + 1 (trisomy) or 2n − 1 (monosomy). Down's syndrome is trisomy 21 (detail in file 39).

**Mitosis compared with meiosis.**

| | Mitosis | Meiosis |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Divisions | One | Two |
| Cells made | Two | Four |
| Chromosome number | Same as parent (diploid stays diploid) | Halved (diploid to haploid) |
| Genetic make-up | Identical to each other and the parent | Different from each other and the parent |
| Homologous pairs | Do not pair | Pair up and cross over |
| Use | Growth, repair, asexual reproduction | Gamete formation for sexual reproduction |

**Unfamiliar life cycles.** Meiosis happens wherever the chromosome number halves (2n → n). In mammals it is in gamete formation; in fungi and many algae it happens straight after the zygote forms; in plants it forms spores.

#### Worked example

1. A species has 2n = 8. How many combinations of chromosomes are possible in its gametes, and in a zygote from two parents (ignoring crossing over)?

n = 4. Gametes: 2⁴ = 16. Zygotes: 16² = 256.

2. A cell with 2n = 4 contains 8 units of DNA at the start of meiosis (after replication). How many units are in each cell after meiosis I and after meiosis II?

After meiosis I: 4 units (two chromosomes, each two chromatids). After meiosis II: 2 units.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying crossing over happens between sister chromatids. It happens between non-sister chromatids of homologous chromosomes.
- Saying independent segregation happens in anaphase II.
- Saying a deletion changes "one amino acid".
- Saying meiosis gives "two cells" or "diploid gametes".

### 3.4.4 Genetic diversity and adaptation

*Refs: AQA 3.4.4, RP6 · Edexcel A 4.4 · Edexcel B 3.2 i · OCR A 4.2.2(f) to (i) · OCR B 3.1.3(e), (g) · Eduqas C2.1(m), (n), C2.6(a), (b). Level: AS & A (A-level only for Eduqas C2.6).*

#### What you need to know

- Genetic diversity as the number of different alleles of genes in a population.
- Genetic diversity is a factor enabling natural selection to occur.
- The principles of natural selection in the evolution of populations: random mutation can result in new alleles of a gene; many mutations are harmful but, in certain environments, the new allele of a gene might benefit its possessor, leading to increased reproductive success; the advantageous allele is inherited by members of the next generation; as a result, over many generations, the new allele increases in frequency in the population.
- Directional selection, exemplified by antibiotic resistance in bacteria, and stabilising selection, exemplified by human birth weights.
- Natural selection results in species that are better adapted to their environment. These adaptations may be anatomical, physiological or behavioural.
- Appreciate the importance of evidence in the theory of natural selection. Use unfamiliar information to explain how selection produces changes within a population.

#### Explained

**Genetic diversity** is the variety of alleles in a population. A population with more alleles is more likely to contain individuals that survive a change, such as a new disease. Small populations (after a bottleneck or founder event) lose diversity (file 22).

**Natural selection, step by step.**

1. Individuals vary because of different alleles, which arise by random mutation.
2. A selection pressure acts (a predator, a disease, a change in climate, an antibiotic).
3. Individuals with an advantageous allele are more likely to survive and reproduce.
4. They pass the allele to their offspring.
5. Over many generations, the frequency of the allele increases in the population.

Selection does not cause the mutation. The allele must already be there.

**Types of selection.**

- *Directional:* individuals at one extreme are favoured, so the mean shifts. Example: bacteria with an allele for resistance survive an antibiotic and reproduce; the population becomes resistant. Others: peppered moths; insecticide resistance; warfarin resistance in rats.
- *Stabilising:* individuals near the mean are favoured and extremes are selected against, so the mean stays the same but the range (standard deviation) narrows. Example: human birth weight; very small babies are more likely to die of cold or infection, and very large babies are more likely to cause problems at birth.
- (OCR A, Edexcel B) *Disruptive:* both extremes are favoured; can lead to two forms and eventually speciation (file 22).

**Types of adaptation.**

| Type | Meaning | Examples |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Anatomical | Body structure | Thick fur in arctic foxes; sunken stomata; long loop of Henle in desert mammals |
| Physiological | Processes inside the body | Hibernation (lower metabolic rate); producing venom; high-affinity haemoglobin at altitude |
| Behavioural | Actions | Courtship displays; migration; woodlice moving to damp places |

**Variation** (OCR A, Eduqas). Variation is differences between individuals. *Continuous variation* has a range of values with no categories (height, mass); it is usually controlled by many genes (polygenic) plus the environment, and gives a normal distribution. *Discontinuous variation* falls into distinct categories (blood group, sex); it is usually controlled by one or two genes with little environmental effect. Variation is genetic (inherited), environmental (diet, light, training), or both. Only genetic variation is heritable and can be acted on by natural selection.

**Convergent evolution** (OCR A). Unrelated species in similar environments evolve similar adaptations: the marsupial mole of Australia and the placental mole of Europe both have spade-like forelimbs and tiny eyes.

**Importance of evidence.** Darwin's theory was accepted because it explained many observations (fossils, selective breeding, distribution of species) and made predictions that later evidence confirmed, such as the genetic basis of inheritance and DNA similarities between related species.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying bacteria "become resistant" or "build up immunity" after exposure to antibiotics. A mutation gives resistance at random; the antibiotic selects those that already have it.
- Saying organisms "adapt" in their lifetime in response to need.
- Saying individuals evolve. Populations evolve.
- Forgetting the last step: "over many generations the allele frequency increases".
- Confusing genetic diversity with biodiversity.

#### Practical

**AQA required practical 6** (similar to Edexcel A CP9, OCR A PAG7 and OCR B 3.2.3(g); culturing detail for those boards is in file 28): use of aseptic techniques to investigate the effect of antimicrobial substances on microbial growth.

1. Spread a liquid culture of a safe bacterium (such as *Bacillus subtilis*) evenly over a nutrient agar plate with a sterile spreader.
2. Soak paper discs in different antibiotics, antiseptics or plant extracts (and one in sterile water as a control). Place them on the agar with sterile forceps.
3. Tape the lid on with two short pieces (not sealed all round, so aerobic conditions stay and anaerobic pathogens do not grow), label the base, and incubate upside down at no more than 25 °C (so human pathogens do not grow well) for 48 hours.
4. Measure the diameter of each clear zone (zone of inhibition); calculate area = πr².

Aseptic technique: wash hands and disinfect the bench; work near a lit Bunsen burner (rising air carries microbes away); flame the neck of the culture bottle; sterilise inoculating loops in the flame until red hot; lift the Petri dish lid only slightly; dispose of plates by autoclaving. These stop contamination of the culture and protect the user.

### Check questions

1. Explain why a base substitution may have no effect on a polypeptide. (2 marks)
2. Explain how meiosis produces genetic variation. (4 marks)
3. A species has a diploid number of 12. Calculate the number of chromosome combinations possible in gametes from one individual. (1 mark)
4. Give three differences between mitosis and meiosis. (3 marks)
5. Describe how non-disjunction can produce a zygote with 47 chromosomes. (3 marks)
6. Explain how a population of bacteria can become resistant to an antibiotic. (5 marks)
7. Describe stabilising selection, using human birth weight as an example. (3 marks)
8. In RP6, explain why plates are incubated at 25 °C and not 37 °C, and why the lid is not sealed all round. (2 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Genetic code is degenerate (1); new triplet may code for the same amino acid (1).
2. Independent segregation of homologous chromosomes (1); random alignment at metaphase I gives different combinations of maternal and paternal chromosomes (1); crossing over between non-sister chromatids of homologous chromosomes (1); exchanges sections, giving new combinations of alleles (1).
3. 2⁶ = 64 (1).
4. Any three: one vs two divisions; two vs four cells; diploid vs haploid; identical vs genetically different; no pairing / crossing over in mitosis.
5. Homologous chromosomes / chromatids fail to separate in meiosis (1); one gamete gets an extra chromosome / 24 chromosomes (1); fertilised by normal gamete (23), giving 47 (1).
6. Random mutation gives an allele for resistance (1); antibiotic kills non-resistant bacteria / selection pressure (1); resistant bacteria survive and reproduce (1); pass on the allele (1); frequency of resistance allele increases over generations (1).
7. Individuals with intermediate / mean values favoured (1); very low and very high birth weight babies less likely to survive (1); mean stays the same, range / standard deviation narrows (1).
8. 25 °C reduces the chance of growing human pathogens (1); taping not all round keeps aerobic conditions, so anaerobic pathogens do not grow (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/biology/05-cell-cycle-mitosis-and-cancer.md -->

## The cell cycle, mitosis, binary fission and cancer

All cells arise from other cells. This file covers the eukaryotic cell cycle, the stages of mitosis, how prokaryotes and viruses replicate, uncontrolled division in cancer, and AQA required practical 2.

### Overview

AS content. Meiosis is in file 13. The genetics of cancer (oncogenes, tumour suppressor genes, methylation) is in file 24; cancer detection and treatment for OCR B in file 44.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.2.2, RP2. Edexcel A 3.10, CP5. Edexcel B 2.3 i to iii, CP3. OCR A 2.1.6(a) to (e). OCR B 3.1.1(a), (b). Eduqas C2.2(a) to (c).
- OCR A asks for cell-cycle checkpoints and how the cycle is regulated (2.1.6(b)); included below.
- AQA alone names binary fission and viral replication in this section. Viral replication for Edexcel B and OCR B is in file 27.

### 3.2.2 All cells arise from other cells

*Refs: AQA 3.2.2, RP2 · Edexcel A 3.10, CP5 · Edexcel B 2.3 i to iii, CP3 · OCR A 2.1.6(a) to (e) · OCR B 3.1.1(a), (b) · Eduqas C2.2(a) to (c). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- In multicellular organisms not all cells keep the ability to divide.
- Eukaryotic cells that can divide go through a cell cycle: interphase (DNA replication) followed by nuclear division (mitosis) and then division of the cytoplasm (cytokinesis).
- Mitosis produces two genetically identical daughter cells. Its stages: prophase, metaphase, anaphase, telophase.
- Mitosis is controlled by genes. Uncontrolled division can lead to tumours and cancers; many cancer treatments target the cell cycle.
- Binary fission in prokaryotes: replication of the circular DNA and plasmids, then division of the cytoplasm to give two daughter cells, each with one copy of the circular DNA and a variable number of plasmids.
- Viruses do not undergo cell division. After injecting their nucleic acid, the host cell's machinery replicates the virus.
- Recognise the stages of the cell cycle and explain the appearance of cells in each stage of mitosis.
- Calculate the mitotic index.

#### Explained

**Interphase** is most of the cycle. It has three parts:

- G1: the cell grows, makes organelles and proteins.
- S: DNA replicates. Each chromosome is now two identical sister chromatids joined at a centromere.
- G2: the cell grows more, makes proteins for division, and checks the copied DNA.

Some cells leave the cycle and enter G0, where they stop dividing (most neurones, for example).

**Mitosis.**

| Stage | What happens |
| --- | --- |
| Prophase | Chromosomes condense and become visible; the nuclear envelope breaks down; centrioles (in animal cells) move to opposite poles and spindle fibres form |
| Metaphase | Chromosomes line up on the equator of the cell; spindle fibres attach to each centromere |
| Anaphase | Centromeres divide; spindle fibres shorten and pull sister chromatids to opposite poles, centromere first (V shapes) |
| Telophase | Chromatids (now chromosomes) reach the poles and uncoil; a nuclear envelope forms around each set |

Cytokinesis then splits the cytoplasm: a cleavage furrow in animal cells; a cell plate forming a new wall in plant cells.

**Why mitosis matters.** It gives genetically identical cells for growth, repair of damaged tissue, replacing worn-out cells, and asexual reproduction (in yeast, many plants, and some animals).

**Regulation and checkpoints** (OCR A). Proteins check the cell before it moves on. The G1 checkpoint checks cell size, nutrients, growth factors and DNA damage. The G2 checkpoint checks that DNA has replicated fully and without damage. The metaphase (spindle assembly) checkpoint checks every chromosome is attached to the spindle before anaphase. If damage cannot be repaired, the cell may enter G0 or undergo apoptosis.

**Cancer.** Cancer starts when mutations in the genes that control the cell cycle let a cell divide without control. Its descendants form a mass called a tumour. Benign tumours stay in one place; malignant tumours invade and spread (metastasis). Many treatments target dividing cells:

- some drugs stop DNA replication in S phase
- some stop spindle formation, so cells stall in metaphase
- radiation damages DNA so cells fail the checkpoints.

Normal cells that divide often (hair follicles, gut lining, bone marrow) are also affected, which is why chemotherapy has side effects.

**Binary fission.** The circular DNA replicates; the two copies move to opposite ends; plasmids replicate; the cytoplasm divides and a new wall forms between the two cells. There is no spindle and no mitosis.

**Viral replication.** A virus's attachment proteins bind to receptors on a host cell; the nucleic acid enters; the host's ribosomes, enzymes and nucleotides make new viral nucleic acid and proteins; new particles assemble and are released, often bursting the cell (lysis).

**Mitotic index** = number of cells with visible chromosomes (in mitosis) ÷ total number of cells counted. A high index means rapid division, as in tumours or root tips.

#### Worked example

1. In a field of view of a root tip, 36 cells are in mitosis and 164 are in interphase. Calculate the mitotic index.

Total = 36 + 164 = 200. Mitotic index = 36 ÷ 200 = 0.18 (18%).

2. A cell cycle lasts 20 hours. In a sample of 400 cells, 30 are in metaphase. Estimate how long metaphase lasts.

Fraction in metaphase = 30 ÷ 400 = 0.075. Time = 0.075 × 20 h = 1.5 h = 90 minutes. This assumes cells are spread evenly through the cycle, so the fraction in a stage is proportional to its duration.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying DNA replicates during prophase. It replicates in S phase of interphase.
- Calling interphase a "resting" stage. The cell is very active.
- Saying chromosomes, rather than sister chromatids, separate in anaphase.
- Saying "cancer is caused by mitosis". It is caused by mutations that make mitosis uncontrolled.
- Saying viruses divide by binary fission.

#### Practical

**AQA required practical 2** (also Edexcel A CP5, Edexcel B CP3, OCR A 2.1.6(d) and PAG1, OCR B 3.1.1(b)): preparation of stained squashes of cells from plant root tips; set-up and use of an optical microscope to identify the stages of mitosis; measurement of the mitotic index.

1. Cut the last 5 mm of a garlic or onion root tip (the meristem, where cells divide).
2. Put it in warm hydrochloric acid (about 1 mol dm⁻³ at 60 °C) for a few minutes. This breaks down the middle lamella so cells separate, and stops division.
3. Rinse in water, then place on a slide and add a stain that binds DNA, such as toluidine blue or acetic orcein.
4. Break up the tip with a mounted needle, add a coverslip, and press down firmly through filter paper (squash) to get a single layer of cells; do not twist the coverslip.
5. Find cells under low power, then switch to high power. Count cells in each stage and calculate the mitotic index.

Points examiners ask about: why the tip is used (only part where cells divide); why squash (single layer so light passes through and cells are seen separately); why stain (chromosomes otherwise transparent); safety (hydrochloric acid is corrosive: eye protection; stain is an irritant). To make counts reliable, count several fields of view chosen at random, and decide in advance how to count cells on the edge of the field.

### Check questions

1. Describe what happens to the chromosomes during prophase and metaphase. (3 marks)
2. Explain why the DNA content of a cell doubles during interphase. (2 marks)
3. A drug stops spindle fibres forming. Explain why it can be used to treat cancer and why it causes hair loss. (3 marks)
4. Describe binary fission. (3 marks)
5. Explain why viruses are not described as dividing. (2 marks)
6. In a root tip squash 45 of 300 cells were in mitosis. Calculate the mitotic index. (1 mark)
7. Explain why the root tip is heated in hydrochloric acid in a root tip squash. (2 marks)
8. A student counted cells in only one field of view. Suggest why their mitotic index might not represent the whole root tip. (2 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Prophase: chromosomes condense / become visible (1); nuclear envelope breaks down / spindle forms (1). Metaphase: chromosomes line up on equator, attached to spindle by centromeres (1).
2. DNA replication (in S phase) (1); so that each daughter cell gets a full / identical set of genetic material (1).
3. Chromatids not separated, so mitosis stops / cells cannot divide (1); cancer cells divide rapidly, so most affected (1); hair follicle cells also divide rapidly (1).
4. Circular DNA replicates (1); plasmids replicate (1); cytoplasm divides into two cells, each with one copy of circular DNA and variable plasmids (1).
5. Viruses are not cells / have no cytoplasm or organelles (1); they are replicated by the host cell's machinery after injecting nucleic acid (1).
6. 45 ÷ 300 = 0.15 (1).
7. Hydrolyses / breaks down middle lamella so cells separate (1); stops cells dividing / allows stain to enter (1).
8. Division rate may differ in different parts of the tip / not representative (1); sample too small, so chance has larger effect; count several random fields (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/biology/14-species-taxonomy-and-biodiversity.md -->

## Species, taxonomy, biodiversity and investigating diversity

Biologists name and group organisms and measure how varied life is in a place. This file covers the species concept, classification, measuring biodiversity, and comparing species using DNA and proteins.

### Overview

AS content. Conservation methods are in files 23 and 42; sampling methods in the field are also in file 23; standard deviation and other statistics in file 26.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.4.5 to 3.4.7. Edexcel A 4.1 to 4.3, 4.6. Edexcel B 3.1, 3.2 ii, 3.3 i. OCR A 4.2.1(a) to (e), 4.2.2(a) to (d). OCR B 3.1.3(a) to (d), (f), (h), (i). Eduqas C2.1(a) to (l).
- AQA and Edexcel A use the index of diversity d = N(N − 1) ÷ Σn(n − 1). OCR A and Eduqas use Simpson's index D = 1 − Σ(n/N)². OCR B asks for both kinds of biodiversity calculation and genetic diversity. Both are shown below.
- OCR A, Edexcel A, Edexcel B and Eduqas ask for the three-domain system against the five-kingdom system. AQA mentions domains in the hierarchy only.
- Edexcel A and Edexcel B ask for the niche and for the role of the scientific community (peer review, conferences) in accepting new classifications.
- OCR B asks for DNA barcoding and uses the evolution of language as a model for phylogenetic thinking (3.1.3(c), (f)).
- OCR A and OCR B ask for genetic biodiversity measured as the proportion of polymorphic gene loci (OCR A 4.2.1(e), OCR B 3.1.3(i)).

### 3.4.5 Species and taxonomy

*Refs: AQA 3.4.5 · Edexcel A 4.6 · Edexcel B 3.1 i to vii · OCR A 4.2.2(a) to (d) · OCR B 3.1.3(a) to (d), (f) · Eduqas C2.1(a) to (g). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Two organisms belong to the same species if they are able to produce fertile offspring. Courtship behaviour as a necessary precursor to successful mating; its role in recognising members of the same species, identifying a mate that is capable of breeding, forming a pair bond, synchronising mating, and becoming able to breed.
- A phylogenetic classification system attempts to arrange species into groups based on their evolutionary origins and relationships. It uses a hierarchy in which smaller groups are placed within larger groups, with no overlap between groups. Each group is called a taxon (plural taxa).
- One hierarchy comprises the taxa: domain, kingdom, phylum, class, order, family, genus and species.
- Each species is universally identified by a binomial consisting of the name of its genus and species, eg *Homo sapiens*.
- Appreciate that advances in immunology and genome sequencing help to clarify evolutionary relationships between organisms.

#### Explained

**The species concept.** A species is a group of similar organisms that can interbreed to produce fertile offspring. A horse and a donkey can mate, but their offspring (a mule) is infertile, so horses and donkeys are separate species.

**Limits of the definition** (Edexcel B 3.1 ii, iii). It cannot be used for organisms that reproduce asexually (bacteria), for extinct organisms known only from fossils, or for populations that never meet. Some species hybridise and produce fertile young (some plants, some gulls). So biologists also use the morphological concept (similar appearance) and the phylogenetic concept (a group sharing a recent common ancestor, defined by DNA).

**Courtship.** Courtship behaviours (displays, songs, dances) are species-specific. They let animals:

- recognise a member of their own species, so they do not waste energy on matings that give no fertile offspring
- recognise a mate of the opposite sex who is sexually mature and fertile (or receptive)
- form a pair bond, so both parents raise the young
- synchronise mating so it happens when eggs are available.

**Classification.** Organisms are placed in a hierarchy: **D**omain, **K**ingdom, **P**hylum, **C**lass, **O**rder, **F**amily, **G**enus, **S**pecies. Each group contains organisms that share a more recent common ancestor the further down you go. Groups do not overlap.

Example, the grey wolf: Eukarya, Animalia, Chordata, Mammalia, Carnivora, Canidae, *Canis*, *lupus*.

**Binomial naming.** Genus (capital first letter) plus species (lower case), in italics or underlined. It is universal, so it avoids confusion from different common names.

**Five kingdoms and three domains** (OCR A, Edexcel A, Edexcel B, Eduqas).

| Kingdom | Main features |
| --- | --- |
| Prokaryotae | Unicellular; no nucleus; murein walls; 70S ribosomes |
| Protoctista | Mostly unicellular eukaryotes; some like plants, some like animals (algae, amoeba) |
| Fungi | Eukaryotic; chitin walls; saprotrophic nutrition; hyphae; spores |
| Plantae | Multicellular; cellulose walls; chlorophyll; autotrophic |
| Animalia | Multicellular; no cell walls; heterotrophic; nervous coordination |

In 1990 Carl Woese proposed three domains above the kingdoms: **Bacteria**, **Archaea** and **Eukarya**. Evidence: sequences of ribosomal RNA showed archaea are as different from bacteria as both are from eukaryotes. Archaea have different membrane lipids, different RNA polymerase (more like eukaryotes'), and histone-like proteins; bacteria have murein walls but archaea do not. This divides Prokaryotae into two domains.

**Validating new ideas** (Edexcel A, Edexcel B). A new classification is published in a scientific journal after peer review (other experts check the method and conclusions), discussed at conferences, and tested by other scientists. If repeated evidence supports it, it becomes accepted. The three-domain model went through this.

**Phylogeny.** A phylogenetic tree shows evolutionary relationships. Branch points show common ancestors; species that branch apart more recently are more closely related. Classification is now based on phylogeny.

**Evidence used to classify** (OCR B, Eduqas, Edexcel B):

- physical features (anatomy, embryology)
- DNA base sequences and the amino acid sequences of proteins (more different = more distant)
- immunological comparisons: antibodies against a protein from one species are mixed with the same protein from another; the more precipitate forms, the more similar the proteins, so the more closely related the species
- DNA barcoding (OCR B): sequencing a short, standard gene region (in animals, part of the mitochondrial gene for cytochrome c oxidase I; in plants, chloroplast genes) and matching it against a database to identify a species quickly
- gel electrophoresis of DNA or proteins (Edexcel B; method in file 25).

**Language as a model** (OCR B). Languages change over time by small changes passed on, and split when groups of speakers become separated, much as species do.
