Linguists group words by shared features and build family trees of languages, using the same kind of methods as phylogenetics. It is a model, not evidence for biological evolution.

#### Common mistakes

- Writing the binomial with a capital letter for the species name.
- Saying classification is based on "what organisms look like". Modern classification is based on evolutionary relationships.
- Saying "the more groups two species share, the less related they are".

### 3.4.6 Biodiversity within a community

*Refs: AQA 3.4.6 · Edexcel A 4.1, 4.2, 4.3 · Edexcel B 3.2 ii, 3.3 i · OCR A 4.2.1(a) to (d) · OCR B 3.1.3(h), 4.3.1(l) · Eduqas C2.1(h) to (j). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Biodiversity can relate to a range of habitats, from a small local habitat to the Earth.
- Species richness is a measure of the number of different species in a community.
- An index of diversity describes the relationship between the number of species in a community and the number of individuals in each species. Calculation of an index of diversity (d) from d = N(N − 1) ÷ Σn(n − 1), where N = total number of organisms of all species and n = total number of organisms of each species.
- Farming techniques reduce biodiversity. The balance between conservation and farming.

#### Explained

**Levels of biodiversity** (OCR A, Edexcel B): habitat diversity (the number of different habitats in an area), species diversity (species richness and evenness) and genetic diversity (the variety of alleles within a species).

**Species richness** counts species but ignores how many of each. **Species evenness** describes how equal the numbers of each species are. Two woods may each have 10 species, but if one is 95% oak it is less diverse than one with all ten in similar numbers. An index of diversity combines both.

**Endemism** (Edexcel A): an endemic species is found naturally in only one place (such as the Galápagos giant tortoise). Areas with many endemic species are priorities for conservation.

**Niche** (Edexcel A, Edexcel B). An organism's niche is its role in its habitat: what it eats, what eats it, where and when it feeds, the conditions it tolerates. No two species occupy exactly the same niche in a habitat for long: one outcompetes the other. Adaptations fit organisms to their niche.

**Farming and biodiversity.** Farming reduces biodiversity by: clearing woodland and removing hedgerows; growing monocultures; using pesticides and herbicides; draining wetlands; overgrazing; using fertilisers that favour a few fast-growing species. Ways to balance farming and conservation: keep and replant hedgerows, leave field margins uncultivated, rotate crops, use integrated pest management, maintain ponds and wetlands, pay farmers through agri-environment schemes.

#### Worked example

A pond survey found: species A 10, B 5, C 3, D 2.

**AQA / Edexcel A index:**

1. N = 20. N(N − 1) = 20 × 19 = 380.
2. Σn(n − 1) = (10 × 9) + (5 × 4) + (3 × 2) + (2 × 1) = 90 + 20 + 6 + 2 = 118.
3. d = 380 ÷ 118 = 3.22.

A higher d means more diversity. The lowest possible value is 1 (only one species).

**OCR A / Eduqas Simpson's index:** D = 1 − Σ(n/N)².

1. (10/20)² = 0.25; (5/20)² = 0.0625; (3/20)² = 0.0225; (2/20)² = 0.01.
2. Σ = 0.345. D = 1 − 0.345 = 0.655.

D lies between 0 and 1; values nearer 1 mean higher diversity.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying species richness takes numbers of individuals into account.
- Using N² instead of N(N − 1) in the AQA formula.
- Comparing an AQA index with a Simpson's index; they are on different scales.

### 3.4.7 Investigating diversity

*Refs: AQA 3.4.7 · Edexcel A 4.2 · Edexcel B 3.1 iv, v · OCR A 4.2.1(b), (e) · OCR B 3.1.3(i) · Eduqas C2.1(e), (k), (l). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Genetic diversity within, or between, species can be made by comparing: the frequency of measurable or observable characteristics; the base sequence of DNA; the base sequence of mRNA; the amino acid sequence of the proteins encoded by DNA and mRNA.
- Interpret data relating to similarities and differences in the base sequences of DNA and in the amino acid sequences of proteins to suggest relationships between different organisms within a species and between species.
- Appreciate that gene technology has caused a change in the methods of investigating genetic diversity; inferring DNA differences from measurable or observable characteristics has been replaced by direct investigation of DNA sequences.
- Quantitative investigations of variation within a species involve: collecting data from random samples; calculating a mean value and the standard deviation of that mean; interpreting mean values and their standard deviations.

#### Explained

**Comparing species.** Closely related species share a recent common ancestor, so they have had less time for mutations to build up. Their DNA base sequences, and so their mRNA and amino acid sequences, are more similar. Count the differences: fewer differences mean a closer relationship.

Comparing observable characteristics was the old method. Its problems: many characteristics are controlled by many genes and affected by the environment, and similar features can evolve separately (convergent evolution). DNA sequencing is now fast and cheap, and compares genes directly.

**Random sampling** (all boards). To avoid bias, choose sample sites at random: lay out two tape measures at right angles and use random numbers as coordinates. Take a large sample to reduce the effect of chance. Non-random sampling (OCR A) includes systematic sampling along a transect (when there is a gradient), opportunistic sampling (quick but biased) and stratified sampling (sampling each area in proportion to its size). Field methods are in file 23.

**Mean and standard deviation.** The mean is the average. The standard deviation (SD) measures the spread of data around the mean: about 68% of values lie within 1 SD of the mean, about 95% within 2 SD. If two samples' means ± SD overlap a lot, the difference between them is probably due to chance. Formula and test in file 26.

**Genetic biodiversity within a species** (OCR A, OCR B). Proportion of polymorphic gene loci = number of polymorphic loci ÷ total number of loci tested. A locus is polymorphic if it has more than one allele in the population (commonly with the most common allele below 99% frequency). A higher proportion means more genetic diversity. Eduqas also lists DNA fingerprinting for comparing genetic diversity (file 25).

#### Worked example

The amino acid sequence of one protein was compared in four species. Differences from species W: X 2, Y 14, Z 9. Which species is most closely related to W?

X: fewest amino acid differences, so it shares the most recent common ancestor with W. Order of relatedness to W: X, then Z, then Y.

Genetic diversity: 40 gene loci were tested in a population; 13 were polymorphic. Proportion = 13 ÷ 40 = 0.325.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying that the same amino acid sequence means identical DNA. The code is degenerate, so DNA can differ.
- Saying a large standard deviation means "the data are wrong". It means more spread.
- Choosing sample sites "randomly" by walking about.

### Check questions

1. Explain why two species that produce hybrid offspring are still classed as separate species. (2 marks)
2. Give three roles of courtship behaviour. (3 marks)
3. Give the correct order of the taxa from domain to species. (1 mark)
4. Calculate the AQA index of diversity for a habitat with species counts of 6, 6, 4 and 4. (2 marks)
5. Calculate Simpson's index (1 − Σ(n/N)²) for the same habitat. (2 marks)
6. Explain why comparing DNA base sequences gives a more reliable picture of relationships than comparing physical features. (3 marks)
7. Describe the evidence that led to the three-domain system. (2 marks)
8. Explain two ways farming reduces biodiversity and one way farmers can increase it. (3 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Hybrid offspring are infertile (1); species definition requires fertile offspring (1).
2. Any three: recognise same species; identify a fertile / receptive mate; form a pair bond; synchronise mating; stimulate release of gametes.
3. Domain, kingdom, phylum, class, order, family, genus, species (1).
4. N = 20; N(N − 1) = 380; Σn(n − 1) = 30 + 30 + 12 + 12 = 84 (1); d = 380 ÷ 84 = 4.52 (1).
5. Σ(n/N)² = 0.09 + 0.09 + 0.04 + 0.04 = 0.26 (1); D = 0.74 (1).
6. Physical features may be affected by environment / polygenic (1); similar features may evolve in unrelated species / convergent evolution (1); DNA differences reflect mutations accumulated since common ancestor / direct comparison of genes (1).
7. Ribosomal RNA sequences / membrane lipids / RNA polymerase differ (1); archaea as different from bacteria as from eukaryotes, so prokaryotes split into two domains (1).
8. Any two (1 each): hedgerow removal; monoculture; pesticides / herbicides; draining wetlands; fertilisers. One (1): keep hedgerows; field margins; crop rotation; integrated pest management; ponds.

<!-- source: notes/a-level/biology/42-climate-change-and-human-impact.md -->

## Climate change, human impact and sustainability

Human activity is changing climates and ecosystems and driving species to extinction. This file covers the evidence for and causes of climate change, its effects on organisms, why biodiversity matters, how species and ecosystems are conserved and managed sustainably, international agreements, farms as ecosystems, human population growth and food security, and Edexcel A core practical 13.

### Overview

Carbon and nitrogen cycles are in file 17; succession, conservation by managing succession, and in situ and ex situ methods in file 23; farming and biodiversity in file 14. AQA covers conservation through file 23 only.

#### Board differences

- Edexcel A 4.1, 4.16, 5.12 to 5.16, 5.18, 5.20 to 5.22, CP13. Edexcel B 3.3 ii, iii, 10.4 i to iv. OCR A 4.2.1(f) to (i), 6.3.2(c) to (e). OCR B 4.3.1(l), 4.3.2(a) to (d). Eduqas C1.5(m), C1.6(a) to (f).
- Edexcel A has a full sub-topic on climate change: evidence, causes, models, effects, and how scientific conclusions about controversial issues are reached.
- OCR A asks for named examples of managed ecosystems (Galápagos, Masai Mara, Terai region, Peak District, Snowdonia, Antarctica) and agreements (CITES, CBD, Countryside Stewardship).
- OCR B asks for farms as ecosystems, human population growth, the importance of biodiversity and food security.
- Eduqas asks for planetary boundaries (C1.6(f)).

### Climate change: evidence, causes and models

*Refs: Edexcel A 5.12 to 5.14, 5.18, 5.20, 5.21 · Eduqas C1.5(m). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- The different types of evidence for climate change and its causes: records of carbon dioxide concentration (ppm) and temperature, pollen in peat bogs, dendrochronology.
- The causes of anthropogenic climate change: the role of greenhouse gases (CO₂ and methane) in the greenhouse effect.
- That data can be extrapolated to make predictions, and that these are used in models of future climate change; the limitations of models.
- How scientific conclusions about controversial issues can be affected by the people and organisations making them.
- How knowledge of the carbon cycle can be applied to reducing atmospheric CO₂.

#### Explained

**Evidence for climate change.**

- *Temperature records:* thermometer records since the 1850s show a rise of about 1.2 °C in global mean temperature. Earlier records were less reliable (fewer places, different methods).
- *Dendrochronology:* trees in temperate areas lay down one growth ring per year; wide rings mean good growing conditions (warm, wet). Ring widths of old trees and timbers give a record of past climate.
- *Pollen in peat bogs:* peat forms in waterlogged, acidic conditions where decomposition is slow, so pollen grains are preserved in layers. Each species' pollen has a distinctive shape. The species present in each layer show the climate when that layer formed (different plants grow in different climates); deeper layers are older (dated by radiocarbon).
- *Ice cores:* bubbles of air trapped in Antarctic and Greenland ice give past CO₂ and methane concentrations; the ratio of oxygen isotopes in the ice gives past temperature. Records go back about 800 000 years and show CO₂ and temperature rising and falling together.
- *CO₂ records:* direct measurements since 1958 (Mauna Loa, Hawaii) show CO₂ rising from about 315 ppm to over 420 ppm, with a seasonal wave (falls in the northern summer when plants photosynthesise more).

**The greenhouse effect.** Short-wave radiation from the Sun passes through the atmosphere and warms the Earth's surface, which emits longer-wave infrared radiation. Greenhouse gases (CO₂, methane, water vapour, nitrous oxide) absorb and re-emit some of this infrared, keeping the lower atmosphere warm. Without it the Earth would be about 33 °C colder. Increasing greenhouse gases enhance the effect, so temperatures rise: **anthropogenic** (human-caused) climate change.

- CO₂: burning fossil fuels; deforestation (less photosynthesis; burning trees); cement making.
- Methane: cattle and other ruminants (gut microorganisms); rice paddies; landfill; leaks from gas extraction; melting permafrost releases more (positive feedback). Methane is a much more powerful greenhouse gas per molecule than CO₂ but lasts a shorter time.

**Models and extrapolation.** Scientists extrapolate trends and use computer models that include many factors (emissions, oceans, clouds, ice) to predict future climate under different emission scenarios. Limitations: data from the past may not hold in future; future emissions depend on human choices; some processes (clouds, feedbacks from ice and permafrost) are not fully understood; extrapolating beyond the data range adds uncertainty. Models are tested by checking how well they reproduce past climate.

**Controversy and the scientific community** (Edexcel A 5.18, 5.20). Conclusions about controversial issues can be affected by who funds or publishes the research (oil companies, environmental groups, governments), by selective use of data, or by media reporting. Peer review, publication of methods and data, repetition by independent scientists, and large reviews of evidence (the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change, IPCC) increase confidence. Decision-makers balance scientific evidence with economic and social factors.

**Reducing atmospheric CO₂** (Edexcel A 5.21, 5.22).

- Use renewable energy and reduce energy use.
- **Biofuels** (bioethanol from fermenting sugar cane or maize starch; biodiesel from plant oils): carbon released when burned was recently absorbed by the plants, so they are close to carbon-neutral (but growing, processing and transporting them uses energy; they may compete with food crops).
- **Reforestation:** growing trees take up CO₂ by photosynthesis and store carbon in wood. Sustainable forestry (replanting as trees are felled) and protecting peat bogs keep carbon stored.
- Carbon capture and storage.

#### Worked example

Atmospheric CO₂ was 340 ppm in 1980 and 412 ppm in 2020. Assuming a linear trend, predict the concentration in 2050. Rate = 72 ÷ 40 = 1.8 ppm per year; 2050: 412 + 30 × 1.8 = 466 ppm. Limitation: the rate of increase has itself been rising, so a linear extrapolation probably underestimates, and future emissions may change.

### Effects of climate change on organisms

*Refs: Edexcel A 5.15, 5.16, CP13 · Edexcel B 10.4 i. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- The effects of climate change (changing rainfall patterns and changes in seasonal cycles) on plants and animals (distribution of species, development and life cycles).
- The effect of temperature on enzyme activity and its impact on plants, animals and microorganisms, including Q10.
- Practical: effects of temperature on the development of organisms.

#### Explained

- **Distribution:** species shift to cooler areas (towards the poles, or higher up mountains). Species that cannot move or that are already at the top of mountains or on islands may become extinct. Pests and disease vectors (mosquitoes) spread into new areas.
- **Seasonal cycles (phenology):** spring events happen earlier (leaves opening, flowering, egg-laying, insect emergence). If a predator's breeding no longer matches its prey's peak (birds and caterpillars), numbers fall.
- **Rainfall:** droughts and floods change habitats; drought reduces photosynthesis and crop yields.
- **Development:** in ectotherms, development rate depends on temperature; sex of some reptiles (turtles) is set by nest temperature, so warming changes sex ratios.
- **Oceans:** warmer water holds less oxygen; coral bleaching; more CO₂ dissolves, lowering pH (ocean acidification), which harms shell-forming organisms.

**Enzymes and Q10** (Edexcel A 5.16). Within limits, a 10 °C rise roughly doubles enzyme-controlled rates (Q10 ≈ 2; file 02). Warming speeds the metabolism of ectotherms and microorganisms (faster decomposition, releasing more CO₂). Above the optimum, enzymes denature. Rising temperature may favour some species and harm others.

**Evolution in response** (Edexcel A 5.17). Climate change is a selection pressure: individuals with alleles that suit the new conditions are more likely to survive and reproduce, so allele frequencies change. Species with short generations and genetic diversity are more likely to adapt; others may not adapt fast enough.

#### Practical

**CP13: investigate the effects of temperature on the development of organisms** (such as seedling growth rate, or brine shrimp hatch rate).

1. Place equal numbers of brine shrimp eggs in equal volumes of salt water at a range of temperatures (water baths or incubators, for example 15, 20, 25, 30 °C).
2. Every few hours count the number of hatched larvae (use a light: larvae swim towards it), or measure seedling height each day.
3. Plot percentage hatched (or growth) against time for each temperature; compare rates; calculate Q10.

Control variables: salt concentration, light, volume, number of eggs, pH. Ethics: brine shrimp are invertebrates, but handle humanely.

### Biodiversity, conservation and sustainable management

*Refs: Edexcel A 4.1, 4.16 · Edexcel B 3.3 ii, iii, 10.4 i to iii · OCR A 4.2.1(f) to (i), 6.3.2(c) to (e) · Eduqas C1.6(a) to (d), (f). Level: AS & A (Edexcel A Topic 4, Edexcel B 3.3, OCR A 4.2.1); A-level only (rest).*

#### What you need to know

- The factors affecting biodiversity and why species become endangered or extinct.
- The ecological, economic and aesthetic reasons for maintaining biodiversity.
- In situ and ex situ conservation (zoos, seed banks); international and local agreements.
- How ecosystems are managed to balance conservation with human needs; sustainability.

#### Explained

**Why biodiversity is falling** (OCR A 4.2.1(f), Eduqas C1.6(a)).

- **Human population growth** increases demand for land, food, water and energy.
- **Habitat loss and fragmentation:** deforestation, farming, urban development, draining wetlands.
- **Monoculture** farming; pesticides and herbicides.
- **Over-exploitation:** overfishing, hunting, logging, trade in animals and plants.
- **Pollution:** fertilisers (eutrophication), plastics, pesticides.
- **Invasive species** introduced by humans outcompete or prey on native species.
- **Climate change.**

Small populations are vulnerable: low genetic diversity, inbreeding, chance events.

**Reasons to maintain biodiversity** (OCR A 4.2.1(g), Edexcel B 3.3 ii).

- *Ecological:* species depend on each other (pollinators, food webs, nutrient cycling); keystone species have large effects; diverse ecosystems are more stable and recover better from change.
- *Economic (ecosystem services):* food, timber, fibres, fuel; medicines (file 41); genes for crop breeding (wild relatives with disease resistance); pollination of crops; soil formation; water purification; flood control; carbon storage; ecotourism.
- *Aesthetic and ethical:* enjoyment of nature and its benefits to mental health; the view that species have a right to exist and that we have a duty to future generations.

**Conservation and preservation.** Conservation is active management, allowing sustainable use; preservation protects an area from use. In situ and ex situ methods, zoos and seed banks are described in file 23. Eduqas C1.6(b): gene pools are conserved in the wild (protected habitats, wildlife corridors joining fragmented habitats) and in captivity (captive breeding with studbooks; frozen gametes and embryos; seed banks).

**Agreements** (OCR A 4.2.1(i), Edexcel B 10.4 ii).

- **CITES** (Convention on International Trade in Endangered Species, 1973): bans international trade in the most endangered species (Appendix I) and controls trade in others with permits. It has reduced trade in, for example, ivory and some parrots, but illegal trade continues where enforcement is weak and demand is high.
- **Convention on Biological Diversity** (Rio, 1992): countries agree to conserve biodiversity, use it sustainably, and share the benefits of genetic resources fairly (including medicines from plants found in developing countries).
- **Countryside Stewardship Scheme** (UK; now Environmental Land Management schemes): pays farmers to manage land to benefit wildlife (hedgerows, field margins, wetlands).

**Sustainable management** (OCR A 6.3.2(d), (e); Edexcel B 10.4 iii). Sustainability means meeting present needs without compromising future generations.

- *Timber:* **coppicing** (cutting trees to near ground level so new shoots grow; harvested in rotation) and **pollarding** (cutting higher, out of reach of grazing animals); selective felling; replanting; leaving buffer zones.
- *Fishing:* quotas; minimum mesh sizes so young fish escape; closed seasons and no-take marine reserves; fish farming (with problems of disease, pollution and wild fish used as feed).

**Managed ecosystems** (OCR A 6.3.2(e)). Each balances human needs with conservation:

- **Galápagos Islands:** invasive goats, rats and plants threaten endemic species; tourism and fishing pressure. Management: national park and marine reserve, eradicating goats, controlling visitors and fishing.
- **Masai Mara** (Kenya): conflict between wildlife and the Maasai people's cattle grazing and farming. Conservancies pay local people from tourism; managed grazing.
- **Terai region** (Nepal and India): forest and grassland with tigers and rhinos; deforestation and conflict with farmers. Community forestry; wildlife corridors.
- **Peak District** and **Snowdonia** (UK): national parks with farming, quarrying, tourism and moorland conservation; managed by grazing, burning and footpath management.
- **Antarctica:** fishing and tourism; protected by the Antarctic Treaty, with no mining and controlled fishing.

**Agriculture versus conservation** (Eduqas C1.6(c)). Intensive farming increases food production but reduces biodiversity (file 14). Compromises include wildlife-friendly farming, integrated pest management and setting aside land.

**Planetary boundaries** (Eduqas C1.6(f)). A framework that sets limits for nine Earth systems within which humanity can safely operate: climate change, biodiversity loss (biosphere integrity), nitrogen and phosphorus cycles, land-system change, freshwater use, ocean acidification, ozone depletion, atmospheric aerosols, and novel entities (such as plastics and chemicals). Several boundaries, including climate change, biodiversity and the nitrogen cycle, are already thought to have been crossed. Political decisions should be informed by evidence (Eduqas C1.6(e)).

### Human population, crops and food security

*Refs: OCR B 4.3.1(l), 4.3.2(a) to (d) · Eduqas C1.6(d). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Farms as ecosystems; the conflict between agriculture and conservation.
- The factors that alter birth and death rates; the effect of human population growth on biodiversity.
- The ecological, economic and scientific reasons for conservation.
- Global food security.

#### Explained

**Farms as ecosystems** (OCR B 4.3.1(l)). A farm is an ecosystem managed to channel as much energy as possible into crops and livestock: weeds, pests and competitors are removed and nutrients are added. This conflicts with conservation, because hedgerow removal, monocultures, pesticides, fertilisers and drainage reduce biodiversity (file 14). Compromises: keeping hedgerows and field margins, agri-environment payments, integrated pest management, set-aside land and wildlife-friendly farming. The compensation point and crop yields are in file 15; deflected succession through land management in file 23.

**Population growth** (OCR B 4.3.2(a), (b)). Human population grows when birth rate exceeds death rate. Birth rates fall with better education (especially for women), contraception, lower infant mortality and urbanisation; death rates fall with better food, clean water, sanitation, vaccination and medicine. The world population has grown from about 1 billion in 1800 to over 8 billion. This increases demand for land, water and food, leading to habitat loss, pollution and over-exploitation, reducing biodiversity.

**Food security** (OCR B 4.3.2(d)). Food security exists when everyone has access to enough safe, nutritious food. Threats: growing population; climate change; soil degradation; water shortages; pests and disease; conflict; poverty; waste (about a third of food is wasted). Solutions: higher-yield and more resilient crops (including GM); reducing waste; sustainable farming; fair trade; alternative proteins.

### Check questions

1. Explain how pollen in peat bogs provides evidence of past climate. (3 marks)
2. Explain how increased methane concentrations contribute to climate change. (3 marks)
3. Give two limitations of using models to predict future climate. (2 marks)
4. Describe two ways climate change may affect the distribution or life cycles of organisms. (4 marks)
5. Explain why a 10 °C rise in temperature might double the rate of decomposition in soil. (2 marks)
6. Give one ecological, one economic and one aesthetic reason for conserving biodiversity. (3 marks)
7. Evaluate the effectiveness of CITES. (3 marks)
8. Explain why there is often conflict between farming and conservation, and suggest two compromises. (4 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Pollen preserved in waterlogged / acidic peat with little decomposition (1); different species identified from pollen shape, each with known climate requirements (1); deeper layers older / dated, showing change over time (1).
2. Methane is a greenhouse gas (1); absorbs and re-emits infrared radiation from the Earth's surface (1); enhanced greenhouse effect, global temperature rises (1).
3. Any two: future emissions uncertain; not all processes / feedbacks understood; extrapolation beyond data; limited past data.
4. Any two with explanation (2 each): range shifts towards poles / uphill; earlier flowering / breeding; mismatch with food supply; changed rainfall affects habitats / reproduction; extinction where species cannot move.
5. Decomposition is enzyme-controlled (1); more kinetic energy / more enzyme–substrate collisions, Q10 about 2 (1).
6. Ecological: food webs / stability / keystone species / nutrient cycling (1); economic: food / medicines / timber / ecotourism / pollination (1); aesthetic: enjoyment / wellbeing / beauty (1).
7. Has reduced legal trade in endangered species (e.g. ivory) (1); but illegal trade continues / poaching (1); depends on enforcement and funding in each country / not all countries signed (1).
8. Farming maximises yield by removing other species / monocultures / pesticides / hedgerow removal (1); this reduces habitats and biodiversity (1); any two compromises (1 each): hedgerows / field margins; agri-environment payments; integrated pest management; set-aside / wildlife areas.

<!-- source: notes/a-level/biology/23-populations-in-ecosystems.md -->

## Populations in ecosystems, succession and conservation

Populations of different species live together in ecosystems, and their sizes are set by biotic and abiotic factors. This file covers carrying capacity, competition and predation, estimating population size, succession, and conservation, with AQA required practical 12.

### Overview

A-level only for AQA. Energy flow and nutrient cycles are in file 17. Biodiversity indices are in file 14. Human impact, conservation agreements and sustainable management (OCR A 6.3.2(d), (e), Edexcel B 10.4, Eduqas C1.6) are in file 42. Statistical tests on field data are in file 26.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.7.4, RP12. Edexcel A 4.3, 4.16, 5.1 to 5.4, CP10. Edexcel B 3.3 ii, iii, 10.1 i, iv, v, 10.3, CP15, CP16. OCR A 4.2.1(b), (h), 6.3.1(a), (d), (e), 6.3.2(a) to (c). Eduqas C1.5(a) to (e), (g), (i), (j), C1.6(b).
- Eduqas asks for population growth curves and density-dependent and density-independent factors (C1.5(a) to (c)).
- OCR A asks for the difference between conservation and preservation (6.3.2(c)) and predator–prey cycles (6.3.2(b)).
- Edexcel B CP15 compares sampling methods for estimating population size; Edexcel B CP16, Edexcel A CP10 and OCR A PAG3 are field studies like AQA RP12.
- Edexcel A, Edexcel B and OCR A ask for in situ and ex situ conservation (zoos, seed banks).

### 3.7.4 Populations in ecosystems

*Refs: AQA 3.7.4, RP12 · Edexcel A 4.3, 4.16, 5.1 to 5.4, CP10 · Edexcel B 3.3 ii, iii, 10.1 i, iv, v, 10.3 i, ii, CP15, CP16 · OCR A 4.2.1(b), (h), 6.3.1(a), (d), (e), 6.3.2(a) to (c) · OCR B 4.3.1(m) · Eduqas C1.5(a) to (e), (g), (i), (j), C1.6(b). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Populations of different species form a community. A community and the non-living components of its environment together form an ecosystem. Ecosystems can range in size from the very small to the very large.
- Within a habitat, a species occupies a niche governed by adaptation to both biotic and abiotic conditions.
- An ecosystem supports a certain size of population of a species, called the carrying capacity. This population size can vary as a result of: the effect of abiotic factors; interactions between organisms: interspecific and intraspecific competition and predation.
- The size of a population can be estimated using: randomly placed quadrats, or quadrats along a belt transect, for slow-moving or non-motile organisms; the mark-release-recapture method for motile organisms. The assumptions made when using the mark-release-recapture method.
- Ecosystems are dynamic and are subject to change over time. The concept of succession from pioneer species to climax community. At each stage in succession, certain species may be recognised that change the environment so that it becomes more suitable for other species with different adaptations. The new species may change the environment in such a way that it becomes less suitable for the previous species. Changes that organisms produce in their abiotic environment can result in a less hostile environment and change biodiversity.
- Conservation of habitats frequently involves management of succession.
- Evaluate evidence and data concerning issues relating to the conservation of species and habitats and consider conflicting evidence. Use given data to calculate the size of a population estimated using the mark-release-recapture method.

#### Explained

**Terms.**

- *Population:* all the organisms of one species in an area at one time.
- *Community:* all the populations of different species in an area.
- *Ecosystem:* a community plus the abiotic (non-living) parts of its environment.
- *Habitat:* the place where an organism lives.
- *Niche:* the role of a species in its habitat, including its interactions with other species and the abiotic conditions it needs. Two species cannot occupy the same niche in the same habitat indefinitely.

**Carrying capacity.** The largest population an environment can support long term. A population introduced to a new area grows slowly at first (lag phase: few individuals; adjusting), then rapidly (log or exponential phase: plenty of resources), then levels off (stationary phase) as resources, predators or disease limit it around the carrying capacity. (Eduqas, OCR A.)

**Abiotic factors:** temperature, light, pH, water availability, mineral ions, oxygen, wind, soil type. Each species has an optimum range.

**Biotic factors.**

- *Intraspecific competition:* within a species. When resources are short, the population falls; with less competition it rises again; numbers fluctuate around the carrying capacity.
- *Interspecific competition:* between species for the same resource. If niches overlap, one species may outcompete the other (red squirrels replaced by grey squirrels in most of the UK).
- *Predation:* predator and prey populations cycle. Prey increase → more food for predators, so predators increase → prey eaten, so prey decrease → predators starve, so predators decrease → prey recover. Predator peaks lag behind prey peaks. Real cycles are also affected by food supply and disease.

**Density-dependent and density-independent factors** (Eduqas). Density-dependent factors have a bigger effect as the population gets denser: competition, predation, disease, parasitism. Density-independent factors affect a population whatever its density: floods, fire, extreme cold.

**Estimating population size.**

*Quadrats* (for plants and slow-moving animals):

1. Use random coordinates (two tape measures at right angles; random number generator) to place quadrats.
2. Record abundance in each: count individuals (density), estimate percentage cover, or record presence in a point frame or gridded quadrat (frequency).
3. Use enough quadrats that the running mean levels off.
4. Population estimate = mean number per quadrat × (area of habitat ÷ area of one quadrat).

*Transects* (when conditions change across an area, such as from a hedge into a field, or down a seashore):

- Line transect: record species touching the line at regular intervals.
- Belt transect: place quadrats at regular intervals along the line (continuous or interrupted).

*Mark-release-recapture* (for mobile animals):

1. Capture a sample, count them (M), mark them harmlessly (paint dot under the shell), release.
2. After time to mix back in, capture a second sample; count the total (C) and how many are marked (R).
3. Population estimate N = (M × C) ÷ R.

Assumptions: marked individuals mix fully back into the population; the mark does not affect survival (not more visible to predators) and does not rub off; no births, deaths, immigration or emigration between samples; the population is in a defined area.

**Sampling methods and techniques** (Edexcel B 10.1 iv, v, CP15; OCR A 4.2.1(b)). Choose the method for the organism and habitat: kick sampling and nets for freshwater invertebrates; pitfall traps for ground insects; pooters; sweep nets; Tullgren funnels for soil invertebrates; light traps for moths. Measure abiotic factors with light meters, thermometers, pH probes, oxygen probes. Random sampling avoids bias; systematic sampling along a transect shows patterns; stratified sampling covers each part of a habitat in proportion. More samples give a more representative estimate.

**Succession.** The change in a community over time.

*Primary succession* starts on bare ground with no soil (bare rock, sand dunes, cooled lava):

1. **Pioneer species** colonise: lichens, mosses, algae. Adaptations: spores or seeds spread by wind, tolerance of extreme conditions, ability to fix nitrogen, rapid germination, photosynthesis.
2. Pioneers break down rock and, when they die and decompose, form a thin soil with some organic matter (humus).
3. Grasses and small plants can now grow. The soil deepens, holds more water and more nutrients.
4. Shrubs, then trees, colonise. Each stage changes the abiotic environment (more shade, deeper soil, more shelter) in ways that suit new species and may make it less suitable for the previous ones, which are outcompeted.
5. A stable **climax community** forms (in the UK, usually deciduous woodland, such as oak).

As succession proceeds: biomass increases; the number of species (biodiversity) usually increases; conditions become less hostile; food webs become more complex.

*Secondary succession* starts where soil is already present but vegetation has been removed (after fire, ploughing or felling). It is faster.

*Deflected succession / plagioclimax:* human activity (grazing, mowing, burning) stops succession at an earlier stage. Heather moorland is kept by controlled burning; chalk grassland by sheep grazing.

**Conservation.**

- *Conservation* means active management to maintain or increase biodiversity, and allows sustainable human use. *Preservation* (OCR A) means protecting an area from any human interference.
- *Managing succession:* conserving a habitat at an early stage (heathland, meadow) means preventing the climax: grazing, mowing, cutting back scrub, controlled burning.
- *In situ conservation:* protecting species in their natural habitat — national parks, nature reserves, marine conservation zones, controlling invasive species, legal protection.
- *Ex situ conservation:* outside the natural habitat — zoos with captive breeding programmes (keeping studbooks to avoid inbreeding, reintroducing animals to the wild), botanic gardens and seed banks (seeds dried and frozen; tested regularly for viability; such as the Millennium Seed Bank). Advantages: protects species from immediate threats; seeds store many alleles in little space. Disadvantages: small captive populations lose genetic diversity; animals may lose natural behaviours; reintroduction fails if the habitat is still damaged; expensive.

**Conflicting evidence.** Conservation decisions balance biological evidence with economic and social needs. When evaluating data, consider sample size, how the data were collected, whether a correlation shows cause, and who funded or published the study.

#### Worked example

1. In a first sample, 40 woodlice were caught and marked. In the second sample, 50 were caught, 8 of them marked. Estimate the population.

N = (40 × 50) ÷ 8 = 250.

2. Ten 0.25 m² quadrats in a 200 m² meadow contained a total of 36 daisies. Estimate the number of daisies in the meadow.

Mean per quadrat = 3.6; per m² = 3.6 ÷ 0.25 = 14.4; total = 14.4 × 200 = 2880.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying the climax community "is reached when no more species can live there".
- Saying pioneers "make soil" without saying they break down rock and decompose to add organic matter.
- Mixing up population, community and ecosystem.
- In mark-release-recapture, assuming marking "has no effect" without saying what effects it must not have.
- Placing quadrats where there is "the most" of a plant.

#### Practical

**AQA required practical 12** (also Edexcel A CP10, Edexcel B CP16, OCR A PAG3 and 6.3.1(e)): investigation into the effect of a named environmental factor on the distribution of a given species.

Example: the effect of light intensity on the distribution of ivy from the edge of a wood into its interior.

1. Lay a tape measure from the open field into the wood (a belt transect).
2. Place a quadrat at regular intervals (every 2 m).
3. In each, estimate percentage cover of ivy and measure light intensity with a light meter at the same height and time of day.
4. Repeat with several transects.
5. Plot percentage cover against light intensity and use Spearman's rank correlation coefficient to test for a correlation (file 26).

Control variables: same time of day; same weather; same person estimating cover. Risks: uneven ground, stinging plants, ticks; work in pairs; wash hands. Limitations: other factors (soil moisture, pH) also vary along the transect, so a correlation does not show light is the cause.

### Check questions

1. Define the terms niche and carrying capacity. (2 marks)
2. Explain why the populations of a predator and its prey rise and fall in cycles. (4 marks)
3. Describe how a student could estimate the number of dandelions on a school field. (4 marks)
4. In a mark-release-recapture study, 60 beetles were marked; a second sample of 45 contained 9 marked beetles. Estimate the population and give two assumptions. (4 marks)
5. Describe and explain the changes during primary succession on bare rock. (5 marks)
6. Explain why conserving heathland requires management. (3 marks)
7. Give one advantage and one disadvantage of using seed banks for conservation. (2 marks)
8. A student found a positive correlation between soil moisture and the cover of a moss. Explain why this does not show that soil moisture causes greater moss cover. (2 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Niche: role / position of a species in its habitat, including biotic and abiotic interactions (1); carrying capacity: maximum population size an ecosystem can support (long term) (1).
2. More prey means more food so predator numbers rise (1); more predators eat more prey, prey decline (1); less food, predators decline (1); fewer predators, prey recover; predator peak lags behind prey (1).
3. Random coordinates / random number generator (1); place many quadrats and count dandelions in each (1); calculate mean per quadrat (1); multiply by field area ÷ quadrat area (1).
4. (60 × 45) ÷ 9 = 300 (2); any two assumptions (1 each): marks do not affect survival / do not rub off; marked mix fully; no births / deaths / migration between samples.
5. Pioneer species (lichens) colonise (1); break down rock / die and decompose, forming thin soil (1); allows grasses / mosses to grow, soil deepens, more nutrients and water (1); each stage changes environment, making it suitable for new species that outcompete earlier ones (1); shrubs then trees; climax community; biodiversity and biomass increase (1).
6. Heathland is an early stage of succession (1); without management it would become scrub / woodland (1); grazing / burning / cutting prevents succession (1).
7. Advantage (1): stores many alleles / many species in little space / cheap / protects against extinction in the wild. Disadvantage (1): seeds may lose viability; plants not adapting to changing conditions in the wild; samples may not represent full genetic diversity.
8. Correlation does not show cause (1); another factor, such as light or pH, may vary with moisture and affect moss (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/biology/22-populations-evolution-and-speciation.md -->

## Populations, evolution and speciation

Evolution is a change in allele frequencies in a population over time. This file covers gene pools, the Hardy–Weinberg principle, the forces that change allele frequencies, and how new species form.

### Overview

A-level only for AQA. Natural selection and types of selection are introduced in file 13. Classification is in file 14.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.7.2, 3.7.3. Edexcel A 4.4, 4.5, 5.17, 5.19. Edexcel B 3.2 i, iii, 8.3. OCR A 4.2.2(e), 6.1.2(e) to (h). OCR B 3.1.3(e), 5.1.2(a) to (e). Eduqas C2.6(c) to (k).
- OCR A asks for evidence for evolution (fossils, DNA, molecular evidence) and for artificial selection (4.2.2(e), 6.1.2(h)).
- OCR B asks how changes in amino acid sequences give evidence of evolution (5.1.2(b)).
- Eduqas asks for intra- and inter-specific competition and selective agencies (C2.6(c), (d)) and for Darwin's theory by name.
- Edexcel A asks how the scientific community validates evidence about evolution (5.18, file 42).

### 3.7.2 Populations

*Refs: AQA 3.7.2 · Edexcel A 4.5 · Edexcel B 8.3 iv · OCR A 6.1.2(f) · OCR B 5.1.2(c) · Eduqas C2.6(e), (g), (h). Level: A-level only (AS for Edexcel A).*

#### What you need to know

- Species exist as one or more populations.
- A population as a group of organisms of the same species occupying a particular space at a particular time that can potentially interbreed.
- The concepts of gene pool and allele frequency.
- The Hardy–Weinberg principle provides a mathematical model, which predicts that allele frequencies will not change from generation to generation. The conditions under which the principle applies.
- Calculate allele, genotype and phenotype frequencies from appropriate data and from the Hardy–Weinberg equation, p² + 2pq + q² = 1, where p is the frequency of one (dominant) allele of the gene and q is the frequency of the other (recessive) allele of the gene.
- Understand and use the expression p + q = 1.

#### Explained

**Gene pool:** all the alleles of all the genes in a population at a given time. **Allele frequency:** the proportion of all copies of a gene that are one particular allele.

**Hardy–Weinberg.** For a gene with two alleles:

- p + q = 1 (allele frequencies)
- p² + 2pq + q² = 1 (genotype frequencies): p² = homozygous dominant, 2pq = heterozygous, q² = homozygous recessive.

The principle predicts allele frequencies stay the same from one generation to the next, if:

- the population is large
- mating is random
- there is no selection (all genotypes equally likely to survive and reproduce)
- there is no mutation
- there is no migration into or out of the population (no gene flow).

In reality these are rarely all true. If observed frequencies change over generations, or differ from predictions, one of the conditions has been broken, such as selection acting. That is how the equation is used (Edexcel B: to monitor change).

The usual starting point is q², because the homozygous recessive phenotype is the only one whose genotype you can identify by looking.

#### Worked example

1 in 2500 babies in a population is born with a recessive condition. Calculate the frequency of carriers.

1. q² = 1/2500 = 0.0004.
2. q = √0.0004 = 0.02.
3. p = 1 − 0.02 = 0.98.
4. Carriers = 2pq = 2 × 0.98 × 0.02 = 0.0392, about 1 in 26 people (3.9%).

From genotype counts: in 200 snails, 98 are AA, 84 Aa, 18 aa. Allele A count = (2 × 98) + 84 = 280 out of 400 alleles; p = 0.7, q = 0.3. Expected under Hardy–Weinberg: AA = 0.49 × 200 = 98; Aa = 0.42 × 200 = 84; aa = 0.09 × 200 = 18. The population fits.

#### Common mistakes

- Taking the square root of the dominant phenotype frequency to get p. The dominant phenotype is p² + 2pq.
- Forgetting the 2 in 2pq.
- Saying Hardy–Weinberg "proves" that a population is not evolving.

### 3.7.3 Evolution may lead to speciation

*Refs: AQA 3.7.3 · Edexcel A 4.4, 5.17, 5.19 · Edexcel B 3.2 i, iii, 8.3 i to iii · OCR A 4.2.2(e), 6.1.2(e), (g), (h) · OCR B 3.1.3(e), 5.1.2(a), (b), (d), (e) · Eduqas C2.6(c), (d), (f), (i) to (k). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Individuals within a population of a species may show a wide range of variation in phenotype. This is due to genetic factors and, to some extent, the environment.
- The primary source of genetic variation is mutation. Meiosis and the random fertilisation of gametes during sexual reproduction produce further genetic variation.
- Predation, disease and competition for the means of survival result in differential survival and reproduction, ie natural selection.
- Those organisms with phenotypes providing selective advantages are likely to produce more offspring and pass on their favourable alleles to the next generation. The effect of this differential reproductive success on the allele frequencies within a gene pool.
- The effects of stabilising, directional and disruptive selection.
- Evolution as a change in the allele frequencies in a population.
- Reproductive separation of two populations can result in accumulation of differences in their gene pools. New species arise when these genetic differences lead to an inability of members of the populations to interbreed and produce fertile offspring. In this way, new species arise from existing species.
- Allopatric and sympatric speciation.
- The importance of genetic drift in causing changes in allele frequency in small populations.
- Explain why individuals within a population of a species may show a wide range of variation in phenotype.

#### Explained

**Sources of variation.** Mutation creates new alleles. Meiosis (independent segregation, crossing over) and random fertilisation reshuffle them. The environment adds non-heritable variation.

**Selection pressures.** Predation, disease and competition for food, mates and territory mean more offspring are produced than can survive. Individuals with advantageous phenotypes are more likely to survive and reproduce (differential reproductive success), so their alleles become more frequent in the gene pool.

- *Intraspecific competition* (Eduqas): between members of the same species, for food, territory and mates; it drives selection within a species.
- *Interspecific competition:* between different species for the same resources.

**Three types of selection.**

| Type | Favoured | Effect on distribution | Example |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Directional | One extreme | Mean shifts | Antibiotic resistance; longer necks; darker moths in polluted areas |
| Stabilising | The mean | Range narrows; mean the same | Human birth weight; clutch size in birds |
| Disruptive | Both extremes | Two peaks; can lead to speciation | Beak sizes in a finch population where only large and small seeds are available |

**Genetic drift.** Random changes in allele frequency from one generation to the next, because which individuals happen to reproduce is partly chance. In small populations chance has a much bigger effect: an allele can be lost or fixed even if it gives no advantage. Drift reduces genetic diversity.

- *Founder effect* (Edexcel B, OCR A, OCR B): a few individuals start a new population; their allele frequencies are by chance unlike those of the original population (for example a high frequency of a rare disease allele in an isolated community).
- *Genetic bottleneck:* a population is greatly reduced (by disaster or hunting); the survivors carry only a fraction of the original alleles. Northern elephant seals and cheetahs have very low genetic diversity after bottlenecks.
- *Gene flow* (migration) brings alleles in or takes them out.

**Speciation.**

1. Two populations become reproductively isolated, so no gene flow between them.
2. Different mutations arise in each.
3. Different selection pressures act (different environments), and drift acts differently.
4. Allele frequencies in the two gene pools change differently.
5. Eventually the populations can no longer interbreed to produce fertile offspring: they are separate species.

*Allopatric speciation:* populations are separated geographically (by a mountain range, a river, the sea). Darwin's finches on the Galápagos Islands are an example.

*Sympatric speciation:* populations live in the same area but become reproductively isolated because of:

- mutations that change courtship behaviour, flowering time or the shape of genitalia
- a mutation that causes polyploidy in plants (offspring cannot breed with the parent population)
- different feeding preferences, so groups mate in different places (apple maggot flies).

**Isolating mechanisms** (OCR A, Eduqas).

- *Prezygotic* (stop fertilisation): geographical; ecological (different habitats); temporal (breeding at different times); behavioural (different courtship); mechanical (genitalia or flowers incompatible); gametic (gametes cannot fuse).
- *Postzygotic* (after fertilisation): hybrid inviability (embryo dies); hybrid sterility (mule); hybrid breakdown (second generation infertile).

**Evidence for evolution** (OCR A, OCR B, Eduqas).

- *Fossils:* show change over time; simpler organisms in older rock layers; transitional forms. Limits: few organisms fossilise; the record has gaps.
- *Comparative anatomy:* homologous structures, such as the pentadactyl limb, show common ancestry.
- *Molecular evidence:* DNA base sequences and protein amino acid sequences (cytochrome c, haemoglobin) are more similar in closely related species. The number of differences increases with time since the species diverged, so differences act as a "molecular clock" (OCR B 5.1.2(b)).
- *Darwin and Wallace* (Eduqas C2.6(k)): observed variation, over-production of offspring, struggle for existence, survival of the fittest, inheritance of advantageous characteristics; the theory explains the origin of species by natural selection.

**Artificial selection** (OCR A 6.1.2(h)). Humans choose individuals with desired characteristics to breed, over many generations: high-yield wheat, dairy cattle with high milk yield, dogs. It reduces genetic diversity, can cause health problems from inbreeding (breathing problems in flat-faced dog breeds), and makes populations vulnerable to new diseases. Gene banks of wild types and old varieties keep a resource of alleles for future breeding.

#### Worked example

Before an antibiotic was used, 2% of a bacterial population carried a resistance allele. After a course of treatment, 60% did. Explain the change.

The antibiotic was a selection pressure. Bacteria without the allele were killed; those with it survived and reproduced. Their offspring inherited the allele, so its frequency rose. This is directional selection; it took place over many generations because bacteria reproduce quickly.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying mutations happen "in order to" help an organism survive.
- Saying "the population adapts by mutating".
- Saying geographical isolation alone makes a new species. The populations must also change genetically until they can no longer interbreed.
- Saying genetic drift is the same as natural selection. Drift is random; selection is not.
- Saying sympatric speciation needs a physical barrier.

### Check questions

1. Define the terms gene pool and allele frequency. (2 marks)
2. Give three conditions needed for the Hardy–Weinberg principle to apply. (3 marks)
3. In a population, 16% of individuals show a recessive phenotype. Calculate the percentage that are heterozygous. (3 marks)
4. Explain how allopatric speciation happens. (5 marks)
5. Describe how sympatric speciation could occur in a population of insects. (3 marks)
6. Explain why genetic drift has a greater effect in small populations. (2 marks)
7. Describe disruptive selection and explain how it could lead to speciation. (3 marks)
8. Explain why a crop variety produced by artificial selection may be more vulnerable to a new disease than its wild relative. (3 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Gene pool: all the alleles of all genes in a population (1); allele frequency: proportion of an allele of all alleles of that gene in a population (1).
2. Any three: large population; random mating; no selection; no mutation; no migration / gene flow.
3. q² = 0.16, q = 0.4 (1); p = 0.6 (1); 2pq = 0.48, so 48% (1).
4. Populations geographically separated (1); no gene flow / reproductive isolation (1); different mutations (1); different selection pressures change allele frequencies differently (1); eventually cannot interbreed to produce fertile offspring (1).
5. Mutation changes courtship / mating time / food preference (1); some individuals become reproductively isolated from others in the same area (1); gene pools diverge until new species form (1).
6. Chance events affect allele frequencies more when there are few individuals (1); an allele can be lost or fixed regardless of advantage (1).
7. Both extremes favoured, intermediates selected against (1); two distinct groups form (1); if they stop interbreeding, gene pools diverge, leading to new species (1).
8. Artificial selection reduces genetic diversity (1); fewer alleles, so less chance that some individuals have resistance (1); whole crop may be susceptible (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/biology/41-plant-structure-fibres-minerals-and-drugs.md -->

## Plant structure, fibres, minerals and drugs from plants

Plants give us fibres, starch and medicines. This file covers the plant cell wall and fibres, sclerenchyma, xylem and phloem in stems, the mineral ions plants need, how drugs from plants are discovered and tested, and the sustainable use of plant products, with Edexcel A core practicals 6 to 9.

### Overview

Plant cell organelles are in file 04 (including amyloplasts, plasmodesmata, pits and the middle lamella). Starch and cellulose structure are in file 01. Transport in xylem and phloem is in file 11. Clinical trials in detail are in file 44.

#### Board differences

- Edexcel A 4.7 (part), 4.10 to 4.13, 4.15, CP6 to CP9. Edexcel B 1.6 i. OCR B 3.3.2(e). Eduqas C1.2(l).
- Edexcel A treats plants as natural resources: fibres, starch, drugs and sustainability, with four core practicals.
- Edexcel B and Eduqas ask for the roles of mineral ions in plants (nitrate, phosphate, calcium, magnesium); Eduqas has a specified practical on nitrogen and magnesium deficiency.
- OCR B asks for plants as sources of medicines (3.3.2(e)).

### Plant fibres and stem structure

*Refs: Edexcel A 4.7, 4.10, 4.11, CP6, CP8. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- How the arrangement of cellulose microfibrils and secondary thickening in plant cell walls contributes to the physical properties of plant fibres, which can be exploited by humans.
- The similarities and differences between the structures, position in the stem and function of sclerenchyma fibres (support), xylem vessels (support and transport of water and mineral ions) and phloem (translocation of organic solutes).

#### Explained

**The cell wall.** Cellulose molecules (β-glucose chains, file 01) are held in bundles called **microfibrils** by hydrogen bonds. Microfibrils are laid down in layers in a matrix of hemicellulose and pectin. In each layer microfibrils lie at an angle to those in the next layer, like plywood, which makes the wall strong in all directions. This is the primary wall.

**Secondary thickening.** Some cells, once they stop growing, lay down a thick secondary wall inside the primary wall, with more layers of cellulose and **lignin**. Lignin is a hard, waterproof polymer that makes the wall rigid and strong and resistant to compression and decay.

**Plant fibres** (such as flax, hemp, jute, cotton) are long cells or bundles of cells with thick walls. Their strength comes from many microfibrils at angles, and lignin. Uses: rope, textiles, paper, building materials, composites. Fibres are strong, light, cheap, renewable and biodegradable.

**Tissues in a dicot stem.** Vascular bundles are arranged in a ring near the outside.

| | Sclerenchyma fibres | Xylem vessels | Phloem sieve tubes |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Living? | Dead at maturity | Dead at maturity | Living (with companion cells) |
| Wall | Very thick, heavily lignified, with pits | Lignified in rings, spirals or networks; pits | Cellulose only, no lignin |
| Ends | Tapered, overlapping, end walls present | End walls broken down: continuous tubes | Sieve plates (end walls with pores) |
| Position in stem | Outside the phloem in each bundle (fibre cap) | Inner side of the vascular bundle | Outer side of the vascular bundle, between xylem and fibres |
| Function | Support | Transport of water and mineral ions; support | Translocation of sucrose and amino acids |

#### Practical

**CP6: identify sclerenchyma fibres, phloem sieve tubes and xylem vessels and their location within stems** (light microscope).

1. Cut thin transverse sections of a celery or sunflower stem with a sharp blade (cutting towards a board, not the hand), or use prepared slides.
2. Stain with toluidine blue O (TBO): lignified walls (xylem, sclerenchyma) turn blue-green; cellulose walls (phloem, parenchyma) turn pinkish-purple. Phloroglucinol with hydrochloric acid stains lignin red.
3. Mount in water; view under low, then high power. Draw a low-power plan showing the position of the tissues, and labelled high-power drawings of individual cells.

**CP8: determine the tensile strength of plant fibres.**

1. Extract fibres from a plant (for example nettle or celery stems soaked in water, or by pulling vascular strands from celery).
2. Clamp one end of a fibre of a fixed length to a stand; hang a mass hanger from the other end.
3. Add masses one at a time until the fibre breaks; record the mass at which it breaks. Repeat with fibres of the same length and similar thickness.
4. Tensile strength is the force (mass × 9.81 N kg⁻¹) per cross-sectional area at breaking; compare species or treatments.

Safety: wear eye protection (fibres may whip back); a tray of sand under the masses. Control variables: fibre length, diameter, age, water content.

### Water and mineral ions in plants

*Refs: Edexcel A 4.12, CP7 · Edexcel B 1.6 i · Eduqas C1.2(l). Level: AS & A (Edexcel); A-level only (Eduqas).*

#### What you need to know

- The importance of water and inorganic ions (nitrate, calcium ions and magnesium ions) to plants. (Edexcel B and Eduqas also name phosphate.)
- Practical: investigating plant mineral deficiencies.

#### Explained

| Ion | Use | Deficiency |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Nitrate (NO₃⁻) | Making amino acids, proteins, nucleotides (DNA, RNA, ATP) and chlorophyll | Stunted growth; yellow older leaves (chlorosis), because nitrogen is moved to young leaves |
| Phosphate (PO₄³⁻) | Nucleotides, ATP, phospholipids | Poor root growth; purple-tinged leaves |
| Magnesium (Mg²⁺) | Part of the chlorophyll molecule; activates some enzymes | Yellowing between leaf veins (chlorosis); less photosynthesis |
| Calcium (Ca²⁺) | Calcium pectate in the middle lamella binds cells together; membrane function | Weak growing tips; poor growth of new leaves and roots |
| Potassium (K⁺) (some boards) | Opening stomata; enzyme activation | Yellow leaf edges |

Water is needed for photosynthesis (photolysis), as a solvent for transport, for turgor (support), and to cool the plant by transpiration.

#### Practical

**CP7 (Edexcel A), and the Eduqas specified practical: investigate plant mineral deficiencies.**

1. Grow seedlings of the same species and age (for example mung beans, or duckweed) in culture solutions: a complete nutrient solution (control), and solutions each lacking one ion (no nitrate, no magnesium, no calcium) and distilled water.
2. Keep all other conditions the same: light, temperature, volume of solution, aeration.
3. After two to four weeks, measure: height, number and colour of leaves, root length, dry mass, or (for duckweed) number of fronds.
4. Compare with the control.

### Drugs from plants and drug testing

*Refs: Edexcel A 4.13, CP9 · OCR B 3.3.2(e). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- The development of drug testing from historic to contemporary protocols, including William Withering's digitalis soup, double blind trials, placebos, and three-phase testing.
- Plants as potential sources of medicines (OCR B).
- Practical: investigating the antimicrobial properties of plants (Edexcel A CP9).

#### Explained

**Plants as sources of medicines** (OCR B 3.3.2(e)). Many drugs were first found in plants, often used in traditional medicine: digitalis from foxglove (heart failure); aspirin from willow bark (pain, blood thinning); quinine from cinchona bark (malaria); morphine from opium poppies (pain); taxol from yew (cancer); artemisinin from sweet wormwood (malaria). Plants make these chemicals to defend themselves. Many plants are untested, so conserving biodiversity, especially in tropical forests, keeps possible future medicines.

**Historic testing: William Withering (1775).** Withering learned of a herbal remedy for "dropsy" (swelling from fluid build-up, caused by heart failure). He identified foxglove as the active ingredient and tried different preparations and doses on his patients, recording the results carefully over about 10 years. Too much caused poisoning, too little had no effect. He found the dose that worked and published his findings. This was a step forward (careful observation, recording and dose-finding) but would not be acceptable now: no controls, no consent in the modern sense, no standard purity of the drug, and patients were harmed.

**Contemporary drug testing.**

1. *Pre-clinical:* testing on cells and tissues in the laboratory, computer models, and then animals, to find toxicity and likely doses.
2. *Phase 1:* a small group (20 to 100) of healthy volunteers. Tests safety, side effects and how the body handles the drug, starting with very low doses.
3. *Phase 2:* a few hundred patients with the condition. Tests whether the drug works and finds the best dose; may be compared with a placebo or existing treatment.
4. *Phase 3:* thousands of patients, often in many countries. Compares the new drug with the best existing treatment (or placebo), to confirm it works and to detect rarer side effects.
5. If approved by regulators, the drug is licensed; *phase 4* monitoring continues after release.

Key features:

- **Placebo:** a dummy treatment that looks the same but contains no active drug. It controls for the **placebo effect** (patients improving because they expect to). Using a placebo is not ethical if an effective treatment already exists; then the new drug is compared with that.
- **Double blind:** neither the patients nor the doctors know who has the drug or the placebo, so expectations do not bias results or how they are recorded.
- **Randomised:** patients are allocated to groups at random, so groups are similar.
- Large samples; informed consent; ethics committee approval.

#### Practical

**CP9: investigate the antimicrobial properties of plants, including aseptic techniques for the safe handling of bacteria.**

1. Crush plant material (garlic, mint, ginger) with a pestle and mortar in ethanol to extract antimicrobial chemicals; filter.
2. Soak sterile paper discs in each extract; allow the ethanol to evaporate. Soak a control disc in ethanol only and let it evaporate.
3. Spread a culture of a safe bacterium (such as *Bacillus subtilis*) over an agar plate using aseptic technique (file 28).
4. Place the discs on the agar; tape the lid (not sealed); incubate at 25 °C for 48 hours upside down.
5. Measure the diameter of the clear zone around each disc; calculate area.

### Plant products and sustainability

*Refs: Edexcel A 4.15. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- How the uses of plant fibres and starch may contribute to sustainability, including plant-based products to replace oil-based plastics.

#### Explained

A resource is **sustainable** if it can be used without running out and without harming the environment for future generations.

- **Plant fibres** replace synthetic fibres and materials made from oil: hemp and flax in car panels and insulation; cotton and bamboo textiles; paper instead of plastic packaging. They are renewable, biodegradable and often need less energy to produce; growing plants absorbs CO₂.
- **Starch** makes **bioplastics** (for example starch-based packaging and food containers), which biodegrade, and is fermented to make **bioethanol** (biofuel).
- Drawbacks: land used for fibres or biofuel crops may compete with food production or lead to deforestation; fertilisers and pesticides; some bioplastics need industrial composting; may be less durable or more expensive.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying lignin is made of cellulose.
- Saying phloem has lignified walls.
- Saying a placebo is the new drug at a low dose.
- Saying "double blind" means two control groups.

### Check questions

1. Explain how the structure of plant cell walls makes plant fibres strong. (3 marks)
2. Give two differences between sclerenchyma fibres and phloem sieve tubes. (2 marks)
3. Describe the position of xylem, phloem and sclerenchyma in a transverse section of a dicot stem. (3 marks)
4. Explain why a plant lacking magnesium ions has yellow leaves and grows poorly. (3 marks)
5. Describe how you would show that nitrate is needed for healthy growth of duckweed. (4 marks)
6. Give two reasons why Withering's testing of digitalis would not meet modern standards. (2 marks)
7. Describe the three phases of clinical trials. (3 marks)
8. Explain what is meant by a double-blind trial and why a placebo is used. (3 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Cellulose microfibrils held by hydrogen bonds (1); layers at different angles (1); secondary thickening with lignin (1).
2. Any two: sclerenchyma dead vs phloem living; lignified vs cellulose walls; no sieve plates / tapered ends vs sieve plates; support vs translocation.
3. Vascular bundles in a ring (1); xylem on the inside of each bundle (1); phloem outside the xylem with a cap of sclerenchyma outside the phloem (1).
4. Magnesium is part of chlorophyll (1); less chlorophyll, so leaves yellow (1); less light absorbed / less photosynthesis, so less growth (1).
5. Complete nutrient solution as control (1); solution lacking only nitrate (1); same light / temperature / volume / starting number of fronds (1); count fronds / measure dry mass after several weeks and compare (1).
6. Any two: no control group / placebo; no informed consent; not double blind / randomised; dose / purity not standardised; small, uncontrolled sample.
7. Phase 1: small number of healthy volunteers; safety and side effects (1); phase 2: small number of patients; effectiveness and dose (1); phase 3: large number of patients; compared with existing treatment / placebo (1).
8. Neither patients nor doctors know who receives the drug (1); avoids bias in reporting / recording results (1); placebo controls for the placebo effect / psychological improvement (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/biology/11-mass-transport-in-plants.md -->

## Mass transport in plants: xylem and phloem

Plants move water and mineral ions up from the roots in xylem, and sugars from leaves to where they are needed in phloem. This file covers both, with transpiration, the potometer and adaptations to water availability.

### Overview

AS content. Sclerenchyma and plant fibres (Edexcel A) are in file 41. How xylem and phloem form from stem cells (OCR A 2.1.6(l)) is in file 24.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.3.4.2. Edexcel A 4.11 (part). Edexcel B 4.7 i to v, CP8. OCR A 3.1.3(a) to (f). OCR B 2.2.4(a) to (f). Eduqas C3.2(i) to (r).
- OCR A, OCR B, Edexcel B and Eduqas ask for the root: water entering root hairs, apoplast and symplast pathways, the endodermis and Casparian strip. AQA does not; it is included below for those boards.
- OCR A and Eduqas ask for xerophytes and hydrophytes (OCR A 3.1.3(e), Eduqas C3.2(p)); AQA mentions xerophytes only under gas exchange (file 08).
- OCR A asks for the distribution of xylem and phloem in roots, stems and leaves of dicots, and drawing stained sections (3.1.3(b)).
- Potometer practicals: OCR A PAG5 / 3.1.3(c), Edexcel B CP8, OCR B 2.2.4(c). AQA lists the potometer as a skills opportunity, not a required practical.

### 3.3.4.2 Mass transport in plants

*Refs: AQA 3.3.4.2 · Edexcel A 4.11 · Edexcel B 4.7 i to v, CP8 · OCR A 3.1.3(a) to (f) · OCR B 2.2.4(a) to (f) · Eduqas C3.2(i) to (r). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Xylem as the tissue that transports water in the stem and leaves of plants. The cohesion-tension theory of water transport in the xylem.
- Phloem as the tissue that transports organic substances in plants. The mass flow hypothesis for the mechanism of translocation.
- Recognise xylem vessels, sieve tube elements and companion cells in photographs and diagrams.
- Interpret evidence from tracer and ringing experiments and evaluate evidence for and against the mass flow hypothesis.

#### Explained

**Xylem** vessels are long tubes made from dead cells joined end to end. The end walls have broken down, so there is a continuous column. Their walls are thickened with lignin (in spiral, ring or net patterns), which stops them collapsing under tension and makes them waterproof. Pits in the walls let water move sideways between vessels.

**Water uptake in the root** (OCR A, OCR B, Edexcel B, Eduqas).

1. Root hair cells have a large surface area. Mineral ions are actively transported into them, so their water potential is lower than the soil's; water enters by osmosis.
2. Water crosses the cortex by two pathways:
   - Apoplast pathway: through the cellulose cell walls and spaces between cells. Fast, with little resistance; water is pulled along by cohesion.
   - Symplast pathway: through the cytoplasm of cells, passing from cell to cell through plasmodesmata. (Eduqas also names the vacuolar pathway: through vacuoles as well as cytoplasm.)
3. At the endodermis, the Casparian strip (a band of waterproof suberin in the cell walls) blocks the apoplast. Water must enter the cytoplasm, so the plant controls which ions pass.
4. Endodermis cells actively pump ions into the xylem, lowering its water potential, so water follows by osmosis. This creates **root pressure**, which pushes water a short way up the xylem (it explains guttation and why cut stems "bleed").

**Cohesion-tension theory.**

1. Water evaporates from the moist walls of mesophyll cells into air spaces and diffuses out through the stomata: transpiration.
2. This lowers the water potential of the mesophyll cells, so water moves in from neighbouring cells and ultimately from the xylem.
3. Water molecules are held together by hydrogen bonds (**cohesion**), so as water leaves the top of the xylem, the whole column is pulled up.
4. Water molecules also stick to the lignified xylem walls (**adhesion**), helping the column rise (capillarity).
5. The pull creates **tension** (negative pressure) in the xylem.

Evidence for cohesion-tension: tree trunks get measurably narrower during the day, when transpiration is fastest, because tension pulls the xylem walls inwards; if a xylem vessel is broken, air is drawn in rather than water leaking out, and the plant can no longer pull water up that vessel (the column is broken).

**Transpiration** is the loss of water vapour from the leaves, mainly through stomata. Factors:

| Factor | Effect on rate | Reason |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Light intensity | Increases | Stomata open in light |
| Temperature | Increases | Water molecules have more kinetic energy, evaporate faster; air holds more water vapour so gradient is steeper |
| Humidity | Decreases | Smaller water potential gradient between the leaf and the air |
| Air movement (wind) | Increases | Moist air around the stomata is blown away, keeping the gradient steep |

**Phloem** is made of living cells.

- Sieve tube elements: joined end to end; perforated end walls called sieve plates; no nucleus and very few organelles, so little obstruction to flow.
- Companion cells: next to each sieve tube element, linked by many plasmodesmata; have a nucleus and many mitochondria; they provide ATP for active loading of sucrose and keep the sieve tube element alive.

**Translocation** moves organic substances (mainly sucrose, also amino acids) from **sources** (where they are made, such as leaves) to **sinks** (where they are used or stored, such as roots, growing tips and fruits). It can go up or down.

**Mass flow hypothesis.**

1. At the source, sucrose is actively loaded into sieve tubes (companion cells pump H⁺ out using ATP; H⁺ diffuses back in with sucrose through co-transporter proteins).
2. This lowers the water potential in the sieve tube, so water enters from the xylem by osmosis.
3. Hydrostatic pressure rises at the source end.
4. At the sink, sucrose is removed (used in respiration or converted to starch), so the water potential there is higher; water leaves the sieve tube by osmosis, lowering the pressure.
5. The pressure difference pushes the solution from source to sink: mass flow.

**Evidence.**

*Ringing experiments.* Removing a ring of bark (which contains the phloem but not the xylem) from a tree trunk makes the bark above the ring swell with sugary fluid, and tissue below dies. This shows phloem carries sugars down the trunk; the xylem still carries water because leaves above stay alive for a while.

*Tracer experiments.* Leaves supplied with ¹⁴CO₂ make radioactive sugars. Autoradiographs of stem sections show the radioactivity in the phloem. Radioactivity later appears in sinks.

*Aphids.* An aphid's mouthparts (stylet) pierce a single sieve tube; cutting off the aphid leaves the stylet behind, and sap flows out under pressure. Sap closer to the leaves has a higher sucrose concentration.

*For mass flow:* sap is under pressure; there is a sucrose concentration gradient from source to sink; metabolic poisons that stop ATP production stop translocation (needed for loading).

*Against mass flow:* sieve plates would slow flow a lot; different solutes move at different speeds and in different directions in the same phloem, which a simple pressure-driven flow cannot explain; sucrose reaches all sinks at a similar rate, not faster to the lower-concentration ones.

**Adaptations to water availability** (OCR A, OCR B, Eduqas).

- *Xerophytes* (marram grass, cacti): thick waxy cuticle; rolled leaves with stomata on the inner surface; sunken stomata in pits; hairs trapping moist air; fewer stomata; leaves reduced to spines; succulent tissue storing water; long or wide shallow roots; low water potential in cells.
- *Hydrophytes* (water lilies): stomata on the upper surface of floating leaves; little or no cuticle; air spaces (aerenchyma) that keep leaves afloat and supply oxygen to submerged parts; little xylem or lignin, because water supports the plant.
- *Mesophytes* (Eduqas): plants of moderate water supply, such as most crop plants; they close stomata and may wilt in drought.

**Distribution of vascular tissue** (OCR A). In a dicot root the xylem forms a central star (X shape) with phloem between its arms, helping the root resist pulling forces. In a stem, vascular bundles form a ring near the outside, with xylem on the inside and phloem on the outside, resisting bending. In a leaf, they form the midrib and veins, with xylem on the upper side.

#### Worked example

A potometer has a capillary tube with an internal radius of 0.5 mm. An air bubble moves 24 mm in 4 minutes. Calculate the rate of water uptake in mm³ min⁻¹.

1. Volume = πr² × length = π × 0.5² × 24 = π × 0.25 × 24 = 18.85 mm³.
2. Rate = 18.85 ÷ 4 = 4.7 mm³ min⁻¹ (2 s.f.).

#### Common mistakes

- Saying a potometer measures transpiration. It measures water uptake, which is close to transpiration but some water is used in photosynthesis and to keep cells turgid.
- Saying water moves up the xylem by osmosis or by "capillary action" alone.
- Saying sucrose diffuses into sieve tubes. It is actively loaded.
- Saying phloem cells are dead (xylem vessels are dead).
- Saying translocation only goes downward.
- Saying humidity increases transpiration.

#### Practical

**Measuring water uptake with a potometer** (OCR A PAG5, Edexcel B CP8, OCR B 2.2.4(c); AQA skills opportunity).

1. Cut a shoot under water, at a slant, so no air enters the xylem.
2. Assemble the potometer under water; fit the shoot with a tight seal; seal joints with petroleum jelly.
3. Dry the leaves; let the shoot adjust to the conditions for a few minutes.
4. Introduce one air bubble; measure how far it moves in a set time. Reset with the reservoir tap.
5. Change one factor (light distance, a fan, temperature, a plastic bag for humidity) and repeat.

Control variables: same shoot or shoots of equal leaf area, same time, same other conditions. To compare plants, divide by leaf area.

### Check questions

1. Explain how water moves up the xylem according to the cohesion-tension theory. (4 marks)
2. Explain why the diameter of a tree trunk is smallest around midday. (2 marks)
3. Describe the role of the Casparian strip. (2 marks)
4. Describe the mass flow hypothesis for translocation. (5 marks)
5. Give one piece of evidence against the mass flow hypothesis. (1 mark)
6. Explain why a ring of bark removed from a tree causes swelling above the ring. (2 marks)
