7. A potometer bubble moves 15 mm in 3 min. The capillary radius is 0.4 mm. Calculate the rate of water uptake in mm³ min⁻¹. (2 marks)
8. Explain two ways marram grass is adapted to reduce water loss. (4 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Water evaporates from leaves / transpiration (1); lowers water potential, water drawn out of xylem (1); cohesion: hydrogen bonds between water molecules pull a continuous column up (1); adhesion to xylem walls; creates tension (1).
2. Transpiration fastest at midday (1); tension in xylem pulls walls inwards (1).
3. Waterproof band in endodermis cell walls blocks apoplast pathway (1); water / ions must pass through cytoplasm, so their entry is controlled (1).
4. Sucrose actively loaded into sieve tubes at source (1); lowers water potential, water enters by osmosis (1); high hydrostatic pressure at source (1); sucrose removed at sink, water leaves, lower pressure (1); pressure gradient moves solution to sink (1).
5. Any one: sieve plates would hinder flow; solutes move in different directions / at different rates; sucrose delivered to sinks at similar rates regardless of concentration.
6. Phloem removed with the bark (1); sugars carried down cannot pass, so build up above / water enters by osmosis (1).
7. π × 0.4² × 15 = 7.54 mm³ (1); ÷ 3 = 2.5 mm³ min⁻¹ (1).
8. Any two with explanation (2 each): rolled leaves trap moist air, reducing water potential gradient; sunken stomata / hairs trap moist air; thick waxy cuticle reduces evaporation; fewer stomata.

<!-- source: notes/a-level/biology/28-microbiology-and-culturing.md -->

## Microbiology: classifying, culturing and counting bacteria

Microbiologists grow bacteria in the laboratory to identify them, test antibiotics and make products. This file covers bacterial shapes and Gram staining, aseptic technique, growth media, measuring population growth, the bacterial growth curve, and the related practicals.

### Overview

Prokaryotic cell structure is in file 04. Antibiotics and resistance are in file 27. AQA required practical 6 (antimicrobial substances) is in file 13; the culturing skills here apply to it. Industrial culture (batch and continuous fermentation) is in file 29.

#### Board differences

- Edexcel A 4.14, CP9 (part), CP15. Edexcel B 2.1 iv, 6.1 i to v, CP12, CP13. OCR A 6.2.1(g), (h). OCR B 3.2.1(d), 3.2.3(g). Eduqas C1.4(a) to (d).
- Gram staining: Edexcel B, OCR B and Eduqas.
- Eduqas also classifies bacteria by shape and asks for a specified practical counting bacteria in fresh and stale milk.
- Edexcel B asks for broth, agar and selective media and for different methods of measuring growth (cell counts, dilution plating, mass, optical methods).
- OCR A asks for the standard growth curve and the formula N = N₀ × 2ⁿ.

### Classifying bacteria

*Refs: Edexcel B 2.1 iv · OCR B 3.2.1(d) · Eduqas C1.4(a). Level: AS & A (Edexcel B, OCR B); A-level only (Eduqas).*

#### What you need to know

- Bacteria are classified by shape and by their reaction to Gram stain.
- The difference between Gram-positive and Gram-negative cell walls, and why it matters for antibiotic treatment.

#### Explained

**Shapes.**

| Shape | Name | Example |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Spherical | Coccus (cocci); in chains (strepto-), clusters (staphylo-) | *Staphylococcus aureus* |
| Rod | Bacillus (bacilli) | *E. coli*, *Bacillus subtilis* |
| Spiral | Spirillum (spirilla) | *Spirillum* |
| Comma-shaped | Vibrio | *Vibrio cholerae* |

**Gram staining.**

1. Make a smear of bacteria on a slide and heat-fix it.
2. Stain with crystal violet; then add iodine (fixes the stain).
3. Wash with alcohol (decolouriser).
4. Counter-stain with safranin (red).

| | Gram-positive | Gram-negative |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Colour seen | Purple / violet | Red / pink |
| Cell wall | Thick peptidoglycan (murein) layer, which keeps the crystal violet | Thin peptidoglycan layer between two membranes; outer membrane of lipopolysaccharide; alcohol washes out the crystal violet |
| Antibiotics | Sensitive to penicillin and lysozyme (wall exposed) | Outer membrane protects the wall from penicillin and lysozyme; need other antibiotics |

Colony morphology (OCR B): the size, shape, colour, edge and surface texture of colonies on agar also help identify bacteria.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying Gram-negative bacteria have a thicker wall.
- Saying the "Gram-positive" colour is red.

### Culturing and aseptic technique

*Refs: Edexcel A 4.14, CP9, CP15 · Edexcel B 6.1 i to iii, CP13 · OCR A 6.2.1(g) · OCR B 3.2.1(d), 3.2.3(g) · Eduqas C1.4(b), (c). Level: AS & A for Edexcel A 4.14; A-level for the rest.*

#### What you need to know

- The conditions bacteria need to grow.
- Aseptic technique: what it is, why it is needed.
- Culturing in broth and on agar; selective media.
- Streak plating to isolate pure colonies.

#### Explained

**Conditions for growth** (Edexcel A 4.14, Eduqas C1.4(c)).

- Nutrients: a carbon source (glucose), a nitrogen source (amino acids, nitrate), vitamins and minerals.
- Suitable temperature: many pathogens grow best at about 37 °C; school cultures are incubated at no more than 25 °C to avoid encouraging human pathogens.
- Suitable pH (most bacteria near neutral).
- Oxygen: obligate aerobes need it; obligate anaerobes are killed by it; facultative anaerobes grow either way.
- Water.

**Aseptic technique** prevents contamination of the culture by unwanted microorganisms and stops the cultured microorganisms escaping into the environment or infecting the user.

- Wash hands; disinfect the bench before and after.
- Work next to a lit Bunsen burner: the rising convection current carries airborne microbes away from the work.
- Sterilise media and glassware in an autoclave (121 °C under pressure for 15 minutes).
- Flame the wire inoculating loop until red hot; let it cool before touching the culture.
- Flame the neck of culture bottles after removing the lid and before replacing it.
- Lift a Petri dish lid only slightly and for as short a time as possible.
- Secure lids with two pieces of tape (not sealed all round, to keep conditions aerobic and prevent anaerobic pathogens growing).
- Incubate plates upside down (condensation does not drip onto the agar).
- After use, autoclave all plates and cultures before disposal.

**Media.**

- *Broth:* liquid nutrient medium in a flask or tube; used to grow large numbers quickly and to measure growth by turbidity.
- *Agar:* nutrient broth set with agar (a polysaccharide from seaweed) in Petri dishes; single cells grow into visible colonies.
- *Selective media:* contain substances that let only some microorganisms grow (an antibiotic; high salt for *Staphylococcus*; MacConkey agar for Gram-negative gut bacteria). Used to isolate or identify a species, or to select bacteria that have taken up a plasmid (file 25).

**Streak plating** (Edexcel B CP13). Flame a loop, dip it in a mixed culture, and streak it in parallel lines across one section of an agar plate. Flame the loop again, drag it from the end of the first streaks into a fresh section, and streak again; repeat for three or four sections. Each step dilutes the bacteria; in the last section single cells are separated and grow into single colonies, each a pure culture of one species. Pick a single colony with a sterile loop to subculture it.

**Testing antibiotics** (Edexcel A CP15, OCR B 3.2.3(g); also AQA RP6, file 13). Spread a lawn of bacteria on agar; add paper discs soaked in different antibiotics (or a multodisc); incubate; measure the zone of inhibition. A larger clear zone means the bacteria are more sensitive (but the zone also depends on how fast the antibiotic diffuses through agar). **Antimicrobial plants** (Edexcel A CP9): discs soaked in extracts of garlic, mint or other plants, made by grinding in ethanol and letting the ethanol evaporate; a disc soaked in ethanol and dried is the control.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying the Bunsen burner "kills bacteria in the air".
- Sealing the Petri dish all the way round.
- Incubating at 37 °C in a school lab.

### Measuring growth and the growth curve

*Refs: Edexcel B 6.1 iv, v, CP12 · OCR A 6.2.1(h) · Eduqas C1.4(d). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Methods to measure population growth: total and viable counts, serial dilution and plating, haemocytometer counts, turbidity (optical density), dry mass.
- The phases of a bacterial growth curve in a closed culture: lag, log (exponential), stationary and death (decline).
- Use N = N₀ × 2ⁿ and logarithmic scales.

#### Explained

**Total and viable counts.** A *total count* counts all cells, living and dead (haemocytometer, turbidity). A *viable count* counts only living cells that can divide (dilution plating).

**Serial dilution and plating (viable count).**

1. Make a series of ten-fold dilutions: 1 cm³ culture into 9 cm³ sterile water (10⁻¹), then 1 cm³ of that into 9 cm³ (10⁻²), and so on.
2. Spread 0.1 cm³ of several dilutions onto agar plates; incubate.
3. Choose a plate with 30 to 300 separate colonies. Each colony grew from one cell (a colony-forming unit).
4. Number of cells in original culture = number of colonies × dilution factor ÷ volume plated.

**Haemocytometer** (total count; file 49 for blood cells). A slide with a grid of known area and a chamber of known depth (0.1 mm). Count cells in several squares; multiply by the volume factor. Adding trypan blue (or methylene blue) stains dead cells so live and dead can be told apart.

**Turbidity.** As bacteria multiply, the broth becomes cloudy. A colorimeter measures absorbance (optical density); a calibration curve links absorbance to cell number. Fast, but counts living and dead cells.

**Dry mass.** Filter, dry and weigh samples. Accurate for biomass, but slow and destroys the sample.

**The growth curve** in a closed culture (a fixed volume of medium with nothing added or removed).

| Phase | What happens | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Lag | Little increase in number | Cells adjust to new conditions: taking up water, making enzymes for the new nutrients, copying DNA |
| Log (exponential) | Number doubles each generation | Plenty of nutrients and space; little toxic waste |
| Stationary | Number stays roughly constant | Reproduction rate = death rate; nutrients running out; waste (acids, toxins) building up |
| Death (decline) | Number falls | Nutrients exhausted; toxic waste high; death rate exceeds reproduction |

Because numbers rise so quickly, the y-axis often uses a log scale (log₁₀ of cell number). On a log scale the log phase is a straight line.

**N = N₀ × 2ⁿ** (OCR A), where N₀ is the starting number and n is the number of generations (doublings).

#### Worked example

1. Serial dilution: 0.1 cm³ of the 10⁻⁵ dilution produced 64 colonies. How many bacteria per cm³ were in the original culture?

64 ÷ 0.1 = 640 per cm³ in the 10⁻⁵ dilution; × 10⁵ = 6.4 × 10⁷ bacteria per cm³.

2. A culture starts with 500 bacteria and divides every 20 minutes during the log phase. How many after 3 hours?

n = 180 ÷ 20 = 9 generations. N = 500 × 2⁹ = 500 × 512 = 256 000 = 2.56 × 10⁵.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying bacteria "do not grow" in the lag phase. They are active, just not dividing much yet.
- Counting plates with overlapping colonies.
- Forgetting the volume plated when calculating the original concentration.

#### Practical

**Growth of bacteria in liquid culture** (Edexcel B CP12; OCR A PAG7). Inoculate sterile nutrient broth with a known volume of bacteria (or yeast). Incubate at a constant temperature, shaking. Every 30 minutes, take a sample aseptically and measure absorbance with a colorimeter (zeroed with sterile broth), or do dilution plating. Plot absorbance (or log number) against time to show the phases. Vary temperature or glucose concentration to investigate their effect.

**Bacteria in fresh and stale milk** (Eduqas). Use serial dilution and plating, or add resazurin (a redox indicator that changes from blue to pink to colourless as bacteria respire); the faster it changes colour, the more bacteria.

### Check questions

1. Describe how to Gram stain bacteria, and explain why Gram-positive bacteria stay purple. (4 marks)
2. Explain why Gram-negative bacteria are less affected by penicillin. (2 marks)
3. Give three aseptic techniques and explain the purpose of each. (3 marks)
4. Explain why plates are taped but not sealed all round. (2 marks)
5. Describe how streak plating gives a pure culture. (3 marks)
6. 0.1 cm³ of a 10⁻⁶ dilution gave 45 colonies. Calculate the number of bacteria per cm³ in the original culture. (2 marks)
7. Explain the shape of the bacterial growth curve in a closed culture. (5 marks)
8. A bacterium divides every 30 minutes. Starting from 200 cells, how many are there after 4 hours in the log phase? (2 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Smear heat-fixed; crystal violet then iodine (1); wash with alcohol (1); counter-stain with safranin (1); thick peptidoglycan layer keeps crystal violet (1).
2. Outer membrane / lipopolysaccharide layer (1); prevents penicillin reaching the peptidoglycan / murein (1).
3. Any three with reasons: flame loop (sterilises); work near Bunsen (convection current carries microbes away); flame bottle neck (prevents microbes entering); lift lid slightly (reduces airborne contamination); disinfect bench; autoclave media.
4. Allows oxygen in / keeps aerobic (1); prevents growth of anaerobic (pathogenic) bacteria (1).
5. Loop streaked over agar in sections, flamed between (1); each section dilutes bacteria (1); single cells separated and grow into individual colonies of one type (1).
6. 45 ÷ 0.1 = 450 (1); × 10⁶ = 4.5 × 10⁸ per cm³ (1).
7. Lag: adjusting / making enzymes, little division (1); log: doubling each generation, plenty of nutrients (1); stationary: birth rate = death rate (1); nutrients running out / toxins build up (1); death: death rate exceeds reproduction (1).
8. 8 generations (1); 200 × 2⁸ = 51 200 (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/biology/15-photosynthesis.md -->

## Photosynthesis

Photosynthesis traps light energy and stores it in organic molecules. This file covers the light-dependent and light-independent reactions, limiting factors, and AQA required practicals 7 and 8.

### Overview

A-level only. ATP and ATP synthase are introduced in file 03; chemiosmosis is explained alongside respiration in file 16. Energy flow through ecosystems is in file 17. Uses of mineral ions such as magnesium and nitrate (Eduqas C1.2(l)) are in file 41. Crop yields and pest control (OCR B) are in file 42.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.5.1, RP7, RP8. Edexcel A 5.5 to 5.9, CP11. Edexcel B 5.6, 5.7, CP10, CP11. OCR A 5.2.1(a) to (g). OCR B 4.3.1(a) to (f). Eduqas C1.1(b), C1.2(a) to (k).
- AQA calls the products glycerate 3-phosphate (GP) and triose phosphate (TP). Edexcel A and Edexcel B call TP glyceraldehyde 3-phosphate (GALP). Same molecule.
- Edexcel B and Eduqas ask for absorption and action spectra and for photosystems I and II by name. OCR A asks for accessory pigments.
- Edexcel A CP11 and AQA RP8 both use the reduction of a blue dye by chloroplasts (the Hill reaction, DCPIP).
- Edexcel B CP10 asks for the effect of light wavelength on photosynthesis.

### 3.5.1 Photosynthesis

*Refs: AQA 3.5.1, RP7, RP8 · Edexcel A 5.5 to 5.9, CP11 · Edexcel B 5.6 i, ii, 5.7 i to viii, CP10, CP11 · OCR A 5.2.1(a) to (g) · OCR B 4.3.1(a) to (g) · Eduqas C1.1(b), C1.2(a) to (k). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- The light-dependent reaction in such detail as to show that: chlorophyll absorbs light, leading to photoionisation of chlorophyll; some of the energy from electrons released during photoionisation is conserved in the production of ATP and reduced NADP; the production of ATP involves electron transfer associated with the electron transfer chain and passage of protons across chloroplast membranes and is catalysed by ATP synthase embedded in these membranes (chemiosmotic theory); photolysis of water produces protons, electrons and oxygen.
- The light-independent reaction uses reduced NADP from the light-dependent reaction to form a simple sugar. The hydrolysis of ATP, also from the light-dependent reaction, provides the additional energy for this reaction.
- The light-independent reaction in such detail as to show that: carbon dioxide reacts with ribulose bisphosphate (RuBP) to form two molecules of glycerate 3-phosphate (GP), catalysed by the enzyme rubisco; ATP and reduced NADP from the light-dependent reaction are used to reduce GP to triose phosphate; some of the triose phosphate is used to regenerate RuBP in the Calvin cycle; some of the triose phosphate is converted to useful organic substances.
- Identify environmental factors that limit the rate of photosynthesis; evaluate data relating to common agricultural practices used to overcome the effect of these limiting factors.

#### Explained

**Overall equation:** 6CO₂ + 6H₂O → C₆H₁₂O₆ + 6O₂ (using light energy, in chlorophyll).

**The chloroplast.**

- Double membrane (envelope).
- Thylakoids: flattened membrane sacs stacked into grana, joined by intergranal lamellae. Thylakoid membranes hold chlorophyll and other pigments in photosystems, electron carriers and ATP synthase. Site of the light-dependent reaction. Many grana give a large surface area.
- Stroma: fluid around the thylakoids with the enzymes of the Calvin cycle (including rubisco), starch grains, lipid droplets, circular DNA and 70S ribosomes. Site of the light-independent reaction.
- (Eduqas) Chloroplasts are most numerous in palisade cells near the upper surface and can move within the cell towards light. They act as transducers, turning light energy into chemical energy.

**Pigments** (OCR A, Edexcel B, Eduqas). Chlorophyll a and b absorb red and blue-violet light and reflect green. Accessory pigments (carotenoids, xanthophylls) absorb other wavelengths and pass energy to chlorophyll a, widening the range of light used. Pigments are grouped in light-harvesting systems (antenna complexes) around a reaction centre of chlorophyll a. There are two photosystems: PSI (reaction centre absorbs best at 700 nm) and PSII (680 nm).

- *Absorption spectrum:* how much light each pigment absorbs at each wavelength.
- *Action spectrum:* the rate of photosynthesis at each wavelength.

The two match closely, which is evidence that these pigments drive photosynthesis.

**The light-dependent reaction** (thylakoid membranes).

1. Light hits PSII. Energy passes to the chlorophyll in the reaction centre, exciting electrons to a higher energy level so they leave the chlorophyll: **photoionisation**.
2. The electrons pass along an electron transfer chain of carriers in the thylakoid membrane, losing energy at each step.
3. This energy is used to pump protons (H⁺) from the stroma into the thylakoid space, building up a high concentration.
4. Protons diffuse back into the stroma through ATP synthase, which uses the energy to join ADP and Pi: **photophosphorylation**. This is the chemiosmotic theory.
5. The electrons reach PSI, are re-excited by light, and then reduce NADP: NADP + 2H⁺ + 2e⁻ → reduced NADP.
6. PSII must replace its lost electrons. These come from **photolysis** of water: 2H₂O → 4H⁺ + 4e⁻ + O₂. Oxygen is a by-product and diffuses out of the leaf; the protons add to the gradient and are used to reduce NADP.

*Non-cyclic photophosphorylation* uses both photosystems and makes ATP, reduced NADP and oxygen (as above). *Cyclic photophosphorylation* (OCR A, Edexcel B, Eduqas) uses PSI only: excited electrons return to PSI via the electron chain, so ATP is made but no reduced NADP and no oxygen. It tops up the ATP needed for the Calvin cycle.

**The light-independent reaction (Calvin cycle)** (stroma).

1. CO₂ combines with ribulose bisphosphate (RuBP, 5C), catalysed by rubisco. The unstable 6C product immediately splits into two molecules of glycerate 3-phosphate (GP, 3C). This is carbon fixation.
2. GP is reduced to triose phosphate (TP, 3C) using reduced NADP (supplies hydrogen) and ATP (supplies energy).
3. Of every 6 TP made, 5 are used to regenerate 3 RuBP, using more ATP. 1 TP leaves the cycle.

Six turns of the cycle fix 6 CO₂ and make one hexose (from 2 TP).

**Uses of TP** (all boards). Two TP join to make glucose, which can make sucrose, starch and cellulose. TP makes glycerol; GP makes fatty acids; with these, lipids. GP plus nitrogen (from nitrate) makes amino acids. TP also makes nucleotides.

**Limiting factors.** At any moment, the rate is limited by the factor in shortest supply: light intensity, CO₂ concentration or temperature. Increasing a limiting factor raises the rate until another factor becomes limiting.

| Factor | Effect |
| --- | --- |
| Light intensity | Low light: less ATP and reduced NADP made, so GP is not reduced to TP; GP rises, TP and RuBP fall |
| CO₂ concentration | Low CO₂: less RuBP is carboxylated; RuBP rises, GP and TP fall |
| Temperature | The light-independent reaction is controlled by enzymes, so rate rises with temperature to an optimum; above it rubisco and other enzymes denature, and stomata may close to save water |
| Water (OCR A) | Water stress closes stomata, reducing CO₂ uptake |

**Agricultural practice.** Glasshouse growers raise CO₂ (by burning fuel, which also warms the air), use artificial lighting, and heat the glasshouse. The extra cost must be balanced against the extra yield: adding more of a factor that is not limiting just wastes money.

**Respiration and photosynthesis** (OCR A, OCR B). Plants respire all the time. At the light compensation point, the rate of photosynthesis equals the rate of respiration, so there is no net gas exchange and no net gain in biomass. The products of each are the raw materials for the other. (OCR B 4.3.1(g)) For crop production, plants must spend as long as possible above the compensation point: growers extend the hours of light and raise light intensity and CO₂ in glasshouses, but heating at night raises respiration and lowers the net gain, so night temperatures are kept low.

#### Worked example

1. In a Calvin cycle experiment, the light was switched off. Predict and explain the changes in GP and RuBP.

GP rises: it is still being made from RuBP and CO₂, but without ATP and reduced NADP it cannot be reduced to TP. RuBP falls: it is still being used, but it cannot be regenerated from TP without ATP.

2. In chromatography of leaf pigments, a spot moved 4.2 cm; the solvent moved 9.6 cm. Rf = 4.2 ÷ 9.6 = 0.44. Compare with known values to identify the pigment.

3. A plant fixes 12 molecules of CO₂. How many molecules of glucose can it make, and how many turns of the cycle are needed? 12 turns; 12 ÷ 6 = 2 glucose.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying the light-independent reaction "happens in the dark". It happens in light too, and stops soon after dark because ATP and reduced NADP run out.
- Saying oxygen comes from CO₂. It comes from water.
- Saying chlorophyll "absorbs green light".
- Saying ATP is used to reduce GP. Reduced NADP reduces; ATP provides energy.
- Saying rubisco makes GP from TP.
- Saying temperature affects the light-dependent reaction most.

#### Practical

**AQA required practical 7** (also Edexcel B CP11, OCR A 5.2.1(c) and PAG6): use of chromatography to investigate the pigments isolated from leaves of different plants.

1. Grind leaves with a little sand and propanone (or another solvent) to extract pigments.
2. Draw a pencil line (origin) about 1.5 cm from the bottom of the chromatography paper or TLC plate.
3. Spot the extract onto the origin with a capillary tube, letting each spot dry, many times, to get a small, concentrated spot.
4. Stand the paper in a little solvent, with the solvent below the origin. Cover.
5. Remove when the solvent nearly reaches the top; mark the solvent front.
6. Calculate Rf values; compare plants (for example shade and sun leaves, or green and red leaves).

Pencil, not ink (ink would dissolve and run). Solvent below the origin (so spots do not dissolve into the solvent). Risks: propanone is flammable and an irritant; work in a ventilated room, no flames.

**AQA required practical 8** (also Edexcel A CP11): investigation into the effect of a named factor on the rate of dehydrogenase activity in extracts of chloroplasts.

1. Grind leaves in ice-cold, isotonic buffer; filter; centrifuge to get a suspension of chloroplasts. Keep on ice.
2. Add DCPIP (blue) to the chloroplast suspension. DCPIP accepts electrons from the light-dependent reaction in place of NADP and turns colourless.
3. Shine light on the tubes; measure the time for the blue colour to go, or record absorbance with a colorimeter at intervals.
4. Vary one factor: light intensity (distance from lamp), light colour (filters), temperature, or an inhibitor.
5. Controls: a tube kept in the dark (no colour change shows light is needed); a tube with no chloroplasts (no change shows chloroplasts are needed).

Why cold, isotonic and buffered: as in cell fractionation (file 04) — cold slows enzymes that damage chloroplasts; isotonic stops osmosis damaging them; buffer keeps the pH stable.

**Rate of photosynthesis with pondweed** (Edexcel B CP10, OCR A PAG11, OCR B 4.3.1(d)). Count oxygen bubbles or collect gas in a photosynthometer from *Elodea* or *Cabomba* in sodium hydrogencarbonate solution (supplies CO₂). Vary light intensity (light intensity ∝ 1/d²) or use colour filters for wavelength. Keep temperature constant with a water bath or a heat shield.

### Check questions

1. Describe the role of photolysis in the light-dependent reaction. (3 marks)
2. Explain how ATP is made in the light-dependent reaction. (4 marks)
3. Describe the Calvin cycle. (5 marks)
4. Explain what happens to the concentrations of GP and RuBP when the CO₂ concentration falls. (3 marks)
5. Explain how a glasshouse grower could use knowledge of limiting factors to increase profit. (3 marks)
6. Give two differences between cyclic and non-cyclic photophosphorylation. (2 marks)
7. In RP8, explain why a tube with chloroplasts kept in the dark is included. (2 marks)
8. A lamp 20 cm from pondweed is moved to 10 cm. By what factor does light intensity change? (1 mark)

### Mark scheme

1. Water split using light energy into protons, electrons and oxygen (1); electrons replace those lost from chlorophyll / PSII (1); protons used to reduce NADP / add to proton gradient; oxygen released as by-product (1).
2. Light causes photoionisation; electrons pass along electron transfer chain (1); energy used to pump protons into thylakoid space (1); protons diffuse back through ATP synthase (1); ADP + Pi → ATP (1).
3. CO₂ combines with RuBP (1); catalysed by rubisco (1); forms two GP (1); GP reduced to TP using reduced NADP and ATP (1); most TP regenerates RuBP using ATP; some used to make glucose / organic substances (1).
4. Less CO₂ to combine with RuBP (1); RuBP rises, as still regenerated but used less (1); GP falls as less made but still converted to TP (1).
5. Identify limiting factor (e.g. CO₂ in winter) and increase it (1); only increase factor up to point where another becomes limiting (1); compare cost of input with value of extra yield (1).
6. Any two: cyclic uses PSI only; cyclic produces no reduced NADP; cyclic produces no oxygen / no photolysis.
7. Shows DCPIP reduction / colour change needs light (1); so any change in other tubes is due to light-dependent reaction (1).
8. Intensity ∝ 1/d²; halving distance gives 4 times the intensity (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/biology/17-energy-in-ecosystems-and-nutrient-cycles.md -->

## Energy in ecosystems and nutrient cycles

Energy flows through ecosystems and is lost at each step; nutrients are recycled. This file covers productivity, efficiency of energy transfer, ecological pyramids, and the nitrogen, phosphorus and carbon cycles, with fertilisers and eutrophication.

### Overview

A-level only. Populations, succession and sampling are in file 23. Climate change and carbon management (Edexcel A, Eduqas) are in file 42. Decomposition after death (Edexcel A forensics) is in file 43.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.5.3, 3.5.4. Edexcel A 5.10, 5.11, 5.21, 6.2. Edexcel B 10.1 ii, iii, 10.2 i to iii. OCR A 6.3.1(b), (c). OCR B 4.3.1(h) to (k). Eduqas C1.5(f), (h), (k), (l), (n) to (p).
- OCR A, Edexcel A and Eduqas ask for the carbon cycle; AQA does not. Included below.
- AQA alone asks for the phosphorus cycle and mycorrhizae.
- Edexcel B asks for pyramids of numbers, biomass and energy (10.1 iii).
- Eduqas asks about ploughing and drainage (C1.5(o)).

### 3.5.3 Energy and ecosystems

*Refs: AQA 3.5.3 · Edexcel A 5.10, 5.11 · Edexcel B 10.1 ii, iii, 10.2 i, ii · OCR A 6.3.1(b) · OCR B 4.3.1(i) to (k) · Eduqas C1.5(f), (h). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- In any ecosystem, plants synthesise organic compounds from atmospheric, or aquatic, carbon dioxide.
- Most of the sugars synthesised by plants are used by the plant as respiratory substrates. The rest are used to make other groups of biological molecules. These biological molecules form the biomass of the plants.
- Biomass can be measured in terms of mass of carbon or dry mass of tissue per given area.
- The chemical energy store in dry biomass can be estimated using calorimetry.
- Gross primary production (GPP) is the chemical energy store in plant biomass, in a given area or volume. Net primary production (NPP) is the chemical energy store in plant biomass after respiratory losses to the environment have been taken into account: NPP = GPP − R.
- This net primary production is available for plant growth and reproduction. It is also available to other trophic levels in the ecosystem, such as herbivores and decomposers.
- The net production of consumers (N), such as animals, can be calculated as N = I − (F + R), where I represents the chemical energy store in ingested food, F the chemical energy lost to the environment in faeces and urine and R the respiratory losses.
- Primary and secondary productivity is the rate of primary or secondary production, measured as biomass in a given area in a given time, eg kJ ha⁻¹ year⁻¹.
- The ways in which production is affected by farming practices designed to increase the efficiency of energy transfer by simplifying food webs to reduce energy losses to non-human food chains, and by reducing respiratory losses within a human food chain.

#### Explained

**Trophic levels.** Producers (plants, algae) → primary consumers (herbivores) → secondary consumers → tertiary consumers. Decomposers and detritivores feed on dead material from all levels.

**Biomass.** Fresh mass varies with water content, so dry mass is used. Measuring dry mass means killing the organism and drying it in an oven at about 80 °C until the mass stops changing. Usually a small sample is dried and the result scaled up.

**Calorimetry.** A known dry mass is burned in a bomb calorimeter surrounded by a known volume of water. The temperature rise of the water gives the energy released: energy = mass of water × 4.18 J g⁻¹ °C⁻¹ × temperature rise.

**Why only a small fraction of light becomes plant biomass.**

- Most sunlight is reflected, passes through leaves, or is the wrong wavelength.
- Light may not hit a chlorophyll molecule.
- Other factors (CO₂, temperature) limit photosynthesis.
- Energy is lost as heat in the reactions.

Typically 1 to 3% of light energy falling on a plant ends up as GPP.

**Why transfer between consumers is inefficient** (often about 10%, though it varies):

- Not all of the organism is eaten (roots, bones).
- Some is indigestible and lost in faeces.
- Energy is lost in urine (as urea).
- Most energy absorbed is used in respiration and lost as heat, especially by mammals and birds keeping a constant body temperature.

Because so much is lost at each step, food chains rarely have more than four or five levels.

**Ecological pyramids** (Edexcel B).

| Pyramid | Shows | Problems |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Numbers | Number of organisms at each level | Size ignored: one oak supports thousands of insects, so it can be inverted |
| Biomass | Dry mass per area at each level, at one time | Seasonal change; organisms killed to measure; can be inverted in oceans, where phytoplankton reproduce fast but have little standing biomass |
| Energy | Energy passing through each level per area per year | Hard to measure, but always the right way up and allows comparison of ecosystems |

**Farming practices.**

- *Simplifying food webs:* removing other species that would take energy from the crop. Herbicides remove weeds (competitors for light, water, ions); pesticides and fungicides remove pests that eat the crop. More of the NPP goes to the crop.
- *Reducing respiratory losses in livestock:* keep animals indoors in warm conditions (less energy for heat), restrict movement (less energy for muscle contraction), feed high-energy, easily digested food, slaughter young (young animals put more of their energy into growth). More of the food becomes meat. Ethical concerns: animal welfare, antibiotic overuse.

**Ruminants in the human food chain** (OCR B 4.3.1(k)). Cattle and sheep (file 45) can turn grass and other cellulose-rich plants, which humans cannot digest, into meat and milk, so they make food from land unsuitable for crops. But energy transfer from plants to cattle is inefficient (often less than 10%), so more people can be fed per hectare by eating crops directly; ruminants also release methane, a greenhouse gas (file 42).

#### Worked example

1. Grassland GPP = 21 000 kJ m⁻² yr⁻¹; plant respiration = 12 500 kJ m⁻² yr⁻¹. NPP = 21 000 − 12 500 = 8500 kJ m⁻² yr⁻¹.

2. A cow ingests 3000 kJ per day; 1800 kJ is lost in faeces and urine and 1050 kJ in respiration. N = 3000 − (1800 + 1050) = 150 kJ per day. Efficiency of conversion of ingested food to biomass = 150 ÷ 3000 × 100 = 5%.

3. Efficiency of transfer between two trophic levels = (energy in the higher level ÷ energy in the lower level) × 100. If producers have NPP 8500 and primary consumers 900 kJ m⁻² yr⁻¹: 900 ÷ 8500 × 100 = 10.6%.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying energy is "used up" or "lost" as if destroyed. It is transferred to the environment as heat.
- Saying energy is recycled.
- Using fresh mass for biomass.
- Forgetting units for productivity (energy per area per time).

### 3.5.4 Nutrient cycles

*Refs: AQA 3.5.4 · Edexcel A 5.21, 6.2 · Edexcel B 10.2 iii · OCR A 6.3.1(c) · OCR B 4.3.1(h) · Eduqas C1.5(k), (l), (n) to (p). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Nutrients are recycled within natural ecosystems, exemplified by the nitrogen cycle and the phosphorus cycle. Microorganisms play a vital role in recycling chemical elements such as phosphorus and nitrogen.
- The role of saprobionts in decomposition. The role of mycorrhizae in facilitating the uptake of water and inorganic ions by plants. The role of bacteria in the nitrogen cycle in sufficient detail to illustrate the processes of saprobiotic nutrition, ammonification, nitrification, nitrogen fixation and denitrification. (Names of individual species are not required.)
- The main stages of the phosphorus cycle, limited to the roles of: saprobionts in decomposition; mycorrhizae in facilitating uptake of phosphate ions by plants; the movement of phosphate ions into ecosystems from rock, and back into rock in sediments.
- The use of natural and artificial fertilisers to replace the nitrates and phosphates lost by harvesting plants and removing livestock.
- The environmental issues arising from the use of fertilisers including leaching and eutrophication.

#### Explained

**Saprobionts** (saprotrophs: decomposing bacteria and fungi) secrete enzymes onto dead organisms and waste, digest them externally, and absorb the soluble products. This releases inorganic ions such as ammonium and phosphate back into the soil.

**The nitrogen cycle.** Plants need nitrogen for amino acids, proteins, nucleotides and chlorophyll, but cannot use nitrogen gas (N₂).

| Process | What happens | Organisms |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Nitrogen fixation | N₂ → ammonia (NH₃), using the enzyme nitrogenase; needs ATP and anaerobic conditions | Free-living bacteria (*Azotobacter*) and bacteria in root nodules of legumes (*Rhizobium*, which gets sugars from the plant: mutualism). Lightning also fixes some. |
| Ammonification | Nitrogen in proteins, urea and nucleic acids of dead organisms and waste → ammonium ions (NH₄⁺) | Saprobionts |
| Nitrification | NH₄⁺ → nitrite (NO₂⁻) → nitrate (NO₃⁻); oxidation reactions that release energy for the bacteria; needs oxygen | Nitrifying bacteria (*Nitrosomonas*, then *Nitrobacter*) |
| Assimilation | Plants absorb nitrate by active transport and use it to make amino acids | Plants |
| Denitrification | NO₃⁻ → N₂; happens in waterlogged, anaerobic soils | Denitrifying bacteria |

AQA does not require the names of species; the genus names above are given as common examples.

Farmers improve soil nitrogen by ploughing (aerates soil, so nitrification is favoured and denitrification reduced) and draining waterlogged fields (Eduqas C1.5(o)). Ploughing can also increase erosion and the loss of organic matter.

**Studying *Rhizobium*** (OCR B 4.3.1(h)). Cut open the root nodules of a legume (clover, pea): a pink colour shows leghaemoglobin, which binds oxygen so that nitrogenase (which oxygen inactivates) can work. *Rhizobium* can be cultured from a crushed, surface-sterilised nodule on a yeast-mannitol agar plate using aseptic technique (file 28). Legumes are used in crop rotation because their nodules add nitrogen compounds to the soil.

**Mycorrhizae.** Associations between fungi and plant roots. The fungal hyphae spread through the soil, greatly increasing the surface area for absorbing water and ions, especially phosphate. They also store water. The fungus receives sugars from the plant. This is a mutualistic relationship.

**The phosphorus cycle.** Phosphorus is needed for ATP, nucleic acids and phospholipids. It has no gas phase.

1. Phosphate ions are released from rocks by weathering and dissolve in water.
2. Plants absorb phosphate (helped by mycorrhizae); animals get it by eating plants.
3. Saprobionts release phosphate from dead organisms and waste (also guano from seabirds).
4. Phosphate washed into lakes and seas settles into sediments, which over geological time form new rock, later uplifted.

The cycle is slow, so phosphate is often a limiting nutrient.

**The carbon cycle** (OCR A, Edexcel A, Eduqas).

- CO₂ is removed from the air and water by photosynthesis, and dissolves in the oceans (some forming carbonate shells and rocks).
- Carbon passes through food chains as organic compounds.
- CO₂ returns by respiration of plants, animals and decomposers.
- Dead material that does not decompose (waterlogged, acidic or cold conditions) forms peat and, over millions of years, fossil fuels.
- Combustion of fossil fuels and wood releases CO₂.
- Deforestation reduces photosynthesis and, if trees are burned, releases CO₂.

Carbon sinks store carbon for long periods (oceans, forests, peat bogs). Edexcel A 5.21 asks how knowing the carbon cycle helps reduce atmospheric CO₂ (file 42).

**Fertilisers.** Harvesting crops and removing livestock takes nitrates and phosphates away, so they are not returned to the soil by decomposition. Fertilisers replace them.

- *Natural (organic):* manure, compost, sewage sludge. Release ions slowly as they decompose; add organic matter that improves soil structure; cheaper on farms with animals, but the exact ion content is unknown.
- *Artificial (inorganic):* mined or manufactured salts of nitrate, phosphate and potassium. Exact amounts are known; ions immediately available; but soluble, so easily leached.

**Leaching and eutrophication.**

1. Rain washes soluble nitrates (and some phosphates) out of the soil into rivers and lakes (leaching).
2. Nitrate is no longer limiting, so algae grow rapidly, forming an algal bloom on the surface.
3. The bloom blocks light from plants below, which die.
4. Saprobiotic bacteria decompose the dead plants and algae, and their numbers increase.
5. The bacteria respire aerobically and use up the dissolved oxygen.
6. Fish and other aerobic organisms die.

Reduce it by: applying fertiliser in the right amounts at the right time (not before heavy rain); leaving buffer strips by water; using manure; treating sewage.

#### Worked example

A farmer's field loses 120 kg of nitrogen per hectare per year in harvested crops. Ammonium nitrate (NH₄NO₃) is 35% nitrogen by mass. How much fertiliser is needed per hectare to replace the loss?

120 ÷ 0.35 = 343 kg ha⁻¹ (3 s.f.).

#### Common mistakes

- Saying nitrifying bacteria fix nitrogen.
- Saying decomposers release nitrate directly. They release ammonium (ammonification); nitrifying bacteria make nitrate.
- Saying plants absorb nitrogen gas.
- In eutrophication, saying "the algae use up the oxygen" or "fish die because of the algae". The bacteria decomposing dead material use the oxygen.
- Saying phosphorus cycles through the atmosphere.

### Check questions

1. Define net primary production and give the equation that links it to GPP. (2 marks)
2. Give three reasons why less than 10% of the energy in a trophic level is passed on to the next. (3 marks)
3. A sheep ingests 4200 kJ day⁻¹, loses 2500 kJ in faeces and urine and 1500 kJ in respiration. Calculate its net production and the percentage of ingested energy this represents. (2 marks)
4. Explain why keeping pigs indoors in small pens increases the efficiency of meat production. (3 marks)
5. Describe the roles of bacteria in converting protein in a dead leaf into nitrate in the soil. (4 marks)
6. Explain how mycorrhizae benefit plants. (2 marks)
7. Explain how leaching of fertiliser can lead to the death of fish. (5 marks)
8. Explain why ploughing a waterlogged field can increase its nitrate content. (2 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Chemical energy in plant biomass after respiratory losses (1); NPP = GPP − R (1).
2. Any three: not all eaten; some indigestible / lost in faeces; lost in urine / excretion; lost as heat in respiration.
3. N = 4200 − (2500 + 1500) = 200 kJ day⁻¹ (1); 200 ÷ 4200 × 100 = 4.8% (1).
4. Less movement, so less energy used in respiration for muscle contraction (1); warm, so less energy used for heat (1); more energy available for growth / biomass (1).
5. Saprobionts / decomposers digest protein, releasing ammonium (ammonification) (1); nitrifying bacteria (1); oxidise ammonium to nitrite (1); then nitrite to nitrate (1).
6. Increase surface area for absorption (1); of water and ions, especially phosphate (1).
7. Nitrates washed into water (1); rapid algal growth / bloom blocks light (1); plants die (1); saprobionts / bacteria decompose them and increase in number (1); aerobic respiration of bacteria uses oxygen, so fish die (1).
8. Aerates soil / adds oxygen (1); favours nitrifying bacteria / reduces denitrification (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/biology/43-forensic-biology.md -->

## Forensic biology: time of death and decomposition

Forensic scientists estimate when a person or animal died from changes in the body. This file covers the methods used to estimate time of death and the role of microorganisms in decomposition and the carbon cycle.

### Overview

A-level only. Only Edexcel A has this as content. DNA profiling and PCR, used in forensic identification, are in file 25. The carbon cycle is in file 17.

#### Board differences

- Edexcel A 6.1, 6.2 only.

### Determining the time of death

*Refs: Edexcel A 6.1. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- How to determine the time of death of a mammal by examining the extent of decomposition, stage of succession, forensic entomology, body temperature and degree of muscle contraction (rigor mortis).

#### Explained

No single method is precise; investigators combine several, and each is affected by conditions.

**Body temperature (algor mortis).** After death, respiration stops, so no more heat is released and the body cools until it reaches the temperature of its surroundings. A rough rule: about 1 to 1.5 °C per hour for the first several hours (from 37 °C). Temperature is measured in the rectum or liver. Faster cooling with: low surrounding temperature, wind, wet clothes or immersion in water, a thin or small body, no clothing. Slower with: warm conditions, clothing, more body fat. Not useful once the body has reached air temperature (usually after about 24 hours).

**Rigor mortis (muscle stiffening).**

1. After death, respiration stops, so ATP is no longer made aerobically; anaerobic respiration briefly continues, lactate builds up and pH falls.
2. Without ATP, myosin heads cannot detach from actin (file 19); cross-bridges stay locked and muscles become stiff.
3. Rigor starts about 2 to 6 hours after death, in small muscles first (face, neck), spreads to larger muscles, is complete at about 12 hours, and lasts until about 24 to 36 hours.
4. It then wears off as proteins in the muscle are broken down by enzymes (autolysis).

Rigor develops faster at higher temperatures and in people who were very active just before death (less ATP and glycogen in muscle).

**Extent of decomposition.**

1. Hours to days: cells are broken down by their own enzymes (autolysis); the skin may turn greenish (bacteria in the gut digest tissues and produce pigments).
2. Days: bacteria multiply, releasing gases (hydrogen sulfide, methane) that make the body bloat; strong smell; skin blisters.
3. Weeks: soft tissues liquefy and break down (active decay).
4. Months to years: only skeleton, tendons and hair remain.

Decomposition is faster in warm, moist conditions with oxygen and access to insects; slower in cold, dry or waterlogged conditions, or if buried.

**Forensic entomology.** Insects arrive at a body in a predictable order, and their development rate depends on temperature.

- Blowflies arrive within minutes to hours and lay eggs. Eggs hatch into larvae (maggots) that grow through stages, then pupate and emerge as adults.
- From the species present and the stage and length of the oldest larvae, and the temperature records, entomologists estimate how long ago eggs were laid (the minimum time since death).
- Later, other insects arrive (beetles feeding on dried tissue; predatory beetles feeding on maggots).
- Limitations: temperature must be known (development is faster when warm); bodies indoors, wrapped or buried may be colonised later; drugs in the body can change larval growth rates.

**Stage of succession.** The community of organisms on a body changes in a sequence, as on bare rock (file 23): bacteria first, then blowflies, then beetles, then mites and others as the body dries; finally, as the skeleton remains, plants may grow through it. Identifying the stage gives an estimate of time since death, especially for longer periods.

#### Worked example

A body is found in a room at 18 °C with a rectal temperature of 31 °C. Assuming cooling of 1.5 °C per hour from 37 °C, estimate time since death and give two reasons the estimate may be wrong.

Fall = 37 − 31 = 6 °C. Time ≈ 6 ÷ 1.5 = 4 hours. Reasons: body temperature at death may not have been 37 °C (fever, hypothermia); cooling rate depends on clothing, body size and air movement; the room temperature may have changed.

### Decomposition and the carbon cycle

*Refs: Edexcel A 6.2. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- The role of microorganisms in the decomposition of organic matter and the recycling of carbon.

#### Explained

Decomposers (bacteria and fungi, file 17) secrete enzymes onto dead organic material and absorb the products: saprotrophic (saprobiotic) nutrition. They respire the products, releasing carbon dioxide (or methane in anaerobic conditions) into the air, where it can be taken up again by photosynthesis. Without decomposition, carbon would be locked in dead bodies and the CO₂ in the air would run out. Detritivores (earthworms, woodlice, maggots) break material into smaller pieces, increasing the surface area for microorganisms.

Conditions that slow decomposition (cold, dry, waterlogged and anaerobic, acidic) lock carbon away for long periods, as in peat bogs, which over millions of years can form fossil fuels.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying rigor mortis is caused by "lack of oxygen making muscles contract". It is caused by lack of ATP, so cross-bridges cannot break.
- Saying insects arrive "at random".
- Saying decomposers "eat" dead material; they secrete enzymes and digest it externally.

### Check questions

1. Explain why a body cools after death and give two factors that affect the rate of cooling. (3 marks)
2. Explain why rigor mortis develops after death and why it eventually wears off. (4 marks)
3. Describe how forensic entomology can be used to estimate time of death, and one limitation. (4 marks)
4. Explain why succession on a dead body can be used to estimate time of death. (2 marks)
5. Describe the role of microorganisms in recycling carbon from a dead animal. (3 marks)
6. A body found in a cold river had a temperature similar to that of a body found indoors 6 hours after death. Explain why the river body may have been dead for less time. (2 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Respiration stops, so no heat released; heat lost to surroundings (1); any two factors (1 each): surrounding temperature; clothing; body size / fat; wind / water.
2. Respiration stops, no ATP produced (1); myosin heads cannot detach from actin (1); cross-bridges stay locked / muscles stiffen (1); wears off when proteins broken down by enzymes / autolysis (1).
3. Blowflies lay eggs soon after death (1); larvae develop through stages at known rates (1); stage / length of oldest larvae and temperature give time since eggs laid (1); limitation: depends on temperature / body may be wrapped or indoors / drugs affect growth (1).
4. Organisms colonise in a predictable sequence (1); the species present show the stage reached and so the time since death (1).
5. Secrete enzymes and digest dead tissue (saprotrophic nutrition) (1); absorb and respire products (1); release CO₂ into air, used in photosynthesis (1).
6. Water conducts heat away faster than air / cold (1); so body cooled faster, reaching the same temperature sooner (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/biology/25-genome-projects-and-gene-technologies.md -->

## Genome projects and gene technologies

Scientists can read whole genomes, copy and cut DNA, move genes between organisms, and compare people's DNA. This file covers genome sequencing, recombinant DNA technology (in vivo and in vitro cloning), gene therapy, DNA probes and screening, and genetic fingerprinting.

### Overview

A-level only. Monoclonal antibodies and ELISA are in file 07. Cloning of whole organisms is in file 29. Genetic counselling is in file 39.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.8.3, 3.8.4.1 to 3.8.4.3. Edexcel A 6.3, 6.4, 8.16 to 8.18, CP14. Edexcel B 3.1 iv, 7.1, 7.4. OCR A 6.1.3(a) to (h). OCR B 5.1.3(b) to (g). Eduqas C2.7(a) to (h).
- OCR A asks for DNA sequencing methods (Sanger and next-generation), bioinformatics and synthetic biology (6.1.3(a), (b)).
- Edexcel B asks for knockout mice, replica plating and GM soya (7.4 iii to v); Edexcel A for drugs from GMOs and personalised medicine (8.16, 8.17).
- OCR B asks for haplotypes and SNPs (5.1.3(e)).
- Eduqas names the Human Genome Project and the 100,000 Genomes Project (C2.7(a)).
- Gene therapy: AQA, OCR A, OCR B (somatic and germ line) and Eduqas.

### 3.8.3 Using genome projects

*Refs: AQA 3.8.3 · Edexcel A 8.16 · Edexcel B 7.1 i · OCR A 6.1.3(a), (b) · OCR B 5.1.3(e), (f) · Eduqas C2.7(a) to (c), (h). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Sequencing projects have read the genomes of a wide range of organisms, including humans.
- Determining the genome of simpler organisms allows the sequences of the proteins that derive from the genetic code (the proteome) of the organism to be determined. This may have many applications, including the identification of potential antigens for use in vaccine production.
- In more complex organisms, the presence of non-coding DNA and of regulatory genes means that knowledge of the genome cannot easily be translated into the proteome.
- Sequencing methods are continuously updated and have become automated.

#### Explained

**Genome:** all the DNA of an organism. The Human Genome Project (completed 2003) read the human genome of about 3 billion base pairs and found about 20,000 protein-coding genes. The 100,000 Genomes Project (England, completed 2018) sequenced whole genomes of patients with rare diseases and cancers, linking DNA to medical records (Eduqas).

**Simple organisms.** Bacteria have little non-coding DNA and no introns, so the proteome can be predicted directly from the genome. This helps identify antigens on pathogens for vaccines, and find genes for antibiotic resistance.

**Complex organisms.** Introns, non-coding regions, regulatory genes and alternative splicing mean the genome cannot simply be "translated" into the proteome.

**Sequencing methods** (OCR A).

- *Chain termination (Sanger):* DNA is copied using normal nucleotides plus a few modified, fluorescently labelled terminator nucleotides that stop the chain. Fragments of every length are made, separated by size in a capillary gel, and the colour of the last base of each read in order.
- *Next-generation (high-throughput):* millions of fragments are sequenced at the same time on a flow cell; automated, faster and much cheaper. A human genome can now be read in a day.

**Uses** (OCR A 6.1.3(b), Edexcel A 8.16, OCR B 5.1.3(e), Eduqas C2.7(h)).

- *Bioinformatics:* computer software and databases to store and compare sequences.
- *Comparing genomes:* between species to work out evolutionary relationships; between individuals to find alleles linked to disease.
- *Epidemiology:* tracking how pathogens spread and mutate (as during the COVID-19 pandemic).
- *Personalised medicine:* choosing the drug and dose that suits a person's genotype (some people metabolise drugs fast or slowly).
- *SNPs and haplotypes* (OCR B): a single nucleotide polymorphism is a single base that varies between people; a haplotype is a set of SNPs inherited together on one chromosome. SNP profiles can show disease risk and response to drugs.
- *Synthetic biology* (OCR A): designing and building new biological parts and systems, such as bacteria that make drugs.

**Ethics** (Eduqas C2.7(b)). Who owns genetic data; insurers or employers discriminating on genetic risk; consent; finding unexpected results (risk of a disease with no cure); privacy.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying sequencing the genome tells you all the proteins in a complex organism.
- Confusing genome (DNA) with proteome (proteins).

### 3.8.4.1 Recombinant DNA technology

*Refs: AQA 3.8.4.1 · Edexcel A 6.4, 8.17, 8.18 · Edexcel B 7.1 ii, 7.4 i to vi · OCR A 6.1.3(d), (f) to (h) · OCR B 5.1.3(b), (c), (g) · Eduqas C2.7(d) to (g). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Recombinant DNA technology involves the transfer of fragments of DNA from one organism, or species, to another. Since the genetic code is universal, as are transcription and translation mechanisms, the transferred DNA can be translated within cells of the recipient (transgenic) organism.
- Fragments of DNA can be produced by several methods, including: conversion of mRNA to complementary DNA (cDNA), using reverse transcriptase; using restriction enzymes to cut a fragment containing the desired gene from DNA; creating the gene in a gene machine.
- Fragments of DNA can be amplified by in vitro and in vivo techniques.
- The principles of the polymerase chain reaction (PCR) as an in vitro method to amplify DNA fragments.
- The culture of transformed host cells as an in vivo method to amplify DNA fragments: the addition of promoter and terminator regions to the fragments of DNA; the use of restriction endonucleases and ligases to insert fragments of DNA into vectors; transformation of host cells using these vectors; the use of marker genes to detect genetically modified (GM) cells or organisms. (Students will not be required to recall specific marker genes.)
- Interpret information relating to the use of recombinant DNA technology; evaluate the ethical, financial and social issues associated with the use and ownership of recombinant DNA technology in agriculture, in industry and in medicine; balance the humanitarian aspects of recombinant DNA technology with the opposition from environmentalists and anti-globalisation activists; relate recombinant DNA technology to gene therapy.

#### Explained

**Getting the gene.**

1. *Reverse transcriptase:* take mRNA from a cell that makes the protein (it has many copies, and no introns). Reverse transcriptase makes single-stranded cDNA; DNA polymerase makes it double-stranded.
2. *Restriction endonucleases:* enzymes from bacteria that cut DNA at specific recognition sequences (often palindromic, such as GAATTC). Some cut straight across (blunt ends); others make staggered cuts leaving single-stranded **sticky ends**, which can pair with complementary sticky ends.
3. *Gene machine:* the protein's amino acid sequence is used to work out a DNA sequence; a computer designs it and makes it from nucleotides in short overlapping pieces (oligonucleotides) that are joined. No introns; sequences can be adjusted.

**PCR (in vitro).**

1. Mix the DNA with free nucleotides, primers (short single-stranded DNA complementary to the start of each target strand) and a heat-stable DNA polymerase (Taq polymerase), in a buffer.
2. Heat to 95 °C: hydrogen bonds break; strands separate.
3. Cool to about 55 °C: primers anneal (bind) to the ends of the target sequence.
4. Heat to 72 °C: Taq polymerase adds nucleotides from the primers, copying each strand.
5. Repeat. Each cycle doubles the number of copies: after n cycles, 2ⁿ copies.

Advantages: very fast, automated, needs only a tiny sample, no living cells. Limitations: very sensitive to contamination; copies only short fragments; needs the sequence to make primers.

**Cloning in host cells (in vivo).**

1. Add a **promoter** (so RNA polymerase can bind and transcription starts) and a **terminator** (so it stops) to the gene.
2. Cut a **vector** (usually a bacterial plasmid, or a virus) with the same restriction enzyme, giving complementary sticky ends.
3. Mix the gene and the vector; their sticky ends pair; **DNA ligase** joins the sugar-phosphate backbones. This is recombinant DNA.
4. **Transformation:** mix plasmids with host bacteria and calcium ions, then heat-shock (or use electroporation) to make the membrane more permeable, so some bacteria take up the plasmid.
5. Only some bacteria take up the plasmid, and only some plasmids contain the gene. **Marker genes** identify them:
   - An antibiotic resistance gene on the plasmid: grow on agar with that antibiotic; only transformed bacteria survive.
   - The gene inserted into a second marker gene, disrupting it. Example: inserting into a second antibiotic resistance gene (replica plating, Edexcel B 7.4 iii: bacteria that grow on the first antibiotic but not the second have the recombinant plasmid); or into the *lacZ* gene (blue/white screening); or using a fluorescent protein gene (GFP) that glows under UV.
6. Culture the transformed bacteria in a fermenter; they make the protein.

In vivo cloning is more accurate (fewer copying errors), can copy long genes and makes the product as well; but it is slower and needs living cells.

**GM organisms** (Edexcel A 8.17, 8.18, Edexcel B 7.4, OCR A 6.1.3(f), (g), Eduqas C2.7(f)).

- *Microorganisms:* bacteria making human insulin; yeast making vaccines; bacteria making enzymes for industry.
- *Plants:* golden rice (genes for β-carotene, which the body converts to vitamin A); herbicide-resistant soya (Edexcel B 7.4 v: a gene from a soil bacterium makes an enzyme not affected by glyphosate, so farmers can spray weeds without harming the crop; more crop yield, but concern about herbicide-resistant weeds and reduced biodiversity); insect-resistant (Bt) crops. Plants are often transformed with *Agrobacterium tumefaciens*, a bacterium whose Ti plasmid enters plant cells.
- *Animals:* sheep or goats making human proteins (such as antithrombin) in their milk ("pharming"); knockout mice (Edexcel B 7.4 iv), in which a gene is deliberately inactivated to study its function or model a human disease.

**Issues.**

| For | Against |
| --- | --- |
| Higher crop yields, less pesticide; more food in poorer countries | Genes may spread to wild relatives (superweeds) |
| Nutritionally improved foods (golden rice) | Reduced biodiversity from monocultures |
| Cheap, plentiful medicines (insulin) | Large companies own patents; farmers may have to buy seed each year; poorer farmers disadvantaged (anti-globalisation concerns) |
| Disease-resistant crops and animals | Unknown long-term effects on health and ecosystems |
| Animal models for research | Animal welfare in GM and knockout animals |

**Gene therapy.** Inserting a functional allele into a person's cells to treat a genetic disorder.

- Recessive disorders (such as cystic fibrosis): add a working allele.
- Dominant disorders: silence the faulty allele (for example with RNAi or by inserting DNA into it).
- Vectors: modified viruses, liposomes (lipid spheres), or direct injection.
- *Somatic* gene therapy alters body cells only; it is not passed on; must be repeated because cells die.
- *Germ line* gene therapy alters gametes or embryos, so all cells and future offspring carry the change. Currently illegal in humans in the UK because of ethical concerns (unknown effects on future generations; consent; "designer babies").
- Problems: immune response to the vector; the allele may insert into another gene and cause cancer; the effect is short-lived; expensive.

#### Worked example

Starting with one DNA molecule, how many copies are there after 20 PCR cycles? 2²⁰ = 1,048,576 (about a million).

If each cycle takes 4 minutes, how long do 30 cycles take? 30 × 4 = 120 minutes = 2 hours.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying restriction enzymes "join" DNA. Ligase joins; restriction enzymes cut.
- Saying primers are "to start the PCR". They give DNA polymerase a starting point and define which section is copied.
- Saying human DNA polymerase is used in PCR. It would denature at 95 °C.
- Forgetting the promoter and terminator.
- Saying marker genes "make the gene work".

### 3.8.4.2 Differences in DNA between individuals of the same species can be exploited for identification and diagnosis of heritable conditions

*Refs: AQA 3.8.4.2. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- The use of labelled DNA probes and DNA hybridisation to locate specific alleles of genes.
- The use of labelled DNA probes that can be used to screen patients for heritable conditions, drug responses or health risks.
- The use of this information in genetic counselling and personalised medicine.
- Evaluate information relating to screening individuals for genetically determined conditions and drug responses.

#### Explained

A **DNA probe** is a short, single-stranded piece of DNA with a base sequence complementary to part of the allele being looked for. It is labelled with a radioactive isotope (detected by X-ray film) or a fluorescent dye (detected under UV light).

1. The patient's DNA is extracted, cut into fragments and made single-stranded (heated).
2. The probe is added. If the allele is present, the probe binds to it by complementary base pairing: **hybridisation**.
3. Unbound probe is washed away. The label shows whether, and where, the allele is present.

**DNA microarrays** hold thousands of different probes on a slide, so many alleles can be tested at once.

**Uses.**

- Screening for heritable conditions (BRCA1 mutations, cystic fibrosis, Huntington's disease).
- Drug responses: choosing the drug that works for that person and avoiding ones that cause harmful reactions (personalised medicine).
- Health risks: alleles that increase the risk of heart disease or some cancers.
- Genetic counselling: explaining to people what results mean, the risks of passing on an allele, and the options (file 39).

**Evaluating screening.** Benefits: early diagnosis and treatment; informed decisions about having children; personalised drugs reduce side effects and wasted prescriptions. Concerns: anxiety from results, especially for conditions with no treatment; false positives and negatives; pressure on decisions about pregnancy; risk of discrimination; cost.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying probes "attach to the protein".
- Saying a positive result means a person will definitely develop a disease (for risk alleles it shows increased probability).

### 3.8.4.3 Genetic fingerprinting

*Refs: AQA 3.8.4.3 · Edexcel A 6.3, CP14 · Edexcel B 3.1 iv, 7.1 ii · OCR A 6.1.3(c), (e) · OCR B 5.1.3(d) · Eduqas C2.7(d). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- An organism's genome contains many variable number tandem repeats (VNTRs). The probability of two individuals having the same VNTRs is very low.
- The technique of genetic fingerprinting in analysing DNA fragments that have been cloned by PCR, and its use in determining genetic relationships and in determining the genetic variability within a population.
- The use of genetic fingerprinting in the fields of forensic science, medical diagnosis, animal and plant breeding.

#### Explained

**VNTRs** (also called short tandem repeats or minisatellites) are short base sequences in non-coding DNA repeated many times in a row. The number of repeats at each locus varies between people. Everyone inherits one set of repeats from each parent.

**Making a genetic fingerprint (DNA profile).**

1. Extract DNA from the sample (blood, hair root, saliva).
2. Amplify the VNTR regions by PCR.
3. Cut with restriction enzymes that cut just outside the repeats, so fragment length depends on the number of repeats. (In modern profiling, PCR primers flank the repeat, so cutting is not needed.)
4. **Gel electrophoresis:** load the fragments into wells in an agarose gel; apply an electric current. DNA is negatively charged (phosphate groups), so it moves towards the positive electrode. Smaller fragments move faster and further. A DNA ladder of known fragment sizes runs alongside.
5. Make the DNA visible: transfer to a nylon membrane and add labelled probes complementary to the VNTRs; or use fluorescent tags added during PCR; then view under UV or with X-ray film.
6. The pattern of bands is the genetic fingerprint. Compare patterns.

**Uses.**

- *Forensics:* matching DNA at a crime scene to suspects; excluding the innocent.
- *Paternity and relationships:* every band in a child must come from either the mother or the father.
- *Medical diagnosis:* identifying alleles linked to disease (for example, the number of repeats in the Huntington's gene).
- *Breeding:* checking pedigrees; avoiding inbreeding by choosing unrelated animals or plants.
- *Genetic variability:* comparing profiles across a population (more varied bands mean more genetic diversity; Eduqas C2.1(l)).
- *Classification* (Edexcel B 3.1 iv): comparing species.

#### Worked example

A child's DNA profile has six bands. The mother's profile shares three of them. Of two possible fathers, man X shares two of the remaining three bands, and man Y shares all three. Which man is more likely to be the father? Man Y: every band not from the mother must come from the biological father.

On a gel, a ladder band of 500 base pairs moved 30 mm and one of 1000 bp moved 18 mm. A sample band moved 24 mm. Estimate its size. It lies between: roughly 700 bp (fragment size is not linear with distance; a plot of log size against distance is used for accuracy).

#### Common mistakes

- Saying genetic fingerprinting uses genes. It uses non-coding VNTRs.
- Saying DNA moves to the negative electrode.
- Saying larger fragments move further.
- Saying identical twins have different fingerprints (they have the same).

#### Practical

**Gel electrophoresis** (Edexcel A CP14; OCR A 6.1.3(e) and PAG6).

1. Pour molten agarose into a casting tray with a comb to form wells; let it set; place in the tank and cover with buffer.
2. Load DNA samples (pre-cut with restriction enzymes, mixed with loading dye) into the wells with a micropipette, with a DNA ladder in one well. Wells at the negative (cathode) end.
3. Run at a set voltage until the dye has moved most of the way.
4. Stain (for example with a safe DNA stain or methylene blue) and view.

Control: run a known DNA ladder; keep voltage and time the same. Safety: electrical hazard; some stains are toxic.

### Check questions

1. Explain why the proteome of a bacterium is easier to determine from its genome than the proteome of a human. (3 marks)
2. Describe how reverse transcriptase is used to produce a gene. Give one advantage of this method. (3 marks)
3. Describe the polymerase chain reaction. (5 marks)
4. Describe how a gene is inserted into a plasmid vector and how transformed bacteria are identified. (5 marks)
5. Give two arguments for and two against growing genetically modified crops. (4 marks)
6. Explain the difference between somatic and germ line gene therapy. (2 marks)
7. Describe how a labelled DNA probe can show whether a person has an allele. (4 marks)
8. Explain why genetic fingerprints of two people are very unlikely to be the same, and how gel electrophoresis separates the fragments. (4 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Bacteria have little non-coding DNA / no introns (1); humans have introns / non-coding DNA / regulatory genes (1); alternative splicing in humans; so cannot easily predict proteins (1).
2. mRNA from cells making the protein (1); reverse transcriptase makes cDNA; DNA polymerase makes double strand (1); advantage: no introns / lots of mRNA available (1).
3. DNA mixed with primers, nucleotides and Taq polymerase (1); 95 °C separates strands (1); cooled to about 55 °C, primers anneal (1); 72 °C, Taq / DNA polymerase joins nucleotides (1); cycle repeated, doubling DNA each time (1).
4. Same restriction enzyme cuts gene and plasmid (1); complementary sticky ends (1); DNA ligase joins (1); plasmid taken up by bacteria (transformation) (1); marker gene (antibiotic resistance / fluorescence) used to select transformed bacteria (1).
5. For (2): higher yield; less pesticide; improved nutrition; drought / pest resistance. Against (2): gene spread to wild species; reduced biodiversity; patents / cost to farmers; unknown long-term effects.
6. Somatic: body cells, not inherited, must be repeated (1); germ line: gametes / embryo, inherited by offspring (1).
7. Probe is short single-stranded DNA complementary to allele (1); labelled with fluorescent / radioactive marker (1); DNA made single-stranded and probe added; binds / hybridises if allele present (1); unbound probe washed off; label detected (1).
8. VNTRs vary in number of repeats (1); many loci, so probability of all matching is very low (1); DNA negatively charged, moves to positive electrode (1); smaller fragments move further (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/biology/27-pathogens-disease-and-antibiotics.md -->

## Pathogens, communicable disease, plant defences and antibiotics

Pathogens are organisms that cause disease. This file covers the kinds of pathogen and how they cause harm, how diseases spread in animals and plants, named diseases, epidemiology, how plants defend themselves, sources of medicines, and antibiotics and resistance. AQA does not have this section; it covers the immune response only (file 07).

### Overview

The immune response is in file 07. Culturing bacteria, Gram staining and practicals with antibiotics are in file 28. HIV structure and replication are in file 07; here HIV is treated as a disease.

#### Board differences

- Edexcel A 6.6, 6.7 (part), 6.11, 6.13 to 6.15. Edexcel B 2.2 i to v, 3.2 iv, 6.2 to 6.6. OCR A 4.1.1(a) to (d), (m), (n), 4.2.2(i). OCR B 3.2.1(a) to (c), (e) to (i), 3.2.2(f), 3.2.3(c) to (f). Eduqas OA.1(a) to (d), OA.2(a) to (c), OA.3(a), (i).
- Named diseases differ by board:
  - OCR A: tuberculosis, bacterial meningitis, ring rot; HIV/AIDS, influenza, tobacco mosaic virus; black sigatoka, ringworm, athlete's foot; potato late blight, malaria.
  - OCR B: TB and HIV/AIDS in detail, with epidemiology (incidence, prevalence, notifiable diseases).
  - Edexcel A: TB and HIV, hospital-acquired infections.
  - Edexcel B: TB as a bacterial pathogen; stem rust of wheat, influenza and malaria; the lytic cycle and latency of viruses; endemic malaria control.
  - Eduqas Option A: cholera, tuberculosis, smallpox, influenza, malaria; penicillin and tetracycline.
- Plant defences are OCR A only (4.1.1(c)).

### Pathogens and how they cause disease

*Refs: OCR A 4.1.1(a), (b) · OCR B 3.2.1(a), (b) · Edexcel A 6.6, 6.11 · Edexcel B 2.2 i to v, 6.2, 6.5 · Eduqas OA.1(a), (b), (d). Level: AS & A (OCR A, OCR B); A-level only (Edexcel, Eduqas).*

#### What you need to know

- The types of pathogen: bacteria, viruses, protoctista and fungi; how each causes disease.
- How pathogens are transmitted between animals and between plants, and the factors that affect transmission.
- The main features of named diseases.
- (Edexcel B) The lytic cycle of a virus and latency; antivirals; ethics of using untested drugs in an epidemic.
- (Eduqas) Terms: pathogenic, infectious, carrier, disease, epidemic, pandemic, endemic, vector, reservoir.

#### Explained

**Terms** (Eduqas, OCR B).

- *Pathogen:* a microorganism that causes disease. *Infectious (communicable) disease:* caused by a pathogen and passed between organisms.
- *Carrier:* an infected individual who shows no symptoms but can pass on the pathogen.
- *Vector:* an organism that carries a pathogen from one host to another (the *Anopheles* mosquito for malaria).
- *Reservoir:* where the pathogen lives and multiplies outside the main host (cattle for some TB).
- *Endemic:* always present at a low level in a population (malaria in parts of Africa; chickenpox in the UK). *Epidemic:* a sudden rise in cases in one area (SARS in China, 2002). *Pandemic:* an epidemic across many countries (H1N1 influenza in 2009).

**How pathogens cause harm.**

- *Bacteria:* damage host tissues and release toxins. Exotoxins are proteins secreted by living bacteria (cholera toxin makes gut cells lose chloride ions and water, causing severe diarrhoea). Endotoxins are lipopolysaccharides in the outer membrane of Gram-negative bacteria, released when they die, causing fever and inflammation (Edexcel B 6.2 ii).
- *Viruses:* invade cells, take over the cell's metabolism to make new viruses, then burst the cell.
- *Fungi:* grow hyphae through tissue and secrete enzymes that digest it (ringworm; stem rust digests wheat leaf tissue).
- *Protoctista:* enter and destroy cells (*Plasmodium* in red blood cells and liver cells).

**The lytic cycle and latency** (Edexcel B 2.2 ii). In the lytic cycle a virus attaches to a host cell, injects its nucleic acid, the cell makes new viral nucleic acid and proteins, these assemble, and the cell bursts (lysis), releasing many viruses. In latency (the lysogenic pathway), the viral DNA is inserted into the host's DNA and copied each time the cell divides, without making new viruses. Later, a trigger (stress, UV, a weakened immune system) can start the lytic cycle. HIV and herpes viruses show latency.

**Antivirals** (Edexcel B 2.2 iii). Viruses have no metabolism of their own, so antivirals target viral processes: blocking attachment proteins or receptors; inhibiting viral enzymes such as reverse transcriptase or protease. Antiviral drugs are fewer than antibiotics and viruses often become resistant.

**Untested drugs in epidemics** (Edexcel B 2.2 iv, v). Because viral infections are hard to treat once established, control relies on prevention (vaccines, hygiene, isolation). In an emergency (Ebola in West Africa, 2014), using drugs not yet fully tested raises ethical questions: who receives them; informed consent; risk of harm against risk of death; whether a control group can be denied the drug.

**Transmission.**

| Route | Animals | Plants |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Direct contact | Skin contact, sex (HIV), body fluids, bites | Plant-to-plant contact |
| Droplets / air | Coughs and sneezes (TB, influenza) | Spores in air (black sigatoka, stem rust) |
| Food and water | Contaminated water (cholera) | Contaminated soil (ring rot bacteria stay in soil) |
| Vectors | Mosquitoes (malaria) | Insects (aphids carry viruses) |
| Mother to child | Across the placenta, in breast milk (HIV) | In seeds (some viruses) |

Factors that increase transmission: overcrowding; poor ventilation; poor sanitation and lack of clean water; malnutrition and other diseases weakening immunity; climate (warm, wet conditions help vectors and fungi); for plants, monocultures and planting crops close together.

**Named diseases.**

| Disease | Pathogen | Host and effects | Transmission | Prevention and treatment |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Tuberculosis (TB) | Bacterium (*Mycobacterium tuberculosis*) | Humans (also cattle); infects lungs; primary infection may stay dormant in a tubercle; secondary TB (when immunity falls) destroys lung tissue, coughing blood, weight loss | Droplets | BCG vaccine; long course of several antibiotics (6 months or more); contact tracing |
| Cholera (Eduqas) | Bacterium (*Vibrio cholerae*) | Humans; small intestine; exotoxin causes severe watery diarrhoea and dehydration | Contaminated water and food | Clean water, sewage treatment; oral rehydration therapy; vaccine |
| Bacterial meningitis (OCR A) | Bacterium | Humans; membranes of brain and spinal cord; can cause septicaemia | Droplets, close contact | Vaccines; antibiotics |
| Ring rot (OCR A) | Bacterium | Potatoes, tomatoes; destroys vascular tissue | Contaminated soil, equipment, seed potatoes | Certified disease-free seed; no cure |
| HIV/AIDS | Virus (retrovirus) | Humans; destroys helper T cells, leading to AIDS and opportunistic infections | Sex, blood, needles, mother to child | Condoms, screening blood, clean needles; antiretroviral drugs (no cure, no vaccine) |
| Influenza | Virus | Humans (and birds, pigs); infects ciliated epithelium of airways; secondary infections | Droplets | Annual vaccine (antigenic variation); antivirals |
| Smallpox (Eduqas) | Virus | Humans; skin and internal organs; high mortality | Droplets, contact | Eradicated in 1980 by worldwide vaccination and isolation |
| Tobacco mosaic virus (OCR A) | Virus | Tobacco, tomatoes; mottled leaves, less photosynthesis, stunted growth | Contact, contaminated tools | Resistant varieties; hygiene |
| Black sigatoka (OCR A) | Fungus | Bananas; leaf spots, less photosynthesis | Spores in wind and water | Fungicides; resistant varieties |
| Stem rust (Edexcel B) | Fungus (*Puccinia graminis*) | Wheat; rusty pustules on stems and leaves; takes nutrients; plants weakened | Spores in wind | Resistant varieties; fungicides |
| Ringworm (OCR A) | Fungus | Cattle (and humans); circular grey patches on skin | Contact | Antifungal creams |
| Athlete's foot (OCR A) | Fungus | Humans; cracked skin between toes | Contact; damp floors | Antifungal creams |
| Potato late blight (OCR A) | Protoctist (*Phytophthora infestans*) | Potatoes, tomatoes; hyphae destroy leaves and tubers | Spores in water and air | Resistant varieties; fungicides; destroying infected plants |
| Malaria | Protoctist (*Plasmodium*) | Humans; infects liver and red blood cells; fever, anaemia, death | Female *Anopheles* mosquito vector | Nets, insecticides, draining water; antimalarial drugs; vaccines now in use in some countries |
