**HIV and TB together** (Edexcel A 6.6, OCR B 3.2.1(b)). TB is a common opportunistic infection in people with AIDS, because without helper T cells the dormant TB in tubercles is no longer contained. *TB in detail:* the bacteria are taken up by macrophages in the lungs but survive inside them; the immune system walls them off in a tubercle (granuloma); later, if immunity weakens, the bacteria multiply, destroy lung tissue and spread.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying viruses are "bacteria" or that antibiotics treat influenza.
- Saying malaria is caused by mosquitoes. The mosquito is the vector; *Plasmodium* is the pathogen.
- Saying endemic means "very many cases".

### Epidemiology and controlling disease

*Refs: OCR B 3.2.1(e) to (i), 3.2.2(f), 3.2.3(c), (d) · Edexcel A 6.13, 6.15 · Edexcel B 3.2 iv, 6.4 ii, 6.6 · OCR A 4.2.2(i) · Eduqas OA.3(i). Level: AS & A (OCR B); A-level only (Edexcel, Eduqas).*

#### What you need to know

- How incidence and prevalence of a disease change over time; calculating incidence, prevalence and mortality rates.
- Interpreting epidemiological data; the importance of notifiable diseases.
- Testing for TB and HIV (OCR B).
- The problems of developing vaccines and controlling endemic disease.
- The "evolutionary race" between pathogens and hosts.
- Hospital-acquired infections and their control.

#### Explained

**Rates** (OCR B).

- *Incidence rate:* new cases in a period ÷ population at risk (often × 100 000).
- *Prevalence rate:* all existing cases at a time ÷ total population.
- *Mortality rate:* deaths from the disease in a period ÷ population.

A disease with a long duration (HIV with treatment) can have low incidence but high prevalence.

**Notifiable diseases** (OCR B): doctors must report cases of certain diseases (TB, measles, meningitis) to public health authorities, so outbreaks are spotted early and contacts traced.

**Testing** (OCR B 3.2.2(f)). TB: the Mantoux skin test (a small amount of TB protein is injected under the skin; a raised, hard area after 2 to 3 days suggests past exposure); interferon-gamma blood tests; chest X-rays; culturing sputum. HIV: blood tests for HIV antibodies (ELISA, file 07) and for viral RNA or antigen; rapid tests.

**The evolutionary race** (Edexcel A 6.13, Edexcel B 3.2 iv). Hosts evolve defences; pathogens evolve ways to evade them, and so on. Examples: HIV kills helper T cells, mutates rapidly so its antigens keep changing, and hides inside cells in latency. TB bacteria survive inside macrophages by stopping lysosomes fusing with phagosomes. *Plasmodium* changes its surface antigens and hides inside red blood cells. In the other direction, the sickle cell allele has been selected in regions with malaria.

**Problems with vaccines** (OCR B 3.2.3(c)). Antigenic variability (HIV, influenza); pathogens that hide inside cells (TB, HIV, malaria); many strains; parasites with complex life cycles and several stages (malaria); storage needing cold chains; cost and distribution in poorer countries; public refusal.

**Controlling endemic malaria** (Edexcel B 6.6). Methods: insecticide-treated bed nets; spraying insecticide indoors; draining standing water; antimalarial drugs; vaccines (RTS,S and R21, now being rolled out). Each has social, economic and ethical implications: cost and who pays; resistance of mosquitoes to insecticides and of *Plasmodium* to drugs; effects of insecticides such as DDT on other wildlife; the scientific community validating each method in trials before use.

**Hospital-acquired infections** (Edexcel A 6.15, Edexcel B 6.4 ii). Infections such as MRSA (methicillin-resistant *Staphylococcus aureus*) and *Clostridioides difficile* spread in hospitals because patients are already ill, have wounds or catheters, are close together, and many receive antibiotics, selecting for resistant bacteria. Control: handwashing and alcohol gel; isolating infected patients; screening patients on admission; cleaning; limiting antibiotic use and choosing narrow-spectrum ones; codes of practice for staff.

### Plant defences

*Refs: OCR A 4.1.1(c). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Plant defences against pathogens, including physical and chemical defences, and the production of callose.

#### Explained

*Passive defences* (always present): the waxy cuticle; cellulose cell walls; bark with lignin and suberin; stomata that can close; chemicals in tissues (tannins, alkaloids).

*Active defences* (after infection is detected, when receptors on plant cells recognise molecules from the pathogen):

- **Callose**, a polysaccharide (β-1,3 glucan), is laid down between the cell wall and membrane near the infection, blocking the pathogen's spread. Callose also blocks sieve plates in phloem and plasmodesmata, sealing off infected tissue.
- Cell walls are thickened with lignin and cellulose.
- Antimicrobial chemicals are made: phenols, terpenes, defensins, saponins; and chitinases that break down fungal cell walls.
- Hydrogen peroxide and other reactive oxygen species kill pathogens.
- Infected cells die by a hypersensitive response, depriving the pathogen.
- Necrosis and canker: deliberately killing tissue around the infection.

### Antibiotics and medicines

*Refs: OCR A 4.1.1(m), (n), 4.2.2(i) · OCR B 3.2.3(e), (f) · Edexcel A 6.14 · Edexcel B 6.3, 6.4 i · Eduqas OA.2(a) to (c). Level: AS & A (OCR); A-level only (Edexcel, Eduqas).*

#### What you need to know

- Possible sources of medicines (plants, microorganisms); the importance of biodiversity for finding new ones; personalised medicine and synthetic biology (OCR A).
- Bactericidal and bacteriostatic antibiotics and how they work, with named examples.
- The benefits and risks of using antibiotics; the development of resistance and how to limit it.

#### Explained

**Sources of medicines** (OCR A 4.1.1(m)). Many drugs come from living things: penicillin from the fungus *Penicillium*; aspirin from a compound in willow bark; morphine from opium poppies; digoxin from foxgloves; taxol (an anti-cancer drug) from Pacific yew bark. Most species have never been tested, so losing biodiversity may lose future medicines. Today, drugs are also found by screening thousands of chemicals, designed using protein structures, and made by GM microorganisms (file 25).

**Antibiotics** are chemicals that kill or inhibit bacteria without harming human cells, because they target bacterial structures and enzymes that human cells lack or have in a different form.

- *Bactericidal:* kill bacteria. Penicillin inhibits the enzymes that form cross-links in the murein (peptidoglycan) cell wall. As the bacterium grows, its wall is weak; water enters by osmosis and the cell bursts (osmotic lysis). It works on growing, mainly Gram-positive bacteria.
- *Bacteriostatic:* stop bacteria growing and dividing; the immune system then removes them. Tetracycline binds to the 30S subunit of bacterial (70S) ribosomes, stopping tRNA binding, so protein synthesis stops. It does not affect 80S human ribosomes.

**Resistance.**

1. A random mutation gives a bacterium resistance (such as an enzyme, penicillinase, that breaks down penicillin; or a change to the target).
2. In the presence of the antibiotic, non-resistant bacteria die; resistant ones survive and reproduce (natural selection, file 13).
3. Bacteria reproduce rapidly, so the resistant population grows quickly.
4. Resistance genes on plasmids spread to other bacteria, even other species, by horizontal gene transfer (conjugation).

**Reducing resistance.** Prescribe antibiotics only when needed (not for viral infections); finish the course as prescribed; use narrow-spectrum antibiotics targeted at the known bacterium; rotate antibiotics; reduce antibiotic use in farming; improve hospital hygiene; develop new antibiotics and alternatives (bacteriophages).

**Benefits and risks** (OCR A 4.1.1(n)). Antibiotics have made infections that used to be fatal, and surgery, much safer. Overuse risks resistant strains (MRSA, multi-drug-resistant TB), side effects and harm to the normal gut bacteria (allowing *C. difficile* to grow).

#### Worked example

A town of 40 000 people had 120 existing cases of a disease on 1 January, with 60 new cases during the year and 6 deaths from it.

- Incidence = 60 ÷ 40 000 × 100 000 = 150 per 100 000 per year.
- Prevalence on 1 January = 120 ÷ 40 000 × 100 000 = 300 per 100 000.
- Mortality = 6 ÷ 40 000 × 100 000 = 15 per 100 000 per year.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying bacteria become resistant because they are "exposed" or "get used to" antibiotics.
- Saying penicillin "breaks down the cell wall". It prevents cross-links forming as the wall is built.
- Saying antibiotics act on human cells' ribosomes.

### Check questions

1. Describe how each of the following causes disease: a bacterium, a virus, a fungus. (3 marks)
2. Explain why TB is more common in people with HIV. (3 marks)
3. Explain the role of callose in plant defence. (2 marks)
4. Explain how penicillin kills bacteria, and why it does not harm human cells. (3 marks)
5. Explain the difference between bactericidal and bacteriostatic antibiotics, giving an example of each. (3 marks)
6. Explain why it has been difficult to produce a vaccine against malaria. (3 marks)
7. Suggest three ways hospitals can reduce the spread of MRSA. (3 marks)
8. Distinguish between the incidence and the prevalence of a disease. (2 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Bacterium releases toxins / damages tissue (1); virus takes over cell metabolism / bursts cell when new viruses released (1); fungus secretes enzymes that digest tissue / hyphae grow through tissue (1).
2. HIV destroys helper T cells (1); immune system cannot keep TB contained in tubercles / cannot activate macrophages / B and T cells (1); dormant TB becomes active / new infection not controlled (1).
3. Polysaccharide deposited between cell wall and membrane / in plasmodesmata / sieve plates (1); blocks spread of pathogen to other cells (1).
4. Inhibits enzymes forming cross-links in murein / peptidoglycan cell wall (1); wall weak, water enters by osmosis, cell bursts (1); human cells have no murein cell wall (1).
5. Bactericidal kills bacteria, e.g. penicillin (1); bacteriostatic prevents growth / reproduction, e.g. tetracycline (1); immune system then destroys them (1).
6. Any three: *Plasmodium* is a protoctist with several life cycle stages, each with different antigens; antigens change; hides inside red blood cells / liver cells; many strains; cost / distribution.
7. Any three: handwashing / alcohol gel; isolate infected patients; screen patients on admission; cleaning; reduce antibiotic use / prescribe narrow-spectrum.
8. Incidence: new cases in a set period (1); prevalence: total number of existing cases at a given time (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/biology/07-immune-system.md -->

## Cell recognition and the immune system

The immune system tells "self" from "non-self" and destroys pathogens. This file covers antigens, phagocytes, T and B lymphocytes, antibodies, vaccination, monoclonal antibodies, and HIV.

### Overview

AQA AS content. Pathogens, disease transmission, plant defences and antibiotics are in file 27.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.2.4. Edexcel A 6.7 to 6.9, 6.12. Edexcel B 6.7 i to vii. OCR A 4.1.1(d) to (l). OCR B 3.2.2(a) to (e), (g), (h), 3.2.3(a), (b), (d), 4.2.1(h). Eduqas OA.3(a) to (j) (Option A only).
- OCR A asks for blood smears, opsonins, agglutinins, anti-toxins and autoimmune diseases (4.1.1(e), (i), (k)). OCR B asks for allergies (3.2.2(h)). OCR B and Edexcel A use the terms cell-mediated and humoral; AQA uses them too.
- AQA alone asks for ELISA and HIV structure in this section; Edexcel A, OCR B and Eduqas cover HIV under disease (file 27).
- Edexcel A names T helper, T killer and T memory cells, and B effector (plasma) and B memory cells.
- Eduqas asks about the ethics and effectiveness of vaccination programmes (OA.3(i), (j)); OCR B asks about vaccine ethics and herd immunity.

### 3.2.4 Cell recognition and the immune system

*Refs: AQA 3.2.4 · Edexcel A 6.7, 6.8, 6.9, 6.12 · Edexcel B 6.7 i to vii · OCR A 4.1.1(d) to (l) · OCR B 3.2.2(a) to (e), (g), (h), 3.2.3(a), (b), (d), 4.2.1(h) · Eduqas OA.3(a) to (j). Level: AS & A (AQA, OCR A, OCR B); A-level only (Edexcel A, Edexcel B, Eduqas).*

#### What you need to know

- Each type of cell has specific molecules on its surface that identify it, including proteins that let the immune system identify pathogens, cells from other organisms of the same species, abnormal body cells and toxins.
- Definition of antigen; antigenic variability.
- Phagocytosis of pathogens; the subsequent destruction of ingested pathogens by lysozymes.
- The response of T lymphocytes to a foreign antigen (the cellular response): the role of antigen-presenting cells; helper T cells (TH) stimulating cytotoxic T cells (TC), B cells and phagocytes.
- The response of B lymphocytes (the humoral response): clonal selection; plasma cells releasing monoclonal antibodies; memory cells.
- The structure of an antibody; the formation of an antigen–antibody complex leading to destruction of the antigen, limited to agglutination and phagocytosis of bacterial cells.
- Primary and secondary immune responses.
- Active and passive immunity; vaccination; herd immunity; ethics of vaccines.
- Monoclonal antibodies: targeting medication to specific cell types; medical diagnosis; ELISA; ethics of their use.
- The structure of HIV and its replication in helper T cells; how HIV causes AIDS; why antibiotics do not work on viruses.

#### Explained

**Antigens.** An antigen is a molecule, usually a protein or glycoprotein on a cell surface, that triggers an immune response. The immune system learns to ignore the body's own antigens (self) and responds to others (non-self). It responds to:

- pathogens (bacteria, viruses, fungi)
- cells from other individuals of the same species (transplant rejection)
- abnormal body cells (cancer cells, virus-infected cells displaying viral antigens)
- toxins.

**Non-specific defences first** (OCR A, OCR B, Edexcel A, Eduqas). Skin (keratin, a physical barrier with acidic oils); mucous membranes and cilia trapping and moving pathogens; stomach acid; lysozyme in tears; blood clotting sealing wounds; inflammation (histamine from mast cells widens vessels and makes them leaky, so more phagocytes and fluid reach the site); fever (higher temperature slows pathogen reproduction and speeds the immune response).

**Phagocytosis.**

1. Chemicals released by pathogens attract the phagocyte (chemotaxis).
2. Receptors on the phagocyte bind to the pathogen's antigens (helped when antibodies or opsonins coat the pathogen).
3. The phagocyte engulfs the pathogen, enclosing it in a vesicle called a phagosome.
4. Lysosomes fuse with the phagosome and release lysozymes, which hydrolyse the pathogen.
5. The phagocyte displays the pathogen's antigens on its surface. It is now an antigen-presenting cell.

Neutrophils are short-lived, lobed-nucleus phagocytes that arrive first. Macrophages are larger, live longer, and are the main antigen-presenting cells.

**The cellular (cell-mediated) response.** T lymphocytes have receptors that bind one specific antigen when it is presented on another cell.

1. A helper T cell with the complementary receptor binds to the antigen on an antigen-presenting cell.
2. The helper T cell is activated and divides by mitosis to make a clone.
3. Clones of helper T cells: release cytokines that stimulate cytotoxic T cells to divide; stimulate B cells to divide and make antibodies; stimulate phagocytes to engulf pathogens; some become memory T cells.
4. Cytotoxic (killer) T cells bind to infected body cells showing the antigen and release perforin, a protein that makes holes in the cell membrane, so the cell dies.

**The humoral response.** B lymphocytes each have antibody molecules on their surface that bind one specific antigen.

1. A B cell's antibody binds to its complementary antigen; the B cell takes the antigen in and presents it.
2. A helper T cell binds to the presented antigen and activates the B cell.
3. The B cell divides by mitosis (clonal selection and expansion).
4. Most daughter cells become plasma cells, which secrete large amounts of one antibody (monoclonal). Some become memory B cells.

Plasma cells live only a few days; memory cells last for decades.

**Antibody structure.** Four polypeptide chains (two heavy, two light) held by disulfide bridges in a Y shape. Each tip has a variable region whose shape is complementary to one antigen: the antigen-binding site. The rest is the constant region, the same for all antibodies of one class, which binds to receptors on phagocytes. A hinge region lets the arms move to bind two antigens at once.

**What antibodies do.**

- Agglutination: each antibody binds two pathogens, clumping them together so phagocytes find and engulf them more easily.
- Marking for phagocytosis: antibodies bound to a pathogen act as markers that phagocytes recognise. (OCR A: antibodies that do this are opsonins.)
- Neutralising toxins (antitoxins, OCR A) and blocking viral attachment proteins.

**Primary and secondary responses.**

| | Primary | Secondary |
| --- | --- | --- |
| When | First exposure | Later exposure to the same antigen |
| Speed | Slow: few B and T cells match the antigen; they must be selected and cloned | Fast: many memory cells divide quickly |
| Antibody level | Low | Much higher, made sooner |
| Symptoms | Usually | Usually none: pathogen destroyed before it causes disease |

**Antigenic variability.** Some pathogens (influenza virus, HIV) mutate often, changing the shape of their antigens. Memory cells from an earlier infection no longer match, so a new primary response is needed and people catch the disease again. This is why a new flu vaccine is needed each year.

**Active and passive immunity.**

| | Active | Passive |
| --- | --- | --- |
| How | Body makes its own antibodies after exposure to an antigen | Antibodies given from outside |
| Natural example | Catching a disease | Antibodies across the placenta; in breast milk |
| Artificial example | Vaccination | Injection of antibodies (antitetanus, antivenom) |
| Memory cells | Yes, long-lasting | No; antibodies broken down in weeks |
| Speed | Slow to develop | Immediate |

**Vaccination.** A vaccine contains antigens (dead or weakened pathogens, isolated antigens, or mRNA that makes cells produce the antigen). It causes a primary response, so memory cells form without the disease. On real infection, the secondary response is fast.

**Herd immunity.** When a large proportion of a population is immune, the pathogen cannot spread easily, so people who cannot be vaccinated (babies, people with weak immune systems) are protected too.

**Ethics of vaccines.** Testing on animals; testing on human volunteers who may suffer side effects; deciding who gets limited supplies; compulsory versus voluntary vaccination; the risk to the whole population when many people refuse (Edexcel B 6.7 vii, Eduqas OA.3(j)).

**Monoclonal antibodies** are many identical antibodies, all with the same binding site.

- *Targeted drugs*: a drug attached to an antibody that binds an antigen found only on cancer cells reaches those cells and harms fewer healthy cells. Some antibodies block a receptor on cancer cells directly.
- *Diagnosis*: pregnancy tests detect hCG (OCR B 4.2.1(h)). The test strip has mobile antibodies with coloured beads that bind hCG; the complex moves up the strip and is caught by fixed antibodies, forming a coloured line. A control line shows the test worked.
- *ELISA* (enzyme-linked immunosorbent assay) detects an antigen or antibody in a sample:
  1. The antigen (for example from HIV) is fixed to the bottom of a well.
  2. The patient's blood is added. If it contains antibodies to the antigen, they bind.
  3. The well is washed to remove unbound antibodies.
  4. A second antibody, with an enzyme attached, that binds to human antibodies is added; the well is washed again.
  5. A substrate is added. If the enzyme is present, it turns the substrate into a coloured product.
  Colour means the patient had the antibodies. Washing matters: unbound enzyme-linked antibody would give a colour even with no patient antibody (a false positive).
- *Ethics*: making monoclonal antibodies uses mice; trials of new monoclonal drugs carry risk to volunteers.

**HIV.** HIV has: an RNA genome (two strands) and the enzyme reverse transcriptase inside a protein capsid; a lipid envelope from the host cell; and attachment proteins (gp120) in the envelope.

Replication:

1. The attachment protein binds to the CD4 receptor on a helper T cell.
2. The capsid is released into the cell; it uncoats, releasing RNA and reverse transcriptase.
3. Reverse transcriptase makes a DNA copy of the viral RNA.
4. The DNA is inserted into the host cell's DNA.
5. The cell transcribes the viral DNA and makes viral proteins; new viruses assemble and bud off, taking some of the cell membrane as an envelope.

AIDS: HIV destroys helper T cells. With few helper T cells, B cells and cytotoxic T cells are not activated, so the body cannot fight other infections (opportunistic infections such as TB and pneumonia), and these cause death.

**Antibiotics** work by targeting bacterial structures and enzymes (murein cell walls, 70S ribosomes). Viruses have none of these and replicate inside host cells, so antibiotics do not affect them.

**Autoimmune disease** (OCR A). The immune system attacks the body's own antigens. Examples: type 1 diabetes (attacks β cells of the pancreas), rheumatoid arthritis (joints), lupus. Treated with immunosuppressant drugs.

**Allergies** (OCR B). An exaggerated immune response to a harmless antigen (allergen), such as pollen. B cells make IgE antibodies that attach to mast cells; on the next exposure the allergen binds, and mast cells release histamine, causing inflammation. Anaphylaxis is a severe, body-wide response treated with adrenaline.

#### Worked example

A vaccine is 90% effective. A population of 50 000 people has 80% vaccine uptake. How many people would you expect to be protected by the vaccine?

1. Vaccinated = 0.80 × 50 000 = 40 000.
2. Protected = 0.90 × 40 000 = 36 000.

Answer: 36 000, which is 72% of the population.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying antibodies "kill" pathogens. They bind and cause agglutination or mark them for phagocytes.
- Saying memory cells "remember the disease". They have receptors complementary to the antigen and divide rapidly on re-exposure.
- Saying the antigen and antibody are "the same shape".
- Saying vaccines contain antibodies (that is passive immunity).
- Saying HIV "is AIDS". HIV is the virus; AIDS is the condition that results.
- Saying antibiotics can kill viruses.
- Writing "T cells make antibodies". Plasma cells (from B cells) do.

### Check questions

1. Define the term antigen. (1 mark)
2. Describe how a phagocyte destroys a bacterium. (4 marks)
3. Describe the role of helper T cells in the immune response. (3 marks)
4. Explain why the secondary response is faster than the primary response. (3 marks)
5. Explain why people can catch influenza many times. (3 marks)
6. Describe how an ELISA test can show that a person has antibodies to HIV. Explain the purpose of washing the well. (5 marks)
7. Explain why HIV infection leads to AIDS. (3 marks)
8. A patient is bitten by a snake and given an antivenom. Explain why this does not give long-term immunity. (2 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. A (foreign) molecule, usually a protein, that stimulates an immune response / antibody production (1).
2. Phagocyte attracted / receptors bind to antigens on bacterium (1); engulfs it into a phagosome / vesicle (1); lysosome fuses with phagosome (1); lysozymes hydrolyse the bacterium (1).
3. Receptor binds to antigen on antigen-presenting cell (1); stimulates cytotoxic T cells (1); stimulates B cells to divide / become plasma cells (1). Accept: stimulates phagocytes; forms memory cells.
4. Memory cells present (1); in larger numbers than original lymphocytes (1); divide rapidly into plasma cells, so antibodies produced faster / in greater amount (1).
5. Influenza virus mutates (1); antigen shape changes (1); memory cells / antibodies no longer complementary, so primary response needed again (1).
6. HIV antigen fixed to well (1); patient's blood / serum added; any HIV antibodies bind (1); wash (1); add enzyme-linked second antibody that binds the first, wash, add substrate (1); colour change shows antibodies present. Washing removes unbound antibodies so colour is only produced if patient antibodies bound (1).
7. HIV infects / destroys helper T cells (1); so B cells / cytotoxic T cells not activated (1); body cannot fight opportunistic infections (1).
8. Passive immunity / antibodies given, not made by body (1); no memory cells formed, and antibodies broken down (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/biology/19-nerve-impulses-synapses-and-muscle.md -->

## Nerve impulses, synapses and skeletal muscle

Neurones carry electrical impulses; synapses pass the signal chemically to the next cell; muscles turn the signal into movement. This file covers the resting and action potential, conduction speed, synaptic transmission and the sliding filament model of muscle contraction.

### Overview

A-level only. Neurone types and the organisation of the nervous system are in file 32. Drugs that act at synapses (beyond the examples here) are in file 34. Bones, joints and muscle energy for Eduqas Option B are in file 47; muscle fibre types and exercise in file 40.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.6.2.1, 3.6.2.2, 3.6.3. Edexcel A 7.1, 7.2, 7.10, 8.1, 8.3, 8.4. Edexcel B 9.5 i to v. OCR A 5.1.3(b) to (d), 5.1.5(l). OCR B 4.1.2(k), (l), 5.2.1(c) to (e). Eduqas C3.5(f) to (l), OB.1(h) to (k).
- Edexcel B asks for excitatory and inhibitory postsynaptic potentials (EPSPs and IPSPs) by name.
- OCR A asks you to compare skeletal, cardiac and involuntary (smooth) muscle (5.1.5(l)).
- Eduqas asks for oscilloscope traces and conduction speed in other organisms, including giant axons (C3.5(h), (i)), and organophosphate insecticides (C3.5(l)).
- Edexcel A 7.1 asks how muscles, tendons, ligaments and the skeleton interact; joints are expanded in file 47.

### 3.6.2.1 Nerve impulses

*Refs: AQA 3.6.2.1 · Edexcel A 8.1, 8.3 · Edexcel B 9.5 i to iii · OCR A 5.1.3(b), (c) · OCR B 5.2.1(c), (d) · Eduqas C3.5(f) to (i). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- The structure of a myelinated motor neurone.
- The establishment of a resting potential in terms of differential membrane permeability, electrochemical gradients and the movement of sodium ions and potassium ions.
- Changes in membrane permeability lead to depolarisation and the generation of an action potential. The all-or-nothing principle.
- The passage of an action potential along non-myelinated and myelinated axons, resulting in nerve impulses.
- The nature and importance of the refractory period in producing discrete impulses and in limiting the frequency of impulse transmission.
- Factors affecting the speed of conductance: myelination and saltatory conduction; axon diameter; temperature.

#### Explained

**A motor neurone** has a cell body (nucleus, many ribosomes and rough ER to make neurotransmitter and proteins), dendrites that receive impulses from other neurones, a long axon that carries impulses away, and axon terminals ending at the effector. Many axons are wrapped in a **myelin sheath** formed by **Schwann cells** wrapping round the axon. Between Schwann cells are small gaps called **nodes of Ranvier**. (Sensory neurones have the cell body part-way along; relay neurones are short and in the CNS: file 32.)

**Resting potential** (about −70 mV: the inside is negative compared to the outside).

1. The sodium–potassium pump actively moves 3 Na⁺ out for every 2 K⁺ in, using ATP.
2. The membrane is more permeable to K⁺ than Na⁺: many K⁺ channels are open; most Na⁺ channels are closed.
3. K⁺ diffuses back out down its gradient. Na⁺ cannot easily diffuse back in.
4. More positive ions are outside than inside, so the axon is polarised.

**Action potential.**

1. *Depolarisation:* a stimulus opens some voltage-gated Na⁺ channels; Na⁺ diffuses in. If the potential reaches the **threshold** (about −55 mV), many more voltage-gated Na⁺ channels open (positive feedback); Na⁺ floods in; the inside reaches about +40 mV.
2. *Repolarisation:* Na⁺ channels close; voltage-gated K⁺ channels open; K⁺ diffuses out; the potential falls.
3. *Hyperpolarisation:* K⁺ channels are slow to close, so too many K⁺ leave and the potential briefly drops below −70 mV.
4. The resting potential is restored by the sodium–potassium pump and K⁺ channels closing.

**All-or-nothing.** If the threshold is reached, an action potential of the same size (+40 mV) always follows; below threshold, none. A stronger stimulus does not give a bigger action potential; it gives more action potentials per second (higher frequency).

**Passage along the axon.** In a non-myelinated axon, Na⁺ entering one region diffuses sideways (local currents), depolarising the next region to threshold; a wave of depolarisation travels along. In a myelinated axon, the myelin insulates the axon, so depolarisation happens only at the nodes of Ranvier; the impulse jumps from node to node (**saltatory conduction**), much faster.

**Refractory period.** After an action potential, voltage-gated Na⁺ channels are closed and cannot open again for a short time. This:

- means the impulse travels in one direction only (the region behind cannot fire)
- produces discrete (separate) impulses rather than merging
- limits the number of impulses per second.

**Speed of conduction.**

- Myelination: much faster (saltatory conduction).
- Axon diameter: wider axons conduct faster (less resistance to the flow of ions; less leakage).
- Temperature: faster up to about 40 °C, because ions diffuse faster and the pump works faster; above this proteins denature.

(Eduqas) Invertebrates such as squid and earthworms have **giant axons** (up to 1 mm wide, non-myelinated) for fast escape responses. A myelinated mammalian axon conducts up to about 100 m s⁻¹; a non-myelinated one about 1 m s⁻¹.

**Oscilloscope traces** (Eduqas) plot membrane potential against time. Read off the resting potential, threshold, peak, and the time of one action potential (about 1 to 2 ms) to calculate maximum frequency.

#### Worked example

An action potential takes 3 ms from the start of depolarisation to the end of the refractory period. What is the maximum frequency of impulses?

1 ÷ 0.003 = 333 impulses per second.

A myelinated neurone 0.9 m long conducts at 90 m s⁻¹. How long does an impulse take to travel its length? 0.9 ÷ 90 = 0.01 s = 10 ms.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying the inside is positive at rest.
- Saying the sodium–potassium pump causes depolarisation. Voltage-gated channels do.
- Saying a bigger stimulus gives a bigger action potential.
- Saying the impulse "jumps from one Schwann cell to another"; it is from node to node.
- Saying myelin conducts the impulse; it insulates.

### 3.6.2.2 Synaptic transmission

*Refs: AQA 3.6.2.2 · Edexcel A 8.4 · Edexcel B 9.5 iv, v · OCR A 5.1.3(d) · OCR B 5.2.1(e) · Eduqas C3.5(j) to (l). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- The detailed structure of a synapse and of a neuromuscular junction.
- The sequence of events involved in transmission across a cholinergic synapse in sufficient detail to explain: unidirectionality; temporal and spatial summation; inhibition by inhibitory synapses.
- A comparison of transmission across a cholinergic synapse and across a neuromuscular junction.
- Use information provided to predict the effects of specific drugs on a synapse.

#### Explained

**Structure.** The presynaptic neurone ends in a swollen knob with many mitochondria and vesicles of neurotransmitter. A gap of about 20 nm (the synaptic cleft) separates it from the postsynaptic membrane, which has receptor proteins for the neurotransmitter. In a cholinergic synapse the neurotransmitter is acetylcholine (ACh).

**Transmission.**

1. An action potential reaches the presynaptic knob.
2. Voltage-gated calcium ion channels open; Ca²⁺ diffuses into the knob.
3. Ca²⁺ causes vesicles to move to and fuse with the presynaptic membrane, releasing ACh into the cleft by exocytosis.
4. ACh diffuses across the cleft and binds to complementary receptors on the postsynaptic membrane.
5. Sodium ion channels in the receptors open; Na⁺ diffuses in, depolarising the postsynaptic membrane. If threshold is reached, an action potential starts.
6. Acetylcholinesterase in the cleft hydrolyses ACh into choline and ethanoic acid (acetyl). These diffuse back into the presynaptic knob and are recombined into ACh using ATP. Removal stops the signal and lets the receptor be used again.

**Unidirectional.** Only the presynaptic knob has vesicles; only the postsynaptic membrane has receptors.

**Summation.** One release of neurotransmitter may not reach threshold.

- *Spatial summation:* several presynaptic neurones release neurotransmitter onto one postsynaptic neurone at the same time; together they reach threshold.
- *Temporal summation:* one presynaptic neurone releases neurotransmitter repeatedly in quick succession; the effect builds up.

**Inhibitory synapses.** Some neurotransmitters (such as GABA) open chloride channels (Cl⁻ diffuses in) or potassium channels (K⁺ diffuses out) on the postsynaptic membrane. The inside becomes more negative (hyperpolarised), so more excitatory input is needed to reach threshold. Edexcel B calls these **inhibitory postsynaptic potentials (IPSPs)**; depolarisations that move towards threshold are **excitatory postsynaptic potentials (EPSPs)**. The postsynaptic neurone sums EPSPs and IPSPs.

**Neuromuscular junction** compared with a cholinergic synapse:

| | Cholinergic synapse | Neuromuscular junction |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Connects | Neurone to neurone | Motor neurone to muscle fibre |
| Effect | Excitatory or inhibitory | Always excitatory |
| Postsynaptic membrane | Smooth | Folded into clefts with many receptors and acetylcholinesterase |
| Result | Action potential in next neurone (if threshold reached) | Every impulse normally causes depolarisation of the muscle fibre membrane |

**Drugs** (predict effects from information).

- A drug that mimics the neurotransmitter (same shape, binds receptors) increases stimulation (nicotine at some ACh receptors).
- A drug that blocks receptors reduces it (curare at the neuromuscular junction causes paralysis).
- A drug that inhibits acetylcholinesterase (organophosphate insecticides, nerve agents) leaves ACh in the cleft, so muscles stay contracted (Eduqas C3.5(l)).
- A drug that stops neurotransmitter release (some anaesthetics) reduces stimulation.
- A drug that stops reuptake leaves more neurotransmitter in the cleft (file 34 for MDMA and others).

#### Common mistakes

- Saying the impulse "jumps" across the synapse.
- Forgetting calcium ions.
- Saying acetylcholinesterase "breaks down the receptor".
- Saying inhibitory synapses "stop the action potential". They make reaching threshold less likely.

### 3.6.3 Skeletal muscles are stimulated to contract by nerves and act as effectors

*Refs: AQA 3.6.3 · Edexcel A 7.1, 7.2, 7.10 · OCR A 5.1.5(l) · OCR B 4.1.2(k), (l) · Eduqas OB.1(h) to (k). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Muscles act in antagonistic pairs against an incompressible skeleton.
- Gross and microscopic structure of skeletal muscle. The ultrastructure of a myofibril.
- The roles of actin, myosin, calcium ions and ATP in myofibril contraction. The roles of calcium ions and tropomyosin in the cycle of actinomyosin bridge formation. (The role of troponin is not required by AQA.)
- The roles of ATP and phosphocreatine in muscle contraction.
- The structure, location and general properties of slow and fast skeletal muscle fibres.

#### Explained

**Antagonistic pairs.** Muscles can only pull. One muscle contracts while its partner relaxes: biceps contracts to flex (bend) the elbow; triceps contracts to extend it. The skeleton is incompressible, so the force moves the bones. Tendons join muscle to bone (strong, inelastic); ligaments join bone to bone at joints (Edexcel A 7.1).

**Structure.** A muscle is made of bundles of muscle fibres. Each fibre is a long multinucleate cell with a cell membrane (sarcolemma) folded inwards as T-tubules, sarcoplasm with many mitochondria, sarcoplasmic reticulum (stores Ca²⁺), and many myofibrils.

**Myofibril.** Made of repeating units called **sarcomeres**, joined end to end at Z-lines.

- Thin filaments: actin (with tropomyosin wrapped round them, and troponin attached).
- Thick filaments: myosin, each with many heads.
- A-band (dark): the length of the myosin filaments, including where they overlap actin.
- I-band (light): actin only.
- H-zone: myosin only, in the middle of the A-band.
- M-line: middle of the sarcomere.

**Sliding filament model.** In contraction the actin filaments slide over the myosin, pulled towards the middle. The sarcomere shortens: the I-band and H-zone get shorter; the A-band stays the same length.

**The cycle.**

1. An action potential arrives at the neuromuscular junction; depolarisation spreads along the sarcolemma and down T-tubules.
2. Ca²⁺ is released from the sarcoplasmic reticulum into the sarcoplasm.
3. Ca²⁺ binds to troponin, which moves tropomyosin away from the binding sites on actin.
4. Myosin heads (with ADP attached) bind to the exposed binding sites, forming actinomyosin cross-bridges.
5. The myosin heads change angle, pulling the actin filament towards the M-line (the power stroke), releasing ADP.
6. ATP binds to the myosin head, causing it to detach from actin.
7. ATP hydrolase (ATPase) in the myosin head hydrolyses the ATP; the energy returns the head to its original position.
8. The head binds further along the actin, and the cycle repeats while Ca²⁺ and ATP are present.

When stimulation stops, Ca²⁺ is actively transported back into the sarcoplasmic reticulum (using ATP); tropomyosin covers the binding sites again; the muscle relaxes.

**ATP sources.** A muscle's ATP lasts only a few seconds.

- Aerobic respiration (main source over time).
- Anaerobic respiration (glycolysis to lactate; fast but short).
- **Phosphocreatine** (PCr), stored in muscle, gives its phosphate to ADP: PCr + ADP → creatine + ATP. Very fast, no oxygen needed, lasts a few seconds (sprints). It is regenerated from ATP when the muscle is resting.

**Slow and fast fibres.**

| | Slow-twitch | Fast-twitch |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Contraction | Slower, less powerful, for a long time | Rapid, powerful, short bursts |
| Where common | Calves, back (posture), endurance | Arms, legs used for sprinting |
| Respiration | Aerobic | Anaerobic; also phosphocreatine stores |
| Mitochondria | Many | Few |
| Blood supply | Many capillaries | Fewer |
| Myoglobin | Lots (red colour; stores oxygen) | Little (white) |
| Fatigue | Resistant | Tire quickly (lactate) |
| Other | | Thicker, more myosin filaments; more enzymes for glycolysis; more glycogen |

**Three muscle types** (OCR A).

| | Skeletal | Cardiac | Involuntary (smooth) |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Appearance | Striated, long multinucleate fibres | Striated, branched cells joined by intercalated discs | Not striated; spindle-shaped cells, one nucleus |
| Control | Voluntary (somatic nervous system) | Myogenic; rate changed by autonomic nerves | Involuntary (autonomic) |
| Contraction | Rapid; fatigues | Rhythmic; does not fatigue | Slow, sustained |
| Where | Attached to bones | Heart | Walls of gut, blood vessels, bronchioles |

#### Worked example

A sarcomere is 2.4 µm long when relaxed; the A-band is 1.6 µm. When contracted the sarcomere is 2.0 µm. Find the A-band and total I-band lengths when contracted.

1. The A-band stays the same: 1.6 µm.
2. Sarcomere − A-band = I-band total (the two half I-bands): 2.0 − 1.6 = 0.4 µm (relaxed it was 0.8 µm).

#### Common mistakes

- Saying the filaments themselves shorten.
- Saying the A-band shortens.
- Saying ATP is needed for the power stroke. ATP is needed to detach the head and recock it; the power stroke releases ADP.
- Saying calcium binds to tropomyosin (it binds to troponin; AQA accepts "calcium ions cause tropomyosin to move").
- Saying slow-twitch fibres "contract slowly so they use less energy" without linking to aerobic respiration.

### Check questions

1. Explain how a resting potential is maintained in an axon. (3 marks)
2. Describe the events that produce an action potential, from the stimulus to repolarisation. (5 marks)
3. Explain why myelinated axons conduct impulses faster than non-myelinated axons of the same diameter. (2 marks)
4. Explain why impulses travel in only one direction along an axon and across a synapse. (3 marks)
5. Describe the role of calcium ions at a cholinergic synapse. (2 marks)
6. An organophosphate inhibits acetylcholinesterase. Explain why it causes muscle spasms. (3 marks)
7. Describe how the binding of calcium ions leads to the sliding of filaments. (5 marks)
8. Explain why sprinters' leg muscles have many fast-twitch fibres. (3 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Sodium–potassium pump moves 3 Na⁺ out and 2 K⁺ in (1); membrane more permeable to K⁺ / K⁺ diffuses out (1); fewer Na⁺ channels open, so Na⁺ cannot diffuse back in (1).
2. Stimulus opens some Na⁺ channels; Na⁺ diffuses in (1); threshold reached, more voltage-gated Na⁺ channels open (1); inside becomes positive / +40 mV / depolarisation (1); Na⁺ channels close and K⁺ channels open (1); K⁺ diffuses out, repolarisation (1).
3. Myelin insulates; depolarisation only at nodes of Ranvier (1); impulse jumps between nodes / saltatory conduction (1).
4. Axon: refractory period / Na⁺ channels behind cannot open (1). Synapse: vesicles only in presynaptic knob (1); receptors only on postsynaptic membrane (1).
5. Ca²⁺ enter presynaptic knob through voltage-gated channels (1); cause vesicles to fuse with membrane / release ACh (1).
6. ACh not hydrolysed (1); remains bound to receptors / Na⁺ channels stay open (1); continuous depolarisation / muscle stimulated (1).
7. Ca²⁺ bind to troponin (1); tropomyosin moves, exposing binding sites on actin (1); myosin heads bind forming cross-bridges (1); heads change angle, pulling actin / power stroke (1); ATP detaches heads; hydrolysis re-cocks them (1).
8. Contract rapidly / powerfully (1); anaerobic respiration / phosphocreatine supplies ATP quickly (1); sprinting is short so fatigue is not a problem (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/biology/47-human-musculoskeletal-anatomy.md -->

## Human musculoskeletal anatomy

Bones, cartilage, joints and muscles together support and move the body. This file covers cartilage and bone tissue, bone disorders, the human skeleton and vertebral column, fractures and posture, joints and levers, arthritis, antagonistic muscles, and the energy supply for muscle contraction.

### Overview

A-level only. The sliding filament theory, muscle ultrastructure, fast and slow fibres and phosphocreatine are in file 19 (AQA 3.6.3), which Eduqas OB.1(h) to (k) also uses. Keyhole surgery and joint prostheses for sport are in file 40.

#### Board differences

- Eduqas Option B: OB.1(a) to (l), OB.2(a) to (e), OB.3(a) to (f). Edexcel A 7.1 (muscles, tendons, the skeleton and ligaments interacting to cause movement).
- Only Eduqas students taking Option B need the detail on bone tissue, the vertebral column, fractures, posture, arthritis and levers.

### Skeletal tissues

*Refs: Eduqas OB.1(a) to (g), (k), (l). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- The structure of cartilage: hyaline, yellow elastic and white fibrous.
- The components of compact bone: a matrix about 30% organic (mainly collagen) and 70% inorganic (mainly hydroxyapatite, containing calcium and phosphate); the functions of each.
- Osteoblasts and osteoclasts; bone is constantly broken down and re-formed.
- The structure and function of Haversian systems.
- Rickets and osteomalacia; osteoporosis and brittle bone disease.
- The effects of anaerobic conditions in muscle (creatine phosphate, lactic acid) and the use of glycogen and other stores as energy sources during contraction.

#### Explained

**Cartilage** is a firm, flexible connective tissue. Cells called chondrocytes sit in spaces (lacunae) in a matrix of collagen fibres and a gel of proteoglycans. It has no blood vessels, so it heals slowly.

| Type | Structure | Where | Property |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Hyaline | Fine collagen fibres, smooth glassy matrix | Ends of bones in joints (articular cartilage), trachea rings, nose, embryonic skeleton | Smooth, reduces friction; flexible support |
| Yellow elastic | Collagen plus many elastic fibres | Outer ear (pinna), epiglottis | Flexible; returns to shape |
| White fibrous | Dense bundles of collagen fibres | Intervertebral discs, between pelvic bones, knee menisci | Very strong; resists compression and tension; absorbs shock |

**Compact bone.**

- *Organic part* (about 30%): mainly collagen fibres. Gives flexibility and tensile strength, so bone bends slightly rather than snapping.
- *Inorganic part* (about 70%): mainly hydroxyapatite, a calcium phosphate mineral. Gives hardness and strength under compression.
- Together they make bone strong and slightly flexible, like reinforced concrete.

**Haversian systems (osteons)** are the units of compact bone: a central Haversian canal containing blood vessels and nerves, surrounded by concentric rings (lamellae) of bone matrix. Osteocytes (mature bone cells) sit in lacunae between lamellae and connect to each other and to the canal by tiny channels (canaliculi), through which nutrients and oxygen diffuse. The cylinders run along the length of the bone, resisting bending.

**Bone remodelling.** Bone is living and constantly renewed.

- *Osteoblasts* lay down new matrix (collagen and then hydroxyapatite); when surrounded by matrix they become osteocytes.
- *Osteoclasts* break down (resorb) bone, releasing calcium into the blood.
- The balance between them, controlled by hormones (parathyroid hormone, calcitonin, oestrogen, testosterone) and by the stresses on bone (exercise increases bone density), sets bone mass. It also lets bones repair and reshape.

**Bone disorders.**

| Disorder | Cause | Effect | Treatment and prevention |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Rickets (children) | Lack of vitamin D or calcium | Bones not mineralised properly; soft, bend under weight (bowed legs) | Vitamin D and calcium supplements; sunlight (vitamin D made in skin) |
| Osteomalacia (adults) | Lack of vitamin D or calcium | Soft bones; pain, weakness, fractures | As above |
| Osteoporosis | Osteoclast activity exceeds osteoblast activity; loss of bone mass with age, especially in women after the menopause (less oestrogen, file 36); also low calcium, inactivity, smoking, steroids | Porous, fragile bones; fractures (wrist, hip, spine); loss of height, stooped posture | Weight-bearing exercise; calcium and vitamin D; HRT; drugs that slow osteoclasts (bisphosphonates); bone density scans |
| Brittle bone disease (osteogenesis imperfecta) | Inherited mutation in a collagen gene (usually dominant) | Faulty or too little collagen; bones break very easily | No cure; drugs to strengthen bone; careful handling; surgery with rods in long bones |

**Energy for muscle contraction** (Eduqas OB.1(k), (l); file 19 for detail).

- ATP stored in muscle lasts only a few seconds.
- **Creatine phosphate** (phosphocreatine) regenerates ATP from ADP rapidly for about 10 seconds of maximum effort.
- **Glycogen** stored in muscle is broken down to glucose for glycolysis: anaerobically in intense exercise (producing lactate, which lowers pH and contributes to fatigue) and aerobically over longer periods.
- In prolonged exercise, fatty acids from fat stores are respired aerobically, sparing glycogen; protein (amino acids) is used to a small extent, especially when glycogen is depleted.

### The human skeleton

*Refs: Eduqas OB.2(a) to (e). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- The axial and appendicular skeleton (pectoral and pelvic girdles, forelimb and hind limb).
- Types of fracture and their causes.
- The structure and function of the vertebral column; the general structure of a vertebra; cervical, thoracic and lumbar vertebrae.
- Medical conditions affecting posture.
- The functions of the skeleton.

#### Explained

**Functions of the skeleton:** support (shape, holds organs); attachment of muscles for movement (bones act as levers); protection (skull protects brain; ribs protect heart and lungs; vertebrae protect the spinal cord); production of blood cells (red bone marrow in flat bones and the ends of long bones); store of calcium and phosphate.

**Axial and appendicular.**

- *Axial skeleton:* skull, vertebral column, ribs and sternum. The central axis of the body.
- *Appendicular skeleton:* the limbs and the girdles that attach them. *Pectoral girdle* (scapula, clavicle) attaches the forelimbs (humerus; radius and ulna; carpals, metacarpals, phalanges). *Pelvic girdle* (pelvis, joined to the sacrum) attaches the hind limbs (femur; tibia and fibula; tarsals, metatarsals, phalanges). The pectoral girdle allows a wide range of movement; the pelvic girdle is strongly fixed to bear weight.

**Vertebral column.** 33 vertebrae: 7 cervical (neck), 12 thoracic (chest), 5 lumbar (lower back), 5 fused sacral (sacrum), and about 4 fused coccygeal (coccyx). Intervertebral discs of white fibrous cartilage between vertebrae absorb shock and allow bending. Its S-shaped curves help balance and absorb shock.

A typical vertebra has a **centrum** (body, bears weight), a **neural arch** surrounding the **neural canal** (spinal cord passes through), a **neural spine** and **transverse processes** (muscle attachment), and articulating surfaces (facets) where vertebrae join.

| | Cervical | Thoracic | Lumbar |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Centrum | Small (light load) | Medium | Large, thick (bears most weight) |
| Features | Holes in the transverse processes (vertebrarterial canals) for arteries to the brain; short spine; first two (atlas and axis) allow nodding and turning the head | Long, downward-pointing spine; facets for the ribs | Short, thick spine and processes for attachment of large back muscles |
| Movement | Most flexible | Limited (ribs) | Bending and some rotation |

**Fractures.**

- *Simple (closed):* bone breaks but skin is not broken.
- *Compound (open):* bone breaks through the skin; risk of infection.
- *Greenstick:* bone bends and cracks on one side (children's flexible bones).
- *Comminuted:* bone shatters into several pieces.
- *Stress fracture:* small cracks from repeated stress (runners).
- Causes: falls, impacts, sport, osteoporosis (fractures with little force), bone disease. Treatment: realign (set) the bone and immobilise it (cast or splint; metal pins or plates for complex fractures); the bone heals as osteoblasts lay down new bone.

**Posture conditions.**

- *Scoliosis:* sideways curvature of the spine; often unknown cause in teenagers; treated with a brace or surgery.
- *Kyphosis:* excessive outward curve of the upper back (hunchback); caused by poor posture, osteoporosis (collapsed vertebrae) or abnormal development; treated with exercise, bracing, surgery.
- *Lordosis:* excessive inward curve of the lower back; caused by obesity, posture, pregnancy; treated with exercise and weight loss.
- *Slipped (herniated) disc:* the soft centre of an intervertebral disc bulges out and presses on nerves, causing back pain or sciatica; treated with rest, physiotherapy, painkillers, sometimes surgery.

### Joints, levers and muscles

*Refs: Eduqas OB.3(a) to (f) · Edexcel A 7.1. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Types of joint: immovable (fused), gliding, hinge, ball and socket.
- Osteoarthritis and rheumatoid arthritis.
- Joints acting as levers: first, second and third order.
- The structure of a synovial joint: cartilage, synovial membrane, synovial fluid, ligaments.
- Antagonistic muscle action in the forelimb; the role of tendons.

#### Explained

**Types of joint.**

| Type | Movement | Example |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Immovable (fused, fibrous) | None | Sutures of the skull |
| Gliding | Bones slide over each other | Between carpals; between vertebral facets |
| Hinge | Movement in one plane | Elbow, knee |
| Ball and socket | Movement in all planes and rotation | Shoulder, hip |

**Synovial joint** (freely movable joints).

- *Articular (hyaline) cartilage* covers the ends of the bones: smooth, reduces friction and absorbs shock.
- *Synovial membrane* lines the joint capsule and secretes synovial fluid.
- *Synovial fluid* lubricates the joint, reduces friction and nourishes the cartilage.
- *Joint capsule:* fibrous tissue enclosing the joint.
- *Ligaments:* strong, slightly elastic bands of collagen joining bone to bone; stabilise the joint and prevent dislocation.
- (Knee) *menisci* of white fibrous cartilage absorb shock.

**Tendons** are tough, inelastic bands of collagen joining muscle to bone; because they do not stretch, the full force of contraction moves the bone.

**Arthritis.**

- *Osteoarthritis:* wear and tear; articular cartilage thins and breaks down, so bones rub together; pain, stiffness, bony growths. Risk increases with age, obesity, joint injury, repetitive use. Treatment: exercise, weight loss, painkillers and anti-inflammatory drugs, physiotherapy, joint replacement.
- *Rheumatoid arthritis:* an autoimmune disease (file 07): the immune system attacks the synovial membrane, which becomes inflamed and thickened, damaging cartilage and bone; usually affects several joints on both sides, with swelling and stiffness, especially in the morning. Treatment: anti-inflammatory drugs, steroids, drugs that suppress the immune system, biological drugs (monoclonal antibodies against inflammatory cytokines), physiotherapy, surgery.

**Levers.** A joint is the fulcrum (pivot, F), the muscle supplies the effort (E), and the weight of the body part or object is the load (L).

| Order | Arrangement | Example | Property |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| First | F between E and L | Nodding the head (the top of the spine is the fulcrum; neck muscles at the back are the effort; weight of the face is the load) | Balance |
| Second | L between F and E | Rising onto tiptoe (ball of foot is the fulcrum; body weight load; calf muscle effort at the heel) | Large load moved with less effort |
| Third | E between F and L | Bending the elbow (elbow fulcrum; biceps attaches just below; load in the hand) | Most common; small muscle shortening gives large, fast movement of the hand, but needs more force |

**Antagonistic muscles in the forelimb.** Biceps (origin on the scapula, insertion on the radius) contracts to flex the elbow while the triceps relaxes; triceps (insertion on the ulna) contracts to extend the elbow while the biceps relaxes. The muscle that contracts is the agonist; the one that relaxes is the antagonist. Tendons transmit the force to the bones; the incompressible skeleton allows the force to cause movement.

#### Common mistakes

- Confusing ligaments (bone to bone) with tendons (muscle to bone).
- Saying osteoarthritis is autoimmune.
- Saying bone is not living tissue.
- Mixing up osteoblasts (build) and osteoclasts (break down).

### Check questions

1. Explain how the organic and inorganic components of bone together give bone its properties. (3 marks)
2. Describe the structure of a Haversian system. (3 marks)
3. Explain why osteoporosis is more common in women after the menopause. (3 marks)
4. Compare rickets and brittle bone disease. (3 marks)
5. Give two differences between a thoracic and a lumbar vertebra and relate them to function. (4 marks)
6. Describe the structure of a synovial joint and the function of each part. (4 marks)
7. Explain why the elbow is a third-order lever and the advantage of this arrangement. (3 marks)
8. Compare osteoarthritis and rheumatoid arthritis. (3 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Collagen (organic) gives flexibility / tensile strength (1); hydroxyapatite (inorganic) gives hardness / compressive strength (1); together strong but not brittle (1).
2. Central Haversian canal with blood vessels / nerves (1); concentric lamellae of matrix (1); osteocytes in lacunae connected by canaliculi (1).
3. Oestrogen falls after the menopause (1); oestrogen normally limits osteoclast activity / bone resorption (1); bone broken down faster than it is made, so bone density falls (1).
4. Rickets: lack of vitamin D / calcium, poor mineralisation, soft bones (1); brittle bone disease: inherited mutation in collagen gene (1); bones fragile and break easily; rickets treatable with diet / sunlight, brittle bone disease not curable (1).
5. Any two with function (2 each): lumbar larger centrum bears more weight; thoracic has facets for ribs; thoracic long downward spine limits movement / lumbar short thick spine and processes for large muscles.
6. Articular cartilage reduces friction / absorbs shock (1); synovial membrane secretes synovial fluid (1); synovial fluid lubricates / nourishes cartilage (1); ligaments join bones / stabilise joint; capsule encloses (1).
7. Effort (biceps insertion) is between fulcrum (elbow) and load (hand) (1); small contraction gives large / fast movement of the hand (1); but more force is needed (1).
8. Osteoarthritis: wear and tear of cartilage, usually older people, often weight-bearing joints (1); rheumatoid: autoimmune attack on synovial membrane, inflammation, often symmetrical / several joints (1); treatment differs, e.g. immune suppressants for rheumatoid (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/biology/16-respiration.md -->

## Respiration

Respiration releases energy from organic molecules to make ATP. This file covers glycolysis, anaerobic respiration, the link reaction, the Krebs cycle, oxidative phosphorylation, other respiratory substrates, the respiratory quotient, and AQA required practical 9.

### Overview

A-level only. ATP's structure is in file 03. Lactate, oxygen debt and exercise (Edexcel A, OCR B) are in file 40.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.5.2, RP9. Edexcel A 7.3 to 7.7, CP16. Edexcel B 5.1 to 5.5, CP9. OCR A 5.2.2(a) to (l). OCR B 4.1.1(a) to (g). Eduqas C1.1(a) to (d), C1.3(a) to (f).
- OCR A and OCR B ask for the respiratory quotient (RQ) and the relative energy values of carbohydrate, lipid and protein. Eduqas asks how lipids and amino acids enter respiration. AQA asks only that lipids and proteins can be respiratory substrates.
- OCR A asks for the structure of the mitochondrion and the roles of coenzymes NAD, FAD and coenzyme A by name.
- Eduqas C1.1 compares the membranes of mitochondria and chloroplasts in ATP synthesis.
- Edexcel A and Edexcel B name triose phosphate as GALP, and Edexcel A uses the name "acetyl coenzyme A".

### 3.5.2 Respiration

*Refs: AQA 3.5.2, RP9 · Edexcel A 7.3 to 7.7, CP16 · Edexcel B 5.1 to 5.5, CP9 · OCR A 5.2.2(a) to (l) · OCR B 4.1.1(a) to (g) · Eduqas C1.1(a) to (d), C1.3(a) to (f). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Respiration produces ATP.
- Glycolysis is the first stage of anaerobic and aerobic respiration. It occurs in the cytoplasm and is an anaerobic process: phosphorylation of glucose to glucose phosphate, using ATP; production of triose phosphate; oxidation of triose phosphate to pyruvate with a net gain of ATP and reduced NAD.
- If respiration is only anaerobic, pyruvate can be converted to ethanol or lactate using reduced NAD. The oxidised NAD produced in this way can be used in further glycolysis.
- If respiration is aerobic, pyruvate from glycolysis enters the mitochondrial matrix by active transport. Pyruvate is oxidised to acetate, with the production of reduced NAD; acetate combines with coenzyme A in the link reaction to produce acetylcoenzyme A; acetylcoenzyme A reacts with a four-carbon molecule, releasing coenzyme A and producing a six-carbon molecule that enters the Krebs cycle; in a series of oxidation-reduction reactions, the Krebs cycle generates reduced coenzymes and ATP by substrate-level phosphorylation, and carbon dioxide is lost.
- Synthesis of ATP by oxidative phosphorylation is associated with the transfer of electrons down the electron transfer chain and passage of protons across inner mitochondrial membranes, and is catalysed by ATP synthase embedded in these membranes (chemiosmotic theory).
- Other respiratory substrates include the breakdown products of lipids and amino acids, which enter the Krebs cycle.

#### Explained

**The mitochondrion** (OCR A, Edexcel B). Outer membrane; inner membrane folded into cristae (large surface area for the electron transfer chain and ATP synthase); intermembrane space (where protons build up); matrix containing Krebs cycle and link reaction enzymes, mitochondrial DNA and ribosomes.

**Coenzymes.** NAD and FAD carry hydrogen (protons and electrons) from the earlier stages to the electron transfer chain. Coenzyme A carries the acetyl group into the Krebs cycle.

**Glycolysis** (cytoplasm; no oxygen needed).

1. Glucose is phosphorylated twice using 2 ATP, forming hexose bisphosphate. This makes it more reactive and traps it in the cell.
2. It splits into two triose phosphate (TP) molecules.
3. Each TP is oxidised (hydrogen removed) to pyruvate. The hydrogen reduces NAD; 2 ATP are made per TP by substrate-level phosphorylation.

Per glucose: 4 ATP made − 2 ATP used = **net 2 ATP**, plus **2 reduced NAD** and **2 pyruvate**.

**Anaerobic respiration.** Without oxygen, the electron transfer chain stops, so reduced NAD cannot be reoxidised there. Glycolysis would stop when NAD ran out. Instead pyruvate accepts the hydrogen from reduced NAD, regenerating NAD so glycolysis continues and makes 2 ATP per glucose.

- Animals (and some bacteria): pyruvate + reduced NAD → lactate + NAD (lactate dehydrogenase). Lactate is carried to the liver, where it is converted back to pyruvate or glucose when oxygen is available.
- Plants and yeast: pyruvate → ethanal + CO₂; ethanal + reduced NAD → ethanol + NAD. This cannot be reversed.

Only 2 ATP per glucose are made anaerobically, against about 32 aerobically, because most of the energy is still in lactate or ethanol.

**The link reaction** (matrix). Pyruvate is actively transported into the matrix.

1. Pyruvate (3C) is decarboxylated (CO₂ removed) and oxidised (hydrogen removed, reducing NAD), forming acetate (2C).
2. Acetate combines with coenzyme A to form acetylcoenzyme A.

Per pyruvate: 1 CO₂, 1 reduced NAD. Per glucose: double.

**The Krebs cycle** (matrix).

1. Acetylcoenzyme A (2C) combines with a 4C molecule (oxaloacetate) to form a 6C molecule (citrate); coenzyme A is released to collect more acetate.
2. The 6C molecule is decarboxylated and oxidised to a 5C molecule, then to the 4C molecule, which is regenerated.
3. Per turn: 2 CO₂, 3 reduced NAD, 1 reduced FAD and 1 ATP (by substrate-level phosphorylation).

The cycle turns twice per glucose.

**Oxidative phosphorylation** (inner mitochondrial membrane, the cristae).

1. Reduced NAD and reduced FAD release hydrogen atoms, which split into protons and electrons.
2. Electrons pass along the electron transfer chain, a series of carrier proteins, in a series of oxidation-reduction reactions, losing energy.
3. The energy is used to pump protons from the matrix into the intermembrane space, creating an electrochemical gradient.
4. Protons diffuse back into the matrix through ATP synthase, which makes ATP from ADP and Pi (chemiosmosis).
5. At the end of the chain, oxygen is the final electron acceptor: it combines with electrons and protons to form water.

Without oxygen, electrons cannot leave the chain, so the whole process stops, and NAD and FAD stay reduced, stopping the Krebs cycle and link reaction too.

Reduced NAD gives about 2.5 ATP; reduced FAD about 1.5 (FAD's electrons enter the chain further along, so fewer protons are pumped).

**ATP tally per glucose (approximate).**

| Stage | ATP directly | Reduced NAD | Reduced FAD |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Glycolysis | 2 | 2 | 0 |
| Link reaction | 0 | 2 | 0 |
| Krebs cycle | 2 | 6 | 2 |
| Total | 4 | 10 | 2 |

From oxidative phosphorylation: 10 × 2.5 + 2 × 1.5 = 28. Total about 32. (Older texts give 38; the real yield is lower because some energy is used to move pyruvate and ADP into the mitochondrion and protons leak through the membrane.)

**Comparing mitochondria and chloroplasts** (Eduqas C1.1). Both make ATP by chemiosmosis: an electron transfer chain in a membrane pumps protons across it, and protons flow back through ATP synthase. In mitochondria protons are pumped into the intermembrane space; in chloroplasts into the thylakoid space. The energy source differs: excited electrons from light in chloroplasts; electrons from reduced NAD and FAD in mitochondria.

**Other respiratory substrates.**

- *Lipids:* triglycerides are hydrolysed to glycerol and fatty acids. Glycerol is phosphorylated and converted to triose phosphate, which enters glycolysis. Fatty acids are split into many 2C fragments, which become acetylcoenzyme A and enter the Krebs cycle, producing a lot of reduced NAD and FAD.
- *Proteins:* hydrolysed to amino acids; the amino group is removed in the liver (deamination, file 30) and the rest enters the Krebs cycle or link reaction as pyruvate, acetylcoenzyme A or a Krebs intermediate.

**Energy values** (OCR A, OCR B). Lipids about 39 kJ g⁻¹; proteins about 17 kJ g⁻¹; carbohydrates about 16 kJ g⁻¹. Lipids yield the most because they contain the most hydrogen atoms per gram, so they supply the most protons and electrons for oxidative phosphorylation.

**Respiratory quotient** (OCR A, OCR B). RQ = CO₂ produced ÷ O₂ used. Carbohydrate 1.0; protein about 0.9; lipid about 0.7. An RQ above 1 suggests some anaerobic respiration (CO₂ made without oxygen use).

#### Worked example

1. Calculate the RQ for oleic acid: C₁₈H₃₄O₂ + 25.5O₂ → 18CO₂ + 17H₂O.

RQ = 18 ÷ 25.5 = 0.71.

2. In a respirometer, the coloured liquid in the capillary moved 30 mm in 10 minutes towards the organism. The capillary has a radius of 0.5 mm. Calculate the rate of oxygen uptake in mm³ min⁻¹.

Volume = π × 0.5² × 30 = 23.6 mm³. Rate = 23.6 ÷ 10 = 2.4 mm³ min⁻¹.

3. How many molecules of CO₂ are produced from one glucose in aerobic respiration, and where? 2 in the link reaction, 4 in the Krebs cycle: 6 in total.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying glycolysis happens in the mitochondria.
- Saying anaerobic respiration "produces lactate to make ATP". Lactate is made to regenerate NAD so glycolysis can continue.
- Saying oxygen "makes ATP" or "combines with ADP". Oxygen is the final electron acceptor.
- Saying the Krebs cycle produces most of the ATP. Oxidative phosphorylation does.
- Saying "energy is made". Energy is released or transferred.
- Saying protons are pumped into the matrix.

#### Practical

**AQA required practical 9** (also Edexcel A CP16, Edexcel B CP9, OCR A 5.2.2(i), (l) and PAG10/11, OCR B 4.1.1(g)): investigation into the effect of a named variable on the rate of respiration of cultures of single-celled organisms.

*Method 1: respirometer.* Put small organisms (yeast culture, germinating seeds or woodlice) in one tube with soda lime (or potassium hydroxide) to absorb CO₂. A second tube has glass beads of the same volume (control, to account for temperature and pressure changes). As oxygen is used, the pressure in the tube falls and the coloured liquid moves towards the organisms. Measure the distance moved in a set time and convert to volume. Change temperature with a water bath.

*Method 2: redox indicator.* Methylene blue (or TTC) accepts hydrogen from dehydrogenase enzymes in respiring yeast and turns colourless (TTC turns red). Time how long it takes at each temperature or glucose concentration.

*Method 3: CO₂ production.* Count bubbles or measure gas collected from yeast in glucose solution; or measure pH change with a datalogger.

Control variables: volume and concentration of yeast; glucose concentration; temperature (water bath); time allowed to equilibrate. Ethics: invertebrates must be returned unharmed; keep them away from soda lime (corrosive).

### Check questions

1. Describe glycolysis. (4 marks)
2. Explain why anaerobic respiration in mammals produces lactate. (3 marks)
3. Describe the link reaction. (3 marks)
4. Explain how the electron transfer chain leads to ATP production. (5 marks)
5. Explain why the Krebs cycle stops if oxygen is unavailable. (3 marks)
6. Explain why the energy yield per gram of lipid is higher than that of carbohydrate. (2 marks)
7. Calculate the RQ for glucose: C₆H₁₂O₆ + 6O₂ → 6CO₂ + 6H₂O, and state what an RQ of 0.7 suggests. (2 marks)
8. In a respirometer, explain the purpose of the soda lime and the control tube. (3 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Glucose phosphorylated using 2 ATP (1); splits into two triose phosphate (1); triose phosphate oxidised to pyruvate, reducing NAD (1); 4 ATP made, net gain of 2 ATP (1).
2. No oxygen to accept electrons, so electron transfer chain stops / NAD not regenerated (1); pyruvate reduced to lactate using reduced NAD (1); NAD regenerated so glycolysis continues / some ATP made (1).
3. Pyruvate decarboxylated / CO₂ removed (1); oxidised, reducing NAD (1); acetate combines with coenzyme A to form acetylcoenzyme A (1).
4. Reduced NAD / FAD release hydrogen / electrons (1); electrons pass along carriers losing energy (1); energy used to pump protons into intermembrane space (1); protons diffuse back through ATP synthase (1); oxygen is final electron acceptor forming water (1).
5. Oxygen is final electron acceptor; without it electrons cannot leave the chain (1); reduced NAD / FAD not oxidised (1); no NAD / FAD for Krebs cycle (1).
6. More hydrogen atoms per gram (1); more protons / electrons for oxidative phosphorylation, so more ATP (1).
7. 6 ÷ 6 = 1.0 (1); 0.7 suggests lipid being respired (1).
8. Soda lime absorbs CO₂ so change in volume / pressure is due to oxygen uptake only (1); control tube with beads / no organisms (1); accounts for changes in temperature / pressure (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/biology/40-exercise-and-sports-physiology.md -->

## Exercise and sports physiology

Exercise changes how the body supplies oxygen and energy to muscles. This file covers the control of breathing and heart rate during exercise, cardiac output, oxygen deficit and lactate, aerobic fitness and VO₂ max, the effects of exercise on health, medical technology for sports injuries, and performance-enhancing substances and their ethics.

### Overview

Respiration is in file 16; muscle structure and fibre types in file 19; control of heart rate in file 18; haemoglobin in file 10. AQA does not have this content as a topic.

#### Board differences

- Edexcel A 7.7, 7.9, 7.10 (part), 7.13 to 7.15, CP17. Edexcel B 5.5 iii. OCR B 2.2.1(e), (f), 4.1.2(a) to (f), (j).
- Edexcel A asks for the control of ventilation and heart rate during exercise, the health effects of too much or too little exercise, keyhole surgery and prostheses, and the ethics of performance-enhancing substances. CP17 investigates exercise and breathing.
- OCR B asks for the effects of exercise, aerobic fitness, VO₂ max, oxygen deficit and excess post-exercise oxygen consumption (EPOC), and performance-enhancing methods including blood doping and altitude training.
- Edexcel B 5.5 iii asks what happens to lactate after anaerobic respiration.

### Responses to exercise

*Refs: Edexcel A 7.7, 7.9 · Edexcel B 5.5 iii · OCR B 2.2.1(e), (f), 4.1.2(a), (j). Level: A-level only (AS for OCR B 2.2.1).*

#### What you need to know

- Cardiac output; how ventilation and heart rate are controlled and change during exercise.
- What happens to lactate after anaerobic respiration; oxygen deficit and EPOC.
- The short-term and long-term effects of exercise on the body.

#### Explained

**Cardiac output** = stroke volume × heart rate. At rest about 5 dm³ min⁻¹; in hard exercise it can rise above 20 dm³ min⁻¹ (more in trained athletes).

**Control of heart rate during exercise.**

- Chemoreceptors (aorta, carotid arteries, medulla) detect the rise in CO₂ and fall in pH; stretch receptors in muscles and tendons detect movement.
- The cardiovascular centre in the medulla sends more impulses along sympathetic nerves to the SAN: heart rate rises.
- Adrenaline (released even before exercise starts, in anticipation) raises heart rate and stroke volume.
- More blood returns to the heart (venous return), stretching the ventricles, which contract more strongly (larger stroke volume).

**Control of ventilation.**

- The **ventilation centre** in the medulla controls breathing rate and depth. It sends impulses along nerves to the intercostal muscles and diaphragm.
- During exercise, more CO₂ lowers the pH of blood and of cerebrospinal fluid; chemoreceptors in the medulla, aorta and carotid arteries detect this and send impulses to the ventilation centre; breathing rate and depth (tidal volume) increase.
- Stretch receptors in the lungs stop over-inflation; receptors in muscles and joints signal movement, increasing ventilation quickly at the start.
- Minute ventilation = tidal volume × breathing rate.

**Oxygen deficit and lactate.**

- At the start of exercise, the oxygen supply cannot rise quickly enough, so muscles use phosphocreatine and anaerobic respiration. The difference between the oxygen needed and the oxygen supplied is the **oxygen deficit**.
- After exercise, breathing and heart rate stay raised. The extra oxygen taken in is **EPOC** (excess post-exercise oxygen consumption; older name: oxygen debt). It is used to:
  - oxidise lactate back to pyruvate (and respire it), or convert it to glucose and glycogen in the liver (Cori cycle)
  - resynthesise phosphocreatine and ATP
  - reoxygenate myoglobin and haemoglobin
  - meet the raised metabolic rate while temperature returns to normal.
- Lactate lowers pH (as lactic acid), inhibiting enzymes and contributing to muscle fatigue. It is carried in the blood to the liver.

**Short-term effects of exercise** (OCR B): higher heart rate, stroke volume and cardiac output; deeper, faster breathing; vasodilation of arterioles to muscles and vasoconstriction to the gut; more sweating; rising body temperature; more lactate in hard exercise.

**Long-term effects of training:**

- Heart: enlarged, with a thicker ventricle wall; larger stroke volume; lower resting heart rate (bradycardia).
- Lungs: stronger respiratory muscles; slightly larger vital capacity; more efficient ventilation.
- Blood: more red blood cells and plasma; more capillaries in muscles.
- Muscles: more mitochondria and myoglobin in slow fibres (endurance training); larger fast fibres (strength training); more glycogen stores; more enzymes for aerobic respiration.
- Health: lower blood pressure, better blood lipid profile, better insulin sensitivity.

#### Worked example

At rest an athlete's heart rate is 52 beats min⁻¹ and stroke volume 100 cm³. During a race the heart rate is 180 beats min⁻¹ and stroke volume 160 cm³. Calculate the percentage increase in cardiac output.

Rest: 52 × 100 = 5200 cm³ min⁻¹. Race: 180 × 160 = 28 800 cm³ min⁻¹. Increase = (28 800 − 5200) ÷ 5200 × 100 = 454%.

### Aerobic fitness and VO₂ max

*Refs: OCR B 4.1.2(b) to (d), (f) · Edexcel A CP17. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Factors affecting aerobic fitness; the significance of VO₂ max as a measure of aerobic fitness.
- Practical investigations into the effects of exercise.

#### Explained

**Aerobic fitness** is the ability to take in, transport and use oxygen. It depends on: lung function; heart size and stroke volume; haemoglobin and red blood cell count; capillary density in muscles; numbers of mitochondria and myoglobin in muscles; proportion of slow-twitch fibres (partly inherited); age (falls with age); sex; training; health (smoking reduces it).

**Training and the F.I.T.T. principles** (OCR B 4.1.2(d)). Aerobic fitness improves with training planned by four factors: **F**requency (how often, such as three to five sessions a week), **I**ntensity (how hard, often judged as a percentage of maximum heart rate, roughly 220 minus age), **T**ime (how long each session lasts) and **T**ype (the kind of exercise, matched to the goal: running or cycling for endurance). Training must be progressive (gradually increased) to keep producing the long-term effects described above; the effects are lost if training stops. Practical investigations measure resting heart rate, breathing rate or recovery time before and after a training programme, or in people with different training histories.

**VO₂ max** is the maximum rate at which a person can take in and use oxygen during intense exercise, measured in cm³ of oxygen per kg of body mass per minute (cm³ kg⁻¹ min⁻¹). Typical values: untrained adults 35 to 45; elite endurance athletes 70 to 85. It is measured on a treadmill or exercise bike with increasing intensity while expired air is analysed, or estimated from tests such as a multi-stage shuttle run. A higher VO₂ max means more work can be done aerobically before lactate builds up.

#### Practical

**Edexcel A CP17** (also OCR B 4.1.2(c)): investigate the effects of exercise on tidal volume, breathing rate, minute ventilation and oxygen consumption, using data from spirometer traces.

1. The subject breathes in and out of a spirometer (oxygen-filled chamber, with soda lime to absorb CO₂) at rest for a minute; the trace records volume against time.
2. The subject exercises (for example step-ups for 2 minutes), then breathes into the spirometer again.
3. From the traces: tidal volume = height of one wave; breathing rate = waves per minute; minute ventilation = tidal volume × breathing rate; oxygen consumption = the fall in the overall level of the trace per minute (CO₂ is absorbed, so the volume falls as oxygen is used).

Safety: the subject must be healthy (no asthma or heart problems); disinfect the mouthpiece; use a nose clip; stop if dizzy; soda lime is corrosive; check the spirometer has enough oxygen. Simpler heart rate practicals (OCR B 2.2.1(e)): measure pulse at the wrist for 15 seconds × 4 at rest and after exercise, or with a monitor, and investigate recovery time.

### Exercise, health and medical technology

*Refs: Edexcel A 7.13, 7.14. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Analyse data on the possible disadvantages of exercising too much (wear and tear on joints, suppression of the immune system) and exercising too little (CVD, obesity, type 2 diabetes).
- How medical technology, including keyhole surgery and prostheses, is enabling those with injuries and disabilities to participate in sports.

#### Explained

**Too little exercise:** higher risk of obesity, CVD, high blood pressure, type 2 diabetes, some cancers, osteoporosis, depression.

**Too much exercise:**

- *Wear and tear on joints:* repeated impact damages cartilage (osteoarthritis); damage to ligaments, tendons and bursae (sacs of fluid that reduce friction) causes inflammation (bursitis).
- *Immune suppression:* very intense or long exercise (marathons) is followed by a period of lower numbers and activity of natural killer cells, phagocytes and lymphocytes, and less IgA in mucus, so athletes are more likely to catch respiratory infections. Moderate exercise improves immunity, giving a J-shaped curve of infection risk against amount of exercise.
