**Keyhole surgery (arthroscopy).** Repairs to damaged joints (such as a torn anterior cruciate ligament or cartilage in the knee) through small cuts, using a fibre-optic camera and fine instruments. Compared with open surgery: less tissue damage, less blood loss, less pain, smaller scars, lower risk of infection, shorter hospital stay and faster recovery, so athletes return to sport sooner.

**Prostheses.** Artificial body parts: knee and hip replacements (metal and plastic) relieve pain from damaged joints; prosthetic limbs (carbon-fibre running blades) allow people with amputations to compete. Some include electronics that respond to muscle signals.

### Performance-enhancing substances and ethics

*Refs: Edexcel A 7.15 · OCR B 4.1.2(d), (e). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Performance-enhancing substances and methods, including anabolic steroids, erythropoietin (EPO), creatine, stimulants, blood doping and altitude training.
- Different ethical positions on whether their use is acceptable.

#### Explained

| Substance or method | Effect | Risks |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Anabolic steroids (testosterone-like) | Bind to receptors and act as transcription factors, increasing protein synthesis: bigger, stronger muscles, faster recovery | Liver damage, high blood pressure, heart disease, infertility, mood changes, acne; banned |
| EPO | Increases red blood cell production: more oxygen carried, more endurance | Thicker blood: clots, stroke, heart attack; banned |
| Blood doping | Removing blood, storing it, and reinfusing red cells before competition: more red cells | As EPO; infection; banned |
| Altitude training | Low pO₂ at altitude stimulates natural EPO release and more red blood cells | Legal; altitude sickness; effect wears off |
| Creatine supplements | Increase phosphocreatine stores: more power in short bursts | Legal; water retention, stomach upset; possible kidney strain at high doses |
| Stimulants (caffeine, amphetamines) | Increase alertness, heart rate, reduce fatigue | Some banned; addiction, heart problems |
| Narcotic painkillers | Mask pain, so injured athletes compete | Further injury, addiction; banned in competition |

Testing for steroids and EPO uses urine and blood samples (file 30).

**Ethical positions.**

*Against use:*

- It is unfair (cheating) to athletes who do not use them.
- Health risks; athletes may feel pressured to use them to compete.
- Athletes are role models for young people.
- Rules are agreed by all who take part; breaking them is wrong (a deontological, rule-based view).

*For allowing use:*

- Athletes are adults who can choose for themselves (autonomy), if informed of the risks.
- If everyone could use them, the playing field would be level; legal, monitored use might be safer than secret use.
- Other advantages are allowed (altitude training, expensive equipment), so the line is arbitrary.

A utilitarian view weighs the total benefits (entertainment, records) against total harms (health, fairness).

### Check questions

1. Explain how ventilation rate is increased during exercise. (4 marks)
2. Explain why breathing rate stays high for several minutes after exercise has stopped. (4 marks)
3. Calculate cardiac output for a heart rate of 150 beats min⁻¹ and stroke volume of 120 cm³, in dm³ min⁻¹. (1 mark)
4. Give three long-term effects of endurance training on the cardiovascular system. (3 marks)
5. Explain why VO₂ max is a useful measure of aerobic fitness. (2 marks)
6. Describe how a spirometer trace can be used to find oxygen consumption. (3 marks)
7. Give two advantages of keyhole surgery compared with open surgery for an athlete. (2 marks)
8. Explain how EPO improves endurance performance and why it is dangerous. (3 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. CO₂ rises, pH of blood / cerebrospinal fluid falls (1); detected by chemoreceptors in medulla / aorta / carotid arteries (1); impulses to ventilation centre in medulla (1); more impulses to intercostal muscles and diaphragm, increasing rate and depth (1).
2. EPOC / oxygen debt being repaid (1); lactate oxidised / converted to glucose in liver (1); phosphocreatine / ATP resynthesised (1); myoglobin / haemoglobin reoxygenated / metabolic rate still raised (1).
3. 150 × 120 = 18 000 cm³ = 18 dm³ min⁻¹ (1).
4. Any three: larger heart / thicker ventricle wall; larger stroke volume; lower resting heart rate; more capillaries; more red blood cells / blood volume; lower blood pressure.
5. Measures maximum rate of oxygen uptake / use (1); reflects ability of lungs, heart, blood and muscles together to supply and use oxygen (1).
6. CO₂ absorbed by soda lime (1); volume in spirometer falls as oxygen is used (1); gradient of overall fall in trace / volume decrease per minute = oxygen consumption (1).
7. Any two: smaller cuts / less tissue damage; less pain / blood loss; lower infection risk; faster recovery / return to sport.
8. Stimulates bone marrow to make more red blood cells (1); more oxygen carried to muscles, more aerobic respiration (1); blood thicker / more viscous, risk of clots / stroke / heart attack (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/biology/18-stimuli-receptors-and-control-of-heart-rate.md -->

## Survival and response, receptors and control of heart rate

Organisms detect changes and respond to them. This file covers plant tropisms and IAA in outline, taxes, kineses and reflexes, receptors (the Pacinian corpuscle, rods and cones), and how the heartbeat is started and its rate controlled, with AQA required practical 10.

### Overview

A-level only for AQA. Plant hormones in depth are in file 31; the eye as an organ and visual problems in file 35; the organisation of the nervous system and reflex arcs in file 32; nerve impulses in file 19; behaviour (Eduqas, Edexcel A habituation) in file 48.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.6.1.1 to 3.6.1.3, RP10. Edexcel A 7.8, 7.9 (part), 8.5. Edexcel B 4.4 iv, v, 9.7 ii, iii, 9.8 i. OCR A 3.1.2(g), (h), 5.1.3(a), 5.1.5(k). OCR B 2.2.1(d), (e), (h), 5.3.1(b). Eduqas C3.2(c) (part).
- OCR A, OCR B, Edexcel A and Edexcel B ask for electrocardiograms (ECGs); AQA does not. Included below.
- OCR A uses the Pacinian corpuscle as its example of a transducer; Edexcel B and Edexcel A use rod cells and rhodopsin (included here; retina structure in file 35).
- Taxes, kineses and tropisms appear in AQA here, in Eduqas OC.3 (file 48) and in OCR A 5.1.5 (file 31).

### 3.6.1.1 Survival and response

*Refs: AQA 3.6.1.1, RP10. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Organisms increase their chance of survival by responding to changes in their environment.
- In flowering plants, specific growth factors move from growing regions to other tissues, where they regulate growth in response to directional stimuli.
- The effect of different concentrations of indoleacetic acid (IAA) on cell elongation in the roots and shoots of flowering plants as an explanation of gravitropism and phototropism in flowering plants.
- Taxes and kineses as simple responses that can maintain a mobile organism in a favourable environment.
- The protective effect of a simple reflex, exemplified by a three-neurone simple reflex in a mammal. The importance of simple reflexes in avoiding damage to the body.

#### Explained

**Tropisms** are growth responses of plants towards or away from a directional stimulus. IAA (an auxin) is made in shoot and root tips and moves to the cells behind them.

- *Phototropism in shoots:* light from one side causes IAA to move to the shaded side. IAA makes shoot cells elongate (by loosening cell walls, in an acid-growth process). The shaded side grows faster, so the shoot bends towards light: positive phototropism.
- *Phototropism in roots:* IAA also moves to the shaded side, but in roots high IAA concentrations inhibit elongation. The shaded side grows less, so the root bends away from light: negative phototropism.
- *Gravitropism:* IAA gathers on the lower side. In shoots the lower side elongates more, so the shoot grows upwards (negative gravitropism). In roots the lower side elongates less, so the root grows downwards (positive gravitropism).

**Taxes and kineses.**

- A *taxis* is a directional movement towards (positive) or away from (negative) a stimulus. Examples: woodlice move away from light (negative phototaxis), keeping them hidden from predators and in damp places; single-celled algae move towards light.
- A *kinesis* is a non-directional response where the rate of movement or turning changes with the intensity of a stimulus. Example: woodlice in dry air move faster and turn less, so they are more likely to leave; in humid air they slow down and turn more, so they stay.

**Simple reflexes.** A reflex is a rapid, automatic response to a stimulus. The three-neurone reflex arc: receptor → sensory neurone → relay neurone (in the spinal cord) → motor neurone → effector (muscle). Example: withdrawing a hand from a hot object. Because the pathway does not go through the brain's conscious areas and has few synapses, it is fast and protects the body from damage before the brain even becomes aware.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying light "destroys" IAA on the lit side. The accepted explanation for AQA is that IAA moves to the shaded side.
- Saying IAA "inhibits growth" in all tissues. It promotes elongation in shoots and, at high concentration, inhibits it in roots.
- Calling a kinesis a "directional response".

#### Practical

**AQA required practical 10**: investigation into the effect of an environmental variable on the movement of an animal using either a choice chamber or a maze.

*Choice chamber.* A dish divided into sections with a gauze platform. Put a drying agent (silica gel or anhydrous calcium chloride) under one side and water under the other to compare humidity; or cover half with black card to compare light. Place ten woodlice in the centre; after 10 minutes count how many are on each side. Repeat with fresh woodlice.

*Maze.* Record which way an animal (maggot, woodlouse) turns at a T-junction when one arm is dark or damp.

Controls: the same number and species of animal; the same time; only one factor changed (cover the whole chamber in the humidity experiment so light is the same); return animals after each trial. Analysis: a chi-squared test compares the observed numbers with those expected if there were no preference (file 21, file 26). Ethics: handle animals gently with a soft brush, keep them for a short time, return them to their habitat.

### 3.6.1.2 Receptors

*Refs: AQA 3.6.1.2 · Edexcel A 8.5 · Edexcel B 9.7 ii, iii · OCR A 5.1.3(a). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Receptors respond only to specific stimuli. Stimulation of a receptor leads to the establishment of a generator potential.
- The basic structure of a Pacinian corpuscle as an example of a receptor. How a Pacinian corpuscle produces a generator potential as a result of a pressure stimulus, by the opening of stretch-mediated sodium ion channels.
- Differences in sensitivity to light, sensitivity to colour and visual acuity as explained by differences in the optic pigments of rods and cones and the connections rods and cones make in the optic nerve.

#### Explained

Receptors are **transducers**: they convert energy from a stimulus (pressure, light, chemicals) into electrical energy, as a generator potential.

**Pacinian corpuscle.** Found deep in the skin (fingertips, soles) and joints; detects pressure. It is a sensory neurone ending wrapped in many layers of connective tissue (lamellae) separated by gel.

1. At rest, the stretch-mediated sodium ion channels in the neurone membrane are too narrow for Na⁺ to pass, so there is a resting potential.
2. Pressure deforms the lamellae, which press on the neurone ending and stretch its membrane.
3. The stretch-mediated sodium channels widen and Na⁺ diffuses in.
4. This depolarises the membrane: a generator potential.
5. If the generator potential reaches the threshold, an action potential is sent along the sensory neurone.

Stronger pressure opens more channels, giving a larger generator potential and a higher frequency of action potentials.

**Rods and cones.** Light-sensitive cells in the retina.

| | Rods | Cones |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Pigment | Rhodopsin | Iodopsin (three types, each most sensitive to red, green or blue light) |
| Sensitivity to light | High: work in dim light | Low: need bright light |
| Colour | Black and white only | Colour vision |
| Connections | Many rods share one bipolar neurone (retinal convergence) | Each cone has its own bipolar neurone |
| Visual acuity | Low | High |
| Distribution | Mostly at the edges (periphery) of the retina | Concentrated in the fovea |

*Why rods are sensitive:* rhodopsin is broken down by low light intensity; several rods connect to one bipolar cell, so their small generator potentials add together (summation) to reach the threshold.

*Why rods give low acuity:* because several rods feed one neurone, the brain cannot tell which rod was stimulated, so two close points of light are seen as one.

*Why cones give high acuity:* each cone has its own route to the brain, so separate points are distinguished. But iodopsin needs high light intensity to be broken down, and each cone alone must reach threshold.

*Colour:* the brain compares how much each of the three cone types is stimulated.

**Rhodopsin** (Edexcel B, Edexcel A). Rhodopsin is retinal (a light-absorbing molecule) bound to opsin (a protein). Light changes retinal from *cis* to *trans*, so it no longer fits opsin and rhodopsin splits ("bleaching"). In the dark, sodium ions continually enter the rod and it releases an inhibitory neurotransmitter (glutamate) that stops the bipolar cell firing. When rhodopsin bleaches, a cascade closes sodium channels; the rod hyperpolarises and stops releasing glutamate, so the bipolar cell depolarises and an impulse is sent along the optic nerve. In bright light rhodopsin is bleached faster than it is remade, which is why it takes time to see after going into a dark room.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying the Pacinian corpuscle detects "touch" without saying pressure deforms the membrane.
- Saying cones are found at the edge of the retina.
- Saying rods are more sensitive "because they have more pigment" rather than because of convergence.
- Saying generator potentials are "all or nothing". Action potentials are; generator potentials vary in size.

### 3.6.1.3 Control of heart rate

*Refs: AQA 3.6.1.3 · Edexcel A 7.8, 7.9 · Edexcel B 4.4 iv, v, 9.8 i · OCR A 3.1.2(g), (h), 5.1.5(k) · OCR B 2.2.1(d), (e), (h), 5.3.1(b) · Eduqas C3.2(c). Level: A-level only (AS for OCR A, OCR B and Edexcel B on the initiation of the heartbeat and ECGs).*

#### What you need to know

- Myogenic stimulation of the heart and transmission of a subsequent wave of electrical activity. Roles of the sinoatrial node (SAN), atrioventricular node (AVN) and bundle of His.
- The roles and locations of chemoreceptors and pressure receptors and the roles of the autonomic nervous system and effectors in controlling heart rate.

#### Explained

**Myogenic.** Cardiac muscle contracts on its own, without nerve impulses. The nervous system only changes the rate.

**The sequence.**

1. The SAN, in the wall of the right atrium (the pacemaker), sends out a wave of electrical activity (excitation) at regular intervals.
2. The wave spreads across both atria, which contract (atrial systole).
3. A layer of non-conducting tissue between the atria and ventricles stops the wave passing directly to the ventricles.
4. The AVN picks up the wave and, after a short delay (about 0.1 s), passes it on. The delay lets the atria empty fully before the ventricles contract.
5. The wave passes down the bundle of His (in the septum) to the base (apex) of the ventricles.
6. It spreads up through Purkyne (Purkinje) fibres in the ventricle walls, so the ventricles contract from the bottom up, pushing blood up into the arteries.

**Changing the rate.** The cardiac control centre is in the medulla oblongata of the brain. It sends impulses along two parts of the autonomic nervous system:

- Sympathetic nerves (accelerator) to the SAN increase the rate (and force).
- Parasympathetic nerves (the vagus nerve, inhibitory) to the SAN decrease the rate.

Receptors:

| Receptor | Location | Detects | Response |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Chemoreceptors | Aorta, carotid arteries, medulla | Rise in CO₂ concentration (fall in blood pH), as during exercise | More impulses to the medulla, more sympathetic impulses to the SAN, heart rate rises; extra CO₂ is removed faster in the lungs |
| Pressure receptors (baroreceptors) | Aorta and carotid arteries | Rise in blood pressure | More parasympathetic impulses to the SAN, heart rate falls |
| | | Fall in blood pressure | More sympathetic impulses, heart rate rises |

Adrenaline from the adrenal glands also raises heart rate (OCR A 5.1.5(k), Edexcel B 9.8 ii; file 32).

**Electrocardiograms** (OCR A, OCR B, Edexcel A, Edexcel B). Electrodes on the skin record the electrical activity of the heart.

- P wave: depolarisation (excitation) of the atria, leading to atrial systole.
- QRS complex: depolarisation of the ventricles, leading to ventricular systole. Large, because the ventricles have more muscle.
- T wave: repolarisation of the ventricles (diastole).
- The P–R interval includes the AVN delay.

Abnormal traces:

- *Tachycardia:* heart rate over 100 beats per minute at rest.
- *Bradycardia:* under 60 beats per minute (normal in trained athletes).
- *Ectopic heartbeat:* an early, extra beat, followed by a pause.
- *Atrial fibrillation:* atria twitch irregularly instead of contracting; no clear P waves and an irregular rhythm of QRS complexes.
- A raised or depressed S–T segment can show a heart attack (damage to ventricle muscle).

#### Worked example

On an ECG the paper moves at 25 mm s⁻¹ and the distance between two R peaks is 20 mm. Calculate the heart rate.

1. Time per beat = 20 ÷ 25 = 0.8 s.
2. Heart rate = 60 ÷ 0.8 = 75 beats min⁻¹.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying the brain starts each heartbeat.
- Saying the AVN "slows the heart rate". It delays the wave so atria empty first.
- Saying the SAN is in the left atrium, or that the bundle of His carries blood.
- Saying chemoreceptors detect oxygen in the AQA answer: the main stimulus at A-level is CO₂ / pH.
- Mixing up which branch of the autonomic system speeds the heart.

### Check questions

1. Explain how IAA causes a shoot to grow towards light. (3 marks)
2. Woodlice in a choice chamber gathered in the damp half. Explain whether this is a taxis or a kinesis and why it aids survival. (3 marks)
3. Describe how a Pacinian corpuscle produces a generator potential. (4 marks)
4. Explain why rods give low visual acuity but high sensitivity to light. (4 marks)
5. Describe how a wave of electrical activity spreads through the heart. (5 marks)
6. During exercise, CO₂ concentration in the blood rises. Explain how this increases heart rate. (4 marks)
7. On an ECG with paper speed 25 mm s⁻¹, R peaks are 15 mm apart. Calculate heart rate. (2 marks)
8. Explain the importance of the delay at the AVN. (2 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. IAA moves to shaded side (1); stimulates cell elongation on shaded side (1); shaded side grows faster, so shoot bends towards light (1).
2. Could be taxis (directional movement to damp) or kinesis (slower movement / more turning in damp); credit either with correct justification (1); damp prevents water loss / desiccation (1); also dark / hidden from predators (1).
3. Pressure deforms lamellae / stretches membrane (1); stretch-mediated sodium channels open (1); Na⁺ diffuses in (1); depolarisation / generator potential (1).
4. Many rods connect to one bipolar neurone (1); brain cannot distinguish separate points / low acuity (1); generator potentials of several rods summate to reach threshold (1); rhodopsin broken down by low light intensity (1).
5. SAN sends wave over atria, causing atrial contraction (1); non-conducting tissue prevents direct spread to ventricles (1); AVN delays then passes wave (1); down bundle of His to apex (1); up Purkyne fibres so ventricles contract from base upwards (1).
6. Chemoreceptors in aorta / carotid arteries / medulla detect fall in pH (1); more impulses to cardiac centre in medulla (1); more impulses along sympathetic nerve (1); SAN increases rate of impulses (1).
7. 15 ÷ 25 = 0.6 s per beat (1); 60 ÷ 0.6 = 100 beats min⁻¹ (1).
8. Atria contract and empty before ventricles contract (1); so ventricles fill fully / maximum blood ejected (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/biology/20-homeostasis-blood-glucose-and-water.md -->

## Homeostasis: blood glucose and blood water potential

Homeostasis keeps the internal environment within narrow limits. This file covers negative and positive feedback, the control of blood glucose by insulin, glucagon and adrenaline, diabetes, and the control of blood water potential by the kidney and ADH, with AQA required practical 11.

### Overview

A-level only. Temperature control is in file 33, with diabetes treatment and management for OCR A and OCR B. The liver, urea formation, kidney failure and dialysis are in file 30. Hormones in general and the adrenal glands are in file 32.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.6.4.1 to 3.6.4.3, RP11. Edexcel A 7.11, 7.12 (part). Edexcel B 9.1, 9.9 i, iii, iv. OCR A 5.1.1(c), 5.1.2(c), (d), 5.1.4(c) to (e). OCR B 5.3.1(a), 5.3.2(a) to (c), 5.3.3(c), (d). Eduqas C3.4(a) to (g).
- OCR A asks for the histology of the pancreas (islets of Langerhans) and the kidney (5.1.4(c), 5.1.2(c)), with a kidney dissection.
- Edexcel B asks for aquaporins and osmoreceptors in the hypothalamus by name (9.9 iv); AQA asks for the same mechanism.
- Edexcel A 7.11 asks for positive feedback as well as negative; AQA asks for both.
- Eduqas C3.4(e) asks for adaptations of proximal tubule cells (microvilli, mitochondria).
- OCR A and OCR B ask how insulin secretion is controlled in β cells (potassium channels, calcium ions); included below.

### 3.6.4.1 Principles of homeostasis and negative feedback

*Refs: AQA 3.6.4.1 · Edexcel A 7.11, 7.12 · Edexcel B 9.1 i to iii · OCR A 5.1.1(c) · OCR B 5.3.1(a) · Eduqas C3.4(a), (b). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Homeostasis in mammals involves physiological control systems that maintain the internal environment within restricted limits.
- The importance of maintaining a stable core temperature and stable blood pH in relation to enzyme activity.
- The importance of maintaining a stable blood glucose concentration in terms of availability of respiratory substrate and of the water potential of the blood.
- Negative feedback restores systems to their original level. The possession of separate mechanisms involving negative feedback controls departures in different directions from the original state, giving a greater degree of control.
- Positive feedback results in greater departures from the original levels. Positive feedback is often associated with a breakdown of control systems, eg in temperature control.

#### Explained

**Why keep things stable.**

- *Temperature:* too low and enzymes work slowly (too little kinetic energy); too high and they denature.
- *pH:* changes break ionic and hydrogen bonds in enzymes, changing the active site.
- *Blood glucose:* too low and cells (especially brain cells) lack respiratory substrate; too high and blood water potential falls, so water leaves cells by osmosis.

Homeostasis is a dynamic equilibrium (Edexcel B): values fluctuate around a set point but are kept within limits.

**Components.** A receptor detects a change → a coordinator (often the brain or a gland) → an effector (muscle or gland) responds.

**Negative feedback.** The response reverses the change, bringing the value back to the set point. Having separate mechanisms for a rise and a fall (insulin and glucagon; sweating and shivering) gives finer control than one mechanism that is simply switched on or off.

**Positive feedback.** The response increases the change. Examples: the opening of voltage-gated sodium channels during depolarisation; oxytocin in childbirth (contractions cause more oxytocin, causing stronger contractions); blood clotting. In hypothermia, a low body temperature slows metabolism, so less heat is made and temperature falls further: control breaks down.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying negative feedback "keeps things exactly constant".
- Saying positive feedback is "good feedback".

### 3.6.4.2 Control of blood glucose concentration

*Refs: AQA 3.6.4.2, RP11 · OCR A 5.1.4(c) to (e) · OCR B 5.3.2(a) to (c) · Eduqas C3.4(f) (part). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- The factors that influence blood glucose concentration.
- The role of the liver in glycogenesis, glycogenolysis and gluconeogenesis.
- The action of insulin by: attaching to receptors on the surfaces of target cells; controlling the uptake of glucose by regulating the inclusion of channel proteins in the surface membranes of target cells; activating enzymes involved in the conversion of glucose to glycogen.
- The action of glucagon by: attaching to receptors on the surfaces of target cells; activating enzymes involved in the conversion of glycogen to glucose; activating enzymes involved in the conversion of glycerol and amino acids into glucose.
- The role of adrenaline by: attaching to receptors on the surfaces of target cells; activating enzymes involved in the conversion of glycogen to glucose.
- The second messenger model of adrenaline and glucagon action, involving adenylate cyclase, cyclic AMP (cAMP) and protein kinase.
- The causes of types I and II diabetes and their control by insulin and/or manipulation of the diet.
- Evaluate the positions of health advisers and the food industry in relation to the increased incidence of type II diabetes.

#### Explained

**What changes blood glucose.** Rises after eating carbohydrate. Falls with exercise or fasting (glucose used in respiration).

**Three liver processes.**

- **Glycogenesis:** glucose → glycogen (when glucose is high).
- **Glycogenolysis:** glycogen → glucose (when glucose is low).
- **Gluconeogenesis:** making glucose from non-carbohydrates, such as glycerol and amino acids (when glycogen runs low).

**The pancreas.** The islets of Langerhans contain α cells (make glucagon) and β cells (make insulin). The rest of the pancreas (acinar tissue, in clusters around ducts) makes digestive enzymes. In a stained section, islets are lighter-staining patches (OCR A).

**When blood glucose rises:**

1. β cells detect the rise. (OCR A, OCR B: glucose enters β cells and is respired, raising ATP; ATP closes K⁺ channels, depolarising the membrane; voltage-gated Ca²⁺ channels open; Ca²⁺ entry causes vesicles of insulin to be released by exocytosis.)
2. Insulin binds to specific receptors on liver and muscle cells (and others).
3. Vesicles carrying glucose transporter proteins (GLUT4) fuse with the cell-surface membrane, so more glucose enters by facilitated diffusion.
4. Insulin activates enzymes that convert glucose to glycogen (glycogenesis) and to fat; respiration rate increases.
5. Blood glucose falls; less insulin is released (negative feedback).

**When blood glucose falls:**

1. α cells detect the fall and secrete glucagon.
2. Glucagon binds to receptors on liver cells only (muscle has no glucagon receptors).
3. It activates enzymes for glycogenolysis and gluconeogenesis.
4. Glucose is released into the blood; blood glucose rises; less glucagon is secreted.

**Adrenaline**, released by the adrenal glands under stress or exercise, also binds to liver cells and activates glycogenolysis, raising blood glucose to supply muscles.

**Second messenger model** (for adrenaline and glucagon, which cannot enter the cell).

1. The hormone (first messenger) binds to its receptor on the cell-surface membrane.
2. This changes the shape of the receptor, which activates the enzyme adenylate cyclase on the inside.
3. Adenylate cyclase converts ATP into cyclic AMP (cAMP), the second messenger.
4. cAMP activates protein kinase A.
5. Protein kinase A activates, through a cascade, the enzymes that break down glycogen.

The cascade amplifies the signal: one hormone molecule leads to many glucose molecules being released.

**Diabetes.**

| | Type I | Type II |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Cause | Autoimmune attack destroys β cells; no insulin made | Receptors on target cells become less responsive to insulin; β cells may also make less |
| Onset | Usually in childhood | Usually in adults; linked to obesity, poor diet, lack of exercise, family history, ethnicity |
| Control | Insulin injections (or pump), matched to diet and exercise; regular blood glucose tests | Diet (less sugar and refined carbohydrate), exercise, weight loss; drugs such as metformin; sometimes insulin |

Insulin cannot be taken by mouth: it is a protein and would be digested.

**Type II and society.** Health advisers recommend less sugar and more exercise, and support measures such as a tax on sugary drinks and clearer labels. The food industry has reduced sugar in some products, but points out that it provides choice and that sweeteners have their own questions; some argue that pressure has been resisted for commercial reasons. When evaluating, look at who funded the evidence and whether claims are supported by data.

#### Common mistakes

- Confusing glycogen, glucagon and glycogenesis.
- Saying insulin "turns glucose into glycogen". Insulin activates the enzymes that do this.
- Saying glucagon acts on muscle cells.
- Saying type I diabetes is caused by eating too much sugar.

#### Practical

**AQA required practical 11**: production of a dilution series of a glucose solution and use of colorimetric techniques to produce a calibration curve with which to identify the concentration of glucose in an unknown "urine" sample.

1. Make a dilution series of glucose (for example 0, 2, 4, 6, 8, 10 mmol dm⁻³) from a stock solution (file 01 shows the calculation).
2. Add the same volume of Benedict's reagent (in excess) to the same volume of each solution. Heat in a water bath at the same temperature for the same time.
3. Allow to cool; filter or centrifuge out the precipitate if using the remaining blue colour, or use quantitative Benedict's reagent.
4. Zero the colorimeter with distilled water; use a red filter; measure absorbance (or transmission) of each.
5. Plot absorbance against glucose concentration: the calibration curve.
6. Test the unknown samples the same way and read their concentrations from the curve.

Risks: Benedict's reagent is an irritant; hot water bath. Limitations: colorimeter readings are affected by cloudiness; the method only measures reducing sugars; the unknown must lie within the range of standards.

### 3.6.4.3 Control of blood water potential

*Refs: AQA 3.6.4.3 · Edexcel B 9.9 i, iii, iv · OCR A 5.1.2(c), (d) · OCR B 5.3.3(c), (d) · Eduqas C3.4(c) to (g). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- The structure of the nephron and its role in: the formation of glomerular filtrate; reabsorption of glucose and water by the proximal convoluted tubule; maintaining a gradient of sodium ions in the medulla by the loop of Henle; reabsorption of water by the distal convoluted tubule and collecting ducts.
- The roles of the hypothalamus, posterior pituitary and antidiuretic hormone (ADH) in osmoregulation.

#### Explained

**The kidney.** The renal artery brings blood; the renal vein takes it away; the ureter carries urine to the bladder. Outer cortex; inner medulla; the pelvis where urine collects. Each kidney has about a million nephrons.

**The nephron.**

| Part | Where | Role |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Glomerulus and Bowman's (renal) capsule | Cortex | Ultrafiltration |
| Proximal convoluted tubule (PCT) | Cortex | Selective reabsorption of glucose, amino acids, most water and ions |
| Loop of Henle | Dips into medulla | Creates a high sodium ion concentration (low water potential) in the medulla |
| Distal convoluted tubule (DCT) | Cortex | Fine-tunes water and ion balance |
| Collecting duct | Cortex to medulla | Water reabsorbed under the control of ADH |

**Ultrafiltration.**

- The afferent arteriole entering the glomerulus is wider than the efferent arteriole leaving it, so hydrostatic pressure in the glomerulus is high.
- Water, glucose, amino acids, urea and ions are forced out through the gaps in the capillary endothelium, through the basement membrane (the actual filter, which holds back proteins) and between the podocytes (cells of the capsule with foot-like processes) into the capsule.
- Blood cells and large proteins stay in the blood. The fluid in the capsule is the glomerular filtrate.

**Proximal convoluted tubule.** All glucose and amino acids, and about 85% of water and much of the sodium, are reabsorbed here.

- Na⁺ is actively pumped out of PCT cells into the blood, keeping Na⁺ low inside the cells.
- Na⁺ diffuses from the filtrate into the cells by co-transport, carrying glucose (or amino acids).
- Glucose diffuses out into the blood by facilitated diffusion.
- Water follows by osmosis.
- (Eduqas) Adaptations of PCT cells: microvilli give a large surface area; many mitochondria supply ATP for active transport; many co-transporter proteins; close to capillaries.

If blood glucose is very high (untreated diabetes), there are not enough co-transporters to reabsorb it all, and glucose appears in urine.

**Loop of Henle (countercurrent multiplier).**

1. In the ascending limb, Na⁺ (and Cl⁻) are actively transported out into the medulla. The ascending limb is impermeable to water, so water cannot follow.
2. This makes the tissue fluid of the medulla have a low water potential.
3. The descending limb is permeable to water. Water leaves it by osmosis into the medulla and is carried away by the blood capillaries (vasa recta).
4. The filtrate becomes more concentrated going down, and less concentrated going up.
5. The deepest part of the medulla has the lowest water potential.
6. The collecting duct passes through this region, so water can be drawn out of it by osmosis.

Animals living in deserts (kangaroo rat, file 30) have longer loops of Henle, giving a steeper gradient and more concentrated urine.

**Distal tubule and collecting duct.** Reabsorption of water depends on how permeable their walls are, which ADH controls.

**Osmoregulation with ADH.**

1. Osmoreceptors in the hypothalamus detect a fall in blood water potential (for example after sweating or eating salty food). Water leaves the osmoreceptor cells by osmosis and they shrink, sending impulses.
2. The hypothalamus makes ADH; it passes down neurones to the posterior pituitary gland, which releases it into the blood.
3. ADH binds to receptors on cells of the DCT and collecting duct.
4. This activates a second messenger pathway: vesicles containing **aquaporins** (water channel proteins) fuse with the cell-surface membrane facing the filtrate.
5. More water is reabsorbed by osmosis into the medulla and blood. A small volume of concentrated urine is produced.
6. Blood water potential rises; less ADH is released (negative feedback).

When blood water potential is too high (after drinking a lot), less ADH is released; fewer aquaporins are in the membranes; less water is reabsorbed; a large volume of dilute urine is produced.

#### Worked example

The glomerular filtration rate is 125 cm³ min⁻¹. Urine is produced at 1 cm³ min⁻¹. What percentage of filtrate is reabsorbed?

(125 − 1) ÷ 125 × 100 = 99.2%.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying ADH "makes the kidney reabsorb salt".
- Saying the loop of Henle reabsorbs water by active transport. Water always moves by osmosis.
- Saying the descending limb pumps out sodium ions.
- Saying ADH is made in the pituitary. It is made in the hypothalamus and released from the posterior pituitary.
- Saying proteins are filtered then reabsorbed. They are too large to be filtered.

### Check questions

1. Explain why it is important to keep blood glucose concentration within narrow limits. (2 marks)
2. Describe how insulin lowers blood glucose concentration. (4 marks)
3. Describe the second messenger model of glucagon action. (4 marks)
4. Explain why people with type I diabetes cannot take insulin as a tablet. (2 marks)
5. Describe how ultrafiltration happens in the glomerulus. (3 marks)
6. Explain how the loop of Henle creates a low water potential in the medulla. (4 marks)
7. Describe and explain the response of the body to dehydration. (5 marks)
8. In RP11, explain why the same volume of Benedict's reagent and the same heating time must be used for every tube. (2 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Too low: not enough respiratory substrate (for brain) (1); too high: lowers blood water potential, water leaves cells by osmosis (1).
2. Insulin binds to receptors on liver / muscle cells (1); more glucose transporter / channel proteins inserted in membrane (1); more glucose enters cells (1); activates enzymes for glycogenesis / increases respiration (1).
3. Glucagon binds to receptor on liver cell membrane (1); activates adenylate cyclase (1); converts ATP to cAMP (1); cAMP activates protein kinase, which activates enzymes for glycogenolysis (1).
4. Insulin is a protein (1); digested / hydrolysed by proteases in the gut (1).
5. High hydrostatic pressure in glomerulus (afferent wider than efferent) (1); small molecules / water forced out through capillary endothelium, basement membrane and podocytes (1); large proteins and cells remain (1).
6. Na⁺ actively transported out of ascending limb (1); ascending limb impermeable to water (1); medulla has high Na⁺ / low water potential (1); water leaves descending limb by osmosis, concentration greatest deep in medulla (1).
7. Osmoreceptors in hypothalamus detect fall in water potential (1); more ADH released from posterior pituitary (1); ADH binds to receptors on DCT / collecting duct (1); more aquaporins inserted, walls more permeable to water (1); more water reabsorbed by osmosis; small volume of concentrated urine (1).
8. Colour / absorbance depends on these as well as glucose concentration (1); so differences are only due to glucose concentration and the curve applies to the unknown (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/biology/33-thermoregulation-and-endocrine-health.md -->

## Temperature control, thyroxine and managing diabetes

Mammals and birds keep their core temperature almost constant; many other animals rely on their behaviour. This file covers ectotherms and endotherms, how the hypothalamus controls body temperature, thyroxine, measuring body temperature, hypothermia and hyperthermia, and how diabetes is diagnosed, treated and managed.

### Overview

A-level only. Principles of homeostasis and the control of blood glucose are in file 20. AQA does not ask for thermoregulation or diabetes management beyond file 20.

#### Board differences

- OCR A 5.1.1(d), 5.1.4(e), (f). OCR B 5.3.1(c) to (f), 5.3.2(c) to (g). Edexcel A 7.12 (part). Edexcel B 9.9 vi, vii.
- OCR B alone asks for thyroxine, measuring body temperature, hypothermia and hyperthermia, diabetes tests, the care team and the future impact of diabetes.
- OCR A asks for potential treatments for diabetes, including insulin from GM bacteria and stem cells (5.1.4(f)).
- Edexcel A 7.12 and Edexcel B 9.9 vi, vii ask how endotherms make heat and regulate temperature.

### Ectotherms, endotherms and the control of body temperature

*Refs: OCR A 5.1.1(d) · OCR B 5.3.1(c), (e), (f) · Edexcel A 7.12 · Edexcel B 9.9 vi, vii. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- The physiological and behavioural responses involved in temperature control in ectotherms and endotherms.
- How an endotherm produces heat through metabolism and regulates its temperature through the hypothalamus.
- (OCR B) Techniques for measuring body temperature; the causes, symptoms and treatment of hypothermia and hyperthermia.

#### Explained

**Ectotherms** (most fish, amphibians, reptiles, invertebrates) get most of their heat from the environment. Their body temperature varies with it. They control it mainly by **behaviour**:

- basking in the sun; lying on warm rocks; pressing against warm ground
- orienting the body to expose more or less surface to the sun
- seeking shade, burrows or water when hot
- becoming inactive when it is cold.

Some have physiological responses too: changing heart rate; darkening skin; locusts ventilating more to lose water vapour.

Ectotherms need less food (less energy goes into heat), but are less active in cold conditions and more at risk from predators then.

**Endotherms** (mammals, birds) produce heat by metabolism and keep their core temperature nearly constant, regardless of the environment. Heat comes mainly from respiration in the liver and muscles. They can be active in cold conditions, but need much more food.

**Control by the hypothalamus.**

1. **Thermoreceptors** in the hypothalamus detect the temperature of the blood (core temperature). **Peripheral thermoreceptors** in the skin detect external temperature and send impulses to the hypothalamus, giving early warning.
2. The hypothalamus has a heat-loss centre and a heat-gain centre. It sends impulses through the autonomic nervous system to effectors.

| | Too hot | Too cold |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Skin arterioles | **Vasodilation**: arterioles near the skin surface widen, more blood flows through skin capillaries, more heat is radiated | **Vasoconstriction**: arterioles narrow, less blood to skin, less heat lost |
| Sweating | Sweat glands secrete sweat; evaporation uses latent heat from skin | Less sweating |
| Hairs | Erector muscles relax; hairs lie flat | Erector muscles contract; hairs stand up, trapping an insulating layer of air (less effective in humans) |
| Muscles | | **Shivering**: rapid involuntary contractions release heat from respiration |
| Metabolism | Lower metabolic rate | Adrenaline and thyroxine raise metabolic rate |
| Behaviour | Seek shade; remove clothes; panting (dogs); licking fur | Seek warmth; huddle; add clothes |

Brown fat (in babies and hibernating mammals) releases heat directly in respiration instead of making ATP.

**Measuring body temperature** (OCR B 5.3.1(e)). Clinical thermometers (digital probes) under the tongue, in the armpit, rectum or ear (infrared tympanic thermometer measures the eardrum, close to the hypothalamus); forehead infrared thermometers; thermal imaging for many people. Core temperature is about 37 °C; the mouth and armpit read slightly lower; the reading varies with time of day and activity. Monitoring is important in illness (fever), surgery and newborns.

**Hypothermia and hyperthermia** (OCR B 5.3.1(f)).

- **Hypothermia:** core temperature below 35 °C. Causes: cold exposure, wet clothing, immersion in cold water; more likely in the elderly and babies. Symptoms: shivering (stops in severe cases), confusion, slurred speech, slow heart rate and breathing, unconsciousness. As temperature falls, enzymes work slowly, metabolism slows, less heat is made: positive feedback. Treatment: remove wet clothing; warm slowly with blankets and warm drinks (if conscious); warmed fluids by drip in hospital; avoid rapid rewarming of the limbs.
- **Hyperthermia** (heat exhaustion and heat stroke): core temperature above about 38 to 40 °C. Causes: hot, humid conditions, heavy exercise, dehydration (no water for sweating), some drugs. Symptoms: headache, dizziness, heavy sweating then hot dry skin, rapid pulse, confusion, seizures. Enzymes may denature; organ damage. Treatment: move to a cool place; cool with water and fans; drink fluids; medical treatment for heat stroke.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying blood vessels "move" closer to or further from the skin.
- Saying capillaries dilate. Arterioles do.
- Saying ectotherms are "cold-blooded" and cannot control their temperature at all.

### Thyroxine

*Refs: OCR B 5.3.1(d). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- The regulation of thyroxine release and the effect of thyroxine on metabolic rate.

#### Explained

Thyroxine is a hormone made by the thyroid gland in the neck, containing iodine. It increases the metabolic rate of most cells (more respiration and heat), and is needed for normal growth and brain development.

**Control by negative feedback.**

1. When metabolic rate or temperature falls, the hypothalamus releases TRH (thyrotropin-releasing hormone).
2. TRH stimulates the anterior pituitary to release TSH (thyroid-stimulating hormone).
3. TSH stimulates the thyroid to release thyroxine.
4. Rising thyroxine inhibits the release of TRH and TSH, so thyroxine levels stay in a narrow range.

Cold exposure increases thyroxine release.

**Disorders.** Hypothyroidism (too little thyroxine): tiredness, weight gain, feeling cold; treated with synthetic thyroxine tablets. Hyperthyroidism (too much): weight loss, rapid heart rate, feeling hot, anxiety; treated with drugs, radioactive iodine or surgery. Lack of iodine in the diet causes a goitre (swollen thyroid), because low thyroxine removes the negative feedback and TSH keeps stimulating the gland.

### Diagnosing, treating and managing diabetes

*Refs: OCR A 5.1.4(e), (f) · OCR B 5.3.2(c) to (g). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- The differences between type 1 and type 2 diabetes (file 20 covers causes).
- (OCR B) The fasting blood glucose test, glucose tolerance test, HbA1c test and urine tests.
- The treatment and management of type 1 and type 2 diabetes; potential treatments, including insulin from GM bacteria and stem cells.
- (OCR B) The care team for people with diabetes; the future impact of diabetes on human health and the NHS.

#### Explained

**Types.** Type 1: autoimmune destruction of β cells; no insulin. Type 2: cells become resistant to insulin and the pancreas may later make less. (OCR B also notes *gestational diabetes*, which develops in pregnancy and usually goes after birth, but raises later risk of type 2.)

**Tests** (OCR B 5.3.2(d)).

- *Fasting blood glucose:* blood glucose after no food for 8 hours or more. Normal below about 6 mmol dm⁻³; 7 or above suggests diabetes.
- *Glucose tolerance test:* after fasting, the person drinks a set amount of glucose, and blood glucose is measured at intervals (usually after 2 hours). In diabetes, glucose rises higher and stays high for longer, because it is not taken up into cells.
- *HbA1c test:* measures the proportion of haemoglobin with glucose attached (glycated). Red blood cells last about 3 months, so it shows the average blood glucose over the last 2 to 3 months. Used to diagnose and to check control.
- *Biosensors:* handheld blood glucose meters use a biosensor: glucose oxidase immobilised on a test strip oxidises glucose in a drop of blood, and the reaction produces a small electric current proportional to the glucose concentration, shown as a reading (file 01). Continuous glucose monitors use a sensor under the skin.
- *Urine test:* dipsticks with glucose oxidase show glucose in urine (glucose exceeds the kidney's reabsorption capacity). Ketones in urine show the body is breaking down fat because glucose cannot enter cells (dangerous in type 1).

**Treatment.**

- *Type 1:* insulin injections several times a day (or an insulin pump), with doses matched to food intake and exercise; regular blood glucose monitoring (finger-prick testing or continuous glucose monitors). Too much insulin causes hypoglycaemia (low glucose: shaking, confusion, unconsciousness; treated with sugar or a glucagon injection). Pancreas or islet cell transplants are possible in some cases.
- *Type 2:* lose weight; eat less refined sugar and more complex carbohydrates and fibre; exercise regularly (increases insulin sensitivity). Drugs such as metformin (reduces glucose release by the liver and increases sensitivity); later, insulin if needed.

**Sources of insulin** (OCR A 5.1.4(f)). Insulin was once extracted from the pancreases of pigs and cattle, which risked allergic reactions and ethical or religious objections, and supply was limited. Now human insulin is made by GM bacteria or yeast (file 25): identical to human insulin, cheap, made in large amounts, fewer side effects, and acceptable to more people. Modified insulins act faster or for longer.

**Future treatments.** Stem cells grown into β cells and implanted (possibly in a protective capsule to avoid immune attack); an artificial pancreas (a glucose monitor linked to an insulin pump by software); gene therapy.

**The care team** (OCR B 5.3.2(f)). A GP, diabetes specialist nurse, consultant (diabetologist), dietitian, podiatrist (foot care: diabetes damages nerves and blood vessels in the feet), optometrist (eye screening for diabetic retinopathy), pharmacist and psychologist. Regular checks for complications: eyes, kidneys, nerves, heart.

**Future impact** (OCR B 5.3.2(g)). The number of people with type 2 diabetes is rising fast, linked to obesity and inactive lifestyles. Effects: more complications (heart disease, stroke, blindness, kidney failure, amputations); a large and growing share of NHS spending; lost working days. Prevention (healthy diet, exercise, weight loss, sugar taxes, NHS prevention programmes) is cheaper than treatment.

#### Worked example

In a glucose tolerance test, person A's blood glucose was 5.0 mmol dm⁻³ fasting and 6.5 at 2 hours; person B's was 7.4 fasting and 13.2 at 2 hours. Interpret.

A is normal: glucose rose slightly and fell back as insulin caused uptake into cells. B has a fasting level above 7 and a 2-hour level above 11.1, which suggests diabetes: glucose is not taken up from the blood efficiently.

### Check questions

1. Describe two behavioural ways a lizard can raise its body temperature. (2 marks)
2. Explain how vasoconstriction helps a person conserve heat. (3 marks)
3. Explain why shivering raises body temperature. (2 marks)
4. Explain why hypothermia can become life-threatening once core temperature falls below a certain point. (3 marks)
5. Describe how thyroxine release is controlled by negative feedback. (4 marks)
6. Explain why an HbA1c test is useful in monitoring diabetes. (2 marks)
7. Give two advantages of insulin made by GM bacteria over insulin from pigs. (2 marks)
8. Suggest why type 2 diabetes is expected to have a growing effect on the NHS. (3 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Any two: basking in sun; lying on warm rock; orientating body to expose more surface area to sun; moving out of shade.
2. Arterioles near the skin constrict (1); less blood flows through skin capillaries (1); less heat lost by radiation from skin (1).
3. Rapid muscle contractions increase respiration (1); respiration releases heat (1).
4. Enzymes slow as temperature falls / less kinetic energy (1); metabolism / respiration slows, so less heat released (1); positive feedback, temperature falls further (1).
5. Hypothalamus releases TRH (1); stimulates anterior pituitary to release TSH (1); TSH stimulates thyroid to release thyroxine (1); high thyroxine inhibits TRH / TSH release (1).
6. Shows average blood glucose over 2 to 3 months / lifetime of red blood cells (1); not affected by a single meal / shows long-term control (1).
7. Any two: identical to human insulin / fewer allergic reactions; cheaper; large quantities; no ethical / religious objections.
8. Rising obesity / inactive lifestyles increase cases (1); complications need long-term treatment (1); high cost / more staff / resources needed (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/biology/32-nervous-and-hormonal-systems.md -->

## Organisation of the nervous and hormonal systems

Multicellular organisms coordinate their cells using nerves and hormones. This file covers cell signalling, types of neurone, the organisation of the nervous system, the spinal cord and reflexes, the regions of the brain, the endocrine system, the adrenal glands and the fight-or-flight response.

### Overview

A-level only. AQA covers the three-neurone reflex in file 18 and nerve impulses in file 19, but not the brain or the autonomic system in this detail. Hormone action on blood glucose and ADH is in file 20; brain imaging, development and drugs in file 34; the eye in file 35.

#### Board differences

- OCR A 5.1.1(a), (b), 5.1.3(b), 5.1.4(a), (b), 5.1.5(g) to (k). OCR B 5.2.1(a), (b), (f). Edexcel A 8.1, 8.2, 8.7, 8.8. Edexcel B 9.2 i, ii, 9.4 i to v, 9.8 ii. Eduqas C3.5(a) to (e), OC.1(a) to (d).
- Eduqas asks for the spinal cord (including drawing a transverse section), spinal nerves and the nerve net of Cnidaria (C3.5(b), (c), (e)). Edexcel B asks for spinal cord structure (9.4 ii).
- Edexcel B asks for the two modes of hormone action (second messenger; steroid hormones acting on gene expression) (9.2 ii).
- OCR A asks for the adrenal glands and the fight-or-flight response; Edexcel B for adrenaline release by the autonomic system.
- Brain regions are asked by OCR A, OCR B, Edexcel A, Edexcel B and Eduqas Option C. Language areas and the motor and sensory cortex for Eduqas are in file 34.

### Communication and cell signalling

*Refs: OCR A 5.1.1(a), (b) · Edexcel A 8.7 · Edexcel B 9.2 i, ii · OCR A 5.1.4(a). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- The need for communication systems in multicellular organisms.
- Communication between cells by cell signalling.
- Endocrine communication by hormones; the difference between nervous and hormonal coordination.
- (Edexcel B) The two main modes of action of hormones.

#### Explained

Cells in a multicellular organism are specialised and depend on each other. They must respond to changes in the internal and external environment, and coordinate their activities. Two systems do this: the nervous system and the hormonal (endocrine) system. Both rely on **cell signalling**: one cell releases a chemical that binds to a receptor on a target cell. In nerves the chemical (neurotransmitter) crosses a tiny gap; in hormones it travels in the blood.

| | Nervous | Hormonal |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Signal | Electrical impulses along neurones, chemical at synapses | Chemicals (hormones) in the blood |
| Speed | Very fast | Slower |
| Duration of effect | Short | Can be long-lasting |
| Target | Very localised (specific cells) | Widespread (any cell with receptors) |
| Example | Withdrawal reflex | Growth, menstrual cycle, blood glucose |

**Endocrine glands** secrete hormones directly into the blood (ductless): pituitary, thyroid, pancreas (islets), adrenal glands, ovaries, testes. **Exocrine glands** secrete through ducts (salivary glands; the enzyme-making part of the pancreas).

**Two modes of hormone action** (Edexcel B 9.2 ii).

1. *Peptide and amine hormones* (insulin, glucagon, adrenaline): water-soluble, cannot cross the membrane. They bind to receptors on the cell-surface membrane and act through a **second messenger** such as cAMP (file 20).
2. *Steroid hormones* (oestrogen, testosterone, cortisol): lipid-soluble, diffuse through the membrane, bind to receptors in the cytoplasm or nucleus, and the hormone–receptor complex acts as a transcription factor, changing gene expression (file 24).

### Neurones and the organisation of the nervous system

*Refs: OCR A 5.1.3(b), 5.1.5(g) · OCR B 5.2.1(a) · Edexcel A 8.1 · Edexcel B 9.4 i, iv, v · Eduqas C3.5(a), OC.1(c). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- The structure and functions of sensory, relay and motor neurones.
- The organisation of the mammalian nervous system: central and peripheral; somatic and autonomic; sympathetic and parasympathetic.
- Why the autonomic system is divided into sympathetic and parasympathetic parts.

#### Explained

**Neurones.**

| | Sensory | Relay | Motor |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Carries impulses | From receptor to CNS | Within the CNS, between sensory and motor | From CNS to effector |
| Shape | Long dendron to the cell body; cell body part-way along, outside the CNS (in a ganglion); short axon | Short, many branched dendrites and axon | Cell body in the CNS, many dendrites; long axon |
| Myelin | Often | Usually not | Often |

**Organisation.**

- **Central nervous system (CNS):** brain and spinal cord.
- **Peripheral nervous system (PNS):** all the neurones outside the CNS; sensory neurones bring impulses in; motor neurones take them out.
  - **Somatic** (voluntary) system: motor neurones to skeletal muscle, under conscious control.
  - **Autonomic** (involuntary) system: motor neurones to smooth muscle, cardiac muscle and glands, not under conscious control.
    - **Sympathetic:** prepares the body for action ("fight or flight"). Neurotransmitter at the effector: noradrenaline. Increases heart rate and breathing, dilates pupils, inhibits digestion.
    - **Parasympathetic:** "rest and digest". Neurotransmitter: acetylcholine. Slows heart rate, constricts pupils, stimulates digestion.

The two autonomic divisions are **antagonistic**: they act on the same organs with opposite effects. Having both gives fine control in both directions (Edexcel B 9.4 v), like the separate negative feedback mechanisms in homeostasis.

### The spinal cord and reflexes

*Refs: OCR A 5.1.5(i) · OCR B 5.2.1(f) · Edexcel A 8.2 · Edexcel B 9.4 ii · Eduqas C3.5(b) to (e). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- The structure of the spinal cord and spinal nerves.
- Reflex actions: the knee-jerk and blinking reflexes; their survival value.
- (Eduqas) The nerve net of Cnidaria compared with the mammalian nervous system.

#### Explained

**Spinal cord.** In cross-section: a central canal filled with cerebrospinal fluid; an H- or butterfly-shaped area of **grey matter** (cell bodies of relay and motor neurones, and synapses) surrounded by **white matter** (myelinated axons running up and down the cord). The cord is protected by the vertebrae and membranes (meninges).

**Spinal nerves** leave the cord in pairs between vertebrae. Each joins the cord by two roots: the **dorsal root** carries sensory neurones in, with their cell bodies in a swelling called the dorsal root ganglion; the **ventral root** carries motor neurones out.

**Reflex arc** (file 18 for the basic arc): receptor → sensory neurone (dorsal root) → relay neurone (grey matter) → motor neurone (ventral root) → effector. Reflexes are rapid, involuntary and protective, and do not need the brain to decide (though the brain may be informed and can sometimes override them).

- **Knee-jerk reflex** (a spinal reflex with no relay neurone: sensory neurone synapses directly with motor neurone). Tapping the patellar tendon stretches the quadriceps muscle; stretch receptors (muscle spindles) send impulses; the motor neurone makes the quadriceps contract and the leg kicks. At the same time a relay neurone inhibits the antagonistic hamstring. It helps balance and posture. An absent or exaggerated knee-jerk can indicate nerve damage.
- **Blinking reflex** (a cranial reflex, through the brain stem). Touching the cornea (or a sudden bright light) causes both eyelids to close quickly, protecting the eye. Doctors test it to check brain stem function in unconscious patients.

**Survival value.** Speed (few synapses); no conscious decision needed; protects from damage; automatic control of posture and balance.

**Nerve net** (Eduqas). Cnidarians such as *Hydra* have no CNS. Neurones form a diffuse net through the body wall, with impulses spreading in all directions from the stimulus; most neurones are not myelinated and many synapses can pass impulses either way. The response is slow and spreads through the whole body. Mammals have a centralised system with a brain, one-way synapses, myelinated neurones and specialised neurone types, giving fast, precise, varied responses.

### The brain

*Refs: OCR A 5.1.5(h) · OCR B 5.2.1(b) · Edexcel A 8.8 · Edexcel B 9.4 iii · Eduqas OC.1(a), (b), (d). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- The structure of the human brain and the functions of its parts: cerebrum (cerebral hemispheres), cerebellum, medulla oblongata, hypothalamus and pituitary gland (and for some boards the thalamus, hippocampus and basal ganglia).

#### Explained

| Region | Position | Functions |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Cerebrum (cerebral hemispheres) | Largest part; two hemispheres joined by the corpus callosum; folded outer layer (cerebral cortex) | Conscious thought, memory, language, reasoning, emotions; vision (occipital lobe), hearing, touch and movement (sensory and motor areas). Left hemisphere controls the right side of the body and vice versa |
| Cerebellum | Back of the brain, under the cerebrum | Coordinates movement, balance and posture; fine control of muscles; learned movements (riding a bike) |
| Medulla oblongata | Base of the brain, at the top of the spinal cord | Controls automatic actions: heart rate (cardiac centre), breathing (ventilation centre), blood pressure (vasomotor), swallowing |
| Hypothalamus | Below the thalamus, above the pituitary | Homeostasis: body temperature, water balance (osmoreceptors, ADH); controls the pituitary; sleep, hunger, thirst |
| Pituitary gland | Below the hypothalamus | Posterior lobe releases ADH and oxytocin (made in the hypothalamus); anterior lobe makes and releases hormones (FSH, LH, growth hormone, TSH, ACTH) controlled by releasing hormones from the hypothalamus |
| Thalamus (Edexcel A) | Centre of the brain | Relays sensory information to the cortex |
| Hippocampus (Edexcel A) | In the temporal lobe | Forming long-term memories |
| Basal ganglia (Edexcel A) | Deep in the cerebrum | Selecting and starting movements (damaged in Parkinson's disease, file 34) |

**The hypothalamus links nerves and hormones** (Eduqas OC.1(d)). Neurosecretory cells in the hypothalamus make hormones (ADH, oxytocin) and pass them down their axons to the posterior pituitary for release. Other cells release hormones into blood vessels that carry them to the anterior pituitary, controlling its secretions. So nervous signals can produce hormonal responses.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying the cerebellum controls conscious thought.
- Saying the pituitary controls body temperature.
- Saying the medulla controls voluntary movement.

### The adrenal glands and the fight-or-flight response

*Refs: OCR A 5.1.4(b), 5.1.5(j), (k) · Edexcel B 9.8 ii · Edexcel A 8.7. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- The structure and functions of the adrenal glands.
- The coordination of responses by the nervous and endocrine systems in the fight-or-flight response.

#### Explained

**Adrenal glands** sit on top of the kidneys.

- **Adrenal cortex** (outer layer) makes steroid hormones: glucocorticoids such as cortisol (regulates metabolism; raises blood glucose; helps the body cope with long-term stress), mineralocorticoids such as aldosterone (controls sodium and potassium in the blood, so blood pressure), and small amounts of sex hormones.
- **Adrenal medulla** (inner part) makes adrenaline and noradrenaline, released in response to impulses from sympathetic neurones.

**Fight or flight.**

1. A threat is detected by receptors; impulses go to the brain (cerebrum).
2. The hypothalamus is activated. It stimulates the sympathetic nervous system, which sends impulses directly to many organs and to the adrenal medulla, which releases adrenaline into the blood.
3. The hypothalamus also releases a hormone that makes the anterior pituitary release ACTH, which makes the adrenal cortex release cortisol.

Effects of adrenaline and the sympathetic system:

| Effect | Benefit |
| --- | --- |
| Heart rate and stroke volume increase | More blood to muscles |
| Breathing rate increases; bronchioles dilate | More oxygen taken in |
| Glycogen converted to glucose in liver (glycogenolysis) | More respiratory substrate |
| Blood diverted from gut and skin to muscles (vasoconstriction and vasodilation) | More oxygen and glucose to muscles |
| Pupils dilate | More light enters |
| Sweating; hairs stand up | Cooling; appear larger |
| Mental awareness increases | Faster response |

**Nervous and hormonal control of heart rate** (OCR A 5.1.5(k)): the cardiac centre in the medulla changes heart rate via sympathetic and parasympathetic (vagus) nerves (file 18); adrenaline also binds to receptors on the SAN and increases heart rate and the force of contraction.

### Check questions

1. Give three differences between nervous and hormonal communication. (3 marks)
2. Explain why steroid hormones and peptide hormones act in different ways on their target cells. (3 marks)
3. Describe the structure of a sensory neurone and a motor neurone. (4 marks)
4. Explain why the autonomic nervous system has sympathetic and parasympathetic divisions. (2 marks)
5. Describe the structure of the spinal cord as seen in a transverse section. (3 marks)
6. Describe the knee-jerk reflex and explain its value. (4 marks)
7. State the functions of the cerebellum, medulla oblongata and hypothalamus. (3 marks)
8. Describe how the nervous and hormonal systems work together in the fight-or-flight response. (5 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Any three: electrical vs chemical in blood; fast vs slow; short-lived vs long-lasting; localised vs widespread.
2. Steroids lipid-soluble, cross the membrane (1); bind receptors inside the cell and act as transcription factors / change gene expression (1); peptides water-soluble / large, bind to membrane receptors, act via second messenger / cAMP (1).
3. Sensory: long dendron, cell body outside CNS / part-way along, short axon (2); motor: cell body in CNS, many dendrites, long axon to effector (2).
4. Two divisions are antagonistic / opposite effects (1); allow control in both directions / finer control (1).
5. Central canal (1); grey matter (cell bodies) in an H / butterfly shape in the centre (1); white matter (myelinated axons) around it (1).
6. Tap on patellar tendon stretches quadriceps / stretch receptors stimulated (1); sensory neurone synapses directly with motor neurone in spinal cord (1); quadriceps contracts, hamstring inhibited, leg extends (1); value: maintains posture and balance / rapid / no conscious thought (1).
7. Cerebellum: coordination, balance, fine movement (1); medulla: heart rate / breathing / automatic functions (1); hypothalamus: homeostasis / temperature / water balance / controls pituitary (1).
8. Threat detected; hypothalamus activated (1); sympathetic nervous system sends impulses to organs (1); adrenal medulla releases adrenaline (1); hypothalamus / pituitary release ACTH so cortex releases cortisol (1); effects such as increased heart rate / glycogenolysis / dilated bronchioles (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/biology/35-the-eye-and-vision.md -->

## The eye and vision

The eye focuses light onto the retina, where rods and cones turn it into nerve impulses. This file covers the structure of the eye and retina, the pupil reflex, accommodation, how rods and cones work, how vision is tested, and how vision changes with age.

### Overview

A-level only. Rods and cones compared (sensitivity, acuity, convergence) and rhodopsin are explained in file 18, which AQA uses. The visual cortex and its critical period are in file 34.

#### Board differences

- Edexcel A 8.2, 8.5. Edexcel B 9.7 i to iii. OCR B 5.2.2(a) to (d), 5.2.3(b).
- Edexcel A uses the pupil reflex as its example of a nervous response (8.2) and asks how rods detect light (8.5).
- Edexcel B asks for the structure of the retina, rhodopsin and the distribution of rods and cones (9.7).
- OCR B asks for the structure of the eye, the pupil reflex and accommodation, assessing visual function, and age-related loss of vision.

### Structure of the eye and retina

*Refs: OCR B 5.2.2(a), (c) · Edexcel B 9.7 i, iii · Edexcel A 8.5. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- The structure of the eye and the functions of its parts.
- The structure of the human retina and the distribution of rods and cones.

#### Explained

| Part | Function |
| --- | --- |
| Sclera | Tough white outer coat; protects; muscles attach |
| Cornea | Transparent front; refracts (bends) most of the light entering the eye |
| Conjunctiva | Thin protective membrane over the front |
| Iris | Coloured ring of muscle; controls the size of the pupil |
| Pupil | Hole in the iris; lets light in |
| Lens | Transparent, flexible; fine-focuses light onto the retina |
| Ciliary muscle and suspensory ligaments | Change the shape of the lens (accommodation) |
| Aqueous and vitreous humour | Transparent fluids that keep the eye's shape |
| Retina | Light-sensitive layer of rods and cones and neurones |
| Fovea | Small area of the retina opposite the lens, packed with cones; sharpest vision |
| Blind spot | Where the optic nerve leaves; no receptors |
| Choroid | Layer with blood vessels and dark pigment that stops light reflecting inside the eye |
| Optic nerve | Carries impulses to the brain |

**The retina** has three main layers of cells. Strangely, light must pass through the neurone layers first:

1. **Ganglion cells** (nearest the light): their axons form the optic nerve.
2. **Bipolar neurones** (middle): link rods and cones to ganglion cells.
3. **Rods and cones** (at the back, next to the pigmented layer).

**Distribution.** The fovea has only cones, densely packed, each with its own bipolar neurone: high acuity and colour vision in bright light. Further from the fovea, rods increase and cones decrease; at the edges there are almost only rods, many sharing one bipolar neurone: high sensitivity in dim light, low acuity. This is why you see faint stars better by looking slightly to one side of them. There are about 120 million rods and 6 million cones in each eye.

**How rods respond** (Edexcel B 9.7 ii; detail in file 18). Light breaks down rhodopsin into retinal and opsin; this closes sodium channels; the rod hyperpolarises and stops releasing an inhibitory neurotransmitter onto the bipolar cell; the bipolar cell depolarises and passes impulses on to a ganglion cell, which sends action potentials to the brain.

### The pupil reflex and accommodation

*Refs: Edexcel A 8.2 · OCR B 5.2.2(b). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- How the pupil reflex controls the amount of light entering the eye.
- How the eye focuses on near and distant objects (accommodation).

#### Explained

**Pupil reflex.** Protects the retina from bright light and lets in more light when it is dim.

- *Bright light:* photoreceptors in the retina send impulses along the optic nerve to the brain (midbrain). Impulses pass along parasympathetic motor neurones to the iris. The **circular muscles** contract and the **radial muscles** relax; the pupil constricts.
- *Dim light:* sympathetic motor neurones cause the radial muscles to contract and circular muscles to relax; the pupil dilates.

Both eyes respond even if light is shone into only one. Doctors use the reflex to test brain stem function.

**Accommodation.**

| | Near object | Distant object |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Ciliary muscle | Contracts | Relaxes |
| Suspensory ligaments | Slacken | Pulled tight |
| Lens | Becomes fatter (more convex), refracts more | Pulled thinner, refracts less |

### Testing vision and age-related changes

*Refs: OCR B 5.2.2(d), 5.2.3(b). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- How the activity of the retina and visual function are assessed.
- Age-related changes in vision.

#### Explained

**Assessing visual function.**

- *Visual acuity:* a Snellen chart (rows of letters getting smaller) read from 6 m; 6/6 is normal; 6/12 means the person reads at 6 m what a typical person reads at 12 m. Tests the fovea (cones).
- *Colour vision:* Ishihara plates (numbers made of coloured dots); people with red-green colour blindness (an X-linked recessive condition, file 21, affecting cone pigments) cannot see some numbers.
- *Visual field:* the person looks at a central point and reports when lights appear in different parts of the field; gaps show damage to the retina or optic pathways (for example in glaucoma).
- *Retinal examination:* an ophthalmoscope or retinal camera views the retina and blood vessels (detecting diabetic retinopathy, damage from high blood pressure).
- *Electroretinogram:* electrodes record the electrical response of rods and cones to flashes of light.
- *Eye pressure test* (tonometry) for glaucoma.

**Age-related changes** (OCR B 5.2.3(b)).

- **Presbyopia:** the lens becomes stiffer with age, so it cannot become fat enough to focus on near objects; corrected with reading glasses (convex lenses).
- **Cataracts:** the lens becomes cloudy as its proteins change, causing blurred vision and glare; treated by replacing the lens with a plastic one.
- **Age-related macular degeneration (AMD):** damage to the macula (around the fovea) causes loss of central vision; "wet" AMD (leaky new blood vessels) can be slowed by injections of drugs that block a growth factor; "dry" AMD has no cure. Stem cell treatments are being tested.
- **Glaucoma:** raised pressure in the eye damages the optic nerve, causing loss of the visual field from the edges; treated with eye drops or surgery.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying the radial muscles contract in bright light.
- Saying the lens does most of the focusing (the cornea does most; the lens adjusts).
- Saying cones are spread evenly over the retina.

### Check questions

1. Describe the arrangement of cells in the retina. (3 marks)
2. Explain why vision is sharpest when the image falls on the fovea. (3 marks)
3. Describe the pupil reflex in response to bright light. (4 marks)
4. Describe how the eye focuses on a near object. (3 marks)
5. Explain why we see objects at night better out of the corner of the eye. (2 marks)
6. Describe two tests used to assess vision and say what each detects. (4 marks)
7. Explain why older people often need reading glasses. (2 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Rods and cones at the back / next to pigment layer (1); bipolar neurones in the middle (1); ganglion cells nearest the light, axons form optic nerve (1).
2. Fovea has only cones / densely packed (1); each cone connects to its own bipolar neurone / no convergence (1); brain receives separate information from each cone, so high acuity (1).
3. Light detected by photoreceptors in the retina (1); impulses along optic nerve to brain (1); impulses along parasympathetic / motor neurones to iris (1); circular muscles contract, radial relax, pupil constricts (1).
4. Ciliary muscle contracts (1); suspensory ligaments slacken (1); lens becomes fatter / more convex, refracts light more (1).
5. Rods are concentrated away from the fovea / at the edges (1); rods are sensitive to dim light (because of convergence / summation) (1).
6. Any two (2 each): Snellen chart, visual acuity / fovea; Ishihara plates, colour vision / cones; visual field test, gaps from retina or nerve damage; ophthalmoscope, retinal damage.
7. Lens loses elasticity / stiffens with age (1); cannot become fat enough to focus near objects / presbyopia (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/biology/31-plant-responses-and-plant-hormones.md -->

## Plant responses and plant hormones

Plants respond to light, gravity, touch, water stress and attack by changing how they grow. This file covers plant hormones (auxins, gibberellins, cytokinins, abscisic acid, ethene), the evidence for how auxins and gibberellins work, phytochrome and the control of flowering, and the commercial uses of plant hormones.

### Overview

A-level only. AQA covers IAA in tropisms only (file 18). Seed and flower structure and germination are in file 37.

#### Board differences

- OCR A 5.1.5(a) to (f). Edexcel A 8.6. Edexcel B 9.3 i to iv, CP14. OCR B 4.4.1(a), (d) (part). Eduqas C2.4(g).
- OCR A asks for responses to abiotic stress and herbivory, abscission, stomatal closure (ABA), experimental evidence for auxin in apical dominance and gibberellin in stem elongation and germination, and commercial uses.
- Edexcel A and Edexcel B ask for phytochrome in flowering and other responses (Edexcel B also asks for photomorphogenesis and etiolation). OCR B asks for phytochrome and vernalisation in flowering.
- Edexcel B CP14 and Eduqas (starch agar practical) use gibberellin and amylase in germinating seeds.
- Edexcel B asks how plant growth substances interact: synergism and antagonism.

### Plant responses and hormones

*Refs: OCR A 5.1.5(a) to (f) · Edexcel A 8.6 · Edexcel B 9.3 i to iii, CP14 · OCR B 4.4.1(d) · Eduqas C2.4(g). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Plants respond to abiotic stress and to herbivory.
- The roles of plant hormones in: leaf loss in deciduous plants; seed germination; stomatal closure.
- Experimental evidence for the role of auxins in the control of apical dominance and of gibberellin in the control of stem elongation and seed germination.
- Practical investigations into the effect of plant hormones on growth.
- Commercial uses of plant hormones.
- (Edexcel B) Plant growth substances can interact: synergism and antagonism.

#### Explained

**Why plants respond.** Plants cannot move away from danger, so they change their growth and chemistry. Responses include tropisms (growth towards or away from a directional stimulus: phototropism, gravitropism, hydrotropism, thigmotropism to touch, chemotropism) and rapid responses (nastic movements, such as *Mimosa pudica* leaves folding when touched).

**Responses to herbivory.**

- Chemical defences: tannins (bitter, bind proteins, make leaves hard to digest); alkaloids (nicotine, caffeine: bitter and toxic); pheromones; volatile organic compounds released when attacked that attract predators of the herbivore (parasitic wasps) or warn nearby plants.
- Physical: thorns, hairs; folding leaves.

**Responses to abiotic stress.** Closing stomata in drought (ABA); leaf loss in winter (to avoid water loss and frost damage when light is too low for useful photosynthesis); making antifreeze proteins and sugars in cold.

**Plant hormones (growth substances).** Made in many tissues, transported short distances by diffusion and active transport or long distances in xylem and phloem, and act on target cells with specific receptors. Effects depend on concentration and on other hormones.

| Hormone | Main roles |
| --- | --- |
| Auxins (IAA) | Cell elongation in shoots; apical dominance; root growth at low concentrations; inhibit abscission |
| Gibberellins (GA) | Stem elongation (between nodes); seed germination; fruit growth; bolting |
| Cytokinins | Cell division; delay leaf senescence; promote side shoot growth |
| Abscisic acid (ABA) | Stomatal closure; seed dormancy; inhibits growth under stress |
| Ethene | Fruit ripening; promotes abscission (leaf and fruit fall) |

**How auxins cause elongation (acid growth hypothesis).** IAA binds to receptors on cells, stimulating proton pumps that move H⁺ into the cell wall. The low pH activates proteins (expansins) that break bonds between cellulose microfibrils, loosening the wall. Water enters the cell by osmosis and the cell elongates. Later the wall stiffens again.

**Apical dominance.** Auxin made by the shoot tip moves down the stem and inhibits the growth of lateral (side) buds. Evidence:

- Remove the apical bud: side shoots grow.
- Remove the tip and put auxin paste (in lanolin) on the cut surface: side shoots stay suppressed. Lanolin alone does not suppress them (control).
- Apply an auxin transport inhibitor below the tip: side shoots grow.
- Later work showed cytokinins promote side shoot growth and that ABA in side buds keeps them dormant; auxin may act partly by lowering cytokinins. So apical dominance involves several hormones.

Gardeners use this: pruning the tips makes plants bushier.

**Gibberellins and stem elongation.** Evidence:
