- Rice infected with the fungus *Gibberella fujikuroi* grew very tall ("foolish seedling disease"); the substance from the fungus (gibberellin) did the same when applied to healthy plants.
- Dwarf varieties of peas or maize have low gibberellin (a mutation in a gene for an enzyme that makes active GA); applying gibberellin makes them grow tall. Tall varieties do not respond as much.
- Measurements show high gibberellin concentrations in young leaves and seeds, where growth is fastest.

**Gibberellins and germination.**

1. The seed absorbs water (imbibition); the embryo is activated and makes gibberellin.
2. Gibberellin diffuses to the aleurone layer (a protein-rich layer around the endosperm in cereals).
3. It switches on genes for amylase (gibberellin causes breakdown of DELLA proteins that otherwise block transcription factors).
4. Amylase hydrolyses starch in the endosperm to maltose and glucose.
5. The sugars are respired by the embryo for energy and used to build new cells, so the embryo grows.

Evidence: seeds of mutant plants that cannot make gibberellin do not germinate unless gibberellin is supplied; inhibitors of gibberellin synthesis stop germination; ABA (which keeps seeds dormant) antagonises gibberellin.

**Leaf loss (abscission).** As days shorten in autumn, auxin from leaves falls. The leaf becomes less sensitive to ethene and more ethene is made. Ethene stimulates genes for cellulases, which break down cell walls in the abscission zone at the base of the leaf stalk. A layer of suberin seals the wound before the leaf falls.

**Stomatal closure.** In drought, roots and leaves make ABA. ABA binds to receptors on guard cells, causing calcium ions to enter; this opens channels so K⁺ (and Cl⁻) leave. The water potential of the guard cells rises, water leaves by osmosis, guard cells become flaccid and stomata close.

**Interactions** (Edexcel B 9.3 iii). *Synergism:* two hormones together have a bigger effect than either alone (auxin and gibberellin together cause more stem growth). *Antagonism:* two hormones have opposite effects (gibberellin promotes germination; ABA inhibits it; auxin inhibits side shoots while cytokinin promotes them).

#### Common mistakes

- Saying auxins make cells divide (they make cells elongate).
- Saying gibberellin itself breaks down starch.
- Saying ABA opens stomata.

#### Practical

**Gibberellin and amylase in germinating cereals** (Edexcel B CP14; Eduqas starch agar practical).

1. Cut barley grains in half; keep the halves without embryos (so no natural gibberellin). Sterilise their surfaces.
2. Soak in a range of gibberellin concentrations (and water as a control).
3. Place cut side down on starch agar plates; incubate for 48 hours.
4. Flood with iodine solution. Clear zones show where starch was hydrolysed by amylase.
5. Measure the area of the clear zones; larger zones show more amylase.

Halves with embryos act as a positive control. Aseptic technique prevents bacterial amylase giving false results.

**Effect of auxin on growth** (OCR A 5.1.5(e)). Grow coleoptiles, stem segments or cuttings in a range of auxin concentrations and measure growth or root number. Use the same length segments, the same time, the same temperature and light.

### Phytochrome, photomorphogenesis and flowering

*Refs: Edexcel A 8.6 · Edexcel B 9.3 iv · OCR B 4.4.1(a). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- How phytochrome controls flowering, germination and photomorphogenesis.
- (OCR B) Vernalisation and the timing of flowering.

#### Explained

**Phytochrome** is a light-sensitive pigment (a protein with a light-absorbing part) found in two interconvertible forms.

- Pr absorbs red light (660 nm) and is converted to Pfr.
- Pfr absorbs far-red light (730 nm) and is converted to Pr.
- In darkness, Pfr slowly reverts to Pr.

Sunlight contains more red than far-red, so in daylight Pr becomes Pfr; at night Pfr slowly becomes Pr. The ratio of Pfr to Pr tells the plant how long the night is.

Pfr is the active form. It moves into the nucleus and binds to transcription factors (it interacts with proteins that otherwise block them), switching genes on or off.

**Flowering.** Plants flower at the right time of year so that pollinators are active and seeds develop in suitable conditions. The critical factor is the length of uninterrupted darkness.

- *Long-day plants* (flower in summer, short nights): flower when Pfr stays high, because short nights do not convert enough back to Pr. A flash of red light in the middle of a long night makes them flower.
- *Short-day plants* (flower in autumn or spring, long nights): flower when Pfr falls low during a long night. A flash of red light in the middle of the night (converting Pr to Pfr) stops flowering; a flash of far-red afterwards reverses that.

Pfr, made in leaves, leads to production of a flowering signal (florigen, a protein) that travels in the phloem to the buds.

**Vernalisation** (OCR B). Some plants (winter wheat, many biennials) flower only after a long period of cold. Cold switches off (epigenetically) a gene that blocks flowering, so the plant flowers in spring, not before winter.

**Germination.** Some small seeds germinate only in light: red light converts Pr to Pfr, which stimulates gibberellin synthesis. This stops small seeds germinating deep in soil where they would use up their food before reaching light. Under a leaf canopy, light is rich in far-red (leaves absorb red), so Pfr stays low and seeds stay dormant until a gap opens.

**Photomorphogenesis.** The development of a plant's form in response to light. A seedling growing in the dark is *etiolated*: long, thin stem; small, yellow leaves; hooked tip. This uses its stored food to reach light quickly. When exposed to red light, Pfr forms and the seedling de-etiolates: stem elongation slows, leaves expand, chlorophyll forms.

**Phototropism and IAA** (Edexcel A 8.6). Blue light, detected by a different pigment (phototropin), causes IAA to move to the shaded side of the shoot (file 18), so the shaded side elongates more.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying short-day plants respond to the length of the day; it is the length of the uninterrupted night.
- Saying Pr is the active form.

### Commercial uses of plant hormones

*Refs: OCR A 5.1.5(f). Level: A-level only.*

#### Explained

| Hormone | Use |
| --- | --- |
| Auxins | Rooting powders for cuttings; selective weedkillers (synthetic auxins make broad-leaved weeds grow too fast and die, while narrow-leaved cereals are less affected); seedless fruit (parthenocarpy) by spraying unpollinated flowers; preventing fruit drop |
| Gibberellins | Larger, longer seedless grapes; malting in brewing (speeds amylase production in barley); delaying ripening of citrus fruit; more sugar cane yield (longer internodes); seed germination |
| Ethene | Ripening bananas and other fruit picked unripe for transport; promoting fruit drop for harvesting (cherries); promoting lateral growth |
| Cytokinins | Tissue culture (shoot formation, file 29); delaying senescence of lettuce |
| Inhibitors of ethene / gibberellin | Storing fruit longer (low oxygen, high CO₂); dwarf cereals that do not fall over (lodge) in wind and rain |

### Check questions

1. Describe two chemical defences that plants use against herbivores. (2 marks)
2. Explain how auxins cause cells in shoots to elongate. (3 marks)
3. Describe an experiment to show that auxin from the shoot tip causes apical dominance, including a control. (4 marks)
4. Describe how gibberellin causes the germination of a cereal seed. (4 marks)
5. Explain how ABA causes stomata to close. (3 marks)
6. A short-day plant was kept on long nights but given a 5-minute flash of red light halfway through each night. It did not flower. Explain this result. (3 marks)
7. Explain the advantage of etiolation to a seedling that germinates underground. (2 marks)
8. Give three commercial uses of plant hormones, naming the hormone in each. (3 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Any two: tannins (bitter / make protein indigestible); alkaloids (toxic / bitter); volatile chemicals attract predators of herbivores / warn other plants; pheromones.
2. Auxin causes proton pumping into cell walls / low pH (1); bonds between cellulose microfibrils broken / wall loosened (expansins) (1); water enters by osmosis, cell elongates (1).
3. Remove apical bud from one group: side shoots grow (1); remove tip and apply auxin in lanolin: side shoots do not grow (1); control: remove tip and apply lanolin only: side shoots grow (1); same species / age / conditions (1).
4. Water absorbed, embryo makes gibberellin (1); gibberellin diffuses to aleurone layer (1); switches on genes for amylase (1); amylase hydrolyses starch to maltose / glucose used by embryo for respiration and growth (1).
5. ABA binds to receptors on guard cells / Ca²⁺ enter (1); K⁺ ions leave guard cells (1); water potential rises, water leaves by osmosis, guard cells flaccid, stoma closes (1).
6. Red light converts Pr to Pfr (1); breaks the long dark period / Pfr level stays high (1); short-day plants need low Pfr / a long uninterrupted night to flower (1).
7. Long stem grows fast to reach light (1); food stores not wasted on leaves / chlorophyll until light reached (1).
8. Any three (1 each): auxin rooting powder / weedkiller / seedless fruit; gibberellin seedless grapes / malting / sugar cane; ethene ripening fruit; cytokinin tissue culture.

<!-- source: notes/a-level/biology/34-brain-imaging-development-drugs-and-ageing.md -->

## The brain: imaging, development, drugs, damage and ageing

This file covers how the brain is studied without surgery, how it develops (critical periods, neuroplasticity, nature and nurture), the cortex and language areas, how imbalances in brain chemicals and drugs affect synapses, drug dependency, brain and spinal cord injury, and Alzheimer's disease.

### Overview

A-level only. Brain regions and the nervous system are in file 32; synapses in file 19; the eye in file 35. AQA does not have this content.

#### Board differences

- Edexcel A 8.9 to 8.12, 8.14, 8.15, 8.19. Edexcel B 9.6. OCR B 5.2.1(g) to (j), 5.2.3(a), (b). Eduqas C3.5(l), OC.1(e) to (g), OC.2(a) to (e).
- Edexcel A uses Parkinson's disease and depression as its examples of chemical imbalance, and MDMA as its drug example; it asks for the visual cortex critical period and animal models in research.
- OCR B asks for brain scans in diagnosis, brain and spinal injuries, drug dependency, Alzheimer's disease and age-related loss of hearing and vision.
- Eduqas Option C asks for sensory and motor cortex, the relationship between body areas and cortex size, language areas, imaging techniques, critical periods, neuroplasticity and gene expression in brain development.
- Edexcel B 9.6 asks how drugs influence synaptic transmission.

### The cortex and language

*Refs: Eduqas OC.1(e) to (g). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- The sensory and motor areas of the cortex; the relationship between the size of these areas and the sensitivity or dexterity of body parts.
- The areas of the cerebrum involved in language: Broca's and Wernicke's areas.

#### Explained

**Motor cortex** (a strip at the back of the frontal lobe) sends impulses to skeletal muscles to start voluntary movement. **Somatosensory cortex** (a strip just behind it, in the parietal lobe) receives impulses from touch, pressure, temperature and pain receptors. Each part of the body has a matching area of cortex. Areas that are very sensitive (lips, fingertips) or need fine control (hands, tongue) have much larger areas of cortex than their size suggests. Drawn to scale, this gives a distorted figure called a **homunculus**. Other areas: visual cortex (occipital lobe), auditory cortex (temporal lobe).

**Language areas** (usually in the left hemisphere).

- **Broca's area** (frontal lobe): producing speech, forming sentences. Damage (Broca's aphasia): speech is slow and halting, but understanding is mostly kept.
- **Wernicke's area** (temporal lobe): understanding language. Damage (Wernicke's aphasia): speech is fluent but makes little sense, and understanding is poor.
- They are joined by a bundle of nerve fibres.

### Studying the brain: imaging

*Refs: Edexcel A 8.9 · OCR B 5.2.1(g) · Eduqas OC.2(a). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- How magnetic resonance imaging (MRI), functional MRI (fMRI) and computed tomography (CT) scans are used in medical diagnosis and to study the brain.

#### Explained

| Method | How it works | Shows | Uses and limits |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| CT | X-rays from many angles; a computer builds cross-sections | Structure; denser tissue appears lighter | Quick; detects bleeding, tumours, skull fractures after injury. Uses ionising radiation; less detail of soft tissue than MRI |
| MRI | Strong magnetic field and radio waves; hydrogen nuclei in water give signals that differ between tissues | Detailed structure of soft tissue, distinguishing grey and white matter | Tumours, multiple sclerosis, stroke damage; no radiation. Slow, noisy, expensive; not for people with metal implants |
| fMRI | MRI tuned to detect oxygenated haemoglobin; active areas receive more oxygenated blood | Activity in real time | Mapping which areas are active during tasks (research, planning surgery). Shows blood flow, not neurone activity directly; low time resolution |
| PET (Eduqas, OCR B) | Radioactive tracer (glucose labelled with a positron emitter) injected; active areas take up more | Metabolic activity | Alzheimer's, epilepsy, tumours. Uses radioactivity |
| EEG (Eduqas) | Electrodes on the scalp record electrical activity | Brain waves; timing of activity | Epilepsy, sleep studies. Poor spatial detail |

### Brain development, critical periods and plasticity

*Refs: Edexcel A 8.10, 8.11, 8.12, 8.19 · Eduqas OC.2(b) to (e). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- What happens during the critical period so that mammals can develop normal visual capacities.
- The role animal models have played in research into human brain development and function; the moral and ethical issues of using animals in medical research.
- Neuroplasticity.
- How gene expression affects brain development; how altered gene expression in childhood may predispose adults to disease.
- The methods used to investigate the contributions of nature and nurture to brain development.

#### Explained

**The critical period in vision.** At birth, the visual cortex has many synapses. Neurones from each eye connect to columns (ocular dominance columns) of the visual cortex. During a critical period in early life, visual stimulation is needed: active synapses are strengthened and kept; synapses that receive little stimulation are lost ("use it or lose it").

Evidence (Hubel and Wiesel, 1960s): one eye of newborn kittens or monkeys was kept closed for the first few months. Afterwards the animal was blind in that eye, even though the eye itself was normal, because the cortex columns for that eye had been taken over by the open eye. Closing an eye in an adult animal had no such effect. In humans, cataracts or a squint in young children must be treated early to avoid permanent loss of vision (amblyopia).

**Neuroplasticity** (Eduqas). The brain can change its structure and connections through life: forming new synapses, strengthening or weakening existing ones, and sometimes forming new neurones (in the hippocampus). This underlies learning and memory, and recovery after damage, when other areas take over functions (for example after a stroke, with physiotherapy and practice). Plasticity is greatest in childhood.

**Gene expression and development** (Eduqas). Genes control the migration of neurones, their connections and the release of neurotransmitters. The environment changes which genes are expressed through epigenetic marks (file 24). Stress, poor nutrition or abuse in childhood can change the methylation of genes (for example genes for stress hormone receptors), and these changes can last, increasing the risk of depression, anxiety and other conditions in adulthood.

**Animal models** (Edexcel A 8.11, 8.12). Animals share many genes and brain structures with humans and are used to study development and disease (mice, rats, primates). Knockout mice show what a gene does. Advantages: control over genes and environment; procedures impossible in humans. Issues: animals' welfare and pain; whether results apply to humans; the number of animals used. The 3Rs guide use: **replace** animals where possible (cell cultures, computer models), **reduce** numbers, **refine** procedures to reduce suffering. Ethical positions differ: some argue human benefit justifies careful use; others that animals have rights not to be used. Research is licensed in the UK under the Animals (Scientific Procedures) Act.

**Nature and nurture** (Edexcel A 8.19). How much do genes and environment each contribute?

- *Twin studies:* compare identical (monozygotic) twins, who share all their alleles, with non-identical (dizygotic) twins, who share about half. A higher concordance in identical twins suggests a genetic influence. Twins raised apart separate the effects of shared environment.
- *Adoption studies:* compare children with their biological parents (genes) and adoptive parents (environment).
- *Cross-cultural studies:* compare people with similar genes in different cultures, or different populations in similar environments.
- *Newborn studies:* abilities present at birth are more likely to be innate.
- *Animal experiments:* raising animals in deprived or enriched environments.
- *Brain damage studies:* which abilities are lost or recovered.

Most characteristics depend on both.

### Brain chemicals and drugs

*Refs: Edexcel A 8.14, 8.15 · Edexcel B 9.6 i · OCR B 5.2.1(i), (j) · Eduqas C3.5(l). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- How imbalances in naturally occurring brain chemicals can contribute to ill health (Parkinson's disease, depression) and to the development of new drugs.
- The effects of drugs on synaptic transmission, including L-dopa in Parkinson's disease and MDMA (Edexcel A).
- Psychological and physical drug dependency (OCR B).

#### Explained

**How drugs act at synapses** (Edexcel B 9.6). A drug can:

- increase neurotransmitter release, or mimic it (agonist) → more stimulation
- block receptors (antagonist) or reduce release → less stimulation
- inhibit the enzyme that breaks the neurotransmitter down → more stays in the cleft
- block reuptake into the presynaptic neurone → more stays in the cleft.

**Parkinson's disease.** Dopamine-producing neurones in the substantia nigra (part of the basal ganglia system) die. Less dopamine is released, so movement control is impaired: tremor, stiff muscles, slow movement, poor balance. Treatments:

- **L-dopa:** a precursor of dopamine that can cross the blood–brain barrier (dopamine cannot) and is converted into dopamine in the brain, increasing dopamine release. Effects lessen over time; side effects include involuntary movements.
- Dopamine agonists (mimic dopamine at receptors); MAO-B inhibitors (stop dopamine being broken down).
- Deep brain stimulation; possible future: gene therapy, stem cell transplants.

**Depression.** Linked to low levels of serotonin (and other monoamines) in the brain, although causes are complex. Selective serotonin reuptake inhibitors (**SSRIs**, such as fluoxetine) block the reuptake transporter, so serotonin stays in the synaptic cleft longer, stimulating postsynaptic neurones more. Effects take weeks, suggesting longer-term changes (receptor numbers, neuroplasticity) are involved.

**MDMA (ecstasy).** Binds to serotonin reuptake transporters and reverses them, so serotonin is released into the cleft and not reabsorbed. Serotonin levels rise sharply, causing euphoria and energy; afterwards serotonin is depleted, causing low mood. Risks: overheating, dehydration or drinking too much water (MDMA increases ADH, so hyponatraemia), possible long-term damage to serotonin neurones.

**Other psychoactive drugs** (Eduqas C3.5(l), OCR B 5.2.1(i)): nicotine (mimics acetylcholine at nicotinic receptors; stimulates dopamine release in reward pathways); cocaine (blocks dopamine reuptake); alcohol (enhances GABA, an inhibitory neurotransmitter; depressant); opioids such as morphine (bind to endorphin receptors, reducing pain); benzodiazepines (enhance GABA, reducing anxiety). Organophosphates are in file 19.

**Drug dependency** (OCR B 5.2.1(j)).

- *Physical dependency:* the body adapts to the drug (for example by making fewer receptors or less neurotransmitter), so more drug is needed for the same effect (**tolerance**), and stopping causes physical **withdrawal symptoms** (shaking, sweating, nausea).
- *Psychological dependency:* a craving for the drug's effects (reward pathways involving dopamine), so the person feels unable to cope without it, even without physical withdrawal.

### Damage and ageing of the nervous system

*Refs: OCR B 5.2.1(h), 5.2.3(a), (b). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- The consequences of brain and spinal cord injuries.
- The symptoms and possible causes of Alzheimer's disease.
- The effect of ageing on the nervous system, including reaction time and memory; age-related loss of vision and hearing (vision detail in file 35).

#### Explained

**Brain injury.** Caused by head trauma, stroke (a blocked or burst blood vessel stops blood supply to part of the brain) or tumours. Effects depend on the area: loss of movement or feeling on the opposite side of the body; speech problems (Broca's or Wernicke's areas); memory loss (hippocampus); changes in personality (frontal lobe); coordination problems (cerebellum). Scans (CT, MRI) locate the damage. Rehabilitation uses plasticity.

**Spinal cord injury.** Neurones in the CNS do not regrow well. Damage cuts communication between the brain and the body below the injury: loss of sensation and movement (paralysis). Injury in the neck can cause quadriplegia (all four limbs) and affect breathing; lower down, paraplegia (legs). Autonomic functions below the injury (bladder control) may also be lost. Research: stem cells, nerve growth factors, electrical stimulation.

**Alzheimer's disease.** The most common form of dementia. Symptoms: progressive memory loss (recent memories first), confusion, difficulty with language and planning, mood changes. Changes in the brain: plaques of beta-amyloid protein between neurones; tangles of tau protein inside neurones; loss of neurones and synapses, especially in the hippocampus and cortex; reduced acetylcholine. The brain shrinks. Possible causes and risk factors: age; inherited alleles (such as APOE4); head injury; cardiovascular risk factors. Treatment: drugs that inhibit acetylcholinesterase raise acetylcholine and slow symptoms for a time; newer antibodies that clear amyloid; no cure.

**The ageing nervous system** (OCR B 5.2.3(b)). With age, the brain loses some neurones and synapses, myelin sheaths thin, and less neurotransmitter is made. Impulses are conducted more slowly and synapses work less efficiently, so reaction times lengthen and short-term memory and recall decline (long-term and well-practised skills are kept better). Practical investigations compare reaction times (for example the ruler-drop test or computer tests) and memory tests across age groups, controlling for practice, alertness and time of day. Regular physical and mental activity may slow the decline (plasticity).

**Age-related hearing loss** (presbycusis): hair cells in the cochlea are damaged and lost over a lifetime (especially by loud noise) and do not regrow, so high-frequency sounds are lost first. Hearing aids amplify sound; cochlear implants stimulate the auditory nerve directly.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying dopamine is given as a drug for Parkinson's disease (it cannot cross the blood–brain barrier; L-dopa can).
- Saying SSRIs increase serotonin release.
- Saying fMRI measures electrical activity directly.

### Check questions

1. Explain why the area of motor cortex for the hands is much larger than that for the back. (2 marks)
2. Compare CT and fMRI scans. (4 marks)
3. Describe the evidence for a critical period in the development of vision. (4 marks)
4. Describe how twin studies are used to investigate nature and nurture. (3 marks)
5. Explain how L-dopa treats Parkinson's disease. (3 marks)
6. Explain how SSRIs relieve depression. (3 marks)
7. Distinguish between physical and psychological drug dependency. (2 marks)
8. Describe the changes in the brain in Alzheimer's disease. (3 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Hands need fine / precise control of many muscles (1); more motor neurones / more cortex needed (1).
2. Any four: CT uses X-rays, fMRI uses magnetic field / radio waves; CT shows structure, fMRI shows activity / blood flow; CT uses ionising radiation; fMRI shows soft tissue in more detail; CT quicker / cheaper; fMRI used during tasks.
3. Kittens / monkeys had one eye closed from birth (1); during first months (1); afterwards blind in that eye though the eye was normal (1); visual cortex connections for that eye lost / taken over; adults with one eye closed unaffected (1).
4. Compare identical (all alleles shared) and non-identical (half shared) twins (1); higher concordance in identical twins suggests genetic influence (1); twins raised apart separate environmental effects (1).
5. Dopamine-producing neurones lost, low dopamine (1); L-dopa crosses blood–brain barrier (1); converted to dopamine, increasing dopamine at synapses (1).
6. Block reuptake of serotonin into the presynaptic neurone (1); more serotonin stays in cleft (1); more binding to postsynaptic receptors / more stimulation (1).
7. Physical: body adapts; tolerance and withdrawal symptoms (1); psychological: craving / emotional need without physical symptoms (1).
8. Any three: amyloid plaques; tau tangles; loss of neurones / synapses; hippocampus / cortex shrinks; less acetylcholine.

<!-- source: notes/a-level/biology/48-animal-behaviour.md -->

## Animal behaviour

Behaviour is what an animal does in response to its environment. This file covers innate behaviour (reflexes, kineses, taxes), learned behaviour (habituation, imprinting, conditioning, latent learning and insight), social behaviour in insects and vertebrates, territory and courtship, sexual selection, and Edexcel A core practical 18.

### Overview

A-level only. AQA covers taxes, kineses and simple reflexes in file 18 (with its choice chamber practical); courtship and species recognition are in file 14. Nervous control of reflexes is in file 32.

#### Board differences

- Edexcel A 8.13, CP18 (habituation only). Eduqas OC.3(a) to (h) (Option C).
- Only Eduqas Option C students need social behaviour, dominance hierarchies, territory and sexual selection.

### Innate behaviour

*Refs: Eduqas OC.3(a). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Innate behaviour and the advantage to organisms of escape reflexes, kineses and taxes.

#### Explained

**Innate behaviour** is inherited: it is controlled by genes, is the same in all members of a species, and is performed correctly the first time without learning. It allows a rapid, appropriate response, and is especially important in animals with short lives and little or no parental care.

- **Escape reflexes:** rapid, automatic responses that move an animal away from danger. An earthworm withdraws into its burrow when touched (giant axons conduct the impulse fast, file 19); a startled fish darts away. Advantage: avoiding predators without delay.
- **Kineses:** non-directional changes in the speed of movement or rate of turning in response to the intensity of a stimulus (file 18). Woodlice move faster and turn less in dry conditions, so they end up spending more time in damp places, which reduces water loss.
- **Taxes:** directional movements towards or away from a stimulus. Positive phototaxis in single-celled algae (more light for photosynthesis); negative phototaxis in woodlice and blowfly larvae (avoiding predators and drying out); chemotaxis of bacteria towards food.

### Learned behaviour

*Refs: Edexcel A 8.13, CP18 · Eduqas OC.3(b). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Learned behaviours: habituation, imprinting, classical and operant conditioning, latent learning and insight learning.
- How animals, including humans, learn by habituation (Edexcel A).

#### Explained

**Learned behaviour** is changed by experience. It lets animals adapt to changing or unpredictable environments. It is most important in animals with long lives and long periods of parental care.

**Habituation.** The response to a repeated stimulus that is neither harmful nor beneficial gradually decreases and stops. Example: birds learn to ignore a scarecrow; a snail stops withdrawing its tentacles when gently touched many times; people stop noticing a ticking clock. Advantage: the animal does not waste time and energy responding to harmless stimuli, and can respond to stimuli that matter.

Mechanism (Edexcel A): with repeated stimulation, fewer calcium ion channels open in the presynaptic membrane of the sensory neurone, so less neurotransmitter is released into the synapse with the motor neurone; the postsynaptic neurone is depolarised less and fewer action potentials pass, so the response weakens. Habituation is specific to that stimulus: a different or stronger stimulus restores the response (dishabituation).

**Imprinting.** In a short sensitive (critical) period early in life, a young animal learns to recognise and follow a moving object, normally its parent. Example: goslings and ducklings follow the first large moving object they see after hatching (Lorenz showed geese could imprint on him). Advantages: young stay close to a parent for food, warmth and protection; learn to recognise their own species (later mate choice).

**Classical conditioning.** An animal learns to link a new (neutral) stimulus with a stimulus that already causes a response, so the new stimulus alone comes to cause the response. Pavlov's dogs salivated (unconditioned response) to food (unconditioned stimulus); after a bell was rung each time food was given, the bell alone caused salivation (conditioned response).

**Operant conditioning (trial and error).** An animal learns to link one of its own actions with a reward or punishment, so that rewarded actions are repeated and punished actions avoided. Skinner's rats learned to press a lever for food. Used in training animals.

**Latent learning.** An animal explores its surroundings and learns information without any immediate reward; it uses it later. Example: a rat that has explored a maze without a reward finds its way through faster than a new rat when food is later placed at the end. Wild animals learn the layout of their territory, which helps them escape predators.

**Insight learning.** Solving a new problem by thinking it through, using past experience, without trial and error. Example: chimpanzees stacking boxes or joining sticks to reach bananas; some birds using tools. Found mostly in primates, some birds (crows) and possibly other mammals.

#### Practical

**Edexcel A CP18: investigate habituation to a stimulus.**

1. Place a giant African land snail on a clean, damp surface; let it settle until its tentacles are extended.
2. Touch the snail gently between the eye stalks with a damp cotton bud.
3. Time how long it takes for the tentacles to extend fully again.
4. Repeat the touch as soon as the tentacles are extended, for 10 to 15 touches; record the time for each.
5. The time to re-emerge decreases as the snail habituates.

Control variables: same snail size and species; same force and place of touch (use a fixed method, for example a cotton bud on a set drop); same temperature, humidity and light. Use several snails and calculate means. Ethics: handle gently; keep snails moist; return them to their tank; avoid causing stress or harm.

### Social behaviour

*Refs: Eduqas OC.3(c) to (f). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Primates, including humans, live in very complex societies and show complex social behaviour.
- The advantages and disadvantages of living in social groups.
- The social structure of some insects, based on a caste system.
- Social structure in vertebrates achieved through dominance hierarchies.

#### Explained

**Advantages of living in groups:** protection from predators (more eyes to spot danger; safety in numbers; group defence); cooperative hunting (wolves, lions); finding food (information shared); care of young by several adults; learning from others; division of labour; keeping warm (penguins huddling).

**Disadvantages:** competition for food and mates within the group; disease and parasites spread more easily; groups are more conspicuous to predators; energy used on social interactions; risk of infanticide or aggression; lower-ranking individuals may get little food or few chances to breed.

**Social insects** (bees, ants, termites, wasps). Colonies have a **caste** system: individuals are specialised for different jobs and many do not reproduce. In a honeybee colony:

- One **queen**: lays all the eggs; produces pheromones that control the colony and stop workers developing ovaries.
- **Drones** (males): develop from unfertilised eggs (haploid); mate with new queens.
- **Workers** (sterile females): build comb, feed larvae, clean, guard, collect nectar and pollen. Their job changes with age.

Caste is determined largely by diet as larvae (larvae fed royal jelly throughout become queens), which changes gene expression. Communication by pheromones and by the waggle dance (shows direction and distance of food). Workers share many alleles with the queen's offspring (their sisters), which helps explain why helping the colony spreads their own alleles (kin selection).

**Primate societies.** Primates (including humans) live in complex groups, form long-lasting relationships, recognise individuals, cooperate, form alliances, groom each other (which strengthens bonds and removes parasites), communicate with calls, facial expressions and gestures (language in humans), have long periods of learning in childhood, and pass on learned behaviour (culture, such as tool use in chimpanzees).

**Dominance hierarchies.** In many vertebrate groups (chickens' pecking order, wolves, gorillas, baboons), individuals have a rank. Higher-ranking animals get priority access to food, shelter and mates. Rank is set by displays and occasional fights and is then maintained mainly by signals (posture, threat displays), so less energy is wasted on fighting and fewer animals are injured. Dominant individuals pass on their alleles more; subordinates may still gain protection and may rise in rank later.

### Territory, courtship and sexual selection

*Refs: Eduqas OC.3(g), (h). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- The advantage of territorial and courtship behaviours in increasing reproductive success.
- The role of sexual selection in the evolution of territorial and courtship behaviour.

#### Explained

**Territory.** A territory is an area an animal (or group) defends against others of the same species. Marked with scent, song or displays; defended by threat displays and sometimes fighting. Advantages: secure supply of food for the owner and its young; nesting sites; access to mates; spreads the population out, reducing competition and disease. Animals without territories may not breed, which regulates population size. Defending a territory costs energy and risks injury, so territories are only held where the benefit is greater than the cost.

**Courtship** (file 14). Species-specific displays (songs, dances, colours, gifts) that: allow recognition of the same species; show that a mate is sexually mature and receptive; synchronise mating; form a pair bond in species where both parents care for young; let one sex judge the quality of the other. Example: the great crested grebe's elaborate head-shaking and "weed dance"; the zig-zag dance of the male stickleback.

**Sexual selection** is natural selection acting on characteristics that increase mating success.

- *Intrasexual selection:* competition between members of one sex (usually males) for access to the other: larger size, antlers, horns and fighting ability (red deer stags).
- *Intersexual selection (mate choice):* one sex (usually females, who invest more in each offspring) chooses mates with particular features, such as bright plumage, long tails or elaborate songs (the peacock's tail). These features may signal good health and good genes (only a healthy male can afford to grow and carry them).

Over generations, alleles for these features and for the preference become more common, even if the features make survival harder (more visible to predators). This explains the evolution of exaggerated courtship displays and territorial behaviour.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying habituation is "getting tired" or due to muscle fatigue; it is a change at synapses.
- Confusing imprinting with classical conditioning.
- Saying workers in a bee colony are male.
- Saying innate behaviour can be changed by experience.

### Check questions

1. Distinguish between innate and learned behaviour. (2 marks)
2. Explain the advantage of habituation. (2 marks)
3. Describe the synaptic basis of habituation. (3 marks)
4. In CP18, give three variables that should be controlled. (3 marks)
5. Distinguish between classical and operant conditioning, giving an example of each. (4 marks)
6. Give two advantages and two disadvantages of living in a social group. (4 marks)
7. Explain how a dominance hierarchy benefits members of a group. (2 marks)
8. Explain how sexual selection can lead to the evolution of the peacock's long tail. (4 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Innate: inherited / genetically determined, performed correctly first time, same in all members (1); learned: changed by experience (1).
2. Animal stops responding to repeated harmless stimuli (1); saves time / energy for important stimuli (1).
3. Repeated stimulation reduces Ca²⁺ entry into presynaptic knob (1); less neurotransmitter released (1); less depolarisation of postsynaptic neurone / fewer impulses to effector (1).
4. Any three: same snail / size / species; same force and position of touch; temperature; humidity; light; time between touches.
5. Classical: associates a new stimulus with an existing stimulus / response, e.g. Pavlov's dog salivating to a bell (2); operant: associates own action with reward / punishment, e.g. rat pressing lever for food (2).
6. Advantages (2): protection; cooperative hunting; sharing food information; care of young. Disadvantages (2): competition; disease spread; more conspicuous; aggression.
7. Order established by signals, so less fighting / injury / energy wasted (1); stable group / dominant individuals get resources, others still gain protection (1).
8. Females choose males with longer / brighter tails (1); tail signals health / good genes (1); males with longer tails mate more / have more offspring (1); alleles for long tails (and the preference) increase in frequency over generations (1).
